# The Wrong Verdict

*Book One*

A Star Wars: The Clone Wars alternate-universe fanfiction.

**Summary:** AU from "The Wrong Jedi." Anakin never catches the bomber. Ahsoka is convicted, sentenced to death, then "mercifully" given life in a frozen Republic prison. Anakin sinks deeper into the Chancellor's debt, a guilty friend keeps a terrible secret, and a clone discovers what his orders were always for. Book One of a series.

**Content note:** Dark themes (imprisonment, betrayal, despair, deaths of named characters, choking). Violence without graphic gore. No romance beyond canon Anakin/Padmé.

**Disclaimer:** Star Wars and all related characters belong to Lucasfilm and Disney. This is a non-commercial work of fan fiction. Original characters and story are the author's own.

**Divergence point:** Everything up to the end of Season 5's "Wrong Jedi" arc follows canon, except for one choice Anakin makes in the lower levels of Coruscant.

# Part One: The Verdict

## Chapter 1: The Masked Jedi

The Jedi Council had given Anakin Skywalker until dawn to save his Padawan, and he had spent the first four hours of it wading through runoff in the old munitions district, following a trail he could not see.

The water came up past his boots. It was warm, which was worse than cold, and it smelled of coolant and rust and something sweet that had gone bad a long time ago. Six hundred levels above him the senators were asleep, and the Council was asleep, or meditating, which in Anakin's experience was mostly the same thing with better posture. Admiral Tarkin was probably asleep too. Men like Tarkin slept very well.

*The Council will give the Admiral its answer at first light,* Obi-Wan had said in the corridor outside the chamber, with that careful, reasonable voice he used when he already knew Anakin was going to do something stupid. *Go and rest. There is nothing more to be done tonight.*

There was one thing. There was Ventress.

She had been in the warehouse when they found Ahsoka on her knees among the nano-droid crates, staring at her own empty hands. She had been there, and then she hadn't, and nobody but Ahsoka seemed to think that mattered. Fox's people had written her into the report as an accomplice. Tarkin had read the report aloud to the Council in a tone that suggested he had enjoyed every line.

Anakin stopped at a junction where three drainage tunnels met under a cracked lumen strip. He closed his eyes and let the sounds go: the drip, the distant thunder of a cargo line, the hum of the city pressing down on everything below it.

He had fought Asajj Ventress more times than he could count. On Teth. On Kamino. In the dark, on ships, in places he didn't like to remember. Her presence in the Force had always felt to him like a bruise that had gone cold, an ache you could locate with your eyes shut. It was faint now, fainter than he'd ever felt it. She was hurt. She was trying to be small.

But she was there. Up and to the left.

He opened his eyes and went up.

* * *

The maintenance gallery hung over the Dovrin Line transit platform like a balcony nobody was supposed to stand on. It was a narrow lattice of grating and old cable runs, strung between the support pylons that held up a hab stack twenty stories tall, a whole crooked tower of apartments bolted to the underside of the level above. Through the grating, Anakin could see the platform below: a long slab of ferrocrete under flickering signal lights, and more people than he would have expected at this hour. Shift workers. Haulers. A Gran woman with a sleeping child on her shoulder. A pair of loader droids arguing in binary about a crate.

Ventress was sitting with her back against a pylon at the far end of the gallery, one knee drawn up, a chemical cold pack pressed to the side of her skull. She did not look up when he dropped onto the grating.

"Skywalker." She said his name the way other people spat. "If you've come to arrest me, you'll have to wait. I'm in the middle of being humiliated."

"I'm not here to arrest you."

"Then you're here to fight me, and I'll warn you now, you'll find it disappointing." She lowered the cold pack. A long purple bruise ran from her temple down across her cheekbone, and there was dried blood at the corner of her mouth. Her hands were empty. Her belt was empty. Anakin had never once seen her without the curved hilts at her hips, and the absence of them was so wrong that for a moment he simply stared.

She saw where he was looking. Her mouth thinned.

"Yes," she said. "Go on. Enjoy it."

"Who took them?"

"If I knew that, they'd be dead and I'd be armed." She pushed herself higher against the pylon, wincing. "Someone in a mask came out of the rafters in that warehouse and put me on the floor before I saw them move. When I woke up, my sabers were gone and your little Padawan was being dragged out by a dozen of Fox's toy soldiers. A very productive evening for everyone."

Anakin crouched, putting himself at her eye level, keeping his hands loose and away from his own saber. "They're going to expel her," he said. "At dawn. The Council's going to hand her to a military tribunal so Tarkin can try her for the bombing, and for Letta Turmond, and for the three clones who died outside her cell. He wants her executed."

For a long moment Ventress just looked at him. Something moved behind her face and was put away again.

"And you want me to stand in front of a Republic court," she said, "and tell them she's innocent."

"You were with her. You know she didn't do it."

"I know she *couldn't* have done it. That's not the same thing, and no court in this galaxy cares about the difference." She laughed, a short dry sound that turned into a cough. "Your Padawan couldn't plan a bombing if you drew her the schematics. She spent half of our time together lecturing me about trust. Do you know how tedious that is, Skywalker? Being lectured about trust by someone who's been betrayed by everyone she trusts?"

"Then help her."

"Why would I?"

Because you like her, Anakin thought, and had the sense not to say it. "Because whoever framed her took your sabers," he said instead. "And I'm the only person on Coruscant who wants to find them as badly as you do."

That landed. He saw it land. Ventress turned the cold pack over in her hands, her long pale fingers working at the seam.

"Whoever was in that mask," she said slowly, "knew where we would be. They were already there, waiting in the dark, with a story set up and nano-droids stacked in crates like a gift. Nobody followed us. I would know. And there's only one reason they could have known where to wait, because your girl told—"

Anakin felt it a heartbeat before he heard it. A shift in the air above them, like a held breath let go.

He threw himself sideways. Ventress was already moving.

Two blades of red light came down through the place where her head had been and cut the pylon at her back a hand's width deep. Molten metal sprayed across the grating. The figure that had dropped from the cable runs above landed in a crouch between them, cloak settling, and rose.

It wore a mask. A bone-white thing with dark slanted eye-slits, made to look like something out of a Dathomiri nightmare, and a deep hood over it. The two lightsabers in its hands had curved hilts.

"Oh," said Ventress, very softly. "Oh, you *thief*."

* * *

It went at her first.

That was the thing Anakin would replay for a long time afterward: that it didn't care about him at all. It turned its back on a Jedi Knight with a lit saber to go after an unarmed woman with a head wound, and it moved so fast and so cleanly that Ventress, who had been killing people with those blades for half her life, barely got out of the way.

She didn't try to block. She had nothing to block with. She flung out a hand and the Force went with it, a shove that should have put the masked figure through the railing. The figure leaned into it like someone walking into wind, and kept coming.

Anakin's saber snapped on. He went in low and hard, and the figure turned, one red blade dropping to catch his, the other still chasing Ventress across the gallery.

"Don't let it corner you!" he shouted.

"Thank you, General," Ventress snarled. "I would *never* have thought of that."

She ripped a coil of cable loose from the ceiling and lashed it across the figure's mask. It staggered. Anakin pressed, cutting high, low, high again, driving with his shoulders, using his weight the way he did when he wanted a fight over fast.

The figure gave ground, but it gave it the way water gives. Each of his blows met a blade that was already there. Circles, tight and closed and patient, both red sabers turning in short economical arcs that never wasted a centimeter, never reached, never showed off. It didn't fight like Ventress. Ventress fought like a storm, all flourish and fury. This was something else.

This was the training salle. This was every morning of his childhood, a line of initiates in grey tunics drilling the same defensive sequence until their arms shook, while a Master walked the rows and corrected the angle of their wrists.

The thought chilled him so badly he almost missed the next cut.

"Who are you?" he said.

The mask gave him nothing. The figure feinted at his face, he parried, and it used his parry to spin away from him toward Ventress, who had circled behind it with a length of broken conduit in both hands like a club.

Ventress swung. The figure ducked under it and drove one red blade up, and Ventress twisted aside so that the saber only took the conduit and not her ribs. She stumbled. The figure's free hand came up, open-palmed, and Anakin felt the Force gather in it like a fist closing.

It struck Ventress in the chest.

She went backward through the gallery railing as if the railing weren't there. Anakin lunged, reaching, his own hand outstretched, feeling for her in the Force. For one instant he had her. He could feel her weight, the pull of her falling, the fury in her.

Then something hit him from the side, a second shove, and his grip broke.

Ventress fell into the gap between the gallery and the next pylon, past the edge of the transit platform, down into the dark beneath it where the old cargo lines ran. He saw her pale face turned up toward him. She didn't scream. She was gone before she could have.

"*No*—"

He reached for her again and found nothing. No presence. No cold bruise in the Force. Either she was dead, or she was hiding from him the way she'd been hiding from everyone all night, and from up here he could not tell which.

When he turned, the masked figure was already running.

* * *

It went down, not up. That surprised him. It vaulted the railing on the platform side and dropped three stories onto the roof of a stalled transit car, rolled, and came up sprinting along the car's spine toward the far end of the platform.

Anakin went after it.

He landed hard on the car roof and felt the impact in his knees. Below him, people were starting to look up: at the noise, at the red light, at the Jedi running across the top of the train. Somebody screamed. The Gran woman pulled her child tighter. The two loader droids stopped arguing.

The figure reached the end of the car and leapt, not to the platform but to the nearest of the great support pylons that held the hab stack up, catching a maintenance rung with one hand. The red sabers had gone dark. It clung there for a second, and Anakin saw it press something against the pylon's base, something small and silver that caught the signal lights.

He knew what it was before his mind supplied the word. He had spent the last week staring at pictures of them.

Nano-droids.

"Everybody *off the platform!*" he roared. "*Now!*"

The figure kicked away from the pylon and dropped onto the catwalk on the far side, and the silver thing on the pylon flared white.

There was no fireball. That was the horrible part, how quiet it was. A hiss, a crawling glitter like frost spreading across metal, and then the pylon's base simply came apart, durasteel sloughing off it in sheets as if it had been eaten from the inside. The pylon bowed. The ones on either side of it groaned with the new weight.

Above the platform, twenty stories of hab stack began to lean.

It came slowly at first. A few bolts sheared off the ceiling with sharp cracks like blasterfire, and dust and old paint rained down across the crowd. Then a balcony tore loose from the third level of the stack and fell, and hit the ferrocrete twenty meters from the Gran woman and her child, and the whole platform began to scream.

Anakin looked up at the tower of apartments. Lights were on in some of the windows. There were people in there. There were people *asleep* in there.

He looked across the platform at the catwalk.

The masked figure was standing at the end of it, beside an access hatch into the service tunnels. It wasn't running anymore. It had stopped, half turned, and it was watching him.

Waiting, he thought. It was waiting to see what he would do.

He could reach it. Three jumps. Maybe four. He could have it on the ground with its mask torn off in less time than it would take the hab stack to finish falling. He could drag it up six hundred levels by the hood and throw it on the floor of the Council chamber and say, *There. There's your bomber. Now give her back.*

Somewhere in the hab stack, a child started crying.

Anakin lifted his hands.

He had held up a great many things in his life. Ships. Walls. A gunship's weight, once, while Rex's men climbed out of it. He had never held up anything like this. The Force came to him in a rush, and he flung it upward and *caught* the stack, felt the impossible mass of it settle against him the way a mountain might settle on a man's shoulders. Ferrocrete and durasteel and plumbing and furniture and sleeping people, all of it straining toward the ground, all of it pressing down on the inside of his skull.

The leaning stopped.

He heard himself make a sound through his teeth. His right hand, the metal one, had locked open with the fingers splayed so hard that the servos whined. His left hand trembled.

"*Move!*" he shouted, and his voice cracked. "Get out from under it! Go! *Go!*"

They went. He couldn't watch them, not properly, but he felt them: a tide of panic flowing toward the far exits, a knot of people at the stairs, an old Ithorian who had fallen and someone dragging him up by the arm. Doors in the hab stack banged open. Figures in sleep-clothes came pouring down the external stairways, carrying children, carrying nothing, carrying a caged bird, carrying a holo-projector, of all things. The Gran woman ran past beneath his outstretched hands with her child pressed to her shoulder and did not look back.

The weight shifted. Something inside the stack gave, a floor dropping onto the floor below, and the whole tower slid half a meter toward him. He dropped to one knee. Blood was running warm out of his nose and over his lip. He could taste it.

*Not yet. Not yet. Not yet.*

He did not look at the catwalk. He did not let himself.

When the last of them had cleared the stairs, a pair of dock workers who turned at the bottom to stare up at him, he found the empty end of the platform with his mind. He began to let the stack down. Slowly. A centimeter at a time. Easing it away from the remaining pylons and onto the bare ferrocrete where no one stood, letting each floor settle onto the one below it with long groaning crunches that shook the platform under his knee.

The last of it came to rest with a sound like a ship's hull taking a hit. Dust rolled across the platform in a grey wave and swallowed him.

Anakin let go.

For a while he just knelt there in the dust, breathing. His hands were still raised. He lowered them. The metal fingers would not close properly; he had to force them with his other hand.

Then he made himself look at the catwalk.

The access hatch at the end of it was open. The catwalk was empty.

Of course it was.

* * *

The Coruscant Guard arrived nineteen minutes later, which Anakin knew because he had nothing better to do than count.

They came down the platform stairs in a disciplined red-and-white stream, rifles up, sweeping corners, and spread out across the ruin as if they expected the ruin to open fire. Medics broke off toward the injured huddled at the exits. Two troopers started cordoning off the collapsed stack with glowing tape, which struck Anakin as optimistic.

Commander Fox walked straight to him. He didn't take his helmet off.

"General Skywalker." Fox looked from him to the wreckage and back, and the angle of the helmet suggested a man doing sums. "Sir, are you injured?"

"I'm fine."

"You're bleeding, sir."

Anakin wiped his lip with the back of his glove and looked at the smear. "Then I'm fine and bleeding."

Fox let that go. He had a datapad in one hand and a recorder clipped at his collar, its small light glowing red. "I'll need your account, General. For the record."

So Anakin gave it to him. Ventress in the maintenance gallery. The masked figure dropping from the cable runs. Red lightsabers with curved hilts. The fight, the fall, the charge on the pylon. The stack.

Fox listened without interrupting. When Anakin finished, there was a short silence filled by the hiss of a medic's spray and someone, somewhere, sobbing.

"A masked figure," Fox said, "armed with two red lightsabers. Curved hilts."

"Yes."

"Sir, with respect. That is the description on file for Asajj Ventress."

"Ventress was standing next to me, Commander. It threw her off a ledge."

"You saw her body?"

Anakin opened his mouth, and shut it again.

"No," he said.

Fox made a note. Anakin could not see the note, but he could see the way the trooper's thumb moved on the datapad, short and efficient. "Then it's possible," Fox said, "that what you witnessed was a staged separation. Ventress and an accomplice. The accomplice draws you off, Ventress drops out of sight and goes to ground, and the two of them meet up later. She's done similar things before."

"That's not what happened."

"Yes, sir. I'm only noting the possibility." Fox's helmet tilted toward the ruined pylon. "The accomplice used nano-droid explosives. Same type as the Temple bombing. Same type we recovered from the warehouse where Commander Tano was apprehended."

"*Former* Commander, by morning," said another trooper, quietly, from somewhere behind Fox.

Fox turned his head a few degrees. The trooper found something very interesting to do with his cordon tape.

Anakin stood up. It took him longer than he would have liked.

"Commander Tano," he said, "is locked in a cell at the military base under your own guard. She didn't plant that charge. She didn't throw Ventress off that gallery. Whoever did is still out here, and they fought like—" He stopped.

Fox waited. "Like what, sir?"

*Like a Jedi.* The words were right there, and he could not say them. Not here, not to a Coruscant Guard recorder with Tarkin waiting to read the transcript over breakfast. Not when the Council was going to vote at dawn on whether a Jedi had already done exactly this.

"Like someone who knew what they were doing," Anakin said.

"Yes, sir." Fox made another note. "It'll go in the report. All of it."

He said it as if it were meant to be reassuring.

Anakin looked past him at the dust still drifting over the platform, at the people huddled under foil blankets near the stairs, at the Gran woman sitting on a bench with her child asleep again in her lap as if nothing at all had happened. Nobody was dead. The medics had said so, twice, as if they couldn't quite believe it themselves. A broken arm, a lot of cuts, an old Ithorian with a cracked rib. Nobody was dead.

He had done that. He knew he had done that.

And he knew, with a clarity that made him feel sick, that the one person on Coruscant who could have proved Ahsoka innocent had been standing at the end of that catwalk, watching him, and he had let it walk away.

His comlink chimed. He looked at it. *Kenobi.* The fourth call tonight. Above the city, somewhere far beyond all those levels of durasteel, the sky would be starting to grey.

He didn't answer. He didn't know how he would say it.

*I had it, Master. I had them, and I chose.*

He turned off the comlink and walked toward the stairs, and behind him Fox's recorder went on glowing, small and red and patient, taking down every word.

* * *

Three levels up and half a kilometer east, in an alley that smelled of hot grease and wet stone, Barriss Offee stopped running because her legs would not let her run any further.

She stood with her back against a wall, beneath a vent that was breathing out steam in slow, damp gusts, and listened. Nothing. A speeder's whine, far off. The drip of condensation off a broken gutter. No boots. No shouts. No Anakin Skywalker coming down out of the dark with his saber lit and his face like a thunderhead.

He had not come. She had known he would not. She had built everything on knowing it, and still she had run as though he were a step behind her the whole way.

Her hands were shaking so badly she had to try three times to get her fingers under the edge of the mask.

It came away with a soft sucking sound. The night air hit her face, and she tipped her head back against the wall and breathed it in, cold and filthy and sweet, as if she had been drowning. Her skin was slick under the hood. The diamond markings on her cheeks itched where the mask's padding had pressed against them for hours.

She looked down at the thing in her hand. Bone-white. Eye-slits. A monster's face, made to be remembered. She had chosen it because it would make people think of Ventress, and it had. It had worked. All of it had worked.

In her other hand were the two lightsabers.

They were heavier than her own, and the curve of the hilts sat strangely against her palm, pulling her wrist into angles her training had never asked of it. She could feel the crystals inside them, even dark. They were not like her own crystal. They did not sing. They throbbed, low and sullen, like a wound that had healed wrong, and every time she had ignited them tonight she had felt the red light go into her arms like poison into a vein.

She should not have used them on Ventress. She should not have needed to. She had only meant to see where Skywalker was going when she watched him leave the Temple alone after dark, and she had followed him down, level by level, to the one person in the lower city who could still put a name to the warehouse. The woman had been sitting in the open, talking, and Barriss had crouched in the cables above her and listened to her talk about the warehouse and about who had known where to wait, and had understood exactly where that sentence ended. With a name. With a comlink call to a friend in the Temple, made by a frightened girl in the dark who had trusted the one person she shouldn't.

So she had dropped. And Ventress had gone over the railing. And Barriss had not looked down to see where she landed, because if she had looked, she would have had to know.

She closed her eyes.

*It was necessary.* She had been telling herself that for weeks, in a voice that sounded more and more like someone else's. The Jedi had become soldiers. The Jedi had become generals. The Jedi sent clones to die by the thousand and called it peace, and nobody would see it, nobody would *ever* see it, unless someone made them. Unless someone held up a mirror so terrible they could not look away.

She thought of the platform. Of the pylon coming apart under the nano-droids, and the hab stack leaning, and the lights on in the windows.

She had counted on him. That was the part she could not stop turning over. She had stood on that catwalk and watched Anakin Skywalker raise his hands, and she had known, *known* down to her bones, that he would choose the people over her. Because whatever the Order had made of the rest of them, there was still that much Jedi left in him. She had wagered twenty stories of sleeping strangers on the goodness of a man whose student she was trying to destroy.

And she had won.

Barriss bent over, abruptly, and was sick against the alley wall.

When it was over she wiped her mouth on her sleeve and straightened, and found she was still holding the sabers. She had not let go of them even then.

At the end of the alley, set into the wall beneath a flickering hazard light, was a refuse chute. She knew where it went. All the chutes in this district fed the incinerators on the bottom level, great furnaces that burned day and night and turned everything the city threw away into ash and heat. She walked to it. She pulled the hatch open. A hot breath rose out of the dark, stinking of scorched plastoid.

She held Ventress's lightsabers over the opening.

All she had to do was open her hand. Two curved hilts, gone. The only thing in the galaxy that tied the masked figure in the warehouse to the masked figure on the Dovrin platform to a Padawan of Luminara Unduli who had been in the Temple clinic, as far as anyone knew, all night. Gone. Ash by morning.

Her fingers did not open.

She stood there with her arm outstretched until it began to ache. She told her hand to let go. She thought of Tarkin, of the tribunal, of the evidence lockers and the investigators and the questions. She thought about how simple it would be.

She thought about Ahsoka, alone in her cell, sitting with her back straight because she would not give anyone the satisfaction of seeing it bent.

Her fingers did not open.

Slowly, Barriss drew her arm back. She let the chute's hatch swing shut. She wrapped the sabers in a fold of her cloak, and then wrapped them again, and pushed them down to the bottom of the healer's satchel on her hip, under the bacta patches and the wound-sealer and the small neat rolls of bandage. The mask went in after them.

Tomorrow, she told herself. She would get rid of them tomorrow, when her hands were steady.

She pulled up her hood and walked out of the alley, toward the lift banks that would carry her up and up, through six hundred levels of light, to the Temple. There were memorial candles in an alcove above the rebuilt hangar for the dead of the bombing. She had lit one every morning since it happened. She would light one this morning too.

And then, when the sun came up, she would go and stand quietly among the healers while the Council decided what to do with her best friend.

## Chapter 2: Expelled

The clone on the other side of the energy field was reading her lightsabers their charges.

He did it from a datapad, in the flat voice of a man working through a list, and he did not look up while he did it. "Item four-one-seven. Lightsaber, primary, green crystal, hilt of Togruta-Jedi construction. Recovered at the scene of the deaths of CT-two-two-six-one, CT-four-four-zero-eight, and CT-nine-one-seven-zero, Coruscant Guard. Blade-pattern wounds consistent." He thumbed the screen. "Item four-one-eight. Lightsaber, secondary, yellow-green crystal, shoto length. Recovered at the same scene. Blade-pattern wounds consistent." He looked up then, finally. His helmet was red-striped, and there was a fresh scorch along the edge of one pauldron. "You're required to be informed of what's being held against you. You've been informed."

"I didn't kill them," Ahsoka said.

The guard lowered the datapad. For a moment she thought he might answer. Then he turned on his heel and walked back to his post at the end of the corridor, and she was alone again with the hum of the field and the cold weight of the binders on her wrists.

Consistent. That was the word they kept using. The wounds were consistent with her blades because they had been made with her blades. Somebody had walked into the evidence locker, or wherever Fox had put her sabers after he took them at Letta Turmond's door, and had carried them down this same corridor and cut down three men in their own armor. Then they had left a keycard where a frightened prisoner would find it, and opened the door, and let her walk out into the middle of the bodies.

She had thought it was Anakin, at first. She had picked up that card and believed, for the space of a breath, that her Master had found a way to get her out. She had been wrong about that, and wrong about what was waiting in the warehouse her friend had sent her to, and wrong about how quickly the truth would catch up with her. She was beginning to be very tired of being wrong.

The cell was three paces by four. The bunk was a grey slab jutting out of the wall, the floor was grey, the ceiling was grey with a single light panel that never dimmed. The Republic Center for Military Operations did not believe in windows for people like her. She had not slept. She had sat on the edge of the bunk all night with her back straight and her binders resting on her knees, because she knew there was a holocam behind the light panel, and she knew who would be watching it.

The field at the cell door flickered and went down. Commander Fox came in alone.

He did not take off his helmet. He never did, around her. He stood just inside the threshold with his hands clasped behind him and regarded her through the black T of his visor.

"Thought you'd want to know," he said. "Your partner's friend made another appearance last night. Lower levels. The Dovrin Line platform."

Ahsoka's montrals prickled. "Ventress?"

"Ventress was there. So was a masked accomplice, carrying her red blades. They took a support pylon down with the same nano-droids we found in your warehouse. A twenty-story hab stack nearly went over onto a transit platform full of people."

She was on her feet before she had decided to stand. "How many—"

"None." Fox let that sit, as if he resented having to give it to her. "General Skywalker held the building up. Long enough for everybody to get clear. The accomplice walked away while he did it. So did Ventress, as far as we can tell. We haven't found a body."

Ahsoka sat back down slowly. Her knees did not seem entirely her own.

No one had died. Anakin had held up a building. Of course he had. She could see it as clearly as if she had been there: his feet planted, his jaw set, the whole weight of a tower bearing down on him through the Force while somewhere above him the one person who could have cleared her name slipped away into the dark. And he had let them go. He had looked at a platform full of strangers and at his Padawan's whole future, and he had chosen the strangers.

She was proud of him. It sat in her chest beside something colder, and she could not tell yet which one would win.

"The accomplice," she said. "Masked. Ventress's sabers."

"That's what I said."

"Then they're not her accomplice. That's the person who took those sabers off her in the warehouse. Ventress was unconscious on the floor when I got there. Someone in a mask knocked her out and used her own blades on me. It's the same person, Commander. They're still out there, and they're still—"

"I've logged them as an associate of Asajj Ventress," Fox said, "pending evidence otherwise. You were apprehended in a room full of nano-droid crates. I'll leave the theories to your counsel, when you have some." He turned toward the door. "Be ready in twenty minutes. The Jedi Council has asked to see you."

"Asked," Ahsoka repeated.

Fox paused at the threshold. "The Admiral asked them first," he said, and the field hummed back up behind him.

* * *

They took her to the Temple in a closed transport with no viewports, two Coruscant Guard troopers on either side of her and Fox up front with the pilot. Nobody spoke. When the hatch opened she smelled the Temple before she saw it: stone and old incense and, under that, faintly, the scorched-metal ghost of the hangar that had not yet been fully rebuilt. Her stomach turned over.

The processional hallway was wide and high and lined with columns, and the morning light came through the tall windows in long slanting bars. There were Jedi in the hall. Not many. They stood in twos and threes near the columns, as if they had all happened to be passing, and they went quiet as the guards walked her through. She knew most of their faces. Knights she had sparred with. A Master who had taught her to read star charts when she was nine. A cluster of younglings being hurried away down a side corridor by their crèchemaster, small heads turning to look back over their shoulders.

She kept her chin up. She kept her eyes forward. It was the only thing left she was certain she could do.

Near the great doors to the Council lift, a handful of healers in pale robes stood together beside a column. One of them was Barriss. She had her hood up and her hands folded inside her sleeves, and she looked as though she had not slept either. When Ahsoka passed, Barriss lifted one hand from her sleeve, just slightly, just enough. Her fingers were shaking.

Ahsoka managed something that was almost a smile. Then the lift doors closed between them.

The Council chamber was full. It was always full, she supposed, but she had never stood in it like this, in the center of the ring with her hands bound in front of her and two clones at her back, and the twelve chairs had never seemed so high. Master Yoda sat with both hands folded on the head of his stick and his ears low. Master Windu sat very straight. Master Ki-Adi-Mundi, Master Shaak Ti, Master Saesee Tiin, the holograms of the members who were offworld flickering blue in their seats. Obi-Wan sat with his chin resting against his knuckles, and he was looking at her, at least. That was something.

Master Plo was looking at the floor.

Ahsoka could not see his eyes behind the goggles of his mask. She had never been able to. But she had known him since she was three years old, since he had come to her village on Shili and knelt down on the dirt to be level with her, and she knew the angle of his head the way she knew her own hands. His face was turned toward the tiles at the center of the ring, a little to the left of her feet. It stayed there.

Anakin was standing. There was no chair for him; he was not a Council member. He stood against the curve of the wall behind Obi-Wan's seat with his arms folded and his gloved right hand clenched hard around his left elbow, and when she came in he took one step toward her before Master Windu's glance stopped him.

"Ahsoka Tano," Master Windu said. "You know why you're here."

"I know what I've been accused of," she said. "I didn't do any of it."

"That is not the question before this Council."

"Then what is?"

A ripple went around the ring, small and quiet. Master Ki-Adi-Mundi's mouth tightened.

"Admiral Tarkin has made a formal request on behalf of the Republic military," Master Windu said. "The deaths of Letta Turmond and of three Coruscant Guard troopers occurred inside a military installation. The Senate has demanded a military tribunal, open and accountable to the Republic, and not an internal Jedi proceeding. The Admiral's position is that the Order cannot be trusted to judge one of its own." He paused. "In light of the bombing, and of how many in the Senate already believe the Jedi answer to no one, this Council has concluded that refusing would do the Order more damage than we can afford."

"So you're giving me to them."

"We are removing you from the Order," said Master Windu, "so that the Republic may try you under its own law."

There it was. She had known it was coming since Fox's voice at the cell door. She had known it all night, sitting on the edge of the bunk. Knowing it did not seem to matter at all.

"That's the same thing," she said, "with longer words."

"Master Windu." Anakin had come off the wall. "You can't do this. Not now. Not after last night."

"General Skywalker—"

"There's a second suspect. I saw them. Masked, carrying Ventress's sabers, using the same nano-droids from the warehouse, while Ahsoka was locked in a cell on the other side of the city. Whoever bombed this Temple was on that platform last night. It wasn't her." His voice was rising. He did not seem to notice. "Give me time. A week. Give me three days."

"The masked individual has been logged by Commander Fox as an associate of Ventress," Master Ki-Adi-Mundi said. "Ahsoka herself was apprehended with Ventress. A second conspirator does not clear the first."

"She wasn't with Ventress, she was—"

"The decision has been made, Anakin." Master Windu's voice did not rise to meet his. It simply didn't move. "The vote was taken this morning."

Anakin turned to look at Obi-Wan. It was not a long look, but Ahsoka saw it, and she saw Obi-Wan meet it, and she saw the very small shake of his head. Not *no*. Something more like *I tried*.

"Then vote again," Anakin said.

Nobody answered him.

Master Yoda stirred at last. He looked at Ahsoka for a long moment, his great old eyes heavy-lidded, and she thought he was going to say something to her, something that would make the shape of this make sense. But all he said was, "Sorrow, there is, in this. For all of us." And then he lowered his eyes as well.

Ahsoka found she had nothing to say to that. Sorrow did not get anyone out of a cell.

"There is one more thing," Master Windu said. "Your Padawan beads."

Her hands moved before she could stop them, the binders clicking, her fingers rising halfway toward the beads that hung from the base of her left montral. She had worn them since the day Anakin had taken her as his student. She had added to them on Christophsis and on Ryloth, on Mon Cala, on Felucia. Each bead was a thing she had survived. She could not take them off with her wrists bound, and she found, abruptly, that she would not have taken them off if her hands had been free. If they wanted them, they could come and get them.

Nobody moved.

Master Plo's hands had tightened on the arms of his chair. Obi-Wan had closed his eyes. Anakin stood in the center of the chamber, three steps away from her, and his face had gone white around the mouth.

"Don't," he said, to the whole room. "Don't you dare ask me."

"I would not," Master Windu said quietly. He rose from his chair. "It should be done by someone who voted for it."

He crossed the chamber. His boots made no sound on the tiles. He stopped in front of her, and he was much taller than she was, and for a moment she thought he would hesitate. He did not. His fingers found the clasp beneath the beads and undid it, and the beaded chain came away from her montral with a small dry whisper, a sensation she had felt every night for years when she took it off to sleep and had never once thought about.

The weight was gone. It had not been much of a weight. She had not known how much of her balance it held.

Master Windu closed his hand around the beads. "I'm sorry," he said, low, for her alone, and it sounded like he meant it, and that was somehow the worst part.

He went back to his chair. Fox's hand settled on her shoulder from behind.

Ahsoka turned around without being told. She did not look at Master Plo again. She did not look at Anakin, because if she looked at Anakin she was going to come apart in the middle of the Council chamber, in front of all of them, and she would rather die.

That was a thought she would remember later, and not fondly.

* * *

The senator came in the afternoon.

Ahsoka heard her before she saw her, a voice in the corridor outside, crisp and polite and absolutely unwilling to be turned around. "—and if the Commander would like to file an objection, he's welcome to, but he'll find the Judiciary codes are very clear about a defendant's right to counsel, and I'm afraid I'm in a hurry." Then the field dropped, and Padmé Amidala stepped into the cell with a datapad under one arm and a guard hovering uncertainly behind her.

"Thank you," she told him. "That will be all."

The guard looked at Ahsoka. Then at the senator. Then he retreated, and the field came back up.

Padmé was wearing something dark blue and plain, for her, with her hair pinned back tight. She looked around the cell once, briefly, taking it in—the slab, the light, the binders—and something went across her face and was put away. Then she sat down at the far end of the bunk as if it were a couch in her own apartment and set the datapad on her knee.

"I'm told the Council finished with you this morning," she said.

"They finished with me a while ago," Ahsoka said. "This morning was just the paperwork."

Padmé's mouth tightened at one corner, not quite a smile. "I'm sorry. I wanted to be there. They wouldn't admit anyone from the Senate." She glanced at the side of Ahsoka's head, at the bare place below her montral, and then very deliberately did not look at it again. "I'm here to offer to represent you before the tribunal. If you'll have me."

Ahsoka stared at her. "You're a senator."

"I'm aware."

"You'll be defending someone they think bombed the Temple. Someone they think killed a prisoner and three clones. Half the Senate will—"

"Half the Senate thinks most things I do are a mistake," Padmé said. "The other half just hasn't caught up yet." She tilted her head. "A tribunal is a military court, but the defendant may choose any counsel with standing before the Republic Judiciary. Senators have standing. It's an old provision. Nobody's used it in forty years. I looked." She tapped the datapad. "I'd rather it was me than whoever the military assigns you. They'll give you someone very decent and very junior who has been told exactly how hard to try."

Ahsoka looked down at her binders. Her throat had gone tight, and she was furious with it.

"Did Anakin ask you to do this?"

"Yes," Padmé said. "Last night. Before he went down to the lower levels." A pause. "I'd already decided."

There was something in the way she said his name. Ahsoka had heard it before, on the edges of things—at receptions, on the bridge of a cruiser, once in a hallway in 500 Republica when she had come around a corner too quickly. She had never let herself think about what it was. She did not let herself think about it now.

"All right," she said. "Yes. Thank you."

"Don't thank me yet." Padmé activated the datapad. "Tell me everything. From the bombing to the warehouse. Don't leave anything out because it makes you look bad, or because you think it doesn't matter. I'll decide what doesn't matter."

So Ahsoka told her. She told her about Jackar Bowmani and the nano-droids and Letta Turmond asking for her by name. About Fox taking her sabers at the door. About Letta saying a Jedi had been behind it, and then grabbing at her throat, and dying on the floor of her cell before she could say who. About the keycard and the bodies and running. She told her about the lower levels and Ventress—Padmé's eyebrows went up and stayed there—and about the comm call to a friend in the Temple—she left the name out, and Padmé, watching her, let it go for now—and the warehouse, and the figure in the mask.

"The figure in the mask," Padmé said, when she had finished. "Tell me about that again. Slowly."

Ahsoka closed her eyes and made herself see it. "Hooded. The mask was white, like bone. The eyes were slanted. They didn't say a word. Not once. They came at Ventress from behind while she was turned toward me, knocked her flat, and took her sabers off her belt before she hit the floor. Then they came at me."

"Could you tell anything about them? Size, species?"

"Smaller than Ventress. My height, maybe a little more. Not a droid, not a clone." Ahsoka hesitated. "They were good. Really good."

Padmé's stylus paused over the screen. "Good how?"

Ahsoka opened her mouth, and closed it again.

"I'll tell Anakin," she said.

Padmé held her eyes a while. Then she nodded and wrote something down, and did not push, and Ahsoka liked her more for it than she would have been able to explain.

At the door, waiting for the guard, Padmé turned back.

"I won't lie to you," she said. "This is going to be very hard. The evidence is bad, and the people who want you convicted want it for reasons that have nothing to do with you. But they have to prove it. You don't have to prove anything. Remember that, when they start talking."

"And if they prove it anyway?"

Padmé did not answer right away. "Then we make it cost them," she said.

* * *

Anakin came after the light panel had dimmed one notch, which was the only way the Center for Military Operations admitted it was night.

She knew it was him before the field went down. She would have known him through a durasteel wall. His presence in the Force was a storm on the horizon on the best of days, and tonight it was something worse, something knotted and close and turning in on itself. When he stepped into the cell he stopped just inside the threshold the way Fox had, and for a moment he just looked at her.

He looked terrible. There was a dark crust of dried blood just inside one nostril that he had missed when he washed. His robes were the same ones from the Council chamber, and they had been the same ones the night before, too, she would bet; the hem was grey with lower-levels grime. His right hand hung at his side, and as she watched, he flexed the mechanical fingers once, twice, as if they did not quite want to close.

"Hey, Snips," he said.

She had promised herself she was not going to cry. She managed it, barely.

"I heard you held up a building," she said.

He let out a breath that was almost a laugh. "It was a small building."

"Fox said twenty stories."

"Fox exaggerates."

"Fox has never exaggerated anything in his life." She moved over on the bunk, and after a second he came and sat down beside her, heavily, the way he sat in the cockpit of the *Twilight* after a long run. He smelled of smoke and engine coolant and stone dust. "You had them," she said. "Didn't you. The one in the mask."

He was silent for so long that she thought he was not going to answer.

"They stopped," he said at last. "On a catwalk, while I was holding it. They stopped and they watched me. I could have—" His mechanical hand closed into a fist on his knee and stayed that way. "There were kids on that platform, Ahsoka. A Gran woman with a baby. An Ithorian who couldn't run fast enough. If I'd let go—"

"Then they'd be dead," she said. "And you'd be you, and you wouldn't be able to live with it. I wouldn't want you to." She bumped his shoulder with hers, very lightly, the binders clinking. "You did the right thing, Master."

"I'm not your Master," he said. "They made sure of that this morning."

That landed somewhere under her ribs. She let it.

"Then listen to me as somebody who isn't your Padawan," she said. "Because I need you to hear this, and you're the only one I can tell. Ventress is the only witness. She's the only other person who saw the one in the mask up close. She was in the warehouse. She was on the platform. If anybody knows who they are, or where they came from, or how they knew where we'd be, it's her."

"Ventress was knocked off a ledge into a four-hundred-level drop."

"Ventress," Ahsoka said, "is too spiteful to die from falling."

That time he did laugh, short and surprised, and it seemed to hurt him.

"There's something else." She lowered her voice, though there was nothing to lower it against but the hum of the field and the holocam behind the light. "In the warehouse. When the masked one came at me. I've fought a lot of people, Anakin. Droids, bounty hunters, Ventress, Grievous. I know what it looks like when somebody's learned to fight from somewhere else." She swallowed. "They didn't. They fought like they'd learned it here. In the salles. The way they closed off my high line. The way they covered when I pressed. It was Temple training. I know it was. I'd stake my life on it."

She felt it in him before she saw it on his face. A stillness, sudden and complete, like a hand closing over a candle flame.

"You saw it too," she said.

He did not look at her. He was looking at the opposite wall, at nothing. "On the platform. They used a Soresu turn on me. Clean as anything Obi-Wan ever drilled into me." His jaw worked. "I almost told Fox."

"Almost?"

"What was I going to say? That whoever bombed the Temple fought like a Jedi?" He turned his head and finally met her eyes, and the look on his face was one she had never seen there before. "You know what Tarkin does with that, Snips? He doesn't hear 'somebody else did it.' He hears 'Jedi conspiracy.' He hears 'the Padawan had help from inside.' He'd thank me for it. He'd put it in the record as a character witness."

She wanted to argue. She found she couldn't.

"So who?" she said instead. "Who in the Temple would—"

"I don't know." His voice had gone rough. "I don't know, and I don't know who to ask, because I don't know who it is." He looked down at his clenched hand and seemed to notice it for the first time, and made it open, finger by finger. "But Ventress might. And I'm going to find her. I swear to you, Ahsoka. I'll take the lower levels apart rivet by rivet if I have to. I'll find her, and I'll make her talk, and I'll bring it in front of that tribunal and make every one of them look at it."

She believed him. That was the trouble with Anakin. She always had.

"Did you leave the keycard?" she asked. "In my cell. Before. The night I got out."

He frowned. "What keycard?"

So that was that. She nodded, slowly, and filed it away with everything else she had been wrong about.

The guard came to the edge of the field and said, "Time, General," in a voice that meant it was well past time and he had been generous. Anakin stood. He looked down at her, and she saw his eyes go to the side of her head, to the empty place below her montral, and stay there.

"They shouldn't have done that," he said. "Not in front of everybody. Not like that."

"Master Windu said it should be someone who voted for it."

"Master Windu," Anakin said, very evenly, "says a lot of things."

He went to the door. The field dropped for him. He was halfway through it when she heard herself say it.

"Skyguy."

He stopped. He did not turn around, not all the way. She saw his shoulders go tight under the dirty robe.

"Find her," she said. "Please."

He nodded once, without looking back, and then he was gone, and the field came up behind him with its low steady hum, and Ahsoka sat on the edge of the bunk and pressed the heels of her bound hands hard against her eyes until she saw colors.

* * *

She tried to meditate.

It should have been easy. It was the first thing they taught you, before you could hold a training saber, before you could read: sit, breathe, reach out. Let the Force move through you the way water moves through stone. She had done it on battlefields with artillery falling. She had done it in the belly of a crashing gunship. She folded her legs on the bunk and rested her bound hands in her lap and closed her eyes, and she breathed.

The Force was there. It was always there. But tonight it felt like a room she had been asked to leave, the furniture still in its place, the lights still on, and her standing at the doorway with no right to go back in.

*Jedi are not supposed to feel like that*, she thought. And then: *But I'm not a Jedi.*

She opened her eyes.

Not a Jedi. She turned the words over carefully, the way she would have turned a strange piece of tech, looking for the seam. She had been a Jedi since before she could remember. Master Plo had found her when she was three and brought her to the Temple, and she had grown up inside it, in the crèche and the salles and the archives, under the great vaulted ceilings with the light coming down. She had never been anything else. She had never wanted to be. Every bead on that chain had been a promise, kept on both sides. She would give the Order everything, and the Order would be what it said it was.

And this morning they had sat in their high chairs and taken it back, and the worst of it—the very worst of it, worse than the beads, worse than Master Windu's hand at her montral—was how quietly it had gone. Nobody had shouted except Anakin. Nobody had stood up except Anakin. Obi-Wan had shaken his head. Master Yoda had spoken about sorrow. Master Plo, who had knelt in the dirt on Shili to be level with a frightened little girl, had looked at the floor.

They had not fought for her. Not one of them. They had weighed her against the Senate and the Admiral and the Holonet, against the Order's name and the war and the bombing and the dead, and they had looked at the scales, and they had let her go.

She had always thought that if the worst happened, the Order would be the wall at her back. That was what it meant to belong somewhere. That no matter how far out you went, there was a place that would come and get you.

There was no wall. There had never been a wall. There had only been a door, and it had always opened in both directions, and she had simply never been on the wrong side of it before.

Ahsoka sat very still on the slab and let that settle into her, all the way down, until she was sure it was true. She did not cry. She was past that, for now. Instead she felt something quiet and hard come into the space where the grief had been, something she did not have a name for yet. It was not anger, not quite. It was not peace. It felt like standing up after a fall and finding out which bones still held.

The field went down.

She looked up. Commander Fox stood in the doorway, and behind him the corridor was dim, the guards' lights reflecting off the polished floor. It was very late. He had no business being here at this hour unless someone had sent him.

He held out a datapad.

"The charging document," he said. "Filed with the Judiciary an hour ago. You're entitled to a copy. Your counsel's been sent one."

Ahsoka took it in her bound hands. The text was dense and formal, column after column of it—the bombing, the conspiracy, Letta Turmond, the three troopers, item four-one-seven and item four-one-eight. She read it without taking any of it in. Her eyes slid down to the bottom of the screen, where the block of official seals was, and the names.

*Presiding: Supreme Chancellor Palpatine.*

*Prosecuting officer: Admiral Wilhuff Tarkin, Republic Navy.*

And beneath that, in the same plain official type as everything else, as if it were one more line in an inventory:

*Penalty sought by the prosecution: death.*

She read it twice. Then she looked up at Fox.

He stood in the doorway with his hands behind his back, his visor giving back nothing at all. But he had not left. He was still standing there, and after a moment Ahsoka understood that he was waiting to see what she would do, so that he could put it in his report.

She handed him back the datapad. She sat up straighter on the slab.

"Thank you, Commander," she said. "Tell the Admiral I'll see him in court."

## Chapter 3: The Healer

Every morning Barriss Offee lit a candle for the man who had carried the bomb into the Temple, and every morning she was the only one who did.

She came before dawn, when the memorial alcove above the rebuilt hangar was empty and the great ventilation fans in the walls turned slowly enough that the flames stood straight. The shelf ran the length of the eastern wall, seven tiers of pale stone. On the top tier burned six flames for the six Jedi, each set before a small bronze plate, Tutso Mara's first because he had been a Knight. Below them, row on row, burned the flames for the clone troopers and the hangar crew, their plates stamped with numbers or with names depending on what they had been when they died.

There was no plate for Jackar Bowmani. He had walked into the hangar that morning with the nano-droids already in his blood, put there by a wife who had smiled at him over breakfast, and he had not known what he was until he stopped being anything at all. The Temple had not lit a candle for him. The Temple did not know how.

So Barriss lit one, at the far end of the lowest tier, with nothing in front of it. She held the taper to the wick and watched the flame catch, and the flame shook, because her hand shook.

She set the taper down and pressed both palms flat against her thighs and waited. The tremor did not pass. It had not passed since the night in the old munitions district, and it had grown worse on the gallery above the Dovrin Line. She had slept since then, she supposed. In pieces. An hour folded on a stool in the clinic's supply room. Half an hour on her cot before she woke with her heart going like a struck bell. She was a healer, and she could have written the chart without looking: sleep debt, adrenal exhaustion, fine-muscle tremor, appetite suppressed. She could have prescribed for it. She could not prescribe for the reason.

Below her, through the transparisteel, the hangar was lit for the morning shift. The new deck plating was so clean it shone. The scorch marks were gone. The twisted gantry had been cut away and replaced within days, and two gunships already sat on the pads where the blast had been worst, a squad of clones in white armor running a fuel line between them as if nothing had ever happened on that floor.

That was the part she could not bear. They had rebuilt it as a hangar.

She had thought the war would look different once it came home. For three years she had stood in field hospitals with soldiers' blood to her elbows and listened to Masters she revered talk about acceptable losses and strategic objectives. She had watched Padawans her own age, Ahsoka among them, command battalions of men bred for dying. She had watched the Order that was meant to keep the peace learn to wear a general's rank as though it fit. And she had believed, still believed, that someone had to make the Jedi look at what they had become. The war had to come into the Temple itself, where no one could call it distant.

It had come. They had swept up the dead, laid new plating, parked gunships where Jedi had burned, and called it resilience. And then they had looked for someone to blame, and they had found Ahsoka.

Barriss pushed back her left sleeve. The bruise on her forearm had gone from purple to a sick green at its edges, but its shape was still clear: a long curve, the exact arc of one of Ventress's hilts, where Skywalker's blade had come down on her crossed guard and driven the metal back into her own arm. She had felt that blow to the shoulder. He had not been trying to disarm her. She could have closed the bruise in a breath with the Force, the way she closed a hundred small hurts a week for other people. She had not.

Footsteps in the corridor. She pulled the sleeve down.

"You are up early again," Luminara Unduli said.

Her Master came into the alcove with her hands folded in her sleeves and her hood down, the dark patterns on her chin and cheeks sharp in the candlelight. She did not light anything. She stood beside Barriss and looked at the shelf the way she looked at everything, as though it had something to teach her if she was patient.

"The clinic opens at first light," Barriss said.

"The clinic opens at the second hour. It is barely the first."

"Then I'm early."

Luminara almost smiled. She put a hand on Barriss's left arm, a small gesture of comfort, and Barriss flinched before she could stop herself.

The hand withdrew. "You're hurt."

"The new supply droid stacks the bacta crates too high. One came down on me."

"Then you should tell it to stop."

"I have. It doesn't listen."

"Few things do, lately." Luminara's gaze had gone to the end of the lowest tier, to the flame with no plate. "Who is that one for?"

Barriss looked at it. "Anyone who was missed."

"That is kind of you," Luminara said, and Barriss felt the words go into her like a splinter.

They stood together without speaking. Down in the hangar, someone laughed. Then Luminara drew a breath in the particular way she did before she said a thing she had already decided to say.

"The charges against Ahsoka were filed late last night. The Council was informed at midnight."

Barriss kept her eyes on the candles. "What are they asking for? Prison?"

Luminara was silent long enough that Barriss turned to look at her.

"Admiral Tarkin has asked for death."

The taper in Barriss's hand snapped.

She did not remember picking it up again. She looked down and there it was, two pieces of wax held together by the wick, and her fingers were white around it. The cold went through her from the top down, scalp to spine to the backs of her knees, the way it did in the moment before a patient crashed.

She had seen the shape of it so clearly. Expulsion first, because the Order would never hand one of its own to the Republic, so the Order would cut her loose. A hearing. A conviction, perhaps, and years in some clean Republic facility where they would keep her safe until the war ended and the truth came out of its own accord, and the Jedi would have to live with the knowledge of what they had thrown away. That had been the price. A terrible price, but survivable. She had never once let herself picture the other shape. She had not let it into the room.

"They can't," she said.

"The Republic can," Luminara said quietly. "She is no longer ours to protect. The Chancellor presides. He is a fair man, and Senator Amidala is no fool. We must trust that."

Barriss heard herself say, "Yes, Master."

Luminara touched her shoulder, the right one this time, and left for the Council chamber. Barriss stood alone with the broken taper in her fist and the plateless candle burning at the end of the row, and she found that she could not tell anymore whom it was for.

* * *

The satchel had been under her cot for two nights.

She had told herself the first night that she was too tired, and the second that there were too many people awake in the residential wing. On the third night, after a double shift in the clinic, she took it down.

Her first plan had been the hangar's waste reclamation furnace, which burned hot enough to reduce durasteel to slag. But the hangar had changed since the bombing. There were two Coruscant Guard troopers at every access now, with scanners, and a duty officer who logged every Jedi in and out by name. Barriss had stood at the end of the corridor and looked at them, their red-marked helmets turning to watch the doors, and understood with a kind of sick humor that they were guarding the entrance she had already walked through.

So she went down instead.

The Temple stood on older foundations than most of its residents liked to think about. Beneath the training levels and the archives' deep stacks and the power trunks that fed the spires, there were maintenance galleries no one used anymore, cut when the ventilation had been built along a different plan, then abandoned when it was rebuilt. Barriss had found them as a youngling, on a dare from two crechemates who had lost their nerve at the second ladder. Luminara had found her afterward, filthy and delighted, and had said nothing to her for an entire day. It had been the most effective punishment of her childhood.

She climbed down past the humming trunks, past a service droid that swiveled its photoreceptor at her and went back to its work. The air grew colder and smelled of dust and old lubricant. The light strips here were dim and many were dead; she lit the way with a glow rod from the clinic stores. Three levels below the eastern foundation she found the crawlway she remembered: a square duct mouth at knee height, its durasteel grille stenciled with a faded yellow C-31 and crossed with brittle seal tape from some survey a generation gone.

The latches had rusted shut. She reached into them with the Force and they gave with a shriek that went echoing down the gallery. She froze, listening. Nothing. Only the distant pulse of the power trunks, like a heart in another room.

She knelt on the cold floor and opened the satchel.

The mask came out first. Bone-white, the eye-slits slanted, a crack along one cheek she did not remember making. She set it face-up on the floor and it looked at the ceiling with nothing behind it. Then the sabers, one and then the other. Ventress's hilts were heavier than her own, curved like the neck of some long-legged bird, cold even after days wrapped in cloth.

She had come down here to destroy them. She knew how. Every Jedi knew, in theory, though no Jedi was supposed to do it: you reached into the hilt, found the crystal, and closed the Force around it until it broke.

She held the first hilt in both hands and reached.

The crystal was there at the center, and it was wrong. She had touched her own crystal a thousand times and it had always felt like clear water. This one felt like a wound. It had been bled, forced into red by someone's will, and it hummed with an old pain that had never been allowed to close. Barriss had spent her life closing wounds. She pressed on it, carefully, the way she would probe a fracture, and the crystal answered with something so like a cry that she dropped the hilt in her lap.

It would be easy. One push. The thing was already broken; she would only be finishing it.

She told herself that breaking a crystal would send a ripple through the Force, and that some Master meditating in the spires far above her might feel it and come looking. That was true, possibly. It was also a lie, and she knew it while she was telling it. She could have taken her own blade to the mask in a heartbeat. She did not do that either.

Instead she took a folded surgical drape from the bottom of the satchel, the kind the clinic used for sterile instruments, and laid it open on the floor. She set the two hilts in it side by side, then the mask on top. She folded the corners in the order she had been taught as an apprentice healer, left, right, bottom, top, so that nothing touched the outside. Her hands were steadier doing it than they had been in days. That frightened her more than anything else.

She pushed the bundle into the crawlway at arm's length, then further with the Force, until she felt it settle around a bend into a dry niche where the duct turned. She closed the grille. She bent the latches back with the Force until they bit, and smoothed the old seal tape down with her thumb until it looked undisturbed, or nearly.

Then she sat back on her heels in the cold with the glow rod in her lap and made herself think it all the way through, the way she would make a frightened patient look at a wound.

Those sabers were the only proof. Anyone who found them, anyone who scanned them, would know the red blades in the warehouse and on the Dovrin gallery had not been Ventress's in Ventress's hands. They were the one thing in the galaxy that pointed away from Ahsoka. And Barriss had just carried them past a furnace and three levels down and wrapped them as carefully as a surgeon's tools.

She did not know why. She sat there until the glow rod dimmed, trying to know, and could not.

*Tomorrow*, she thought. She had thought it before, in an alley, with the incinerator chute open in front of her. She climbed back up the ladders in the dark and did not look behind her.

* * *

The Republic Center for Military Operations smelled of floor polish and recycled air. A Coruscant Guard lieutenant at the security desk scanned her identification, then held out his hand without a word.

Barriss unclipped her lightsaber and laid it in the tray. He sealed it in a numbered drawer and gave her a chit. She looked at the drawer and thought of two other hilts, green and yellow-green, laid in a tray like this one at a door like this one, and of the trooper who had logged them and stood back to let Ahsoka walk into a room where a woman was going to die.

"Detention level four," the lieutenant said. "Fifteen minutes. A guard remains present."

"I'm here as a healer," Barriss said. "The prisoner's counsel reported skin damage from the binders."

"A guard remains present," he said again.

The corridor on level four was long and white and freshly resealed. She knew, because the Holonet had shown it, that this was where the three troopers had died. The floor had been replaced. Nothing marked the place at all. She walked over it with the guard's boots ringing behind her and kept her eyes on the cell doors ahead, and her hands folded in her sleeves where they could shake in private.

Ahsoka was sitting on the slab with her back to the wall and her bound hands in her lap. When she saw who was outside the field, her whole face changed. She was on her feet before the guard had keyed the panel.

"Barriss." She sounded so glad. "They let you in?"

"I told them your wrists needed looking at."

Ahsoka held them up. Beneath the edges of the binders the orange skin had been rubbed raw in a band all the way around, cracked in places. "Huh," she said. "Well. Now they do."

The guard lowered the field and stepped back against the opposite wall, and Barriss went in.

She knelt. Up close Ahsoka looked thinner than she had a week ago, the white markings stark on her face, and her montrals bare where the Padawan beads had hung. Barriss had heard about the beads. Everyone in the Temple had heard. She took Ahsoka's wrists in her hands, careful of the binders, and closed her eyes and reached.

Ahsoka's presence in the Force was the same as it had always been: quick and bright and warm, like standing too near a fire. It was battered now, dimmed at the edges with exhaustion and something harder, but it opened to Barriss at once, without any guard at all. Ahsoka trusted her. The trust came through her hands like heat, and Barriss had to hold very still under it while she knit the broken skin closed, cell by cell, the way she had been taught.

"That's better," Ahsoka said. "That's a lot better. Thank you."

"Don't." It came out too sharp. Barriss opened her eyes. "It's nothing. It's what I do."

"It's not nothing to me." Ahsoka flexed her fingers inside the binders. "I saw you, at the Temple. When they took me in. I'm sorry I didn't say anything. I couldn't look at anybody. If I'd looked at anybody I wouldn't have made it into that lift."

"You don't owe me an apology," Barriss said, and the true weight of that sentence sat in her mouth like a stone.

Ahsoka sat back down on the slab. Barriss stayed kneeling on the floor in front of her, because her legs did not feel reliable.

"You heard what Tarkin's asking for," Ahsoka said.

"Yes."

"Padmé says he always asks for the most so that anything less looks like mercy. She says it's a tactic."

"Is that supposed to comfort you?"

"It's supposed to keep me from screaming." A flicker of the old crooked grin. "Mostly it works."

"I thought—" Barriss stopped.

"What?"

"I thought they would only expel you. I thought that would be the worst of it."

"So did I," Ahsoka said. "Turns out that was the good part."

The guard shifted against the far wall. Ahsoka glanced at him, then leaned forward so that her voice dropped beneath the hum of the field emitters.

"Listen. I haven't told them who sent me to the warehouse. Tarkin's going to ask. Padmé's going to ask, probably. I'm not going to say."

Something in Barriss went entirely still.

"You should tell them," she heard herself say. "It might help you."

"It wouldn't. It'd give Tarkin one more Jedi to drag in front of the Senate, that's all. They'd say you helped a fugitive. The Council would do to you exactly what they did to me." Ahsoka's jaw set. "One of us is enough. You only told me what you knew. It's not your fault someone was waiting there. Whoever it was must have been tracking me since the tunnels. I was careless."

"You weren't careless."

"I walked into a trap with a Sith assassin for backup. That's pretty careless, Barriss." She tried to smile again and didn't manage it this time. "But you picked up. When I called. Everyone else in that Temple was hunting me, and you picked up and told me where to look. I'm not going to forget that."

Barriss could not breathe. The cell was very small. The light from the field emitters lay on Ahsoka's face and on the raw new skin of her wrists, healed now, pink, the only good thing Barriss had done in a week.

"Ahsoka," she said, and did not know what was going to come after it.

"Will you do something for me?"

"Anything."

"There's a memorial shelf, by the hangar. For the bombing." Ahsoka looked down at her hands. "The three troopers who died out there. CT-2261. CT-4408. CT-9170. I had the numbers read to me in the charge sheet. Nobody at the Temple's going to light anything for them, not for the men I'm supposed to have killed. Would you? Light one for each?"

The corridor outside. The replaced floor. Barriss had not known their numbers. She had not let herself know them.

"Yes," she said.

"Thanks." Ahsoka let out a long breath. "Anakin's turning the lower levels upside down looking for Ventress. She was there, at the end. She might have seen something. If anyone can find her, he can."

"Time," said the guard.

Barriss got up. Her knees ached from the floor. At the threshold Ahsoka said, "Barriss?" and she turned.

"Will you be there? Tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"Good." Ahsoka's montrals lifted, just slightly. "I'll look for you."

The field came up between them with a soft electric snap. Barriss walked back down the long white corridor over the place where three men had died under her blades, and the guard's boots rang behind her, and she did not look down.

* * *

She lit the three candles that evening, at the end of the lowest tier beside the one that had no plate. She said each number aloud as the wick caught, softly, so that no one passing in the corridor would hear. Twenty-two sixty-one. Forty-four oh-eight. Ninety-one seventy. Four flames now with nothing in front of them, in a row, like a sentence no one else could read.

Then she went to her Master's quarters.

Luminara was kneeling before the long window in meditation, the lights of the city traffic sliding over the floor behind her. She did not open her eyes when the door slid aside. "Come in, Barriss."

Barriss knelt beside her. For a while she tried to meditate, because it was what they did together in the evenings and had done since she was small, and because if she breathed slowly enough she might find the words. The Force moved around her, vast and patient. It did not offer anything. It never did, lately. She had begun to think it was waiting.

"Master," she said. "May I ask you something?"

"Always."

"If a Jedi knew something. Something that might change what happens tomorrow." She kept her eyes on the window. "What should she do?"

Luminara opened her eyes.

"Hypothetically," Barriss said.

"Hypothetically," Luminara said, "she would bring it to the Council at once. Tonight. And if she had kept it to herself until now, she would answer for that, too." She turned to look at Barriss fully. "You understand what is happening in that chamber tomorrow is not only about Ahsoka. Admiral Tarkin and half the Senate are waiting to see the Jedi protect their own. Every newsfeed on the Holonet is waiting for it. The war effort cannot afford a scandal in the Temple, and so the Order cannot afford even the appearance of one. Whatever we do now, we must do correctly."

*The war effort.* Barriss heard the words land and settle, and something cold in her nodded at them, as if they confirmed a diagnosis. Even now. Even with a girl's life on the table, the first thing the Order counted was what the war could afford.

"Is there something you know?" Luminara asked.

Barriss looked at her. Her Master's face was serene and certain, the face that had taught her to read and to heal and to hold a blade, the face that had said nothing for a whole day to a filthy, delighted youngling and made her understand. She tried to imagine that face as it would be after she spoke. She found that she could imagine it exactly.

"I—" she said.

Luminara waited.

"I'm afraid for her," Barriss said.

Her Master's expression softened. "As am I." She reached out and took Barriss's hand, and as she did, Barriss's sleeve slid back from her wrist.

Luminara looked down at the bruise. The green-edged curve of it lay across Barriss's forearm in the window light, plain as a signature.

"That," Luminara said, "is not from a crate."

"Sparring," Barriss said. "A training remote. I got careless."

"A remote did not leave that shape."

"A practice staff, then. I don't remember. I've been tired."

Luminara studied the bruise a moment longer. Then she laid two fingers over it, and Barriss felt her Master's healing gather, gentle and practiced, ready to close it.

"Don't," Barriss said. "Please. I'd like to keep it. For a little while. To remind me."

"Of what?"

"To be more careful."

Luminara took her fingers away. Her eyes stayed on Barriss's face, and for one long moment Barriss saw something move behind them, a question being turned over and not yet asked. Then it was gone, put away somewhere for later, and her Master only said, "That is a strange discipline. But you were always hard on yourself." She squeezed Barriss's hand. "Ahsoka's fall is not your failing. Remember that tomorrow."

Barriss bowed her head so that she would not have to answer.

"We sit in the gallery for the opening session," Luminara went on. "The Council wishes the Order to be seen there. She should have some friendly faces." She paused. "Master Kenobi tells me Skywalker has gone down into the lower levels again tonight. He has not slept either, it seems. He is convinced Ventress survived the fall, and he says he will not stop until he finds her."

Barriss lifted her head.

"Survived," she said.

"So he believes. There was no body." Luminara closed her eyes again and turned back toward the window. "If she lives, she may know who was behind that mask. The Force may yet give Ahsoka a witness. We can hope for that much."

Barriss went back to her quarters and lay down on her cot fully dressed, with the lights off and her hands folded on her chest to keep them still.

Ventress, who had been on that gallery. Ventress, who had felt the attacker in the Force, close enough to touch. Ventress, who had been with Ahsoka when Ahsoka made a call to a friend in the Temple. For three days she had searched the Holonet for word of a body and found none, and told herself it meant the lower levels had swallowed it. She had not let herself think it might mean nothing had fallen far enough.

If Skywalker found her, Ahsoka might live.

If Skywalker found her, Barriss would be the one in the cell.

She lay in the dark and tried to work out which of those two things she was hoping for, and she could not. That was the worst discovery of the whole week: not the bombing, not the troopers, not the girl thanking her for a lie. All of those she had reasons for. This, she had none.

Far beneath her, past the training levels and the archive stacks and the humming trunks, behind a grille she had bent shut with her own hands, Ventress's sabers lay folded in a sterile drape, waiting for someone to come looking.

*Tomorrow*, Barriss thought, and for the first time in her life she did not know what the word meant.

## Chapter 4: The Tribunal

Someone had placed the evidence table so close to the defendant's chair that Ahsoka could have reached out and touched her own lightsabers. Padmé noticed it the moment she came through the doors, and she knew it was no accident. The two hilts lay in a clear case on grey cloth, each with a small white tag wired to its pommel, #417 and #418. Ahsoka sat a little more than an arm's length away from them with her bound hands in her lap. She was not looking at them, and she was taking great care not to.

The tribunal chamber sat on the Senate side of the complex, a round room built for committee hearings and never meant for this. The seats rose in tiers toward a ceiling of smoked transparisteel, and the morning light came through it the color of weak caf.
