On the high bench at the far end sat Chancellor Palpatine, with a judiciary clerk on either side of him. Below him, along a curved desk, sat the senators who would decide: Riyo Chuchi, her blue face very still; Mon Mothma in white; Bail Organa, who had nodded to Padmé in the corridor and then looked away as if the nod had cost him something; and six others whose votes Padmé had spent two sleepless nights trying to count.

Above them, the gallery was full.

She saw Jedi robes in a cluster on the left: Master Luminara Unduli and her Padawan, two Knights Padmé didn't know, and Plo Koon standing at the back of the cluster because he would not sit. On the right sat the families of the hangar dead, in the deep blue of Coruscanti mourning. And everywhere else were faces she recognized from committee rooms and receptions, senators and aides and Holonet people, here for the same reason crowds gathered at a speeder wreck.

Padmé set her datapad down at the defense table and took the seat beside Ahsoka.

"Good morning," she said.

"Is it?" Ahsoka said. Then, more quietly: "Sorry. Good morning, Senator."

Two days ago her wrists had been raw from the binders, rubbed open in two places. Padmé looked now and saw clean orange skin under the metal. Somebody had finally sent a medic. It was a small decency, and she was angry at how grateful it made her.

At the prosecution table, Admiral Tarkin squared a stack of datapads with his fingertips and did not look up.

* * *

Tarkin built his case the way an engineer built a bridge: one load-bearing piece at a time, no ornament, nothing that would not hold weight.

He began with the hangar. He did not dwell on it, which was cleverer than dwelling on it would have been. He read the names of the six Jedi who had died, then the number of clones, then the number of civilian workers, and let the blue rows in the gallery do the rest. Then he moved to Letta Turmond.

"The Republic's case does not depend on the defendant's character, or her reputation, or the affection in which she is held by her former colleagues," he said. "It depends on what the record shows. I will now show the record."

The lights dimmed. A holo rose above the floor of the chamber, grey and slightly grainy, the angle high in the corner of a cell. Padmé had watched it forty times. It did not get better.

Letta Turmond sat on a bench. Ahsoka stood in front of her. There was no sound. Letta leaned forward, her mouth moving, her hands opening as if she were offering something. Then her hands went to her throat. Her body lifted against the wall. Her heels kicked twice. On the holo, Ahsoka stepped toward her, one arm out, and from that angle, with no sound, the reaching hand looked like the cause of it.

Letta slid down the wall and was still.

Someone in the gallery made a sound like a breath going the wrong way. Padmé did not turn to see who.

"The prisoner had asked to speak to the defendant alone," Tarkin said, as the lights came back. "She was alone with her. No other person entered that room. The prisoner died by strangulation, with no mark of a hand on her. I am told by the Jedi themselves that only one sort of person can do that."

Padmé stood. "Objection. The Admiral is testifying to Force technique. He is not qualified, and no Jedi has been called to support it."

"Sustained," said Palpatine mildly, from the high bench. "Admiral, I am sure the tribunal is aware of the general capacities of the Jedi. Let us keep to what the record shows, as you so rightly said."

"Of course, Chancellor."

It was a small win and it cost Tarkin nothing. Padmé sat down. Beside her, Ahsoka had gone very still, her lekku drawn in tight against her shoulders.

Next came the three troopers. Tarkin called a forensics technician from the Judiciary laboratory, a narrow Bith named Sabo Kell who spoke softly and kept his long hands folded on the witness rail. Kell described the wounds: cauterized, narrow, two different blade widths. He described the residue profile of a kyber-focused plasma blade. He compared it with test cuts made with items #417 and #418.

"Consistent in every respect," Kell said.

"Thank you. The names, please, for the record."

Kell consulted his datapad. "CT-2261. CT-4408. CT-9170."

Padmé watched Ahsoka's mouth move very slightly with each number. She was not praying. Jedi didn't, exactly. She was counting along.

Padmé rose. "The defense asks the tribunal to enter the detention-corridor recordings for the hour of the escape. If my client killed these men, a camera watched her do it."

Palpatine consulted a datapad at his elbow. "The Coruscant Guard reports that the cameras on that level were non-functional during the escape, Senator. A relay fault; it is noted in their filing." He spread his hands, mild and regretful. "I'm afraid the tribunal cannot enter what was never recorded."

Padmé sat. A relay fault, for exactly the hour that mattered. She wrote it down and underlined it, and it did her no good at all.

Commander Fox came after him, helmet under his arm, and gave his account of the escape in the flat voice of a man reading a report he had already filed. The cell door open. The defendant gone. The three bodies in the corridor. The pursuit into the lower levels. The sighting of the defendant in the company of Asajj Ventress, known Separatist assassin. The second capture, in a disused munitions warehouse, where the defendant had been found standing in the middle of crates that held the same nano-droids used in the hangar bombing.

"Standing," Tarkin repeated.

"Standing, sir. Weapon drawn. One of the crates was open."

"Was anyone else present?"

Fox hesitated, which Padmé had not expected. "Not when we arrived, sir."

"Thank you, Commander."

On cross, Padmé asked Fox one question. "What weapon did the defendant have drawn, Commander? Her own lightsabers?"

"No, ma'am. Her own sabers were already in evidence. She had a—" He paused. "A stun baton. One of ours."

"A stun baton. Against a stockpile of explosive droids." She let the room hear it. "Thank you, Commander."

It was something. It was not enough, and she knew it, and so did Tarkin, who was already rising.

"The Republic calls General Anakin Skywalker."

* * *

He came down the aisle from the back of the chamber in his dark robes, and Padmé had to put her hands flat on the table.

He looked like a man who had not slept in four days, which she knew to be true. There was a healing scrape along his jaw. His boots were clean, because he would have cleaned them, but his robes had the faint grey bloom of lower-level damp in the hem. He took the witness stand without looking at the defense table. That was for Ahsoka's sake, Padmé thought, and possibly for hers.

Tarkin was courteous to him. That was the worst part.

"General, on the night before the Jedi Council voted to expel the defendant, you were in the old munitions district of the lower levels. Why?"

"Looking for Ventress."

"You had reason to believe she knew something."

"I had reason to believe she was the only person in the galaxy who could tell us what happened in that warehouse," Anakin said. "So yes."

"And you found her."

"On a gallery over the Dovrin Line platform. She'd been hurt. Someone had beaten her and taken her lightsabers."

"Someone," Tarkin said. "Describe this someone."

Anakin's jaw moved. "Cloaked. A white mask with narrow eyeholes. Fast. Two curved lightsabers, red."

"Two curved red lightsabers," Tarkin said, and turned so that the senators' bench could see his face. "General, are you familiar with the weapons Asajj Ventress is known to carry?"

"Yes."

"Describe them."

"Two curved hilts," Anakin said. "Red blades."

"Thank you."

"She didn't have them," Anakin said. "That's the point. Somebody took them from her. She had a bruise from her temple to here—" he touched his cheekbone with his gloved hand— "and she was scared, Admiral. I've fought Ventress. I've never seen her scared."

"You have testified that the masked figure threw Ventress from a ledge," Tarkin said. "Partners in crime frequently fall out, General. Sometimes literally. You found an assassin who had lost her weapons to someone who knew exactly where to find them, and who then removed her before she could speak. Is that not also the pattern of an accomplice tidying up?"

"Objection," Padmé said. "Speculation."

"I'll withdraw it," said Tarkin, who had already said it. "General. When the figure attacked, did you recognize anything about how it fought? Any style, any training?"

The question was so smooth it took Padmé a moment to understand its danger. If Anakin said yes, if he said it fought like a Jedi, then Ahsoka had a Jedi accomplice, and the case got worse, not better.

Anakin's right hand closed on the rail of the witness stand. The leather of his glove creaked in the quiet. Padmé knew that hand. She had seen it close like that over a dinner table and on a balcony rail and once, a long time ago, around the edge of a sink on Naboo.

"No," Anakin said. "It was dark. It was fast."

Tarkin inclined his head. "Then let us turn to what you did see. The figure used nano-droids on a support pylon. A residential structure began to fall. You chose to hold the structure rather than pursue."

"There were people under it."

"Commendable," said Tarkin, and it sounded like a word he had looked up. "No one died. I am sure the families are grateful. I note only that the one person in the galaxy you say could have cleared the defendant walked away from that platform because you let them."

The room went quiet in a different way.

Padmé was on her feet. "Chancellor, that is not a question."

"No, it isn't," said Palpatine gently. "Admiral, the General's conduct that night is not on trial. I will not have it made to seem so." He turned to Anakin with something close to tenderness. "General Skywalker, you saved a great many lives. The tribunal notes it."

Anakin looked up at the high bench. For an instant his face was naked, a boy's face, grateful and ashamed at once. Then it closed.

"Thank you, Chancellor," he said.

On cross, Padmé had prepared nine questions. She asked three.

"General, where was the defendant on the night you met this masked figure on the Dovrin platform?"

"In a cell," Anakin said. "At the Center for Military Operations. Under guard."

"So whoever wore that mask and carried those weapons was not the defendant."

"No."

"And whoever attacked Ventress that night knew where to find her, and was willing to bring down a twenty-story building to escape. While the defendant sat behind a locked door."

"Yes."

"Thank you, General. That's all."

She sat. It was the strongest point she had: there was someone else. Someone else had been in that warehouse, someone else was still out there, and the girl at the defense table could not possibly have been on that platform.

She watched it land on the senators' bench. She watched Bail Organa write something down. And then she watched Tarkin rise and spend a single sentence to take the point away.

"The Republic has never disputed that the defendant had help," he said. "The only question is whether she directed it."

* * *

The second morning, Ahsoka testified.

She had asked to. Padmé had argued against it, the night before, through the hum of a detention-cell field, and lost. "If I don't speak," Ahsoka had said, "they'll decide what I would have said. I'd rather they hear it wrong from me."

She took the stand in grey detention clothes with her binders still on, because Tarkin had requested it and Palpatine had regretfully allowed it. She sat straight. Without the Padawan beads, the place on her montral where the chain had hung looked bare, and Padmé saw more than one senator's eyes go there and come away.

Padmé took her through it gently, from the beginning. The hangar, and the investigation. Letta Turmond asking for her by name. Fox taking her comlink and her sabers at the door.

"What happened in that room?"

"She told me a Jedi was behind it," Ahsoka said. "She was going to tell me who. And then she couldn't breathe. I tried to get to her. I couldn't stop it. I couldn't see who was doing it, I couldn't feel where it came from, and she died in front of me." Her voice did not shake. Her hands, in the binders, had locked together until the knuckles paled. "I've watched people die in the war. Not like that. Not a prisoner. Not someone who was asking me for help."

She told them about the cell, the keycard she had thought came from a friend, the bodies of the three troopers in the corridor. "I knew what it would look like," she said. "I ran because I knew what it would look like. That's the truth. I'm not proud of it."

"Why did you go to the warehouse?"

"Because someone told me that was where the nano-droids came from. I thought if I could find the source, I could find who'd done this."

Padmé had come to the place she had been dreading. She had told Ahsoka she would ask it. Ahsoka had told her it would do no good. Padmé had decided the tribunal should at least hear her refuse; there was a kind of evidence in that, too, if the right senators were listening.

"Ahsoka," she said. "Who told you where the warehouse was?"

The pause went on long enough that someone in the press seats shifted.

"A friend," Ahsoka said.

"Can you tell the tribunal their name?"

"No."

"This person could corroborate your account. They could tell these senators that you went to that warehouse to find the bomber, not to protect a stockpile."

"I know."

"Then why won't you name them?"

Ahsoka lifted her chin. "Because they helped a fugitive. I was a fugitive. If I say their name, they lose everything I've already lost. The Order would put them out the same way it put me out. I'm not going to do that to someone for being kind to me."

It was said with complete simplicity, and Padmé felt it go into the room like a stone into deep water. She had hoped for sympathy. She saw, instead, three senators exchange glances that said *convenient*.

Ahsoka's eyes moved once, up and to the left, toward the gallery. It was very quick. Padmé followed the look without meaning to and saw only the cluster of Jedi robes: Master Unduli with her hands in her sleeves, the young Mirialan beside her sitting very upright with her own hands pressed flat on her knees, Plo Koon behind them with his head bowed. Then Ahsoka was looking at Padmé again, and her face said *please don't ask me again*.

Padmé didn't.

Tarkin did.

He stood without hurry and approached no closer than he had to. "You have told us a great deal about loyalty, Miss Tano. Loyalty to a friend you will not name. Loyalty to an Order that has expelled you. I wonder if you might extend some to the Republic you swore to serve, and to the three troopers who died in that corridor."

"Don't," Ahsoka said.

"I beg your pardon?"

"Don't use them." Her montrals had darkened at the stripes. "I led those men's brothers on Christophsis and Ryloth and a dozen places that never made the Holonet. I know their numbers. I asked for candles for them. You read them off a datapad."

"Miss Tano—"

"I didn't kill them. I didn't kill Letta. I didn't help bomb the hangar. I've been fighting for this Republic since I was fourteen, Admiral. Where were you?"

The gallery murmured. Palpatine raised one hand, and the murmur died.

"The defendant will confine herself to answering questions," he said. Then, more softly, almost as a kindness: "I understand this is very difficult, my dear."

Tarkin waited until the silence was complete. "The only witness who could support your story is one you refuse to produce," he said. "The only other witness is a Separatist assassin who has vanished. Everything else, Miss Tano, is the record. And the record has your weapons in it."

"No further questions," he said, and sat.

Ahsoka stayed on the stand a moment after she was dismissed, as if she had forgotten how to stand up. Then she rose, and the guards closed in, and she walked back to the defense table without looking at the lightsabers in their case.

Padmé looked at the senators' bench, and she saw it.

She had sat in the Senate long enough to know what a room sounded like when it had already decided. It sounded calm. The senators were writing less. Chuchi was looking at her own hands. Two of the others had their datapads angled toward each other. Mon Mothma was still listening, and Bail was still listening, and the rest had started listening the way people listened to a speech they had already read.

It was not that they believed Ahsoka was a monster. It was that the hangar had burned in the middle of the Temple, on Coruscant, where the war was not supposed to reach. The people in blue in the gallery wanted a name. The people who voted for war funding wanted to know it was safe to keep voting for it. A seventeen-year-old who had been found holding a stun baton in a room full of bombs was a name, and if the name was wrong, no one in this room would ever have to know.

* * *

At the noon recess she found Anakin in a robing room off the east corridor, a narrow space with a coat rack and a water dispenser and a window that looked onto the backside of a ventilation tower. He was standing at the window with his arms folded. He did not turn when she locked the door.

"You were supposed to sleep," she said.

"I did."

"When?"

He thought about it. "Before the vote. Some of it."

She came and stood beside him. Up close he smelled of rain and cheap fuel and the scorched, metallic air of the deep levels. The scrape on his jaw was newer than she'd thought.

"Obi-Wan called me this morning," she said. "He asked if I knew where you go at night."

"What did you tell him?"

"That I'm Ahsoka's counsel, not your keeper. Which is true." She waited. "He says you haven't answered him in days."

"I'll answer him when I have something to say."

"Anakin, if you're caught down there, breaking into gang holds and throwing people through walls—"

"I haven't thrown anyone through a wall."

"—Tarkin will stand up in that room and say the Jedi are hunting down witnesses for the defense. Do you understand? He'll say you're tampering. Anything Ventress says after that is worthless. Anything *you* say is worthless."

"Anything Ventress says is already worthless." He turned at last, and his face was terrible. "You heard him. Separatist assassin. He'll make her a co-defendant the second she walks in. He's not trying to find out what happened, Padmé. He's trying to finish."

"Then help me stop him finishing."

"With what? Another speech?" It came out cutting and he heard it; she watched him hear it. He looked away. "Sorry."

"I'm doing what I can do in that room. You can do something in it too. You can sit there. You can let them see you there, every day, beside her." She heard her own voice tighten. "She looks for you. When she comes in, before she sits down, she looks for you."

His jaw worked. "And when I'm sitting there, I'm not finding the person who did this."

"You've been down there four nights."

"Five tonight."

"Ani."

The name stopped him the way it always did, and she hated using it for leverage, and she used it anyway. He put his mechanical hand flat on the window, gloved fingers spread against the glass, and leaned on it.

"I had them," he said, very low. "On that catwalk. They stood there and watched me hold the stack. Ten meters away. They *watched*, Padmé, like they wanted to see what I'd do. I could have had them. And I let go of the one thing that would have saved her so I could hold up a building."

"You saved two hundred people."

"Tell Snips."

"She knows. She'd have done the same. You trained her to."

He laughed, without any humor at all. "Great. So it's my fault twice."

She put her hand over the back of his on the glass. Leather and cold metal underneath it. She thought of the moment on the stand, his fist closing on the rail, the pause before *No*.

"Is there anything you saw on that platform that you haven't told me?" she said.

He didn't move his hand. He didn't look at her, either. "It was dark," he said. "It was fast."

That was what he had told Tarkin, word for word. She took her hand away.

"All right," she said. "Then I'll go and do my job, and you'll do whatever you're going to do. But be in that chamber for closing. Please. Be where she can see you."

He nodded. He did not promise.

* * *

Tarkin's closing took eleven minutes. Padmé timed it, because she could not stand to do anything else.

He did not raise his voice. He laid out the pieces in the order he had built them: the footage, the weapons, the three troopers, the escape, the assassin, the stockpile. He reminded the senators that the defendant had admitted to running. He reminded them that she had admitted to keeping company with Asajj Ventress. He reminded them that she had refused, under oath and before this tribunal, to identify the one person who might have spoken for her.

"Each of these facts, alone, might have an innocent explanation," he said. "I invite the senators to consider how many innocent explanations they are being asked to accept at once. The Republic is at war. It has been struck in its own capital, in the most protected building on Coruscant, by someone who knew its corridors. The defendant knew those corridors. She has given this tribunal sentiment where it asked for evidence. The Republic seeks the penalty the law provides for the murder of its soldiers in a time of war. Nothing more, and nothing less."

He sat. The penalty the law provided. He had not said the word. He had not needed to.

Padmé rose.

She had written the closing three times and thrown all three away at dawn. She spoke now mostly without the datapad, standing at the end of the defense table where the senators could see both her and the girl behind her.

"The Admiral has asked you to count explanations," she said. "I'm going to ask you to count something else.

"Count the reasons. In two days of evidence, the prosecution has not offered you one reason why Ahsoka Tano would bomb the Temple she was raised in. Not one. No grievance. No payment. No message. No contact with any Separatist before the night she ran for her life. You have heard that she was angry, from the stand, this morning. Of course she was angry. She was accused of murdering soldiers she had served beside for three years.

"Count the years. Since the age of fourteen she has commanded troops of this Republic. She held the line on Christophsis. She fought on Ryloth. She brought younglings back alive from Florrum when she might have saved herself. There is not a single disciplinary entry in her record that involves cruelty, or disloyalty, or a lie. Senators, I have read it. I would invite you to read it. I do not think everyone in this chamber has.

"And count the people in the room. The prosecution needs you to believe there was someone else: an accomplice who struck down three troopers, who attacked Asajj Ventress and stole her weapons, who brought down a pylon on the Dovrin Line. They need there to be someone else, because they know Ahsoka Tano was in a locked cell when that pylon fell. They only need you to believe she was giving the orders. They have not shown you one message, one signal, one meeting. They have shown you a mask that was not her and a weapon that was not hers.

"There is someone else," Padmé said. "There is someone out there, tonight, who knew where Ventress would be, who knew how to fight a Jedi Knight to a standstill, who had access to those cells and to those lightsabers. That person is free. If you convict this girl, they will stay free, and the Republic will be a little less safe and a great deal less just, and you will have given the families in that gallery a name instead of an answer."

She looked up at the blue rows. A woman in the front of them was crying silently with her hands in her lap.

"I am sorry for your loss," Padmé said to her. "I am so sorry. And this will not mend it."

She turned back to the bench. "The defense rests, Chancellor."

She sat down. Her hands were shaking and she put them under the table. Beside her, Ahsoka did not look at her, but under the table, her bound hands moved an inch to the side until the cuff of her binder touched Padmé's wrist and stayed there.

On the high bench, Palpatine leaned forward and folded his hands.

"I thank counsel for both the Republic and the defense," he said. "You have each served this tribunal with diligence, and in the case of the defense, with considerable eloquence. Whatever is decided, let no one say the defendant was poorly represented." He smiled at Padmé, the same warm, slightly tired smile she had seen across a dozen Naboo state dinners when she was fourteen and he was the senator who always remembered her name. "The members of the tribunal will now retire to deliberate. We will reconvene when a verdict has been reached. I would expect that to be tomorrow morning."

The senators rose. They filed out through the door behind the bench in a soft rustle of robes, Chuchi first and fast, as if she couldn't bear the room, Mon Mothma last, pausing once to look back at the defense table. Then the door closed behind them and the chamber came apart into noise.

The guards came for Ahsoka. She stood. She let them turn her by the elbow. At the last moment she twisted to look over her shoulder, not at Padmé but past her, up the aisle, to the seat in the third row behind the defense table where Anakin had sat for every hour of the first day.

Padmé turned to look too.

The seat was empty. It had been empty all through Tarkin's closing, she realized, and all through hers. She had been speaking, and she had not looked, and he had not come.

"He'll be here," Padmé said.

Ahsoka's face did something complicated and then went smooth again. "Sure," she said.

The guards took her out through the side door. The case with her lightsabers went after her on a hover-cart, tagged and sealed, #417 and #418, rattling faintly over the seam in the floor.

Padmé gathered her datapads with hands that would not quite work. When she reached the corridor, her comlink chimed. It was a text-only message, no signature, sent from a relay so deep in the lower levels it barely registered a location at all.

*Someone sold a Dathomirian a med kit two nights ago. Purple bruise, left side of the face. I'm going down. Don't let them read it without me.*

She stood very still in the corridor while the crowd went around her. Then she looked at the timestamp, and at the deliberation chamber doors at the end of the hall, and understood that she had one night to keep a verdict from being read, and Anakin had one night to find a woman the entire Republic wanted to hang beside his Padawan.

## Chapter 5: The Chancellor's Mercy

The med-kit vendor had a burn scar across both jowls and a stall made of three welded cargo crates, and somewhere in the last ninety seconds he had decided to tell Anakin Skywalker everything he knew, because Anakin's mechanical hand was resting flat on the top crate and the durasteel under it had begun, very quietly, to bend.

"Bacta patches," the Sullustan said. "A sealant gun. Two ampoules of the good painkiller, not the cut stuff they sell down by the outflow. She paid in Hutt coin. Nobody pays in Hutt coin this high up."

Anakin looked around. They were somewhere beneath the old munitions district, under a drainage arch that wept rust-colored water onto the walkway in a steady patter. A sign above the next stall advertised fresh nerf in letters that had not been fresh in a decade. If this was high up, he did not want to know what low looked like.

"What else did she buy?"

"Nothing else. She asked." The vendor's eyes went to the hand on his crate. His name, according to the stall's faded license tag, was Tibbo Nask. "She asked where a person could find a berth-master who didn't scan faces. I told her the Undercroft. Berth Nine. I tell everyone the Undercroft. It's not a secret, General, it's just a long walk."

"When?"

"Two nights ago. Three now, I suppose." Tibbo glanced up at the light-strips along the arch, which had begun their slow shift from amber to white. Somewhere far above, it was nearly morning. "She looked bad. Purple all down the side of her face. She moved like her ribs were held together with wire. I gave her extra patches and didn't charge her. Don't tell anyone that."

Anakin took his hand off the crate. It left a shallow dent the shape of four fingers and a palm. He put a stack of credits beside it, more than the crate was worth, and was already walking before Tibbo could decide whether to be insulted.

The Undercroft was a long walk. He ran it.

He had been running for five nights. He had stopped feeling his feet sometime on the third, and on the fourth he had stopped answering the comlink at all, because the only people who called were Obi-Wan, who wanted him to come back to the Temple and rest, and Rex, who wanted to know if there was anything he could do, and the answer to both was the same and he could not say it to either of them. He had questioned spice runners and dock-rats and a Toydarian who sold forged clearance cards out of a laundry. He had broken one jaw he was not proud of. He had stood on the rim of the Dovrin platform where the fallen hab stack still lay heaped inside its emergency cordon, looking down into the drop where the masked figure had thrown Ventress, and he had stretched out with the Force until his head pounded, searching for one cold, furious presence among a billion others.

Coruscant gave him nothing. It never did. The planet was so loud with life that finding one person in it was like listening for a single voice in a stadium, and Ventress had spent years learning how not to be heard.

But a med kit was a fact. A berth-master was a fact. He could hold facts.

Berth Nine was a pit cut into the side of a dead foundation pillar, wide enough for a light freighter and not much else. The landing clamps were open and empty. A Pantoran woman in a stained work coat sat in a booth at the edge of the pit, eating noodles out of a carton and watching a podrace replay on a cracked screen. She did not look up when his shadow fell across her.

"The ship that was here," Anakin said. "Two nights ago, maybe less. Who flew it, and where did it go?"

"Lots of ships are here." She slurped. "That's what a berth is for."

He took out his lightsaber. He did not ignite it. He only set it on the counter between them, the way he had set his hand on Tibbo's crate, and let her look at it.

The Pantoran put down her noodles.

"The *Marrow Lark*," she said. "Corellian hauler, older than me. Captain's a Nikto named Grell. He took one passenger. Pale, bald, tattoos on her chin, face like somebody had used it to stop a speeder. She paid for the whole cabin." She tapped her screen and the podrace vanished, replaced by a departure log. "Lifted thirty-one hours ago. Filed for Ord Mantell."

Thirty-one hours. He had been in a cantina six levels up, thirty-one hours ago, holding a Rodian against a wall.

"Is that where they're going?"

The berth-master laughed, one short breath through her nose. "Everybody files for Ord Mantell, General. It's the flight-plan version of 'I was home all night.' Grell could be halfway to the Corporate Sector. He could be dumping her out an airlock over Nar Shaddaa. Could be anywhere that isn't here."

"Did she say anything? Leave anything?"

"She said if anyone in a Jedi tunic came asking, I should tell him—" The Pantoran stopped. She looked at the lightsaber on the counter and seemed to reconsider the exact wording. "She said to tell him she had better things to do than die for his Padawan."

Anakin stood very still.

Then he picked up his lightsaber and walked to the edge of the empty pit, and looked down at the scorched clamps and the oil-stained duracrete, and the stillness broke. He drove his right fist into the support beam beside him. The beam boomed the length of the pit. When he pulled his hand back, the glove had split across the knuckles and the metal underneath gleamed, unhurt, and the beam had a crater in it, and none of that changed the fact that the only witness who could say a second person had stood in that warehouse was somewhere in hyperspace, going anywhere that wasn't here.

His comlink chimed. Not Obi-Wan this time. Text only.

*Motion to delay denied. They reconvene at the eighth hour. Where are you?*

He looked at the chrono. It was the sixth hour and a half. The Senate district was the better part of a thousand levels above him.

He started running again.

* * *

He reached the tribunal chamber with lower-level grime still on his boots and the smell of the drainage arch in his clothes, and the Coruscant Guard sentries at the doors took one look at his face and stepped aside without asking for his credentials. The doors parted on a silence so complete he could hear the hum of the ceiling lights.

The clerk was already reading.

"—on the first count, conspiracy in the bombing of the Jedi Temple hangar, resulting in the deaths of Jedi, clone personnel, and civilian workers, the tribunal finds the accused guilty."

Anakin stopped in the aisle.

Ahsoka stood at the defense table in binders, her back to him, her shoulders squared. She did not move at the word. Beside her, Padmé had one hand flat on the table, as if she were holding it down.

"On the second count, the murder of Letta Turmond while in Republic military custody: guilty."

The gallery was full. The families of the hangar dead sat together on the right in their deep blue mourning, a band of dark color like a bruise across the seats. Someone in that band made a small sound, not quite a sob. On the left, in the section reserved for the Order, Anakin saw Master Windu with his arms folded and Ki-Adi-Mundi with his eyes half-closed, and Plo Koon beside them with his masked face tipped toward the floor. Obi-Wan sat at the end of the row. He was looking at Anakin. Anakin did not look back.

"On the third count, the murders of Coruscant Guard troopers CT-2261, CT-4408, and CT-9170: guilty. On the fourth count, escape from lawful custody and assault upon officers of the Republic: guilty."

The clerk lowered the datapad. "Guilty on all counts, Supreme Chancellor."

On the raised bench, Palpatine inclined his head, slowly, the way a man bows at a graveside. The senators at the curved desk below him sat like carvings. Riyo Chuchi had her eyes shut. Bail Organa had his hands folded and was studying them as if they belonged to someone else.

Ahsoka turned her head. Not much. Just enough that Anakin, still standing in the aisle like an idiot, could see her profile, and she could see him.

She looked at his boots first. At the filth on them. Then at his face. He saw her read it there, the whole night, the Undercroft and the empty pit and the *Marrow Lark*, and he gave her the smallest shake of his head because there was nothing else to give.

Her chin came up a fraction. Then she faced the bench again.

Tarkin rose from the prosecution table. He had not even put on a show of waiting.

"Your Excellency. The Republic is at war. Its enemies are not only beyond our borders but, as this tribunal has now found, within our most trusted institutions. The accused used the training, the trust, and the weapons given to her by this Republic to murder those who served it. Leniency in such a case would not be mercy. It would be an invitation." He folded his hands behind his back. "The prosecution asks that the sentence be death."

The word went through the chamber like cold air through an open hatch.

Anakin heard his own breath. He heard Padmé's chair scrape as she rose to object, and Palpatine lift one hand to still her, gently, the way one quiets a restless child.

"Senator Amidala, you will have your opportunity to speak to the sentence." The Chancellor's voice was soft and carried to every corner. "And I will not pronounce a judgment of this weight without reflection. This tribunal stands in recess until the fourteenth hour."

The chamber came apart into noise. Anakin was moving before the gavel finished sounding, down the aisle against the current of bodies, and Padmé met him at the rail with her eyes too bright and her voice very low.

"Did you find her?"

"She's gone. Off-world. Thirty-one hours ago."

Padmé closed her eyes for exactly one breath. When she opened them, she was a senator again. "Then I'll argue sentence with what we have. Service record. Age. The war. Anakin, your hand—"

He looked down. The split glove, the bare knuckles. He closed his fingers over them. Across the room, the guards were taking Ahsoka out through the side door, and she did not look back this time, and somehow that was worse.

"I have to go," he said.

"Go where?"

He was already walking. He did not answer, because he was not sure she would want to hear it, and he was not sure he wanted to say it aloud: that there was only one man in the chamber who could change the word Tarkin had said, and that man had looked down at Anakin from the bench, just for a moment, before the gavel fell, and his face had been full of sorrow.

* * *

The Chancellor's office was red and warm, too warm, the way it always was. The heat pressed on Anakin's face after the chill of the lower levels and made the grime on his skin feel thick. The tall windows looked out over the Senate district, all glittering spires and orderly traffic lanes, so far above the Undercroft that it might have been a different planet.

Palpatine did not keep him waiting. That was the first thing that undid him a little. The Chagrian aide at the outer door had barely spoken his name before the inner doors opened and the Chancellor himself was there, robes rustling, holding out both hands.

"Anakin. My dear boy. Come in, come in. You look as though you haven't slept in a week."

"Five days," Anakin said, and then, because his mouth had decided to say true things without consulting him: "Six, maybe."

"Sit. Please."

He didn't sit. He stood in the middle of the carpet with his ruined glove and his filthy boots, and Palpatine went to the window and stood beside it, half-turned toward him, patient as stone.

"She didn't do it," Anakin said.

"I know that is what you believe."

"It's not what I believe. It's what *happened*. There was someone else in that warehouse. There was someone else on the Dovrin platform. I fought them. I saw them." He nearly said it then: *they fought like us*. The words were right there, a Soresu turn on a catwalk, a parry he had seen a hundred times in the Temple training halls. He swallowed them. If the figure had been trained in the Temple, then the Temple had produced the bomber, and Tarkin would only say that Ahsoka had trained them. Every truth he had was a weapon that cut her. "I almost had the one witness who could prove it. She's gone. Thirty-one hours. I was thirty-one hours too slow."

"And so you have come to me." Palpatine's voice was not unkind. It was the opposite of unkind, which was harder to bear. "You understand what you are asking, Anakin. You are asking me to set aside the finding of a lawful tribunal on the strength of a witness who cannot be found and a fight that no one else saw."

"I'm asking you not to kill her."

The words came out much too loud for the room. Palpatine did not flinch. He only studied Anakin in silence, and then he crossed to him and laid a hand on his arm, just above the mechanical wrist, as if he knew precisely where flesh ended and metal began.

"Do you think I want to?" he said quietly. "I have known that child since she first came to you. I have watched her grow up in the reports from Christophsis, from Ryloth, from a hundred places whose names the public will never learn. I do not want to sign this. But I am not a king, Anakin. I am a servant of the Senate and of the law, and the law is a blunt instrument even in the gentlest hands."

"Then don't use it."

"If only it were so simple." Palpatine sighed and let him go. He went to his desk and stood behind it, touching the edge of it with his fingertips. "Tell me. Where were the Jedi this morning?"

Anakin stared at him.

"I saw Master Windu in the gallery," Palpatine went on, in the same mild tone. "I saw Master Mundi, and Master Koon, and your own Master Kenobi. They were very still. Did any of them come to this office to plead for her? Did any of them petition the tribunal, or speak to the senators, or offer so much as a character statement?" He paused. "You are the only one, Anakin. The only one who came."

Something in Anakin's chest tightened and burned. He thought of Windu's hand at Ahsoka's montral, unclasping her beads. He thought of Plo Koon's face turned to the floor.

"They expelled her so you could try her," he said. "They washed their hands."

"I will not speak against the Order. They have their reasons, I am sure." Palpatine's mouth moved in something that was not a smile. "But I will say that loyalty is a rarer thing than it ought to be, and that I have always admired it in you."

Anakin's knees were shaking. He realized it with a kind of distant surprise, the way he sometimes noticed a wound only after a fight. He thought about kneeling. He thought he might do it, here on the red carpet, if it would help.

"Please," he said. "I'll do anything. Whatever you need. Just—not this. Not her."

The Chancellor's face changed. For a moment it looked almost stern.

"Don't say that, my boy. Not to anyone. Not even to me." He came around the desk again and rested both hands on Anakin's shoulders. They were light hands, an old man's hands, and they held him perfectly still. "Men in my position remember such things, whether we wish to or not. And you should never put yourself in anyone's debt for something they ought to do freely."

"Then will you?"

Palpatine held his gaze. The window light was behind him, so that his face was in shadow and only his eyes were clear, and they were wet.

"I can promise you nothing," he said. "I wish I could. I can promise only that I will listen to my conscience, and that I will remember everything you have said to me today. Now go back. Wash your face. Let her see you there when it is read." His hands tightened, briefly. "Whatever happens, she should not be alone for it."

Anakin went. He did not wash his face.

* * *

At the fourteenth hour the chamber was fuller than it had been in the morning, as if word had spread through the Senate that something worth watching was about to happen. Holonet drones hovered at the back, their lenses glinting. Anakin took the seat in the third row behind the defense table, the one he had left empty during closings, and gripped the rail in front of him with both hands.

Padmé spoke to the sentence for twenty minutes. She spoke about a girl who had been given to a war at fourteen and had never once refused an order that would save lives. She spoke about Christophsis and Ryloth and an ice world where Ahsoka had kept younglings alive. She spoke about the Republic's long tradition of distinguishing between justice and vengeance. It was the best speech Anakin had ever heard her give. He watched the senators while she gave it. Mon Mothma looked at Padmé with something like grief. The others looked at their hands.

Tarkin rose and said four sentences and sat down. He did not need more.

Then Palpatine stood.

"Ahsoka Tano," he said. "Please rise."

She rose. The binders clinked once. She was so much smaller than the bench, Anakin thought. She had always been small, and she had always moved so that nobody noticed, and now she was standing still and there was nothing to hide it.

"This tribunal has found you guilty of crimes against the Republic, against the Jedi Order you once served, and against the soldiers who defended you. The law of the Republic in a time of war is clear. For crimes of this nature, committed in this manner, the sentence prescribed is death."

Ahsoka did not move.

Anakin's mechanical hand closed on the rail. He felt the metal give under his fingers and could not make them stop.

"However."

The word was very quiet. The whole room leaned toward it.

"The Senate, in its wisdom, has entrusted this office with certain emergency powers for the duration of the present conflict. Among them is the power of clemency." Palpatine looked down at Ahsoka, and his voice softened until it was almost tender. "I cannot ignore that the accused has bled for this Republic since childhood. I cannot ignore that she is young, and that the war has asked of her what it should ask of no one. Let it never be said that the Republic forgot those who served it, even in their darkest hour. By the authority vested in me, I commute the sentence of death."

A sound went through the gallery, part gasp and part protest. In the deep blue band on the right, someone stood up and was pulled down again.

"In its place, the condemned will serve a sentence of imprisonment at the Republic Penal Facility on Narkis, for the remainder of her natural life."

Anakin was watching Ahsoka. He would remember that afterward, that he had been watching her and not the bench, and so he saw it.

At *death*, she had not moved at all. She had been ready for death. He had seen her ready for it a dozen times, on a dozen battlefields, the stillness she wore like armor when the odds had stopped mattering. But at *life*, something went out of her. Her shoulders did not drop; they locked. Her lekku, which had been still, drew in tight against her back. Her lips parted, and he saw them shape the word without sound, *life*, as if she were testing whether it would hold her weight.

She was seventeen.

Then she turned her head and found him in the third row, and for one long moment she looked at him the way she used to look at him across a battlefield when the plan had gone wrong and she needed to know what came next. He did not know what came next. He had no plan. All he had was the relief, which had arrived all at once and was so enormous that he could barely see around it.

She was alive. She was going to stay alive.

He looked up at the bench. Palpatine was gathering his robes to sit, and for an instant, just before he did, the Chancellor's eyes met his across the chamber. There was no triumph in them. Only a small, tired nod, as if to say: *I listened. It was all I could do.*

Gratitude went through Anakin like heat into frozen hands. It hurt the way that heat hurt, sharp and total, and he did not care. Someone had done something. After five nights of nothing, after a Council vote and a stolen set of beads and a gallery full of Jedi sitting with their arms folded, someone had finally reached out and caught her before she fell.

He looked at the Jedi gallery then. He made himself.

They were standing to leave. Windu's face was unreadable. Ki-Adi-Mundi was speaking quietly to Shaak Ti. Plo Koon had not risen; he sat with his clawed hands on his knees and his head bowed, and Anakin wanted to cross the room and ask him whether it had been worth it, whether it had felt like wisdom when he raised his hand in the Council chamber, whether it felt like wisdom now. Of all of them, only Obi-Wan was looking at Ahsoka, and he looked as if he had swallowed something sharp.

None of them had come to the Chancellor's office. None of them had gone into the lower levels. They had expelled her and sat down to watch.

The guards took Ahsoka out through the side door. She went without looking back. The evidence case with her lightsabers went after her on the hover-cart, #417 and #418, and Anakin watched it all the way to the door.

Obi-Wan caught him in the corridor outside. He had the look he wore before a negotiation, careful and tired.

"Anakin. You weren't at closings. Padmé—Senator Amidala—said you were following a lead."

"It went off-world."

"I'm sorry." And Obi-Wan sounded it, which made it worse. "For what it's worth, I believe the Chancellor did the best that could be done."

"The best that could be done." Anakin heard his own voice from a distance, very calm. "He did *something*, Master. That's more than I can say for the people who sent her to him."

Obi-Wan's face tightened. "The Council was in an impossible position."

"She was in an impossible position. You were in a gallery."

He walked away. Obi-Wan did not follow, and Anakin told himself he was glad.

* * *

The Chancellor did not watch the Holonet coverage. He had no need to. The commentators would be saying what he had arranged for them to say: that the verdict was just and the mercy was greater, that Palpatine had shown the galaxy the Republic still had a heart. By morning, a dozen systems that had wavered toward the Separatists would be reminded that the Supreme Chancellor was a kind man. That was useful. It was not the point.

He sat alone in the red office with the lights low and turned a small object over in his fingers, a stylus he had used to sign the commutation order. Narkis. Tarkin had recommended the place himself, and its warden, a careful woman of his own training who would keep the girl very cold and very quiet. A Jedi without the Force, in the ice, at the edge of nothing. Let her be forgotten for a while. Things that were forgotten could be remembered later, when remembering became convenient.

But the girl had never been the point either.

He thought of the boy standing on the carpet in his torn glove, shaking, offering *anything*. He had refused it, of course. A debt that was demanded could be resented. A debt that was refused could only grow, and the one who owed it would keep paying in installments of his own invention, for years, and call each payment love. Gratitude was the finest chain ever forged, because the prisoner made every link himself and polished it.

And the Jedi had helped. They always helped. They had given him the boy's anger as neatly as if they had wrapped it.

He set the stylus down and touched a control. Above the desk, the tribunal's internal recording bloomed in blue light: the gallery, frozen at the moment the word *death* was spoken. He let it run forward slowly, frame by frame, studying faces the way other men studied cards. The mourning families. The senators. The Jedi, so very still.

And there, at the end of the Order's row, half-hidden behind her Master's shoulder: a young Mirialan, her hood fallen back. At *death*, every other Jedi face in the gallery had gone smooth. Hers had come apart. Her mouth was half open, her eyes wide and wet, and they were not on the bench, and not on the condemned, but on the floor beneath her own feet, the way the guilty look at the place they expect to open.

The Chancellor stopped the recording. He looked at that face for a long time.

None of it was news. Days before the tribunal convened, an auxiliary maintenance camera in the upper service passage of the Military Operations Center had given him a hooded healer and a raised hand, in the very second Letta Turmond began to die. The camera had given him everything but a face. The gallery had just supplied it.

Then he saved the frame, and smiled for the first time all day.

## Chapter 6: Good Soldiers

The order came down an hour after the sentencing, one line on a datapad: *Remove TANO, A. (former Commander) from 501st command roster, effective immediately.* Captain Rex had been looking at it for twenty minutes, thumb resting over the confirm key, when Fives threw his helmet across the barracks.

It hit the far bulkhead hard enough to leave a white scar in the paint and came back across the deck spinning on its crown. Nobody moved to stop it. It rocked to a halt against Tup's bunk, the black visor staring up at the ceiling like a man who'd been shot and hadn't finished being surprised about it.

"Life," Fives said. "*Life.* And we're supposed to call that mercy."

"Lower your voice, Fives."

"Why? Who's listening, Captain? The Chancellor?" Fives turned in a slow circle, arms out, taking in the long room: the two rows of bunks, the weapons racks, the strip of blue tactical lighting along the floor that never quite went off. "Hey, Chancellor. Thank you. Thank you so much. Very generous."

"Fives."

"She's seventeen. She held the line on Christophsis when she was fourteen and didn't know which end of a scope to look through. She pulled Jesse out of a hole with her own two hands—"

"I remember," Jesse said from his bunk. He was lying on his back with his boots crossed at the ankle and his forearm over his eyes, the tattoo on his face hidden. "I was there. In the hole."

"Then say something."

"I am saying something. I'm saying I remember." Jesse didn't move his arm. "Also, I'm saying the Republic just told every trooper on this planet what we're worth, and it's less than one thin Togruta girl. Which I could have told you for free."

Kix sat at the end of the medic's bench with his kit open in front of him, inventorying bacta patches he had inventoried that morning. He had not said a word since they'd come back from the Senate district. He counted twenty patches, closed the kit, opened it again and started over.

Rex set the datapad face-down on his footlocker.

"That's enough," he said, not loudly. He had learned a long time ago that loudly was for battlefields. In a barracks, quiet carried further. "All of you. Fives, pick up your bucket. Jesse, sit up when your captain's talking. Kix—"

Kix looked up.

Rex found he had nothing for Kix. "Carry on," he said.

Fives didn't pick up his helmet. He stood over it, breathing hard through his nose, and for a moment Rex thought he was going to have to make it an order. Then Tup leaned off his bunk, scooped it up by the chin guard, and held it out.

"You dented it," Tup said. "Nice work. That'll scare the clankers."

Fives took it. Some of the stiffness went out of his shoulders, not all. "Thanks."

Tup sat back and pressed the heel of his hand against his right temple, hard, the way a man pushes on a door that won't stay shut.

"You all right?" Kix said. It was the first thing he'd said since the Senate district.

"Fine. It's the lights." Tup squinted at the blue strip along the deck. "Somebody in maintenance thinks we're fish. Been giving me a headache since the briefing this morning."

"Let me scan you."

"It's a headache, Kix, not a blaster bolt. I'll sleep it off." Tup lay down and turned toward the wall. "Wake me when the galaxy makes sense."

"You'll be asleep a long time," Jesse said.

Kix watched Tup's back a moment longer, then went back to his bacta patches. Rex marked it the way he marked everything, filed it, and let it go. There were too many other things tonight. A trooper with a headache was the least of them.

He picked up the datapad again.

*Remove TANO, A.*

He had removed names before. That was part of the job, and the job had never once been kind about it. Hardcase. Echo. Too many from Umbara, whose numbers he could still recite in order. Each time the roster software asked if he was sure, and each time he was sure, because the dead were dead and the living needed accurate numbers. You did it quickly. You did it yourself, so that no one under you had to. You did it and then you went and ran drills until your legs gave out.

But she wasn't dead. That was what made his thumb stay where it was. She was alive, and in a cell eight kilometers from here, and the Republic had decided to remove her anyway.

"Captain." Fives had come up beside him, helmet under his arm. His voice had dropped. "You don't have to do it tonight."

"It's an order."

"Orders keep. She's still—" He stopped. "She's still ours until tomorrow."

Rex looked at him. Fives held his gaze. ARC training put a kind of steel in a man, but Fives had been stubborn before Kamino ever gave him a pauldron, and the steel had only given the stubbornness something to stand on.

"Get some sleep," Rex said. "That's an order too."

Fives went. Rex waited until the bunks had settled, until Jesse's breathing evened out and Kix finally put the kit away and Tup stopped shifting against the wall. Then he took his helmet off and set it on the footlocker beside him, so that whatever his face was doing, it was doing it alone, and he sat there in the blue dark with the datapad and did not press the key.

* * *

He found General Skywalker where he'd expected to: in the Temple's rebuilt hangar, under the belly of a fighter that did not need any work.

The new hangar smelled of fresh sealant and ozone. The scorch marks were gone. The memorial candles had been moved to an alcove near the east doors, a few hundred small flames in a recess of pale stone, and the maintenance crews walked around that part of the floor without seeming to think about it. Rex had been here the day after the bombing. He walked around it too.

"General."

A hydrospanner clanged against something. "If this is about the roster, I already know."

"It's not about the roster, sir."

Skywalker rolled out on the mechanic's creeper and looked up at him. He looked like he'd been awake since the verdict and possibly since the trial began. His right glove was still split across the knuckles, and the metal showed through, dull under the work lights. He'd been using that hand on the fighter. There were scratches on the plating that hadn't come from any tool.

"Then what is it?"

"The transfer's at dawn, sir. Platform Six at the Military Operations Center. I'd like to see her before it goes."

Skywalker sat up. For a moment he didn't say anything. Then he wiped his hands on a rag, slowly, with care, as if this mattered.

"They won't let you in," he said. "Fox has the Guard on it. Nobody but counsel and the Judiciary."

"Yes, sir. That's why I'm asking you."

"You think they'll let me in?"

"I think they'll let in anyone you sign for, sir. You're a Jedi General, and you just watched the Chancellor do you a favor in front of the whole Senate. People will remember that for about a week." Rex paused. "I'd like to use it while it lasts."

That got something. Not a smile, not quite. The ghost of the expression that used to come before a really bad plan on a really bad day. "Rex. Was that a joke?"

"Observation, sir."

Skywalker pulled a datapad from his belt and keyed something into it, thumb moving fast. He signed it with his mechanical hand. Rex watched the bare metal fingers press the stylus and thought about the nosebleed the General had come back with from the Dovrin platform, the way those same fingers had locked open, splayed, and Kix had needed a servo driver to make them close again.

"Here." Skywalker held out the pad. "Authorization for one visit, escort of the 501st command staff. Thirty minutes. If Fox argues, tell him to comm me. Better yet, tell him to comm the Chancellor's office." His mouth twisted. "Apparently that works now."

Rex took it. "You're not coming, sir?"

The General looked past him, at the alcove of candles. "I saw her tonight. After."

Rex waited.

"She asked me not to come again." Skywalker's voice was very level. "She said once was enough. She said if I came to the platform she'd have to be brave twice, and she didn't think she had it in her." He laughed, short and without humor. "She's always been better at strategy than me."

"Sir—"

"Go, Rex. Tell her—" He stopped. His jaw worked. The metal hand closed around the rag and kept closing until the fabric tore. "No. Don't tell her anything from me. She knows. She'll just have to keep knowing."

He lay back down on the creeper and rolled under the fighter, and Rex stood there for a little while listening to the hydrospanner work on a bolt that was already tight.

* * *

The detention level at the Republic Center for Military Operations was all white light and right angles, and Commander Fox was waiting at the end of it with his arms folded.

"Captain."

"Commander."

Fox read the authorization. He read it again. Under the red-trimmed helmet his posture said nothing; Rex had always found that the most irritating thing about Fox, and also the thing he respected most. A good officer did not let you see him think.

"Thirty minutes," Fox said finally. "Weapons stay with me. You'll be monitored."

Rex unholstered both DC-17s and handed them over butt-first. "I'd expect nothing less."

"She's been no trouble." Fox said it to the pistols, not to Rex. "Since the verdict. Ate what we gave her. Slept, or lay still enough that the monitors said so. Asked for water once." He paused. "I've had senators give my men more grief over a parking violation."

It was, Rex understood, the closest thing to an apology the Coruscant Guard was capable of. He didn't say anything. Fox keyed the door.

The cell was three meters by three. A bench, a sink, a ray shield where the fourth wall should have been. Commander Tano—he could not make his mind call her anything else—sat on the bench with her back straight and her hands loose in her lap. There were no binders on her wrists. Someone had healed the raw places the binders had made during the trial, and the skin there was a little paler than the rest, new and clean.

Her montrals were bare. He had known about the beads. Every trooper in the 501st had heard about Master Windu taking them; Jesse had said a few things about it that Rex had pretended not to hear. But knowing it and seeing it were different. The place where the chain used to hang was just a stripe of her own pattern now, white and blue, and he kept looking at it, and then making himself not look at it.

She stood when the ray shield dropped.

"Rex."

"Commander."

"You know I'm not, right?" Her voice was light, nearly the old voice. "They had a whole ceremony. There were forms."

"I didn't fill out any forms, sir."

That earned him the smallest flicker at the corner of her mouth. She looked him up and down, all of him, the armor with the blue markings and the scuffed plate on his left thigh where a droid had clipped him on Anaxes, and he realized she was doing what she always did before a mission: taking inventory. Checking that her people were whole.

"Take your bucket off," she said. "I want to see if you've been sleeping."

He took it off. She looked at his face for a long moment.

"You haven't."

"Neither have you, sir."

"I've been sleeping fine. It's very quiet here. Nobody's shooting at me." She sat back down on the bench and nodded at the far end of it, and after a second he sat too, helmet on his knee. The bench was too short for two people in armor and a prisoner. Their shoulders almost touched. "How are the boys?"

He could have lied. He thought about it. Then he thought about how many times she'd caught him at it, on how many worlds.

"Fives threw his helmet at a wall. Jesse's angry. Kix has counted the same bacta patches about forty times. Tup's got a headache and won't let Kix look at it." Rex turned the helmet a quarter turn on his knee. "We're all right."

"That's not all right, Rex. That's a squad report."

"It's a squad report that says we're all right."

She laughed. It was short and a little ragged at the edges, but it was real, and the sound of it in that white room did something to his chest that he did not care for at all.

"Tell Fives he owes the quartermaster a helmet," she said. "Tell Jesse he's allowed to be angry, but not at you. Tell Kix to make Tup get scanned, and tell Tup I said so, and that if he argues I'll come back and do it myself." A pause, very slight. "He won't know I can't."

"I'll tell him."

"Good."

They sat. Down the corridor a door hissed, and boots went by, and the sound faded. The monitor in the corner of the ceiling blinked its small red light.

"They're sending me to Narkis," she said. "Did you know that?"

"I heard."

"I looked it up. They let me have a datapad for the trial, and nobody took it back until this morning." She was looking at the ray-shield emitters, not at him. "It's an ice moon at the edge of Wild Space. The prison's cut into a glacier. They mine tibanna there, or refine it, or something. It's always cold, and it's always twilight, and nobody's ever been released from it, because nobody's ever been sent there for anything you get released from." She smiled at the emitters. "So. Bring a coat."

"Commander."

"I'm all right, Rex."

"With respect, sir, that's a squad report."

She turned and looked at him then. Her eyes were very bright, and she held them that way, the way you hold a position you know is going to be overrun: not because it'll matter in the end, but because the people behind you need the time.

He understood, then, that she was doing this for him. That she had decided, sometime in the night, how she would be when he came, and she was going to be that way if it killed her. She was composed because a commander is composed in front of her troops. And she still thought of him as one of hers, even if the roster didn't.

He looked down at his helmet. He had never in his life wanted so badly to put it back on.

"I need you to do something for me," she said.

"Anything."

"Don't say that. People keep saying that." Something passed over her face and was gone. "It's an order. My last one, so you'd better follow it."

"Sir."

"Look after Anakin."

He'd been expecting something else. He wasn't sure what. *Find who did this. Clear my name. Get me out.* He would have tried any of those. He'd been half-hoping she would ask.

"He's going to tear himself apart," she said. "He already is. He thinks this is his fault, because he stopped to save those people on the platform instead of chasing whoever it was. He'll never say it, but he thinks it. And he's going to go looking for someone to blame who isn't him, and he's going to find someone, and it'll be the wrong person, and he'll do something stupid." She drew a breath. "He's not as strong as he thinks he is, Rex. He's strong. Stars, he's strong. But not the way he thinks. Somebody has to be standing next to him when he finds that out, and it can't be me."

"He's a Jedi General," Rex said, and heard how thin it sounded.

"I was a Jedi Commander. Look how well that went." She shook her head. "Promise me."

Rex thought of the General under the fighter, tightening the tight bolt. The torn rag. *Apparently that works now.*

"I promise," he said.

"Good." She let out a breath, and with it some of the straightness went out of her spine, just for a moment, so that she was a tired girl on a short bench in a white room. Then she gathered it back up. "Good. That's done, then."

"Is there anything else? Anything you need? Anything I can—"

"Rex." Gently. "There isn't."

The red light on the monitor blinked. Fox's voice came over the speaker, flat. "Five minutes, Captain."

They didn't use them, in the end. There was nothing else that would fit. They sat side by side and listened to the quiet, and when the four-minute mark passed she stood, and he stood, and she put her hand on his vambrace the way she used to on the gunships before a drop. Two quick taps. *Ready.*

"Take care of them," she said. "Take care of you."

"Yes, sir."

He stepped back to the line of the ray-shield emitters. He put the helmet under his left arm. His right hand started to come up, the way it had come up a thousand times on a thousand decks, and stopped halfway, because she was not his commander. The Jedi Council had seen to it, and the tribunal, and the Chancellor, and the roster software with its little confirm key. There were regulations about rendering honors to a convicted prisoner. He knew them. He had taught them to shinies.

She saw his hand stop. She gave him a small nod, as if to say: *That's all right. I understand.*

Rex finished the salute.

He held it square, palm flat, the way they'd drilled it into him on Kamino before he was old enough to understand what any of it meant. He held it until the ray shield hummed back to life between them and painted her in faint blue. She didn't return it. Prisoners couldn't. But she stood very straight, and her chin came up, and that was enough.

Fox didn't say anything when he came out. He handed back the pistols. If the monitors had recorded the salute, and they had, it never appeared in any report Rex ever saw.

* * *

Platform Six was on the windward side of the Military Operations Center, high enough that the morning air came in cold and smelling of the upper traffic lanes. Rex stood just short of the restricted line with his helmet on. He was not supposed to be there. Nobody told him to leave.

The prison transport was a squat grey Judiciary hull with no markings except a registry number and the Republic crest, small, beside the boarding ramp. It looked like what it was: a box built to move people who would not be coming back. A platoon of Coruscant Guard formed a corridor from the detention lift to the ramp. Red armor, stun batons, rifles at port arms. Rex counted them out of habit. Twenty-four, for one unarmed girl in binders.

They brought her out at first light. She'd been given grey thermals and a heavy grey coat with a Judiciary number stenciled across the breast. She walked between two guards without being pushed. At the foot of the ramp she stopped and looked up, once, at the sky over the city, as though she were memorizing the color of it.

She did not look toward the restricted line. He had not expected her to. She'd said once was enough, and she'd meant it for him too.

Then she went up the ramp, and it closed behind her.

The engines lit. Heat came across the platform in a slow wave, and the hull rose and turned on its repulsors and began to climb, and Rex watched it the way he had watched a hundred gunships lift toward a hundred bad drops: not because watching did anything, but because the people inside deserved to have somebody see them go.

He thought about the lower levels. He had been down there with the General when she ran. He had been the one who set the squads to cover the drainage outflows; he'd drawn the grid himself, carefully, the way he did everything. *Stun settings,* he'd told them. *She's ours. Bring her in safe.* He had said it as though it were a kindness. As though bringing her in had ever been anything but handing her to this.

He had followed every order. He had followed them well. And every one of them, every clean, correct order, had been a step on the road to this platform.

It wasn't the first time he'd wondered what a good soldier was for. It was the first time the question had a face.

"Captain."

Fox had come up beside him without a sound. He'd taken his helmet off, which he almost never did on duty. His face was Rex's face, more lines at the mouth, a thin scar along the jaw. He was watching the transport too, now a dark shape against the brightening clouds.

"She'll be all right," Fox said. "For a while. Narkis is clean. The warden runs it by the book."

"That supposed to comfort me?"

"It's supposed to be accurate." Fox was quiet a moment. "There's one other thing. You'll hear about it eventually. Better from me."

Rex turned his head.

"The Judiciary sent a crate down this morning, before the prisoner came out. Sealed. Medical-grade handling, two technicians, a Senate courier to sign it over." Fox's eyes stayed on the sky. "It's a new device. For Force-users in long-term custody. The warden on Narkis requested it by name. They're fitting it on her during the flight."

"Fitting what?"

"A collar." Fox's jaw tightened. "The technician called it a dampener. He was very proud of it. He said once it's switched on, a Jedi can't feel the Force at all. Not a whisper. He said it was like—" Fox stopped, and Rex saw him decide to finish it anyway. "He said it was like going deaf. That they don't even hear themselves anymore."

The transport was a speck now. Then it was a glint. Then the clouds took it, and there was nothing over Coruscant but morning.

Rex stood on the platform for a long time after Fox had gone. When he finally walked back to the barracks, Fives was awake, sitting on his bunk with his dented helmet in his lap, and he looked up as Rex came in and didn't ask.

Rex picked up the datapad from his footlocker. *Remove TANO, A.* The confirm key glowed under his thumb.

He set the pad down again, unconfirmed, and turned to Fives.

"Tell me again," he said quietly, "everything you remember about that night in the lower levels. Every single thing."

## Chapter 7: The Deep

The collar came out of its crate in two curved halves, nested in grey foam like something precious, and the technician lifted them into the cabin light the way a jeweler lifts a setting to look for flaws.

"Chin down, please," he said. "It's cold for a moment. After that you won't notice it at all."

Ahsoka didn't move. She sat on the steel bench bolted along the transport's bulkhead, her binders locked to a ring between her boots, and looked at the thing in his hands. Grey metal, dull, no wider than two fingers. A seam where the halves would meet. A small glass eye set at the front, dark for now, waiting.

They had been in hyperspace for nine hours. The Judiciary transport had no viewports in the hold, only a long strip of white lights that buzzed when the hull flexed, and twenty-four men of the Coruscant Guard sitting in rows along the opposite wall with their rifles upright between their knees. She had felt them since Platform Six. Not their faces, which were helmets, but the steady pressure of them in the Force: a cold, contained hostility, like standing beside a sealed reactor. Three of their brothers had died outside her cell with her lightsabers in the wounds. Nothing said in the tribunal had changed what they felt when they looked at her.

She didn't blame them. That was the part she couldn't get past. If she'd been one of them, she'd have hated her too.

"Prisoner." The sergeant at the hatch had red markings on his pauldron and a voice with no give in it. "Chin down."

She put her chin down.

The metal was colder than she'd expected. It touched the back of her neck below her montrals, then the sides of her throat, and the technician's gloved fingers fitted the halves together with a fussy precision that made her want to bite him. There was a click. A second click. A faint hiss as the seam sealed itself, and then the collar sat against her skin as if it had been grown there.

"Registering," the technician murmured, tapping his datapad. "Keyed to the facility master. The warden will hold the control. And—one moment—activation."

She had a moment. She knew it, the way you know the last step before a drop.

So she used it. She closed her eyes and opened herself, all the way, the way she hadn't dared to since the verdict, because she had been afraid of what she'd feel. She let it come. The hyperdrive's deep thrum, not as noise but as something almost alive, a note held under everything. The Guard troopers, each one distinct now that she was looking: the sergeant's tight, banked anger; the trooper nearest the bench, who was very young and, under the anger, afraid of her; the one at the end of the row who was thinking about something else entirely, someone else, far away. The technician's eager pride, bright as a struck match. Beyond the hull, hyperspace itself, a current she'd ridden a hundred times and never once really listened to. And somewhere behind all of it, thin as a thread and getting thinner, a pull back toward Coruscant that she didn't let herself name.

She had been three years old when Plo Koon found her. The Force had been there before that. It had been there before her name.

The light came on. Blue.

It wasn't like a door closing. A door closing made a sound. This was the opposite of a sound—the sudden absence of one she hadn't known she'd been hearing her whole life. Like a hum in a room that stops, and only when it stops do you realize it was there, and that the silence underneath it is enormous.

The sergeant's anger was gone. Not gone—he was still sitting there, rifle across his chest, still angry, she was sure of it. She just couldn't feel it. The young trooper was only armor. The technician was only a thin man with ink on his cuffs. The hyperdrive was noise. The ship was a box. She was inside a box, and the box was inside nothing.

She reached. She didn't decide to; it was reflex, the way a hand goes out in the dark for a wall. She reached, and reached, and there was no wall. There was no dark. There was nothing to reach into.

Her breath went short. Her lekku drew tight against her back and her hands closed so hard on the binder chain that the links bit her palms. She could still feel the shape of the hold around her, the ceiling close above her montrals, the bulkhead at her shoulder—that much was her own body, and she clung to it the way someone overboard clings to wreckage. But under it, where the Force had always been, there was a silence so complete it felt like a wound.

"There," the technician said. "Readings are clean. Suppression at full." He looked up at her, interested. "How does it feel?"

She opened her mouth. It took her two tries.

"Quiet," she said.

He smiled, delighted, and made a note. "That's exactly what it ought to feel like."

The young trooper by the bench turned his helmet toward the far wall and kept it there.

They made two more jumps. Thirty hours, maybe more; there was no chrono in the hold and she lost count after the second meal. She tried to meditate and found that meditation without the Force was sitting very still in a cold room while your heart pounded. She stopped trying. On the third jump she tried again, out of something like hope, and the silence was worse for having been tested. After that she counted rivets. There were four hundred and twelve on the bulkhead opposite. She counted them nine times and got four hundred and twelve every time, and it was the only thing in the galaxy that seemed to be holding still.

When the transport finally dropped out of hyperspace, someone opened the cockpit hatch and a pale blue light fell across the hold. The young trooper leaned to look forward through it, and she heard him say, under his breath, "Stars. That's bleak."

She leaned too. Through the hatch and the cockpit canopy beyond it hung a moon the color of old ice, white streaked with blue, hard-edged against nothing. A dim orange sun sat on its horizon like something that had given up halfway through rising. No city lights. No lights at all, except one dark pock in the glacier, ringed in faint amber, getting larger.

Narkis. The edge of Wild Space. The place they'd chosen instead of killing her.

The light at her throat blinked, slow and patient, as if it were counting.

* * *

The cold reached her before the ramp had finished lowering.

It came up the incline like water, through the grey coat, through the thermals beneath it, straight to the bone. Her first breath of it burned. Her second made her lekku ache all the way to the tips. Togruta came from a warm world; she had been cold before, on Ilum and worse places, but always with the Force banking a little heat in her, the way you'd shield a candle with your hand. There was no hand now.

The hangar had been cut into the glacier itself. Its walls were ice, blue-white, veined with darker bands where centuries of snowfall had compressed into layers, and the roof was a durasteel dome set into the ice like a lid. Amber floodlights hung from it on chains. Everything was frosted—the deck, the cargo cranes, the rails along the gantries—and everything breathed: guards, mechanics, the six clone troopers in insulated grey-white armor standing at the hangar's edge, all of them trailing white plumes like small engines.

Judiciary guards met the ramp. They wore dark grey greatcoats and padded helmets with amber visors, and they did not look at her the way the Guard troopers had. They looked at her the way you'd look at cargo that had arrived with a warning label.
