The Guard sergeant handed a datapad down to one of them. She saw him hesitate on the last line before he signed it, and then he signed it and walked back up the ramp without a word, and the transport's hatch began to close behind him.

"Twenty-two fourteen."

The woman at the foot of the ramp was the only person in the hangar not wearing a greatcoat. She wore a uniform of olive-grey, every seam pressed, the collar fastened to the throat, as if the cold had been informed it was not to touch her and was complying. Fifties, Ahsoka guessed. Human. Iron-grey hair drawn back into a knot so tight it seemed to hold her face in place. She had a datapad in one hand and nothing at all in her expression.

Ahsoka looked down at her own coat. Stenciled across the left breast in black: NK9-2214.

"That's me," she said.

"That's you. Your name is in the file and it will stay there. Here you are a number. That isn't an insult. It's the same for every person in this facility, including people who were once considerably more important than you." The woman did not offer her hand. "I am Commandant Ilsa Vorne. I am the warden of Narkis-Nine. The inmates call it the Deep. I prefer the designation, but you'll hear both. Walk with me."

One of the guards took Ahsoka's elbow and pushed, hard enough to make her stumble on the frosted ramp.

"Sergeant Holt." Vorne didn't raise her voice. "She's walking. You needn't help her."

The hand let go.

They went through a pressure door into a corridor where the cold was very slightly less cold, and Vorne walked a half-step ahead with her datapad and spoke the way someone reads a manifest.

"Three counts a day. You will be at your cell grate for each. Labor shifts are ten hours. You'll be assigned to the ice cut in two days, once medical clears you. Meals on Ring Two at the posted times. If you miss a meal, you've missed it. Holonet access one hour a week, monitored. Correspondence by permission, also monitored." She glanced back. "You were a soldier. You understand schedules."

"I was a Jedi," Ahsoka said.

"You were a commander in the Grand Army of the Republic, which is the only part of your former life the Republic recognizes, and the part it has now concluded." Vorne stopped at a second door and turned. "Hold still."

She stepped close and touched the collar's light with one gloved fingertip, as if checking for dust. Then she unclipped a small black cylinder from her belt—no longer than a finger, a single recessed stud at one end—and pressed it once. The blue light flickered and steadied.

"This is keyed to my office and to my person," Vorne said. "There is no other control on this moon. If the collar is damaged or tampered with, my office knows within a second, and you will spend thirty days in the Well." She clipped the cylinder back onto her belt. "The Well is the bottom of the shaft. It is isolation. I don't recommend it."

"Do you put a lot of Jedi in collars?"

"You are the first Force-user this facility has held. You may be one of the first anywhere to wear that device for any length of time." Vorne's voice did not change. "I requested it because I don't intend for your presence here to become a problem. Admiral Tarkin recommended this facility to the tribunal. He recommended me. I served under him for six years on the Outer Rim, and I don't intend to disappoint him." She paused, very slightly. "Neither do I intend to be cruel to you. I want you to understand that those two things are not in conflict."

Ahsoka looked at her. At the pressed seams and the tight knot of hair and the eyes that held no interest in her at all, beyond whether she would be a problem.

"The other prisoners," she said. "Do they know who I am?"

"They have the Holonet. Your trial was extensively covered."

"Then you know what they'll want."

"I know exactly what they'll want. What they want, and what they're permitted to do about it, is the business of my guards." Vorne turned back to the door. "It isn't yours. You are not a commander here, Twenty-two fourteen. You don't get to worry about anyone but yourself."

Ahsoka thought of Rex in the cell doorway, his salute stalling halfway up. Of the order she'd given him.

"I was never very good at that," she said.

"You'll learn." The door opened onto a gust of colder air and a sound like wind in a long pipe. "Everyone here does."

Medical was a Twi'lek droid-tech who scanned her twice, frowned at the collar readings, and said nothing. Supply issued her one thermal blanket, rated for conditions she suspected it had never met; one cup; one bowl; one spoon, short-handled, soft metal. A holo was taken. Her fingers were printed. Somebody read her the facility regulations aloud, all forty-one of them, while she stood on a painted square.

Then the guards walked her out onto the shaft.

It was the size of a city block across, a vast round hole bored straight down into the glacier, and the prison was built around its walls. Cell galleries ringed it in tiers, grated walkways bolted into the ice, each ring a little narrower than the one above, so that looking down was like looking into a funnel. Orange heat strips ran along the walls at waist height and did almost nothing. Frost furred every rail. Far overhead, a disc of blue twilight showed through the dome; far below, the shaft narrowed into darkness, and at the very bottom there was a single small square of white light, like a coin at the bottom of a well.

"Ring One is administration," Holt said, in the bored voice of someone who'd given this tour a hundred times. "Ring Two, mess and medical, and the tunnel to the Yard. Three through Five, cells. Below Five is the Well." He jabbed a gloved thumb downward. "You don't want the Well."

"So I keep hearing."

He gave her a look through the amber visor and didn't answer.

Her cell was on Ring Four. A number on the frosted lintel: 4-17. Three walls of ice sealed under a clear coating that sweated in the warmth of the heat strip and froze again when it faded. A shelf cut into the ice for a bunk. A grate for a door, so that everyone on the gallery could see in, and she could see out—across the shaft, down the funnel, up to the dome.

The grate slid shut behind her. The lock engaged with a sound she felt in her teeth.

* * *

The mess on Ring Two went quiet when she walked in.

Not all at once. It went quiet the way ice cracks, a line of silence running out from the door across the long durasteel tables, one conversation stopping, then the next, until the only sound in the hall was the scrape of the serving droid's ladle and somebody's spoon set down very deliberately on a tray.

There were maybe two hundred of them. Every species she'd ever fought beside or against: humans and Rodians, a pair of Aqualish, a Weequay with half his face frostbitten grey, Sullustans, a Gran, a broad-shouldered Zabrak with tattoos faded to the color of a bruise. All in the same grey thermals. All looking at her.

She took a tray. She held it out. The droid filled her bowl with something beige that steamed, and she turned to find a seat, and somebody said, loud enough to carry, "There she is. The Temple's little bomber."

She kept walking.

"Killed her own troopers." Another voice. "Cut 'em down in the corridor and walked out over the bodies."

"Heard she cried at sentencing."

"I'd cry too. Jedi don't do time. Jedi do the sentencing."

The Zabrak stood up as she passed his table. He didn't step into her way. He just stood, so she had to pass close, and said quietly, "My brother served under one of your generals. Three hundred men in his company. Your general walked off that field with a scratch on his arm." He looked at the collar. "Hope it chokes you."

She didn't stop. She didn't answer. She was aware—distantly, the way you're aware of weather—that her hands were steady on the tray, and that this was the only thing she had any control over at all.

There was an empty table at the far end, near the heat vent that didn't work. She sat with her back to the ice wall and ate, because the regulations said if you missed a meal you'd missed it, and because the beige stuff was hot, and because Rex had once told her that the first rule of any bad posting was eat what they give you while it's warm.

She had three spoonfuls before the bench across from her creaked under a great deal of weight.

The Trandoshan who sat down was big even for his kind, his scales a dun, mottled green, a notch cut out of the ridge above his left eye where something had once bitten or burned it. He still wore a labor harness over his thermals, its buckles rimed with frost. He didn't have a tray. He folded his clawed hands on the table and studied her with his slit-pupiled eyes, unhurried, and then he leaned forward and breathed in, slowly, through his nostril slits.

"Wasskah," he said.

Every muscle in her back went tight.

"You remember." He sounded pleased. "I can smell that you remember. The hunting moon. The lodge in the trees." His tongue flicked once. "Garnac was my father's brother. I am Krasso. I was to go to Wasskah the next season, to hunt beside him. I was to earn my first Jedi pelt for his wall." He tilted his head. "Instead there was a message that he was dead. And a name."

"Your uncle hunted children," Ahsoka said. "For sport. He dropped them in a forest and ran them down."

"He hunted Jedi." Krasso's voice didn't rise. "You were never children."

She was reaching before she knew it—reaching to read him, to feel which way the threat would move, the way she'd done in every fight since she was old enough to hold a training saber. She reached, and hit the silence, and in the half-second it cost her, his hand shot across the table and closed on the collar.

He hauled. She came half over the table, tray clattering, the edge of the collar biting up under her jaw so hard that light burst behind her eyes. The status light flickered against his knuckles. Someone was laughing. Someone was cheering.

Her body didn't need the Force for the next part. It had been trained by Anakin Skywalker, who did not believe in fighting fair. She drove the heel of her hand up into the soft joint of Krasso's wrist, twisted against the thumb, and turned her whole weight out from under the grip. He let go—she didn't know if she'd made him or if he'd chosen to—and she landed on her own side of the table on her knees, gasping, with her hand at her throat.

Krasso hadn't moved from his seat. He was looking at his wrist, flexing it, and his lipless mouth had pulled back from his teeth.

"Good," he said. "The Scorekeeper prefers prey that fights."

On the gallery above the mess, two Judiciary guards stood at the rail with their rifles slung, watching. Neither of them moved.

"Krasso." A new voice, dry and rasping, from behind Ahsoka's shoulder. "You're sitting in my light."

The woman who'd come up to the table was human, and old—sixties at least, with white hair cropped close to a skull that looked like it had been in a few fights of its own. Her face was weathered into a permanent squint, the kind spacers got from too many years staring through bad viewports. Two fingers of her left hand ended short and blunt at the first joint, old frostbite, and she was holding her tray in the other hand as if she'd never had a problem in her life.

Krasso looked up at her. Something passed between them that Ahsoka couldn't read, and couldn't feel, and hated not feeling.

"Varro," he said.

"That's right. And you've got a shift at the cut in an hour, and Holt's watching, and Holt's in a mood because his heat strip's out. So." The woman tilted her head toward the door. "Go on."

Krasso stood. He looked down at Ahsoka on the floor for a long moment.

"The cold is long, little hunter," he said. "I am patient."

He went. The mess went back to its noise, a little louder than before, as if to make up for the show ending early. The old woman set her tray down where Krasso had been sitting, lowered herself onto the bench with a grunt, and started eating.

Ahsoka got up off the floor. She picked her bowl up. Half of it was still in it. She sat down.

"Thank you," she said.

"Don't." The woman didn't look up. "Krasso kills you, there's a lockdown. Lockdown, nobody moves. Nobody moves, no trade. No trade, I don't get my heat packs. I hate being cold." She chewed. "Tess Varro. Life. A Hutt's enforcer, on Nar Shaddaa, before you ask. He had it coming, and he had it coming at me."

"I wasn't going to ask."

"Everybody asks eventually. Gets it out of the way." Tess looked at her for the first time. It was a long look, thorough, the look of someone pricing a cargo. "You're the Jedi."

"Not anymore."

"Good. Keep practicing that. It almost sounds true." Tess went back to her bowl. "Here's what's free, and then after this, nothing's free. One. Nobody in here is innocent. Stop looking like you are. It makes people want to fix that."

"I am innocent."

"Sweetheart, so's every third person in this room, and the other two-thirds are lying about it. I'm not. I killed him. Makes a nice change, doesn't it?" She pointed her spoon. "Two. Eat all of it, every time, even when it tastes like that. Especially when it tastes like that. Three. The cold takes the ones who sit still. You'll want to curl up in your bunk and not come out. Don't." The spoon pointed at the collar. "Four. Down here, a Jedi isn't a hero. A Jedi's the face on the recruiting poster that got your son killed. Every one of these people lost somebody to this war, and you're the closest thing to the people who ran it they're ever going to get their hands on."

Ahsoka looked down at her bowl. Her throat ached where the collar had bitten. "And five?"

"Five's not free." Tess scraped her bowl clean. Then, a little less dry: "That thing. What's it like?"

Ahsoka was quiet for a while.

"Quiet," she said finally.

Tess looked at her, and for just a moment the squint went somewhere softer, and then it came back.

"Yeah," Tess said. "I figured." She stood, tray in her good hand. "You want to feel better about your evening, look down the shaft tonight after lights. Bottom of the Well. There's one fellow in this place they hate worse than you. Separatist officer, Raxus man. Republic caught one of the high-collar types alive, which I hear doesn't happen much, and nobody knows what to do with him, so they keep him down there by himself with the lights on and wait to see if he breaks." She shrugged. "Eleven months, so far. He hasn't."

She walked off. Ahsoka sat alone at the end of the table with half a bowl of beige food going cold in front of her, and ate all of it.

* * *

The lights went down on the shaft at what the regulations called night. It made no difference to the sky. The disc of blue twilight at the top of the dome stayed exactly the same color, the way it had stayed the same color all day, and would, she understood, stay the same color for as long as she was here.

The cold came into the cell the moment the heat strip dimmed to its night setting. It came through the blanket, through the coat she wore under the blanket, through the thermals under that. It found her lekku first and settled into them like a hand closing. She curled on the ice shelf with her knees to her chest and her hands tucked into her armpits and shook, and tried to remember the last time she had been truly cold without being able to do anything about it.

She couldn't. That was its own kind of answer.

She didn't mean to sleep. She slept.

In the dream she was in the Temple hangar, before. She had never been there before—she'd come afterward, with Anakin, to the smoke and the scorched deck and the sheets laid over shapes—but in the dream she was there in time, standing on the deck while the mechanics worked and the gunships idled and Jedi walked past her talking about nothing. She knew what was coming. She opened her mouth to warn them and nothing came out. She reached for the Force to throw them clear and there was nothing to reach for, and then the light came, white, rolling, and the sound came after it, and she was standing in fire that did not burn her while it burned everyone else.

Then it was the corridor outside her cell, and the three troopers were on the floor, and her lightsabers were in her hands, and she didn't remember picking them up. Then it was Letta Turmond's throat, closing on nothing. And on a catwalk above all of it, as there was always someone above all of it, the bone-white mask, tilted slightly to one side, watching her with interest, the way the technician had.

She woke with her hand already at her own throat.

The cell was dark except for one thing. The light on the collar threw a faint blue glow across the ice in front of her face, and her breath went up through it in short, shredded plumes. Her heart was going so hard she could feel it in her montrals. She reached—of course she reached; she would reach every time, she understood that now, a thousand times a day for the rest of her life—and there was nothing there, and the nothing went on and on.

Her fingers found the seam of the collar at the back of her neck. They dug in. For one long, bright second she didn't care about Vorne or the Well or thirty days or anything at all except getting it off, getting it off, getting back the thing that had been there before her name—

She stopped.

She made herself stop. She unhooked her fingers one at a time and put her hand flat on the ice beside her face, and the cold of it burned, and she held it there.

Then she cried, because there was nothing else left to do. She tried to do it quietly. She didn't manage it very well.

After a while, through the ice wall to her left, a dry voice said, "Everybody does it the first night."

Ahsoka went still.

"Do it in through the nose," Tess's voice went on, muffled, unbothered. "Out through the mouth, slow. Warms the air before it hits your chest. And for stars' sake, do it quieter. Some of us have a shift in the morning."

Silence. Then the creak of a bunk on the other side of the wall as someone rolled over.

Ahsoka lay on her back and looked at the dark.

In through the nose.

She knew that. She'd known it since she was four years old, sitting cross-legged on a mat in the crèche with a dozen other younglings, before any of them had ever lifted so much as a pebble. *Sit. Breathe. Count.* The crèche master's voice had been patient and very old. *The Force will come when you are still. But first, be still.* She had always thought the breathing was a door. That it was the thing you did to get to the Force, and once you were through, you didn't need it anymore.

In through the nose. Count four. Out through the mouth. Count six.

It wasn't a door. It was just breathing. There was nothing on the other side of it.

But her heart slowed anyway.

She did it again, and again, and the plumes of her breath grew longer and steadier in the blue light, and the shaking in her hands went from a rattle to a tremor to something she could almost ignore. It turned out that being still was something her body could do on its own. It turned out that it had been doing it for her all along, and she'd been giving the credit to something else.

She lay there and thought, very clearly, the way she used to think on the bridge of a cruiser before an engagement.

Tarkin wanted her finished. The Council had wanted her gone. The Senate had needed someone to be guilty, and she had been standing closest, holding a stun baton in a room full of nano-droids. Somewhere on Coruscant, the person who had actually done it—who had fought like a Jedi, Anakin had said, a Temple turn in the dark—was walking around free. Waking up every morning. Doing whatever they did.

And everyone, everyone in the galaxy who mattered, had agreed that the best thing Ahsoka Tano could do now was disappear quietly into the ice and let it all be finished.

She thought about that for a long time.

Then she sat up on the shelf, pulled the blanket around her shoulders, and decided, with the cold in her bones and the silence where the Force had been, that she was not going to do that.

She was going to live. Not because she had a plan, and not because she believed anyone was coming. She would live because a great many powerful people had decided she shouldn't matter anymore, and she had never once in her life been good at doing what she was told.

She got up. Her legs were stiff. She went to the grate and put her hands on the frozen bars and looked out.

The shaft was dark, ring under ring of black galleries spiraling down, the heat strips glowing faint orange along the walls like embers in a dead fire. Nothing moved. Here and there a cough echoed off the ice. And at the very bottom, where the funnel narrowed to its tightest point, there was the white square she'd seen when she arrived. The Well. The only cell in the Deep with its lights still on.

She could see into it from here, just barely, foreshortened. A bunk. A bare floor. A man sitting on the edge of the bunk, upright, his back very straight. He wore the remains of a uniform—a dark tunic with a high collar, its insignia gone, pale patches on the shoulders where rank had once been stitched. His hair was neat. His hands rested on his knees. He wasn't sleeping. He looked as if he had not slept in a long time, and had decided, with great discipline, that this was not going to be anyone's business but his own.

Eleven months, Tess had said. With the lights on.

As she watched, he lifted his head.

He looked straight up the shaft—past Ring Five, past the dark galleries, up through the cold—and she realized, a beat too late, what he must be seeing. In all that blackness, there was exactly one other light. A small blue one, at the throat of a figure on Ring Four, standing at her grate.

They looked at each other across the whole height of the Deep.

Then the man in the Well stood. He did it the way officers on parade grounds stand, all at once and without waste. He squared his shoulders in the ruined tunic, and he raised his hand, and he saluted her.

It wasn't a Republic salute. She didn't know the form. But she knew what it was. She'd been saluted by clone troopers since she was fourteen years old. Two days ago Rex's salute had stalled halfway before he made himself finish it.

This one didn't stall at all.

Ahsoka stood at the grate of a Republic cell, in a Republic collar, on a moon the Republic had chosen for her, and looked down at the only person in the Deep who had offered her a courtesy.

He was the enemy.

She returned the salute.

# Part Two: The Deep

## Chapter 8: The Weight of Gratitude

The third gun on the Ordaine cliffs had been firing for nine hours when Anakin Skywalker decided he was done waiting for it to stop.

"Bombers are forty minutes out, sir," the gunship pilot said over his shoulder. Rain sheeted across the canopy in grey ropes. Below them the sea threw itself at the base of the cliffs, and above the spray, cut into the black rock like the eye sockets of a skull, three Separatist artillery batteries took turns pounding the beach where the 212th and the 501st were dug in behind their own dead walkers. "General Kenobi's orders are to hold at altitude until—"

"I heard General Kenobi's orders." Anakin unbuckled his harness and stood. The deck bucked under his boots. "Take us down to the second ledge. Under the gun's depression angle."

"Sir, that's inside the flak envelope."

"Then go fast."

The pilot went fast. The gunship dropped out of the clouds so steeply that the troopers in the bay grabbed the overhead straps, and for three long seconds the whole world was black cliff filling the open hatch, close enough that Anakin could see water streaming out of the cracks. Flak burst above them. Something punched through the rear stabilizer with a sound like a hammer on a pot.

"Sir!" Rex's voice crackled in his ear from the beach. "Your gunship is off its heading."

"No, it isn't."

He jumped.

It was not a long fall, as falls went. He had fallen farther on worse planets. The ledge came up wet and slick, and he landed in a crouch with his saber already lit, the blue blade hissing as the rain hit it. Over his head the third gun fired. The sound was physical, a wall of pressure that went through his chest and rattled his teeth, and a few seconds later he heard it land on the beach, and heard the comm channel fill with voices.

Two B1s in the access tunnel turned around. One of them said, "Uh—" and that was all it got to say.

He went up the tunnel at a run. The droids were slow in the wet, their joints grinding, and he went through them the way rain goes through a gutter. A super battle droid stepped out of an alcove with its arm cannon raised, and Anakin didn't slow down, didn't deflect, just went under the bolt and opened the droid from hip to shoulder as he passed. The bolt hit the wall behind him and splashed molten rock across his back. He felt it through his tunic, a line of heat, and ignored it.

The gun chamber was enormous, a cavern hollowed out of the cliff, with the barrel run out through a slot in the rock toward the sea. The loading crew were droids. The fire-control station was droids. All of it was droids, and all of it turned toward him at once.

He did not count them. Later, Rex would count them from the helmet recordings and say the number in a flat voice, and Anakin would shrug.

What he remembered afterward was not the fighting. It was the elevation gear: a great toothed wheel as tall as two men, turning with a slow grinding patience to lower the barrel for the next shot. He remembered putting his saber through the axle, and then reaching out with the Force and taking hold of the barrel itself, all those tons of durasteel, and pulling it down—not toward the beach, but toward the rock face beneath the slot.

He remembered thinking, quite clearly, *She'd tell me this is stupid.*

The gun fired into the cliff.

The chamber came apart. He was already running, but not fast enough, and the blast wave caught him in the tunnel and threw him the last ten meters out onto the ledge. Something hot and heavy slid across his ribs on the left side. He rolled. Rock rained past him into the sea. Above him the first and second guns had gone quiet, their crews no doubt wondering what had just happened to the third.

He lay on his back in the rain for a moment with his saber still in his fist. The burn along his side felt very far away.

"General Skywalker." Rex, on the comm. "Was that you?"

"Bombers can take the other two," Anakin said. "Send someone to pick me up."

There was a pause just long enough to be a comment.

"Yes, sir," said Rex.

* * *

Kix cut his tunic away on the beach under a field awning that was failing to keep out the rain. The burn ran in a long ragged stripe from his left armpit to the bottom of his ribs, and the medic looked at it for a while without saying anything, which from Kix was a speech.

"It's fine," Anakin said.

"It's a second-degree plasma burn with rock fragments in it, sir." Kix began picking the fragments out with a pair of fine forceps. "But if the General says it's fine."

"The General says it's fine."

"Then I'll just do this for fun." Another fragment. Anakin didn't flinch. He had stopped flinching at most things in the last six weeks. It was surprising how little effort it took, once you had decided that nothing that happened to you counted.

Out on the water the bombers came in low and the remaining two batteries went up in sequence, orange flowers under the cloud, the sound arriving late and soft across the bay. A cheer went down the trench line. It was a tired cheer. The 212th had lost a lot of men on this beach before the 501st arrived, and the 501st had lost some too.

Not as many as they would have, Anakin told himself. Not as many as if he'd waited.

He was still telling himself that when Obi-Wan ducked under the awning, his robes black with rain, his beard dripping. He looked at the burn. He looked at Kix. Kix discovered an urgent need to fetch more bacta from somewhere else.

"I gave you an order," Obi-Wan said.

"You gave the pilot an order."

"Anakin."

"The bombers were forty minutes out. That gun was firing every ninety seconds." Anakin pulled what was left of his tunic around himself. "Do the arithmetic, Master. I did."

"I have done the arithmetic. I do it every time you go out ahead of your men." Obi-Wan didn't sit. He stood with his hands folded into his wet sleeves, the way he had stood in a hundred briefing rooms, and his voice stayed level, and only someone who had known him for thirteen years would have heard what was under it. "That was the fourth time in six weeks. Kelvrin Reach, the convoy at Brenn, the refinery. And now this. Each time you've survived by a margin I would not care to measure."

"And each time it worked."

"Each time it *happened* to work. That isn't the same thing, and you know it isn't." Obi-Wan's gaze dropped to the burn again, and then came back up. "You aren't sleeping. Your men have noticed. *My* men have noticed."

"Then your men should mind their own business."

It came out harder than he meant it to. He didn't take it back.

Obi-Wan was quiet for a moment. Rain drummed on the awning. Somewhere down the beach a trooper was laughing in the high, cracked way men laughed after a bad day.

"There's something else," Obi-Wan said at last. "I'd hoped to say it somewhere drier. The Council has asked that you not petition the Judiciary for contact with Ahsoka."

Anakin went still.

"Asked," he said.

"For her sake, as much as yours. Master Windu feels—we feel—that any appearance of the Order intervening in a Republic sentence would only harden the Senate against her. And there are those in the Senate who would be very glad to say the Jedi cannot let go of a convicted murderer. If there is ever to be a review of her case—"

"There won't be. You made sure of that when you handed her over." Anakin stood. The burn pulled, and he let it. "And the Council doesn't get to ask me anything about her. It gave that up. It gave it up in the Council chamber, in front of her, and Master Windu took her beads off her himself so everybody would know it was done properly."

"I argued against it. You know I did."

"I know you lost." Anakin reached for his cloak. "That's all anyone remembers, in the end, Master Kenobi. Who lost."

Obi-Wan flinched. It was very small, a tightening at the eyes, gone at once. Anakin saw it and felt nothing he could name. Or rather, he felt something, and buried it under the burn, where it would keep.

Obi-Wan left without another word.

Rex was waiting outside the awning, helmet on, rain beading on the blue paint. He had obviously heard all of it. He fell into step beside Anakin as they walked down the line toward the landing zone, and for a while neither of them said anything.

Then Rex held out his hand, palm down, as though offering a ration bar. Anakin took what was in it. A datachip, small and warm from Rex's glove.

"Fives went back down to the munitions district," Rex said, eyes front. "Twice. Off duty. The crates in that warehouse had their Republic stencils ground off, but somebody got lazy with one of them. There was a reseller's tag on the underside. Inventory sticker. Fives traced it."

Anakin turned the chip in his fingers. "To who?"

"Rodian salvage broker named Dalo Pesk. Stall on the Kesh Arcade, lower levels, about two hundred floors under where the platform fell. Moves surplus. Doesn't ask questions." Rex paused. "If someone bought nano-droids off the black market, sir, it's a fair bet they bought them from him. Or someone he knows."

Anakin closed his hand on the chip. For the first time in six weeks, something in his chest moved that wasn't anger.

"Rex. I owe you."

"Fives found it, sir." Rex's helmet turned slightly toward him. "And we weren't down there. Officially."

"Officially," Anakin said, "I don't even know what a Rodian is."

* * *

The Kesh Arcade was a long gallery of shuttered stalls under a ceiling so low that the advertising holos had to hang sideways. It smelled of fried grubs and coolant and old rain. Anakin came down into it two nights later in a brown civilian cloak with the hood up, his saber clipped out of sight at the small of his back, and his left side still stiff under the bacta patches Kix had made him promise to change.

He had come straight from the hangar. He had told no one but Rex he was coming. He had told no one at all *when*.

Dalo Pesk's stall was near the far end, between a shop selling counterfeit comlink casings and a stall that seemed to sell nothing at all. Its shutter was rolled up halfway. A light was on inside.

Anakin knew before he ducked under the shutter. He couldn't have said how. The Force didn't shout it. It was more as though the Force had stopped talking about that corner of the arcade altogether, the way a crowd goes quiet around something it doesn't want to look at.

The Rodian was sitting in a chair behind his counter. His hands were folded in his lap. His head was bowed a little, as if he'd nodded off over his accounts, and his antennae drooped. He was dead. He had been dead for some hours. There was not a mark on him that Anakin could see.

Anakin stood in the doorway and did not touch anything.

The stall was small, cluttered in the way of every salvage stall in every lower level of every world: shelves of gutted droid parts, bins of power cells sorted by charge, a rack of secondhand blaster barrels, a workbench under a magnifier lamp. It ought to have been filthy. Pesk's counter was spotless. The workbench was spotless. The floor had been cleaned, and recently. The sharp sweet smell of solvent hung in the air underneath the smell of the arcade.

He moved in, slowly, keeping his hands under his cloak.

On the wall behind the counter was a shelf of datapads, the cheap kind, the kind brokers used for inventory because nobody bothered to steal them. There were faint clean rectangles in the dust along that shelf. Seven of them. The datapads that had sat there were gone, and the ones left behind had been nudged along to cover the gaps, but not quite enough.

There was a security cam above the door, a small cracked dome. Anakin reached up and popped the housing with one finger. The memory core was gone. Not smashed. Not ripped out. The four retaining screws sat in a neat little row on top of the cam's casing, where whoever had removed them had set them down, one beside the next, with the care of someone who had done this many times and would do it many times again.

Anakin looked at those four screws for a long time.

A thief would have taken the credits from the box under the counter. He checked; they were there. A rival would have left the place wrecked. CSF would have left tape, holo-markers, a droid. An angry customer would have left blood.

This was none of those. This was someone who had come in, done what they came for, and then put everything away. Even Pesk. Especially Pesk. His hands hadn't fallen in his lap like that. Somebody had folded them.

*Someone's cleaning up,* Anakin thought, and the cold that went through him had nothing to do with the arcade.

He crouched beside the chair. Up close the Rodian looked even more peaceful. Anakin made himself reach out with the Force, carefully, the way he would have touched a wound—and found nothing. Not the faint fading trace death usually left, like the warmth in a chair after someone stood up. Nothing at all. As though the room had been scrubbed of more than fingerprints.

Behind him, someone cleared their throat.

Anakin was on his feet with his hand on his saber before he'd decided to move. It was the Ugnaught from the next stall, the one that sold nothing, peering under the half-shutter with small suspicious eyes.

"He dead?"

"Yes."

The Ugnaught considered this without visible grief. "Figured. Didn't open this morning. Pesk always opens."

"Did anyone come by last night? Late?"

"Nobody." The Ugnaught shrugged. "Light went off at the usual time. Came back on at the usual time. Nobody came, nobody left. I'd have heard. Shutter squeaks."

Anakin looked at the shutter. It was half up. He looked at its runners. They had been oiled. Fresh oil, still glistening.

"Thank you," he said.

He left before the Ugnaught could decide to call anyone. At the end of the arcade he stopped at a public comm booth with a cracked screen and put an anonymous report through to CSF—*deceased Rodian, Kesh Arcade, stall forty-one*—and then walked four levels up before he let himself stop and lean against a wall and breathe.

He had not told anyone but Rex. Rex had not told anyone but Fives. And still someone had been there first. Someone had known which thread he would pull before he pulled it, and had cut it, and had tidied up the ends.

Either that, or someone had simply been more thorough than him from the very start. Someone who'd been cleaning up since the night of the hangar, one loose end at a time, and Pesk had merely been next on a list Anakin had never seen.

He thought of the masked figure on the catwalk above the Dovrin platform, standing still in all that chaos to watch him hold the stack. He thought of the turn the figure had made with Ventress's sabers. The tight, economical circle of a Temple defensive form.

He had told no one about that turn but Ahsoka. Not Fox. Not Tarkin. Not Padmé, not really. Not Obi-Wan.

He told himself, standing in a lower-level stairwell with rain dripping down the wall beside his face, that it was because he didn't trust any of them to do the right thing with it. And that was true. It was just not the whole truth, and he was too tired to look at the rest.

* * *

Padmé had the denial on her desk when he came in. She didn't need to say anything. He saw it in how she sat—shoulders squared, chin up, the way she sat in the Senate when she was losing a vote and refused to let anyone see it.

"They wouldn't hear it," she said.

He crossed the room and picked up the datapad. The seal of the Judiciary Appellate Panel glowed at the top, and beneath it ran three paragraphs of language so smooth he could feel his eyes sliding off it. *No reviewable error. No new evidence of sufficient weight. The petition is respectfully declined.* Three judges. Three signatures. Eleven days from filing to refusal.

"Eleven days," he said.

"They usually take four months to say no." Padmé took the datapad back from him and set it face-down, as if that helped. "I argued that the Dovrin attack happened while Ahsoka was in a cell, and that the tribunal never seriously examined who else could have had access to those nano-droids. I argued that the security footage of Letta's death has no sound and no angle on the door. I argued everything I had."

"And?"

"And there's a second paragraph." She didn't look at it. She'd plainly memorized it. "The panel notes that the sentence presently being served is not the sentence the tribunal imposed. It is a sentence of the Chancellor's clemency. An act of executive grace, they call it. And an act of grace, they say, is not a judgment and cannot be appealed."

Anakin stared at her. "So because he saved her life—"

"Because he saved her life, the law considers her case closed." She rubbed her eyes. "It's not malicious, Ani. I don't think it's malicious. It's just the way the law is shaped. Once mercy enters a case, nobody in the courts wants to touch it, because touching it means questioning the Chancellor."

"Then nobody can do anything."

"The courts can't."

He went to the window. Coruscant spread out below in its endless glittering layers, and far down, under all that light, was an arcade where a Rodian sat in his chair with his hands folded. He hadn't told her about Pesk. He found he didn't want to.

"I applied for a visit," he said instead, to the window. "Two weeks ago. Narkis said no. Prisoners at that facility get no visitors in their first ninety days, at the Commandant's discretion, and the Commandant was very clear about her discretion."

Padmé was quiet for a moment.

"She asked you not to come," she said.

"She asked a lot of things." He heard how that sounded. He didn't take it back either. It was becoming a habit. "She asked Rex to look after me. She asked me not to visit. She didn't ask anyone to get her out, because she didn't think anyone would. I'm not going to prove her right."

Padmé came to stand beside him. She didn't touch him. She knew him well enough to know when he would flinch.

"Something happened," she said. "Today. You've got the same look you had during the trial, when I asked you about the figure in the mask."

"A lead went cold."

"How cold?"

He said nothing. She waited. She could outwait a Senate committee; she could certainly outwait him. But he had learned something, these last weeks, about how long he could stand inside a silence, and it turned out to be quite a long time.

At last she let out a breath.

"Whatever you're keeping from me," she said, "I hope you're keeping it because it's dangerous to know, and not because it's dangerous to say."

He turned and looked at her. In the light from the city she looked tired, and fierce, and he loved her so much it frightened him.

"Who's left?" he said. "If not the courts."

She hesitated. He watched her decide to answer honestly.

"The Council could petition the Senate to reopen the inquiry. They won't. And the Chancellor could do anything he liked." She held his eyes. "Ani. He's been very kind to you. Be careful how much of it you take."

"He saved her life."

"I know. I was there." Her voice was soft. "That's why I'm saying it."

* * *

The Chancellor's office was as warm as ever. Anakin had stopped noticing it after the first few visits. Now he noticed only that his burn stopped aching when he stood in that red room, the way it did beside a heater, and that he never quite wanted to leave.

"Ordaine," Palpatine said, coming around his desk with both hands out. "My boy. I read Admiral Yularen's report. Three batteries, and the third one you took, I am told, more or less by hand."

"The bombers took the other two."

"After you took the one that mattered." Palpatine clasped his forearm, mindful of the side. He always seemed to know where Anakin was hurt. "Sit, please. You look as though you have not slept since the last time I saw you."

Anakin sat. It was a deep chair, and he sank into it further than he meant to.

They spoke for a while about the campaign. Palpatine asked good questions; he always did. He wanted to know what the men thought of the new gunship armor, whether the supply lines on the Rim were holding, whether General Kenobi's strategy on Ordaine had been, in Anakin's honest view, a little too cautious. Anakin said that it had. He didn't mean to say it with quite so much feeling. Palpatine nodded as though he'd long suspected as much.

Then, gently, as though turning to something minor: "And your Ahsoka? I understand Senator Amidala's appeal was heard."

"It was refused."

"Ah." Palpatine sat back. For a moment his face was very old. "I was afraid it might be. I'm sorry, Anakin. I'm genuinely sorry. The courts do not like to look at a case once I have put my hand on it. I hoped they might be braver."

"The panel said it can't be appealed because you commuted it."

"Yes." The Chancellor looked down at his own folded hands. "Do you know, I lay awake over that? Whether by sparing her I would bind her. But a living prisoner may hope, and a dead one may not, and I could not let them take her life. I would make the same choice again tomorrow."

"I know." Anakin's throat was tight. "I didn't mean—"

"Of course you didn't." Palpatine waved it away. Then he was quiet for a little while. Outside the great window, the traffic lanes ran in their slow shining rivers.

"There is a thing I should not say," he said at last. "I should not say it because it is not a promise, and I do not make promises I cannot keep. But I think you need to hear that the door is not shut."

Anakin looked up.

"In wartime," Palpatine said, "the office I hold is a small, frightened thing. Every power it has must be justified to the Senate, debated, renewed, apologized for. Clemency in wartime looks like weakness. I spent a great deal of what little I had to spend on her life, and I will not pretend to you that I could spend any more of it now." He leaned forward. "But wars end. When this one ends—and it will end, Anakin, sooner perhaps than the Council believes—a Chancellor will have a great deal of latitude. There are always pardons at the end of a war. Amnesties. A whole galaxy wanting to put its wounds away and begin again. A great many sentences passed in the heat of the fighting will be looked at once more, in a cooler light."

"Hers," Anakin said.

"I could not single out one case. You understand. It would look like favoritism." A pause, delicate as a held breath. "But a general amnesty would not need to single anyone out."

Anakin found he was gripping the arms of the chair. His mechanical fingers had dug into the upholstery. He made them let go.

"The Council would never allow it," he said. "Not for her."

"The Council would not be asked." Palpatine said it mildly, as if pointing out the weather. "The Jedi gave her up to the Republic. Very publicly, as I recall. They made her a matter of Republic law and not Jedi discipline, and in doing so they made her a matter for me, and not for them. I don't believe they quite understood that when they did it." He sighed. "Master Windu, as it happens, has written to my office suggesting that I deny any request you might make concerning her. For your own well-being, he says. He feels contact would only distress you."

The words landed in Anakin like stones dropped into deep water. He felt them go down and down.

"He wrote to you."

"A very courteous letter." Palpatine's mouth thinned in what might, on a less kindly face, have been distaste. "I have not yet answered it. I find I am in no great hurry."

Anakin stood up. He didn't know why; he only knew he couldn't sit any longer with that in the room. He went to the window and put his flesh hand flat against the glass and looked out at the city without seeing it.

They had taken her beads. They had taken her name off their rolls. They had stood in a circle and voted her out of the only home she'd ever had and then gone back to their meditation. And now they were writing letters, careful letters, to make sure no one who still loved her was allowed to say so.

"How long?" he said to the glass. "Until the war ends."

"That," said Palpatine behind him, quietly, "depends a great deal on people like you."

Anakin closed his eyes.

When he opened them again, the Chancellor had returned to his desk, and was reading something, and had the air of a man who had said all he meant to say and perhaps a little more. Anakin thanked him. Palpatine smiled at him as he left, the warm, tired, grandfatherly smile that Anakin had known since he was nine years old, and told him to get some sleep.

He didn't sleep.

He went back to his quarters at the Temple, passing the memorial wall where the candles for the hangar dead burned in their long rows, and he did not look at them. He changed the bacta patches on his side the way Kix had shown him. He sat on the edge of his bunk in the dark for a long time with his elbows on his knees, thinking about Pesk's folded hands and four screws in a row and a door that was not shut, only very far away, at the end of a war that could last years.

Close to midnight his door chimed.

There was no one in the corridor when he opened it. Only a courier droid, already rolling away, and on the floor at his feet a slim Judiciary dispatch case, sealed in grey.

He opened it standing in the doorway.

Inside was a single datapad. When he thumbed it on, the seal of the Republic Judiciary rose up in pale blue light, and beneath it a single page of plain text.

*VISITATION PERMIT. Republic Penal Facility Narkis-Nine. Prisoner NK9-2214. One (1) visit, not to exceed two standard hours, under supervision of facility staff. Granted by executive authority, the objection of the facility Commandant noted and overruled.*

At the bottom, where a Judiciary clerk's mark would usually go, there was a signature he knew. He had seen it on medals. He had seen it on the order that commuted a death sentence to a life.

Below the signature, in the same hand, someone had added four words.

*So she knows. —P.*

Anakin stood in the doorway of his quarters and held the datapad in his mechanical hand until the screen dimmed.

*Don't come again,* she'd said. She had said it in a cell, with her chin up, the night before they took her away. She had meant it. He knew she had meant it.

He was already calculating the jumps to Wild Space.

## Chapter 9: Garnac's Blood

On her third day in the ice cut, Ahsoka watched a man lose the tip of his finger to a durasteel railing. He didn't scream. He looked at the railing, where a small grey crescent of skin stayed stuck to the frost, and then he looked at the guard on the gantry above, and then he pulled his glove back on and went on working. Nobody stopped. Nobody said anything at all. By the end of the shift the crescent had frozen white and she could no longer tell which part of the railing it had been.

"Cut rules. Different list. One," Tess Varro said that night, through the wall between their cells. "Bare skin never touches bare metal. Not once. Not for a heartbeat. The Deep doesn't give you a second try."

"I noticed," Ahsoka said.

"You noticed. Good. Half of them don't notice until they're counting on nine."

Ahsoka lay on her bunk in 4-17 with her coat pulled over the grey thermals and her hands tucked into her armpits. The heat strip along the wall buzzed and gave off about as much warmth as a memory. Through the bars of the grate the shaft light was the same blue it always was. There was no night on Narkis. There was only the shift, and the time between shifts, and the dark the ice made when you shut your eyes.

"Rule two," Tess said. "You don't run. Not in the Yard, not in the tunnel, not in the cut. A guard sees somebody running, he doesn't ask why."

"And rule three?"

A pause, and then the creak of Tess turning over on her bunk.

"Rule three is you don't make me teach you all of them in one night. I'm sixty-one. I need my sleep."

In the morning, Ahsoka made herself really look at the cut, in daylight, or what passed for it.

The ice cut was a long blue wound in the glacier, a hundred meters deep and terraced like a stairway for something much larger than people. Every shift, the prisoners of Rings Three and Four filed down the Yard tunnel from Ring Two and out onto the terraces, where the cutting rigs waited on their treads under a skin of frost. The rigs cut blocks. The blocks went up on cables to the refinery, where something in the old ice — gas trapped ages ago, Tess said, before anyone had a name for this moon — got cooked out of it and sold to people who would never know that Narkis existed.

The work was simple. That was the worst thing about it. Ahsoka had expected to hate the cold and the weight, and she did, but what she hadn't expected was how much room the simple work left in her head.

You guided the plasma saw down the scored line. You kept your weight back so the kickback didn't take your arm. You set the hooks. You signaled the cable crew. You stood clear while the block went up, swinging gently, shedding a glitter of frost on everyone below. You moved to the next line. And all the while, in the space behind your eyes, there was a silence where the Force used to be.

She'd thought of it, on the transport, as going deaf. It wasn't quite that. Deafness was a lack. This was more like being shut in a room with no windows, knowing there was weather outside, a whole sky of it, and being able to feel nothing through the walls. Twice a shift she would reach for it without meaning to — when a block shifted on its hooks, when someone moved fast at the edge of her vision — and there would be nothing there, and her stomach would drop like a missed stair.

The collar was always cold. That was the other thing nobody had told her. It drank heat from her neck all day, and the little blue light at her throat burned steady as a pilot lamp.

Tess worked the line beside her. She moved without wasted motion, short and wiry in her grey coat, white hair cropped close to her skull, two fingers on her left glove stuffed with rag where the fingertips should have been.

"Quota's forty blocks a crew," Tess said on the fourth day, not looking up. "You make forty, you eat. You make forty-five, Holt marks it, and next week quota's forty-five. So you make forty."

"Even if we could do more?"

"Especially." Tess tapped the saw's guard with her rag-stuffed finger. "Rule five. The one I told you wasn't free. Call it credit. Never show them what you can do. They'll just take it."

Ahsoka almost laughed. It came out as a puff of steam.

"Something funny, Jedi?"

"Not a Jedi." It came out easier than it used to. That frightened her a little. "Someone told me almost the same thing once. About droids, actually. He said if you let them see your best, they'll plan for it."

"Smart someone."

"He was showing off."

Tess grunted, and for a while they cut without talking. Down the terrace, past two rigs and a stacked pallet of hooks, Krasso worked with the Ring Three crew. He was easy to find. Even among Trandoshans he was big, with dun-green scales and a notch in his left brow ridge like someone had once tried to split his skull with an axe and been disappointed. He never looked at her when she looked at him. But every time she turned her back, she felt the place between her shoulder blades go tight, the way it used to before blaster fire, and she knew — without the Force, with only the old, stupid animal part of her — that he was watching.

"He's patient," Tess said, following her glance. "Trandoshans think hunting is prayer. He's not going to do it in the mess, where it's quick. He's going to do it where it means something."

"Where's that?"

Tess put the saw down and looked at her, really looked, for the first time all shift. Her eyes were pale and very tired.

"Rule six," she said. "Cut Three, terrace eleven, at shift change. The gantry cam on that side's been frozen for two years. Guards walk the upper rail at change, and from the upper rail there's a ledge that hides the whole bottom of the terrace. Everybody knows. Guards know. They like that it's there. Saves them paperwork."

"Why are you telling me?"

"Because you're getting moved to Cut Three tomorrow," Tess said. "I saw the roster. Somebody traded somebody something to put you there."

* * *

She could have asked to stay in Cut Two. She thought about it most of the night, lying with her hands under her arms, listening to the heat strip and to Tess's breathing through the wall. She could have gone to Holt. She could have made a complaint. She could have pretended to be sick.

And then what? Krasso would wait. He had told her so, the first night, with his claws locked in her collar: the cold is long. Tomorrow, or in a week, or in a year, somewhere with a frozen cam and a ledge in the way, he would come. The only choice she had was whether she walked into it with her eyes open.

So she went to Cut Three.

The shift was long and quiet. Krasso's crew worked the terrace above hers, and she did not look up at them, and she counted her blocks with the rest of the crew, and she kept her weight back on the saw. Around her the other prisoners were a little too careful not to stand near her. A Zabrak woman she'd never spoken to brushed past her at the hook pallet and said, very low, "Don't be near the edge," and was gone before Ahsoka could turn.

The horn for shift change was a long, flat bray that echoed off the ice for a full ten seconds. The crews began to file toward the ramps. The guards on the upper rail turned and walked away along it, unhurried, the way people walk when they've decided not to see something.

Ahsoka stayed by the rig.

He came down the terrace wall instead of the ramp. She heard his claws bite the ice before she saw him, and she was already moving when he landed, which was the only reason the ice pick — a cutter's pick, half a meter of hooked steel with a cable-grip handle — took a chunk out of the rig's housing and not out of her skull.

The sound rang across the cut.

"Garnac," Krasso said. His voice was a rasp in the cold. "Say his name."

She backed along the rig. Her heart was going hard. Without the Force, the world was so *slow* to tell her things. She couldn't feel where he'd go next. She could only see him — his weight, his shoulders, the angle of the pick — and guess.

"I didn't kill your uncle for sport," she said. "He was hunting children."

"He was hunting *prey*." The pick came round in a flat arc. She dropped under it. It whistled over her montrals close enough that she felt the air move. "You were prey. You were supposed to be prey. And you put him in the dirt."

He was strong. Much stronger than she was, now or ever, and he wasn't stupid. He kept the pick high, kept his feet wide, and herded her, step by step, toward the open lip of the terrace, where there was nothing beyond the edge but blue air and a ten-meter drop to the next shelf of ice.

*Don't be near the edge.*

Her back foot found it. Frost crumbled under her heel and fell away.

On Wasskah she had been hunted without her sabers. She'd been hungry, and frightened, and so angry she shook. But she'd had the Force then, faint and stubborn beneath everything, telling her when to jump. Now there was no one telling her anything. There was only what her body remembered: years of drills in the Temple's training rooms, Master Plo's patience, Anakin's endless, maddening, *again, Snips*, a thousand falls onto the practice mats until falling stopped being a thing that happened to her and became a thing she did.

Krasso lunged.

She went down instead of back. Flat on the frost, sliding, under the pick and between his planted feet, the way she'd once gone under a battle droid's legs on Christophsis. Her shoulder slammed the ice. She hooked his ankle with her boot as she passed and *pulled*.

It wasn't enough to drop a Trandoshan. It was enough to make him stagger. And on terrace ice, with his weight thrown forward into a swing that met nothing, stagger was enough. His claws scrabbled. The pick flew out of his hand and spun away into the blue. He went down on one knee at the very edge, and the frost gave under him, and for a long, terrible moment he hung there half over the drop, scrabbling at a lip of ice that was crumbling as fast as he could grip it.

Ahsoka was on her feet. She didn't remember getting there.

The second cutter's pick was lying on the hook pallet. She took it. The grip was so cold it bit through her glove.

She put the hooked point under his jaw, just where the scales went soft.

Krasso went very still. He was holding himself up on his elbows and one foot. Below him the ice fell away in a sheer blue face. His eyes, yellow with black slits, fixed on hers.

"Do it," he said.

She could. That was the thing that would stay with her afterward, through everything. Not that she wanted to — though some part of her, the part that had stood in a tribunal while Tarkin said *murderer* in that clean, considerate voice, wanted to very much. Only that she could. A push. Not even that. She could take the pick away and step back and let the ice do it, and on the upper rail no one would see, and in the report it would say *accident at shift change* and Commandant Vorne would initial it.

She thought of three troopers she had never met, whose numbers she'd had to hear read aloud in a courtroom. She thought of a Gran child on a falling platform, alive because someone had chosen to hold up the world instead of chasing what he wanted.

She took the pick away from his throat.

Then she reversed it, set the hook into the ice beside his hand, and leaned back on the handle with all her weight.

"Grab it."

Krasso stared at her.

"Grab it, or don't," she said through her teeth. "My arms are cold."

He grabbed it. For a moment his weight nearly pulled her over the lip with him, and she braced both boots against the rig's tread and hauled, and he got a knee onto the terrace, and then the other, and then he was lying on the ice on his belly with his chest heaving and his claws dug in to the root.

She let go of the pick and stepped back out of his reach, breathing hard. Her shoulder was screaming.

"Your uncle hunted people because it made him feel big," she said. "You can be better than him. Or you can come at me again, and I'll put you on the ice again. Your choice. But I'm not going to be your prayer."

Krasso lifted his head. There was something in his face she couldn't read.

Then the gantry lights came on above them, white and blinding, and a voice on the loudhailer shouted for every prisoner on terrace eleven to get on their knees with their hands on their heads, and she understood that the guards on the upper rail had not walked as far away as all that.

An hour later, still in binders, she stood before the person who decided what that meant.

"Fighting," Commandant Ilsa Vorne said. "Possession of a tool outside a work line. Endangering facility equipment."

Her office on Ring One was warm. That was the first thing Ahsoka noticed, and she hated herself a little for how much she noticed it. Warm, and quiet, and spare: a desk with nothing on it but a datapad and a stylus laid parallel to its edge, a viewport onto the eternal twilight of the ice, a single holo of a Republic cruiser on the wall. Vorne sat very straight behind the desk, iron-grey hair pinned without a strand out of place, collar control cylinder at her belt.

Sergeant Holt stood behind Ahsoka's chair. Her hands were bound in front of her. Her shoulder had swollen so that she could not lift her right arm above her chest.

"He attacked me," Ahsoka said.

"I'm aware." Vorne didn't look at the datapad. "Prisoner Krasso has already given his statement. He states that he attacked you, that you disarmed him, and that you pulled him back from the edge of terrace eleven. He was quite insistent on that last point. I don't believe I've ever heard a Trandoshan insist on a debt before. It was almost touching."

Ahsoka said nothing.

"Do you know why I am punishing you anyway, Twenty-two fourteen?"

"Because you can."

"Because the rule is the rule." Vorne folded her hands. "A prisoner who fights goes to the Well. Not a prisoner who starts the fight. Not a prisoner who loses. A prisoner who fights. If I begin making exceptions for prisoners with good reasons, I'll find that every prisoner in this facility has an excellent reason, and then I will have no rules at all, only reasons. And this is a very cold place, and very far away, and it does not survive on reasons." She paused. "Prisoner Krasso will go to the Well too, when medical is done with his hands. Fourteen days for him. Ten for you."

"That's generous."

"It's accurate. You also showed a degree of judgment. I note judgment." Vorne picked up the stylus, made a short mark, set it down parallel to the edge again. "I'll note something else, since we are here. The cam on that terrace is going to be repaired. Sergeant Holt will see to it."

Behind her, Holt shifted his weight. He didn't say anything.

"Take her down," Vorne said.

* * *

The Well was not dark. That was its cruelty.

Ahsoka had imagined black — a hole, a nothing, somewhere she could at least shut her eyes and pretend. Instead the cell was a cube of ice faced in durasteel, three paces by three, with a light panel set flush in the ceiling behind a grille, and the light never went off. It was white, harsher than the blue twilight everywhere else on Narkis, so bright that the ice in the walls glowed with it and she could see the faint pale ghosts of bubbles frozen a hundred thousand years deep. There was a bench, a drain, a thin thermal blanket, a slot for meal trays. There was no window. High in one corner of the wall was a vent grille, the size of two hands, through which came a breath of slightly less frozen air.

The first day, she slept, because her shoulder hurt and the medic had given her something for it. The second day she paced. Three steps, turn. Three steps, turn. She counted the bubbles in one block of the wall and lost count and started again. She tried closing her eyes and found the light came through her eyelids pink and red and would not leave her alone.

On the third day, someone spoke through the vent.

"You're the one who saluted."

She was lying on the bench with her arm across her eyes. She took it away. The voice had been a man's — dry, thin from disuse, with an accent she couldn't place at first. Precise consonants. Every word set down carefully, as if they cost something.

"Who's there?"

"Your neighbor. I'm afraid we haven't been introduced." A pause, and a sound that might have been a man settling himself against a wall. "Merrick Tal. Formerly Commander Tal of the Confederacy of Independent Systems, Third Raxus Picket. Currently nothing very much. And you are Ahsoka Tano, formerly of the Jedi Order, convicted of murder and treason by the Galactic Republic. They talk about you on the guard channel. I hear it through the floor."

She sat up. Her shoulder twinged.

"You saluted me first," she said.

"I did. The night they brought you in. I was looking up the shaft — my cell has a grating for a ceiling, the only one down here, and I can see a slice of the galleries through it — and I saw a Togruta girl on Ring Four with a collar on, looking down at me." He let a breath out. "You looked like someone who'd just been told the rules of a game she didn't know she was playing. I remembered that look. I wore it for a while."

"How long have you been down here?"

"Eleven months and twenty-two days. The light doesn't change, so I count the meal trays. I'm told I'm being kept for exchange." Something dry in his voice. "I'm told that every quarter. I've begun to think the Republic has forgotten what I'm being exchanged for."

Ahsoka pulled her knees up and wrapped the blanket around her shoulders. Up close to the vent the air was a fraction less cold, and she could hear him more clearly. He breathed slowly, like someone who'd learned to make his breath last.

"I've fought your people," she said. "A lot of them."

"I'd imagine. Droids, mostly. We don't put many living soldiers in front of Jedi. It seems a poor use of them." A pause. "Where?"

"Christophsis. Ryloth. Geonosis. Other places."

"Ryloth." He was quiet a moment. "That was a bad business. I won't defend it. Wat Tambor was a disgrace to the uniform he didn't wear. There are disgraces on every side of a war, Miss Tano. I've only noticed lately that your side gets to put its disgraces in charge of prisons."

"You don't get to call it *my* side."

"No?" He sounded genuinely interested. "What side are you on, then?"

She opened her mouth and found she didn't have an answer.

They talked through the vent over the following days. There was nothing else to do. He told her about Raxus — wide grey seas, a capital built of white stone, a Senate where, he said, the delegates still read their own speeches. He had been a schoolteacher's son. He had joined the Confederacy's defense forces at twenty because the Republic Senate had voted, three years running, to tax his world's shipping to pay for a war that wasn't his world's war, and his world had decided to leave, and the Republic had decided they were not allowed to.

"We asked," he said. "That's what nobody on Coruscant will tell you. We asked to go. We filed the petitions, we sent the delegations. And your Senate laughed at us and kept the taxes, and when we left anyway, they called it treason and sent your army. Your clones. A million men grown in jars to kill people who wanted to be left alone."

"Your side built droid armies before we had clones," Ahsoka said. "You bombed cities. I've seen what your Separatist leaders do to people who get in their way. Count Dooku tried to have Senator Amidala killed. More than once. Don't tell me you only wanted to be left alone."

"I didn't say we were good. I said we asked." His voice didn't rise. It never did. "There's a difference between a cause and the people who run it. I'm sure you understand that better than most. You were loyal to an Order. How did your Order repay you?"

She didn't answer. On the other side of the ice she heard him shift, and then sigh.

"I'm sorry. That was cheap."

"It was," she said.

"But it was also true. I've read the transcripts — they let me have a datapad once a week, old Holonet bulletins, heavily trimmed, but they don't trim everything. Your tribunal was a Republic military court, presided over by the Chancellor, prosecuted by an admiral, judged by senators who had already decided. The footage they convicted you on had no sound. The weapon they say you used was taken from you at the door. And when your teacher couldn't produce a witness, they decided you'd *directed an accomplice* — which is the sort of thing you say when the facts won't fit the verdict and the verdict won't move." He paused. "And then the Chancellor commuted your sentence, and the Holonet called it mercy. Mercy. To lock a seventeen-year-old in a glacier forever for something she didn't do."

"You don't know I didn't do it."

"I know you saluted a stranger down a shaft." His voice was quiet. "I know you pulled a Trandoshan off the edge of a cliff who'd come to kill you. I heard about that on the guard channel too. And I know that the Republic that put you here isn't interested in whether you did it. It needed someone. You were convenient." A long pause. "That's what the Republic does, Miss Tano. It needs, and someone pays. Worlds. Clones. Children. You were raised inside the machine, so you couldn't see it. Now you're outside. Look at it."

She lay awake for a long time that night under the light that didn't go off.

He was wrong. She knew he was wrong. She'd stood on worlds where Separatist droids had burned the fields and herded the villagers into camps. She had seen Grievous's work up close. Whatever the Senate had done to Raxus, it did not make what she'd seen right.

But the words stuck. *It needs, and someone pays.* She thought about Tarkin's clean voice. She thought about the Council, and Master Windu's hand closing on her beads. She thought about the way Vorne had said *the rule is the rule*, and how nobody in that whole chain of people had ever needed to be cruel, only correct.

* * *

On the sixth day, she stopped fighting the light.

It happened almost by accident. She had been sitting on the bench, cross-legged, from long habit — the way she had sat for meditation every morning of her life since she was three years old — and she had closed her eyes and, as she always did, reached.

Nothing. The windowless room. The collar's cold kiss under her jaw.

The old panic came up, the missed stair. And she was so tired of it — so tired of falling every time — that instead of reaching again she simply stopped. She let the reaching go. She sat in the nothing and did not ask it for anything.

And she found she could still hear.

Not the Force. Just the world, the plain one. The tick of the ice settling, somewhere deep in the walls. The hum of the light panel, which she'd thought was steady and was not — it rose and fell, faintly, with some pulse in the power grid far above. The drip in the drain, every eleven or twelve heartbeats. Merrick Tal's slow breathing through the vent. Her own heart. Her own breath, going in cold and coming out warm, the way it had on that first night on Ring Four when she'd been about to claw the collar off her throat and had found her breathing instead.

When she was six or seven, a crèche master had made the younglings sit by the Temple's fountains for an entire afternoon and listen, and Ahsoka had spent the whole time fidgeting and reaching outward, sure the lesson was somewhere out there in the Force if she could only grab it. She had thought, then, that without the Force there would be nothing to listen to at all.

But here was the ice, and the water, and the hum, and here was she. Still.

It wasn't peace. She didn't want to call it that, didn't trust it enough. It was quieter than peace, and heavier, and it asked nothing of her. It was the kind of stillness the ice had — the kind that came from being exactly what it was and not reaching for anything else.

She sat in it until the meal tray came, and when she opened her eyes the light was the same and the walls were the same, and her hands, she noticed, were not shaking.

"You've gone quiet," Merrick said through the vent, later. "For a while I thought you'd died. People do, down here."

"I was meditating."

"With that collar on? I thought the whole point—"

"So did I," Ahsoka said.

He seemed to consider that. "And?"

"And it turns out I'm still here without it." She leaned her head back against the ice. "I'm not sure what that means yet."

"It means they can't take everything," Merrick said. "It's the first thing you learn in a place like this, and the last thing they want you to know."

On the tenth day, the door hissed open, and Sergeant Holt stood in it with his breath steaming.

"Up," he said. "Time's done."

She stood. Her legs were stiff and her shoulder had settled to a deep, grudging ache. She had expected to feel relief, and there was some. There was also, to her surprise, a small reluctance, as if she were leaving a conversation halfway through.

"Miss Tano," Merrick said through the vent. "One more thing, before they take you up."

Holt made an impatient noise. Ahsoka held up her bound hands — *one moment* — and to her astonishment, he let her have it.

"I've been thinking about your case," Merrick said. "I don't have a great deal else to think about. You say you didn't kill those three troopers. I believe you. So someone else did. Someone who could walk into a Republic military prison, take a Jedi's lightsabers out of an evidence locker, kill three armed men, open your cell door, and walk out again without being seen. Someone who knew exactly where you'd go and when." His voice was very quiet now, and very clear. "That person is still free, Miss Tano. They watched you be convicted. They know you're alive. And they know exactly where you are."

The cold seemed to come through her coat all at once.

"Up," Holt said again, and this time he took her elbow.

She went. Up the shaft, past Ring Five, toward the dim blue glow of her cell and Tess's dry voice and the shift horn and forty blocks a day for the rest of her life. And all the way up, the stillness she'd found held — she was surprised how well it held — but in the middle of it, like a stone dropped into ice water, sat a question she had never once let herself ask.

*Who knew where I'd go?*

## Chapter 10: The Chancellor's Friend

The summons came on Chancellery flimsi, folded once and sealed with a disc of red wax, and for the length of three breaths Barriss Offee was certain it was a warrant.

She stood in the doorway of the Temple medical clinic with the flimsi open in both hands while a youngling with a blistered palm waited on the cot behind her. She read it twice before the words would hold still. *The Office of the Supreme Chancellor requests the attendance of a Jedi healer at the Senate Office Building, on a staff matter, at the healer's earliest convenience.* Below the seal, in the clinic's duty script, someone had scrawled: *Offee — you're nearest the landing platform. Go when you can.*

Not a warrant. A staff matter.

Her hands did not stop shaking, so she put the flimsi in her sleeve where no one would see them shake, and went back to the youngling.

"Training remote?" she asked.

"I was holding it wrong," the boy said. He was Rodian, perhaps eight, and furious with himself in the way only very young Jedi could be, as if the burn were a moral failing. "Master Drallig says a remote can't hurt you if you're paying attention."

"Master Drallig says a great many things." She took his hand and laid her fingers over the blister and reached, and the Force came to her the way it always had, clean and willing, as if it did not know what she was. The angry skin cooled under her touch. "There. It will be pink for a day. Try not to pay so much attention that you forget to breathe."

He flexed his fingers and grinned at her and ran. She sat on the edge of the empty cot for a moment longer than she needed to.

That morning, before the clinic opened, she had lit four candles in the memorial alcove above the hangar, as she had every morning for nearly two months. Four flames, no names. One for Jackar Bowmani, who had carried death into the hangar without knowing it. Three for troopers whose numbers Ahsoka had given her through a detention-cell field: CT-2261, CT-4408, CT-9170. Ahsoka had asked her to light candles for them. Ahsoka had thanked her for it.

She had not slept more than three hours together since the hangar. She had grown used to the grey edge it put on everything, the way sounds arrived slightly late, the way her own face in the refresher mirror had begun to look like a portrait of someone she used to know.

*A staff matter,* she told herself, and stood, and collected her satchel.

* * *

The Senate Office Building was a canyon of polished stone turned on its side, and the Chancellor's suite sat near the top of it like a held breath. Two guards in long red robes and faceless helmets flanked the outer doors. They did not move as she approached, and they did not need to; she felt their attention settle on her like a hand on the back of the neck.

The staff matter turned out to be a young human aide named Dace Orrin, who had caught his left wrist in the closing door of a turbolift and was sitting in an antechamber with it cradled against his chest, white around the mouth and apologizing to everyone who passed.

"It's nothing," he told her. "I said it was nothing. They wouldn't hear of a med droid. The Chancellor said — he said if one of his people is hurt, they should have the best." He tried to laugh. "I don't know why he thought that was me."

"Let me see."

It was a sprain, and a small fracture along the outer bone. She could have splinted it and sent him to the district med center with a bacta wrap. Instead she knelt and closed her hands around his wrist and let the Force run through her and into him, coaxing the bone to remember its shape. It took longer than the youngling's burn. When she opened her eyes Dace Orrin was staring at her as if she had pulled a bird out of his sleeve.

"That's — it doesn't hurt."

"It will ache tonight. Don't carry anything heavy until tomorrow." She was already rising, already turning toward the door, already rehearsing the walk back to the platform, when a voice spoke from the inner office.

"Padawan Offee."

It was not loud. It did not need to be. The inner doors stood open, and beyond them the Chancellor's office glowed a deep, rich red, and Palpatine himself stood just inside the threshold with his hands folded into his sleeves.

"Thank you for coming so quickly," he said. "Young Orrin is very dear to this office, though he would never believe it. Would you indulge an old man for a moment? I have tea, and I so rarely have the chance to speak with the Order's healers. You see the war from a side the rest of us are spared."

There was no possible answer but yes. She knew that before he finished speaking. She also knew, in some cold far room at the back of her mind, that the staff matter had never been the matter at all.

"Of course, Chancellor."
