The doors closed behind her with a soft, expensive sound.

The office was warm. Not comfortably warm — warm the way the inside of a closed hand is warm. The carpet was the color of old wine, and the tall windows looked out on a Coruscant that seemed very far away, the traffic lanes moving past in silence. He poured tea from a squat black pot into two cups and set one in front of a chair across from his desk, and he waited until she sat.

"You work at the Temple clinic," he said. "And, I'm told, you've spent a good deal of time in the field hospitals. Geonosis, among others. Master Unduli speaks very highly of your gifts."

"Master Unduli is kind."

"Master Unduli is accurate. I've found that's rarer." He lifted his own cup and did not drink from it. "Tell me — I ask everyone in your line of work, and the answers are always so different — what is the hardest part of it? The healing?"

She thought of lying. She thought of the polite answer, the one about triage and fatigue. Instead, because she was so tired, she heard herself say, "Knowing what they'll be sent back to."

He nodded slowly, as if she had confirmed something he had long suspected and grieved over.

"Yes," he said. "I imagine that would be the hardest thing in the galaxy. To mend a man so that he can be broken again. To mend a Jedi so that she can go back to being a general." He set the cup down. "I have asked myself many times, these last years, what this war has made of the Jedi. Whether the Order that went to Geonosis is the same Order that will come home. I confess I don't like the answers I arrive at. I suspect you don't either."

Her pulse was loud in her ears. She kept her hands flat on her knees.

"I'm only a Padawan, Chancellor. I don't—"

"No," he said gently. "You're not only anything. I've watched you, you see. Forgive me. It's a habit of the office." He reached to the corner of his desk and touched a control, and a holo bloomed in the air between them, grey and blue and soundless.

A corridor. Narrow, institutional, lined with conduit. A timestamp in the corner. She knew the corridor before she understood she knew it, the way you recognize a room in a nightmare: the service passage on the upper level of the Republic Center for Military Operations, the one that ran behind the observation glass above the holding rooms. She had walked it once. Only once.

A hooded figure stood at the end of the corridor with a healer's satchel on one shoulder. The hood was deep. The face beneath it was turned toward a narrow maintenance viewport. The figure's right hand rose, slowly, the fingers drawing together as if closing around something small.

Beside the corridor, a second frame opened. A holding room seen from a high corner. A woman on a bench against the wall, and Ahsoka standing across from her. Letta Turmond's mouth was open. She had been about to speak. The timestamp in the second frame matched the first to the second.

Letta's hands went to her throat.

Palpatine touched the control again, and both images froze before she fell.

"An auxiliary camera," he said into the silence. "Installed by a maintenance contractor some years ago to monitor the coolant lines in that passage. It logged to a separate system that no one had looked at since. Admiral Tarkin's investigators never found it. They were asking a very particular question, and they found the answer they were looking for." He folded his hands. "I asked a different question. I'm afraid I'm a curious old man."

Barriss did not move. She could not have said whether she was breathing. Some trained part of her was reaching for the calm the way a falling person reaches for a ledge, and finding it, and finding that it held nothing. She thought, very clearly, of the hilt at her belt, and then of the two red-robed guards beyond the door, and then of nothing at all.

"I sealed these recordings under the authority of this office," Palpatine said, "the same afternoon I found them. They exist in one place. No one has seen them but me. No one will."

"Why," she said. It did not come out as a question. Her voice sounded as if it belonged to someone in the next room.

"Because I knew, the moment I saw them, that it would be a catastrophe." He leaned forward a little, his face grave and soft in the red light. "Think of it, my dear. A Jedi Padawan, a healer, responsible for the bombing of the Jedi Temple. For the murder of a prisoner in Republic custody. For —" he paused, and let the pause say what it wanted to "— whatever else that night held. I'm told there are later recordings, from the detention level. I have not watched them. I find I don't need to."

She closed her eyes.

"Count Dooku's propagandists would have it on every channel in the Outer Rim by morning," he went on. "The Senate would tear the Order apart, and half the Senate would cheer while it did. The war would be lost in a week, not on any battlefield, but in the hearts of the people who pay for it. I could not allow that. Whatever else I am, I am the steward of this Republic."

"And Ahsoka," Barriss whispered.

"Ahsoka Tano is alive." He said it with great tenderness. "Admiral Tarkin wanted her dead. The tribunal gave him what he wanted. I took it away from him, at no small cost to myself, and I would do it again. That was the most I could give her, and I gave it." He reached across and touched the control once more, and a third image appeared beside the first two.

Her own face.

Not hooded. The tribunal gallery, the day of sentencing, the moment just after he had pronounced the word *death*. She had not known anyone was looking at her. Her hand was pressed hard over her mouth. Her eyes were wide and wet and wrecked, fixed on the floor at her feet. It was the face of someone who had watched a building fall on a person she loved, and knew she had set the charge.

"I kept this as well," Palpatine said. "Forgive me. Of everyone in that chamber, you were the only one whose face looked the way I felt. I wanted to remember that someone else understood what was being done."

She could not look away from it. She could not look at him.

"You could free her," she said at last. "You have the recordings. You could —"

"And put you in her place?" He sounded genuinely pained. "And put the Order in the dock beside you? No. I don't believe you want that, not truly. I don't believe *she* would want that, if she knew. She thinks of you as her friend, doesn't she? She protected you at her own trial. She would not name you even when it might have saved her." He let that settle. "That is a remarkable loyalty. I would not dishonor it."

Barriss's hands had curled into fists on her knees. She uncurled them one finger at a time.

"What do you want from me?"

"Want?" He looked almost hurt. Then he smiled, and it was a kind smile, an old man's smile, the smile of a grandfather at the end of a long table. "My dear girl. I don't want anything from you that you haven't already been trying to give. You did what you did because you believe the Jedi have lost their way. Because they answer to no one, and they have walked into this war without once asking whether they should. Isn't that so?"

She said nothing. He did not need her to.

"I am the elected head of the Republic the Jedi serve," he said. "And do you know how I learn what the Council fears? I read it on the Holonet, like any clerk in the lower levels. They come to this office and tell me what they have decided, never what they are afraid of. They treat me as one treats a storm. Something to be managed, and waited out." He spread his hands. "I'm not asking you to betray anyone. I would never ask that. I'm asking for friendship. Now and then, when something troubles them — when they are afraid — tell me. So that I can help. So that someone outside that tower knows what is happening inside it."

The holo frames hung in the air between them. The corridor. The holding room. Her face.

She thought: *The Senate should oversee the Jedi.* She had said it to Ahsoka once, in a different form, on a long night at the clinic, and Ahsoka had laughed and called her a politician. She had believed it then. She believed it now. An Order that answered to no one had let a seventeen-year-old girl be taken in binders out of its own front doors, and had not even flinched. If someone had been watching them — if anyone at all had been allowed to ask them a question they could not refuse to answer —

It was not betrayal. It was the very thing she had wanted. It was accountability, at last, wearing a kind face.

"Yes," she said.

"Thank you." He touched the control, and the three images folded away into nothing, and he sat back. "Thank you, Barriss. I don't think you know how much it means to an old man to have a friend in the Temple."

*A friend in the Temple.* That was what she had been, the night Ahsoka called from the lower levels with Ventress beside her: the one person in the Temple Ahsoka trusted to tell her where to go. And Barriss had told her.

She rose. Her legs held. That surprised her.

"These recordings are safest with me, I think," Palpatine said, rising too, courteous to the last. "Archives are such fragile things. A change of administration, a curious clerk with too little to do. One never knows." He walked her to the door himself. "And do give Master Unduli my warmest regards."

The doors parted. The antechamber was bright and ordinary. Dace Orrin looked up from his desk and lifted his mended wrist to her in a small, grateful wave.

She did not remember the walk to the platform. She remembered only that, in the moment before the doors had closed between her and that red room, she had realized she was cold — cold to the bone, in a room that had been too warm a moment before — and that she could not tell whether the chill had come from the room, or from her.

* * *

She slept that night.

She did not mean to. She sat down on the edge of her bunk in her robes intending to meditate, the way she always did, the way that never worked, and the next thing she knew grey light was coming through the narrow window and the Temple bells were ringing the second hour of the morning. Nine hours. She lay still for a long time and tried to remember the last time she had slept nine hours and could not.

She had missed the dawn candles.

She went to the alcove anyway. Someone had lit the morning's flames in a long uneven row, the way they did every day, for the hangar dead and the war dead and the people no one else remembered. Her four places at the end were empty. She lit them late, with hands that were — she noticed this as if from a distance — perfectly steady. The flames rose straight in the still air. She waited to feel something. When nothing came, she told herself that was the Force, and went to find her Master.

Luminara was in one of the small courtyard gardens on the western side of the Temple, where the water ran over flat black stones and someone had planted moss from Mirial decades ago and coaxed it to grow. She was kneeling in meditation when Barriss came in, and she did not open her eyes, but she said, "You're late."

"I'm sorry, Master. I overslept."

Now Luminara opened her eyes.

"You *overslept*," she said, and there was something in her voice that was not quite pleasure. She studied Barriss's face for a long moment. "You look rested. You haven't looked rested since before the hangar."

"I suppose I needed it."

"I suppose you did." Luminara gestured to the stones beside her, and Barriss knelt. For a while there was only the water. Then Luminara said, without turning her head, "Where were you yesterday afternoon? The clinic said you were called away."

"The Chancellor's office. One of his aides was hurt. A sprained wrist." True. Every word true. "The Chancellor asked me to stay for tea. He was curious about the field hospitals."

"Was he." Luminara's gaze stayed on the water. "He is a curious man. He has been very curious, lately, about all of us." She was quiet a moment, as if deciding something. "I shouldn't tell you this. But you will be a Knight soon enough, and you may as well know the shape of things. The Council is troubled."

Barriss kept her hands loose in her lap.

"By the war?"

"By Skywalker." Luminara sighed. "Since the tribunal, he goes to the Chancellor more than he comes to us. Master Windu wrote to the Chancellor some weeks ago, asking him to stop granting Skywalker's petitions regarding his former Padawan. There has been no answer. Not a refusal — simply nothing. And now there are whispers that Skywalker holds a permit to visit the prison on Narkis, signed by the Chancellor's own hand, over the warden's objections." Her mouth tightened. "Some on the Council think the Chancellor is cultivating him. Some think it's worse than that — that a Jedi Knight who owes his loyalty to the Chancellor's office before the Order is a Jedi Knight the Order no longer commands. Master Windu fears the emergency powers. Master Yoda fears something he will not name. And all of them fear what Skywalker might do if anyone tells him no again."

Barriss listened. She listened the way she had been taught to listen to a patient's breathing — not to the words, but to the pattern under them. She heard every piece of it. She filed every piece of it away in some quiet, orderly drawer she had not known she owned until yesterday.

*Tell me what they fear.*

"That sounds very hard," she said.

"It is." Luminara turned, finally, and looked at her. "Barriss. You were close to Ahsoka. Closer than anyone in the Order but Skywalker himself. Since the trial you have been — I don't know the word. Burning down. I've watched it and not known how to reach you. And this morning you come to me rested and steady and calm, and I should be glad, and I find that I'm not. Your stillness feels different." She frowned, choosing the words with care. "Not like water that has settled. Like a door that has closed."

The water ran over the stones.

"I think I've only accepted it, Master," Barriss said. "What happened to her. That I can't change it."

"Is that all?" Luminara's voice was very soft. "Is there anything you want to tell me?"

There it was: the question her Master had been holding for two months. Barriss had felt it every day, an unasked weight between them, and every day she had braced against it and nearly broken, and once, kneeling beside her at the long window on the eve of the trial, she had come within a single word of telling the truth.

"No, Master," she said.

It came out easily. That was the thing she would remember afterward. Not that she lied — she had lied to her Master for two months — but that, for the first time, the lie had cost her nothing at all.

Luminara held her gaze a moment longer. Then she nodded and closed her eyes and turned back to the water. But Barriss saw, before she did, the small line that appeared between her brows, and did not go away.

* * *

The healers' duty board hung in the corridor outside the clinic stores, a long panel of soft blue light that listed the requests the Temple received from the rest of the galaxy: field hospitals, refugee ships, a plague ward on some Mid Rim world, a Senator's child with a fever no doctor could break. Most entries had names beside them within the hour. A few did not.

Barriss found the ones that did not near the bottom, in a separate block marked JUDICIARY.

*Republic Penal Authority requests Jedi healer attendance, circuit rotation, at facilities housing Force-sensitive detainees. Complications reported with new restraint devices: nerve inflammation at the cervical contact points, persistent headaches, sleep disturbance, two cases of seizure. Healer to assess and treat. Circuit: Coruscant Central Detention — Kessan Point Facility — Gallia Orbital Holding — Republic Penal Facility Narkis-Nine. Est. duration four weeks outbound. Departure in twenty days.*

No one had signed it. It had been on the board, according to the date in the corner, for nineteen days.

She stood in front of it for a long time.

*Collars,* she thought. She had heard about them, the way the whole Temple had heard: a Judiciary device, new, experimental, fitted to prisoners who could touch the Force. Fitted, the rumor said, to Ahsoka in flight on the way to the ice. She had imagined it more nights than she could count — the silence it must make, the deafness. She had never let herself imagine what it did to the body. *Nerve inflammation at the cervical contact points.* Her healer's mind supplied the rest without asking: the burning along the neck, the dull pressure at the base of the skull, the headaches that never quite stopped.

She did not think it through. She would tell herself later that she had, that she had weighed it carefully. But the truth was that she lifted her hand to the panel and pressed her thumb against the signature field and watched her name appear beside the Narkis line, and it felt less like a decision than like falling.

"You're joking."

Healer-Master Ondra Vey had come up behind her with a crate of bacta canisters balanced on one hip. She was a short, broad human woman in her seventies, grey hair cropped close, a field medic's forearms. She had trained half the healers in the Temple and liked almost none of them.

"No, Master Vey."

"That's a month through the cold back end of nowhere, treating convicts for a device the Judiciary invented without asking anyone who knows what a nerve is." Vey set the crate down. "Nobody's signed it in three weeks for a reason. You're needed here."

"I'm needed there more," Barriss said. "No one else will go."

Vey looked at her the way she looked at a wound that wasn't healing the way it should.

"Is this about the Tano girl?"

The corridor seemed to narrow.

"She's at Narkis," Vey said. "Everyone knows she's at Narkis. You two were thick as thieves. If you're going out there to hold her hand, I won't stop you, but I want to hear you say it."

"I'm going because people are hurt," Barriss said, "and because the Order put them there, or let them be put there, and no one in this building wants to look at it." Her voice was steadier than she had expected. Some of it was even true. "If she's one of them, I'll treat her like any other patient."

Vey grunted. "Well. You'll need clearance. New protocol since the trial — any Jedi going into a Judiciary facility needs a countersign from the Chancellery. Some nonsense about chain of custody." She picked up her crate again. "Don't hold your breath. Last time it took a month and a half and three angry letters."

She went off down the corridor. Barriss stood in front of the board with her name glowing on it and tried to name the thing she was doing.

Penance was the first word that came. She rejected it. Penance was for people who meant to confess, and she had decided — yesterday, in a red room, in a single syllable — that she would not. Courage was the second word. She rejected that too. There was nothing brave about walking into a prison wearing the robes of the Order that had sent your friend there, knowing what you knew.

What, then? To see her. To know she was alive with her own eyes and not through a holo frame. To put her hands on whatever the collar had done to Ahsoka's neck and make it stop hurting, because that, at least, she could do. Because in two months it was the only thing she had been able to think of that would not make anything worse.

Or perhaps that was another lie, and she simply could not stay away from the place where the worst thing she had ever done was still happening, every day, in the cold.

She went back into the clinic. She treated a Padawan with a dislocated finger, a Knight just back from the Mid Rim with a fever and a cough, a maintenance worker who had cut his hand on a durasteel edge in the rebuilt hangar. She worked through the afternoon with steady hands, and did not think about the board, or the red room, or the drawer in her mind where she had put away everything Luminara said.

The datapad in her satchel chimed a little after the sixth hour.

She almost did not look. When she did, it was a clearance notice, stamped with the seal of the Office of the Supreme Chancellor. Her circuit to the Judiciary facilities had been approved. Departure confirmed in twenty days. Transport arranged on a Chancellery courier, at the Chancellery's expense.

She had signed the board six hours ago. She had told no one but Ondra Vey.

Beneath the formal text, in a smaller, plainer script, someone had added a single line:

*She will be so glad of a friendly face. —P.*

Barriss sat very still on the edge of the empty cot. Above the hangar the memorial candles would be burning low by now, four of them at the end of the row, steady in air that did not move. The clinic was warm. It was always warm.

She could not stop shaking.

## Chapter 11: Visitors

Sergeant Holt pulled Ahsoka off terrace nine in the middle of a cut, which never happened, and told her she had a visitor, which could not happen at all. She had been on Narkis fifty-three days. The rule was ninety. She had learned the rules here the way she had once learned saber forms, by repetition and by the bruises that came when she got them wrong, and so she walked up the Yard tunnel behind him with her gloved hands loose at her sides and her shoulders set for the Well.

"Is it the Commandant?" she asked at the second checkpoint.

Holt keyed the lift. He was a broad, tired man who smelled of caf and heat-strip ozone, and in fifty-three days he had never once told her anything he did not have to. "Commandant's going to be in the room," he said. "That's all I know."

The lift climbed out of Ring Two and the smell of the mess, leaving the long curved galleries of cells somewhere below in the blue ice. On Ring One the walls stopped being ice and became plated durasteel, painted a pale Judiciary grey, and the air was warm enough that her eyes stung. Holt stopped at a door with no marking on it and looked at her for a moment, as if there were something in his face he meant to put into words, and then he only said, "Two hours. She'll count them."

He opened the door.

The room was small and bare, a table bolted to the floor, two chairs, a ring welded into the tabletop for binders. Commandant Vorne stood against the far wall with her hands clasped behind her back, her uniform as sharp as a cut edge, her iron-grey hair pinned flat. And at the table, half out of his chair before the door had finished opening, was Anakin.

Ahsoka stopped in the doorway. Holt had to touch her elbow to move her forward.

She had asked him not to come. She had said it on Coruscant, in a detention cell, with her voice steady because she had practiced it, and he had looked at her the way he looked at an order he had decided to disregard later. She had known then. She had told herself he would listen.

"Sit, prisoner," Vorne said. "General Skywalker, you will remain seated as well. The restraints stay on. There will be no physical contact. If either of you stands without my leave, the visit ends."

Holt sat her down and ran her binders through the ring. The metal was cold even through the gloves. Then he went out, and the door sealed, and it was the three of them.

Anakin sat back down slowly. He moved as if something on his left side was holding a grudge against him, a stiffness through the ribs that he was trying not to show and showing anyway. He was thinner. The bones of his face had come forward. His hair was longer than he usually let it get, and there was a shadow under his eyes like a bruise that had settled in to stay.

"Hey, Snips," he said.

"I asked you not to come."

"You did." He tried a smile. It came out lopsided and tired. "You also asked me not to land on that dreadnought, and not to take the shortcut through the asteroid field, and not to call you Snips. My record's not great."

"I was serious."

"So was I." He looked at her neck. She watched his eyes find the collar, the grey band of it, the little blue status light that sat in the hollow of her throat. His jaw moved. "Does it hurt?"

"Sometimes." She lifted her chin a fraction, as if that would make it less visible. "Headaches. You get used to it."

"You shouldn't have to get used to it."

"There are a lot of things I shouldn't have to get used to." She heard how that sounded, sharper than she meant, and softened it. "It's fine. Really. I can still breathe. That's more than some people get."

He reached for her across the table. It was pure reflex, the same reflex that had pulled her out of a hundred bad places, and his gloved fingers were nearly on her wrist when Vorne said, "General," in a voice like a door closing. He stopped. He laid his hand flat on the tabletop instead, the right one, the metal one, a few centimeters from her binders. He left it there.

That was when Ahsoka noticed what was missing.

All her life Anakin had been loud in the Force. Not in a way most Jedi would have liked, but she had grown up beside it, the heat and pressure of him, a sun she could have found with her eyes shut on any battlefield in the galaxy. She had always known what he was feeling a half-second before his face did. Now there was nothing. He was a man sitting across a table. She would have to read him the way anyone else did, with her eyes, and she was out of practice.

"How did you get in here?" she said. "There's a rule. Ninety days."

"There's a permit," Anakin said. He glanced at Vorne. "The Commandant has it."

"I do," Vorne said. "Signed by the Supreme Chancellor, overriding the standing order of this facility. The Chancellor's signature supersedes my rule. It does not supersede my room."

"She's been very clear about that," Anakin said. "Several times. On the landing pad. In the lift. Twice in the corridor."

"Repetition is cheaper than incident reports, General."

Ahsoka almost laughed. It surprised her, and she looked down at the table to hide it, and when she looked up Anakin was grinning at her, the old grin, and for the space of a breath they were on the bridge of the *Resolute* and he had just done something reckless and gotten away with it.

Then it went out of his face, the way everything seemed to go out of it now, quickly, like a light someone was saving power on.

"The Chancellor," she said. "He did this?"

"He's been the only one." Anakin leaned forward. "Snips, listen. He's working on it. He can't say anything officially, not with the war, not with the Senate the way it is, but he as good as told me. When this is over, there's going to be an amnesty. A general one. He'll make sure your name is on it. He doesn't have to do any of this. He's doing it anyway."

She remembered the Senate chamber. Death, and then the long silence, and then *life*, and how she had come apart on that word in front of everyone because it was somehow worse. She remembered Palpatine's kind, sorrowful face above the bench. She owed him her life. That was true. It was also true that she did not know what to feel about a man who had pronounced her death in one breath and taken it back in the next, and she had not let herself think about it for fifty-three days, and she did not want to start now.

"That's good of him," she said.

"It's more than good. It's more than anyone else is doing." Anakin's voice dropped. "You know what the Council did? While I was trying to get a permit? Windu wrote to the Chancellor. Personally. Asked him to deny anything I applied for. Not 'consider.' Deny." He laughed, short and ugly. "They expelled you so they wouldn't have to look at you, and now they don't want me looking either."

"Anakin."

"They had you in front of them. They had you *standing there*, and they voted, and Windu took your beads off with his own hands like he was doing you a favor—"

"Anakin."

"—and Obi-Wan comes to me on Ordaine with a message from the Council, a *message*, like I'm some Padawan who needs to be told to go to bed—"

The cup on the table moved.

It was a plain steel cup of water, set out for the visitor, sitting a hand's width from his elbow. Nobody touched it. Ahsoka watched its rim dimple inward, a small dent, then another, the metal giving under a pressure that came from nowhere, folding with a thin, patient crackle. Water ran out across the table toward her binders.

Against the wall, Vorne's hand went to her belt, to the small black cylinder there. Ahsoka saw it, and saw Vorne realize in the same instant that it was useless, that it was keyed to one collar in this room and that collar was not the problem. The Commandant did not move further. Her face did not change. But her hand stayed where it was.

Anakin looked down at the crumpled cup as if somebody else had done it.

"Sorry," he said.

Ahsoka did not say anything. She could not feel him. That was the terrible part. A year ago she would have felt the anger rising in him like weather and known its shape and known it would pass. Now she only had the cup, and the water creeping across the table, and his face, which was not ashamed. It was only surprised. As if the anger was a fact he had stopped noticing, the way you stopped noticing cold on Narkis after the first week. She did not know what he was becoming. She did not know if he did.

"Who sent you to the warehouse?" he said quietly.

It was not what she had expected. "You asked me that before."

"I'm asking again. Because something's wrong, Snips. There was a broker in the lower levels, a Rodian, the kind of man who might have known who bought nano-droid crates out of an old munitions store. I found him. He was dead. Not a mark on him. Everything he had was gone, his records, his cam core. Someone cleaned up behind him like they were tidying a room." He spread his flesh hand on the wet table. "Whoever framed you is still out there, and they're careful, and they're not done. Whoever sent you to that warehouse knew where you'd be. I need to talk to them."

She had thought about that, in the Well. She had thought about very little else, lying on the cold shelf under lights that never went out, listening to Merrick Tal breathe through the vent. *Who knew where you would go.* She had followed the thought exactly as far as she could stand to, and then she had stopped, because the answer it wanted was impossible and she would not be the kind of person who thought it. Her friend had told her where the crates came from. Her friend had been trying to help. And if someone had known where she would be, it was because someone had been listening to that call, or watching the Temple, or watching her—and if that someone was cleaning up now, then her friend was not a suspect.

Her friend was in danger.

"I made a promise," Ahsoka said.

"To who?"

"That's the promise."

"Snips—"

"They helped me when no one else would. When you were *hunting* me." That landed. She saw it land and was sorry and did not take it back. "If I give you a name, you'll go after them the way you went after Ventress. And whoever killed your Rodian will see you do it."

Anakin was quiet for a long moment. The water had reached the edge of the table and was dripping onto the floor, one drop at a time.

"Then you be careful," he said finally. "In here. Somebody knew to put you on that terrace with the broken camera. You think that was the Trandoshan's idea?"

She had thought about that too. She had not come up with anything she liked.

"I'm always careful," she said.

"You have never once been careful in your life."

"I learned from the best."

That got a laugh out of him, a real one, small and cracked, and they talked after that about smaller things because there was nothing else to do with the time. She told him about Tess and the heat-packs and the rule about bare skin on metal, and he told her Rex had sent a message he'd been made to memorize word for word, and then recited it in a flat, gravelly voice that was so exactly Rex that Vorne's mouth twitched once against the wall. He told her the 501st had painted something on a gunship and refused to say what. He did not mention Padmé, and she did not ask, and both of them noticed.

At an hour and fifty-two minutes Vorne said, "Eight minutes," and Ahsoka felt the room tilt.

She leaned forward until the binders pulled against the ring.

"I need you to promise me something."

He looked at her. "Anything."

"Don't do anything stupid."

"You'll have to narrow that down."

"I'm serious, Skyguy."

The name went into him. She watched it, watched his face open and then close again around the hurt of it, and she was sorry for that too, and she had used it anyway because she needed him to hear her. "Don't do anything stupid," she said again. "Not for me. Not about the Council. Not about anyone. Whatever's going on in your head right now, whatever you're thinking about Windu, or—any of them. Don't."

"I'm not—"

"Promise me."

"I promise." He said it at once. He meant it. She could see that he meant it. He had always meant everything completely, right up until the moment he didn't. "I promise, Snips."

"Time," Vorne said.

Holt came in. Anakin stood when he was told he could and not before, which she understood was costing him something. He stopped at the door and looked back at her with his gloved right hand closed at his side.

"I'll come back," he said.

"Don't," she said, and found that she was smiling, and that her eyes were wet, and that she could not tell anymore which of those things she meant.

* * *

The chain went at the forty-first minute of second shift, thirty-nine days later, and it went with a sound like a blaster bolt.

Ahsoka heard it before she understood it. A sharp metallic crack from the top of the ramp, then a scream of links running loose over a pulley, and then the block—a full-cut slab of glacier ice the size of a speeder cockpit, bound for the refinery sleds—came loose from its harness and started down the chute toward the terrace below.

Toward Pell.

Pell was nineteen, a Rodian kid from Ring Five with a cough he wouldn't get checked, eight days into his sentence and still walking the terraces like they were floors. He was standing in the chute with his back to it, bent over a jammed sled runner, because nobody had told him yet that you never stood in the chute, or because someone had and he'd forgotten. There was no time to tell him now.

Before the collar she would have pulled him clear from twenty meters away without thinking. Her hand actually started to lift, and the old reflex reached for the Force and found the silence where it used to be, a step that wasn't there in the dark. She lurched.

Then she ran, which was against the rules.

The ice was slick and pitted. She took the slope in four strides, hit Pell in the back with her shoulder—the right one, the bad one, and something in it lit up white—and the two of them went sprawling across the terrace. The block came down the chute behind them with a grinding roar, clipped the lip of the terrace edge, and spun. A corner of it caught her left leg below the knee as it went past.

She felt the impact more than the pain at first. A blow like being kicked by a bantha, and then she was lying on her side on the ice, and the block was gone over the edge, and somewhere below there was a long, heavy crash and shouting from the lower crews.

Pell was saying something in Huttese, over and over, high and fast. Tess's face appeared above her, white cropped hair, eyes narrowed.

"Don't move."

"I'm fine."

"You're bleeding into your boot. Don't move." Tess pressed down above her knee with her good hand, the one with all its fingers. "I told you never to run."

"You did," Ahsoka said. The pain was arriving now, a hot, wet throb in her shin, and a second, deeper one in her shoulder. "He was in the chute."

"Everybody's in the chute sooner or later. That's why there's a rule about the chute." Tess looked up and bellowed for a guard, and then looked back down, and for a moment her expression was something Ahsoka had not seen on it before. "Stupid, brave kid," she said, more quietly. "Lie still."

The guards came. The medic on duty was a Judiciary corpsman with a field kit and very little interest, and he cut her boot away, looked at the gash, packed it, and told her it wasn't broken. It was deep, though. A long split down the outside of her shin where the block's corner had opened her up, and the bone beneath it was bruised black already, and when they got her on a stretcher and the stretcher tipped going into the lift, her shoulder made her see sparks.

"Medical," the corpsman told the lift. "Ring Two." And then, to her, with a sort of bored generosity: "You're in luck. The circuit healer's still on station. She was supposed to ship out at the end of this shift."

"Circuit healer?"

"Comes through every few months. Jedi, this time." He snorted. "Wasn't a lot of call for one until you showed up."

Ahsoka closed her eyes. A Jedi. The lift hummed. She thought about Plo Koon, who had looked at the floor. She thought about Windu's hands on her beads. She thought about which Jedi of all of them would agree to come to the edge of Wild Space and stand in a room with her, and could not think of a single one, and decided that whoever it was, she would be polite to them, because that was the only thing in this situation that was still hers to decide.

* * *

The medical bay on Ring Two was the warmest place on Narkis, which was not saying much. Six cots, a scrubbed steel counter, a bacta cabinet with a lock on it, one narrow window looking out into the blue twilight of the Yard. They put her on the cot nearest the window and ran a restraint strap across her right wrist to the frame. The guard went to stand by the door.

Someone in a dark hood was washing her hands at the counter, her back to the room.

"I'm told it's a leg," the healer said, without turning around. "And a shoulder. And that you ran."

Ahsoka knew the voice before the woman turned. She knew it the way she would have known her own name, and she pushed herself up on her good elbow so fast the shoulder screamed at her.

"Barriss?"

Barriss Offee turned from the basin. She was drying her hands on a cloth, carefully, finger by finger, and she pushed her hood back. The same green skin. The same diamond pattern across the bridge of her nose and beneath her eyes. She looked tired. More than tired—worn thin, the way Anakin had looked, as if something had been taking a little from her every night. But she was here. She was standing in a prison infirmary at the end of the galaxy, and she was looking at Ahsoka, and she was here.

"Hello, Ahsoka," she said.

Ahsoka could not think of a single thing to say. Her throat had closed. She lay back down on the cot because her arm would not hold her anymore, and stared at the ceiling, and blinked several times, hard.

"You came," she managed.

"It's a healer circuit." Barriss came over and set her satchel on the end of the cot. "Four facilities. Narkis is the last stop. Someone has to do it." She paused. "I asked for it."

"You asked."

"Yes."

Barriss knelt beside the cot and peeled back the corpsman's packing from Ahsoka's shin, and drew a breath through her teeth at what she found there. Then she put her hands on either side of the wound, not quite touching, and closed her eyes.

Ahsoka had been healed by Barriss before. On Geonosis, after. In the detention cell on Coruscant, her wrists, the night before the trial. Every time she had felt it in the Force first: a cool, clean current, like a hand drawn through water. Now there was nothing at all. Only warmth, spreading slowly from Barriss's palms into the torn meat of her leg. The throbbing eased. She felt, rather than saw, the edges of the wound drawing in, a strange crawling tightness, and she watched Barriss's face instead because she could not watch her leg.

Barriss's lips were pressed together. A thin line of sweat had broken out at her hairline, even in the cold.

"You can't feel it," Barriss said, without opening her eyes. It was not a question.

"No. It's—I can't feel anything. Not you. Not anyone." Ahsoka tried to make it a joke. "I'm a terrible audience."

Barriss's hands did not move, but something passed over her face. "I'm sorry," she said.

"It's not your fault."

Barriss opened her eyes then and looked at her, and it was such a strange look—long, and level, and very far away—that Ahsoka almost asked what was wrong. But Barriss only nodded and moved up to the shoulder, and laid two fingers along the collarbone, and frowned.

"This was injured before."

"A while back. There was a Trandoshan."

"There's always a Trandoshan." Barriss's hand moved, and the warmth sank in under the joint, and Ahsoka let out a breath she had been holding since the chute. "You should have had this seen to properly. It's healed crooked." Her fingers drifted higher. Paused at the edge of the collar, where the skin was red and swollen and had been for weeks. "And this."

"That's just the collar."

"I know what it is." Barriss's voice was very quiet. "The nerves under it are inflamed. They'll keep getting worse. Headaches. Eventually, seizures." She laid her palm flat against the side of Ahsoka's neck, just below the band of grey metal, and held it there.

For the first time in ninety-two days the ache in Ahsoka's skull went quiet.

She hadn't known how loud it was until it stopped. She lay on the cot with her eyes stinging and felt it ebb out of her like a tide going down, and Barriss's hand stayed where it was, warm and steady, and neither of them said anything for a long time.

"Do you remember the supply ship," Ahsoka said eventually. "After Geonosis."

Barriss's hand twitched. Just once. "Yes."

"I keep thinking about it. Here." She looked toward the window, the endless blue dusk of the Yard. "The cold. Everyone in the Deep hates the cold. I hate it. But I keep thinking—it saved us. Those things in your head couldn't stand it. I opened the coolant lines and froze us both half to death and it was the only thing that worked." She smiled at the ceiling. "So I tell myself it's on my side. The cold. It's stupid."

"It isn't stupid," Barriss said.

"You tried to kill me that day, you know."

"I know."

"You weren't yourself."

Barriss did not answer that. When Ahsoka looked, Barriss had turned her face toward the satchel, and was looking into it as if there were something in there she had lost.

"I never told them about you," Ahsoka said. "I want you to know that. Not Tarkin. Not Padmé. Anakin came here, a few weeks ago—he asked me twice. I didn't tell him either."

"Anakin was here?"

"He had a permit from the Chancellor."

Barriss went still. "Of course he did," she said, very softly, and then, before Ahsoka could ask what she meant: "The Chancellor arranged my transport, too. A courier from his own office. He asked to be remembered to you."

"He's been kind," Ahsoka said, and heard her own uncertainty, and let it go. "Anakin says someone's been cleaning up behind the bombing. A man who might have known something turned up dead. So if anyone ever asks you—about the warehouse, about what you told me—you be careful. Promise me. I don't want you anywhere near this."

Barriss laughed. It was a very small sound and there was no humor in it at all, and it stopped quickly. "I'll be careful," she said.

Her hand was still on Ahsoka's neck. Ahsoka could feel a faint tremor in it now, a fine shiver running through the fingers. The cold, she thought. Barriss had been on Narkis since the morning, in robes made for the Temple, not for ice.

"Do you still light candles?" Ahsoka asked. "For the three troopers. You said you would."

"Every morning." Barriss took her hand away at last and began packing her satchel, one item at a time, with great care. "I light four."

"Four?"

"There was—someone else. Who shouldn't have died."

Ahsoka thought of Letta Turmond, choking in a room with no one near her, a woman who had helped kill six Jedi and all those workers and clones, and who had still been a person, and who had died before she could say the one thing that would have saved Ahsoka's life. She thought how like Barriss it was to light a candle even for her, when no one else in the Temple would have. She swallowed.

"That's good of you," she said.

Barriss's hands stopped on the satchel's clasp.

"Ahsoka," she said, without looking up. "Can I ask you something?"

"Anything."

"Do you hate them? The Council. The Order." She did look up then, and her eyes were very bright. "After what they did. Do you hate them?"

It was the question Ahsoka had been asking herself on the cold shelf in the Well, in the dark, in the cut with the pick in her hands. She had never said the answer out loud. She found that she wanted to say it to Barriss, because Barriss would understand. Barriss had always been the one who asked the real questions, about the war and what it was doing to them, while everyone else asked about tactics.

"I'm trying not to," she said.

Barriss looked at her.

"It would be easy," Ahsoka went on. "That's the thing. Hating them would be the easiest thing in the galaxy. Every morning in this place I wake up and it's right there, waiting, and it would keep me warm. But I keep thinking—if I let it in, it won't stop with them. It'll be the guards next. Then the people in the Senate. Then everyone who believed it. And one day I'll look up and I'll be exactly what they said I was." She shook her head against the thin pillow. "So I'm trying not to. Some days I'm better at it than others."

Barriss did not move at all. Then she stood, and put the satchel over her shoulder, and her hands were shaking openly now—Ahsoka could see it from the cot, a tremor in the fingers as she fastened the strap, missed the clasp, fastened it again.

"You should rest," Barriss said. "The leg will hold if you keep off it two days. Tell them I said so. Tell them it's a healer's order." She hesitated. "I've left a note for the medical officer. About the collar. I don't know if anyone will read it."

"Barriss." Ahsoka reached for her with the hand that wasn't strapped down, and Barriss looked at it, the open grey-gloved palm, and after a second took it. Her fingers were ice-cold. "Thank you. For coming all this way. You don't know—" Her voice went, and she waited for it to come back. "You don't know what it means. That somebody from home still—"

"I know," Barriss said. "I know."

She let go. At the door she drew her hood back up, and the guard stood aside to let her pass, and she went out without looking back, very straight, very quickly, like someone walking against a strong wind.

Ahsoka lay on the cot by the window and watched the blue dusk of the Yard. An hour later she saw the courier lift off the landing pad beyond the dome: a small, sleek ship, white and red, nothing like the grey Judiciary hulls that came and went with the prisoners. It climbed through the twilight and was gone.

Her leg was whole under the dressing. Her shoulder moved freely for the first time in weeks. Her head was quiet.

The guard came eventually and unbuckled the strap from her wrist and said she could stay the night, healer's orders, and turned the lights down to half. Ahsoka lay in the dim warmth and thought about Barriss's hands shaking on the satchel clasp. She thought about the cold, and the long journey, and how hard it must have been to walk into this place in the robes of the Order that had sent her here, knowing what everyone in the Temple must be saying about her—*that she would go*—and doing it anyway.

That was what kindness looked like, she decided, when it cost something.

She fell asleep before she could finish the thought, and slept without dreaming for the first time since the hangar. In the morning, the medical officer found the note Barriss had left on the counter, read it, frowned, and carried it straight up to Ring One—and an hour after that, for the first time since the transport, Commandant Vorne came down to Ring Two and stood at the foot of Ahsoka's cot with the collar control in her hand.

## Chapter 12: Ringo Vinda

Ringo Vinda Station turned once every six minutes, and every six minutes the local sun swung around through the shattered dome of the transit hub and blinded half the 501st.

Rex had learned to count it. Six minutes of grey half-light: emergency strips, droid optics, the red stutter of blaster bolts across the deck. Then the white came rolling over the plates like surf, his visor dimming a beat too slowly, and for ten seconds every trooper in the hub was firing at memory. Then grey again.

"Sun in thirty," he said on the squad channel. "Heads down when it comes."

"Copy," Jesse said, from behind a toppled cargo sled. "Somebody tell the clankers."

The hub was a wheel inside the wheel, a round chamber two hundred meters across where six freight spokes met and crossed. Before the war it had moved ore and tibanna and passengers around the station's rim. Now it was the only thing holding the station together, and Admiral Trench's forces had held it for nine days. His flagship sat off the station's dark side, out of reach of the Republic guns and close enough to pour fresh droids down the spokes faster than the 501st could cut them down.

So the plan was simple, the way the bad ones usually were. General Skywalker and most of the legion would keep Trench busy at the docking ring on the far side. Two companies and two Jedi would take the hub and seal the spokes one by one, until the droids had nowhere left to come from.

The two Jedi were already doing their part.

Rex had served with Generals Tiplar and Tiplee twice before, and he still couldn't always tell where one of them stopped. They were Mikkian sisters, twins, head-tendrils bound back with plain cord, and they fought as if they had been issued one mind and four arms between them. Their blades crossed through each other's space and never touched. Tiplar talked through every fight—to her sister, to the troopers, to the droids, once memorably to a tank. Tiplee almost never spoke at all, and when she did, Tiplar usually laughed.

They said thank you, too. Rex had noticed that. Not every general did.

"Spoke four!" Tiplar called now. Her blade swept a volley back up the tunnel mouth and three droids folded. "Captain, I need that door shut before the next lot comes down it!"

"Fives," Rex said.

"On it." Fives was already moving, low and fast along the curve of the wall toward the spoke-four seal controls. Rex went with him, laying fire up the tunnel.

The sun came.

White everywhere. Rex dropped to one knee behind a support column and kept shooting at where the tunnel mouth had been, short bursts, counting. Beside him a trooper from Bravo Company was swearing steadily. Somewhere to the left a spider droid's legs clattered on the plates, and then a lightsaber hummed and the clattering stopped.

Grey again.

Fives had the seal panel open, wires in his teeth. Across the hub, Tiplee had driven the droids out of spoke two alone and was standing in its mouth with her blade up, still as a carving. Rex swept the line out of habit. Jesse, behind his sled. Kix, dragging a wounded trooper toward the medical point by his drag-strap. Tup, on the left at the spoke-three junction—

Tup had his hand against his helmet again. The heel of his palm pressed hard into the right side, where the temple would be. Third time in the last hour.

Three months. The headaches had started, as far as Rex could tell, the night they'd been ordered to strike the Commander off the roster. Kix had nagged him about it every week since. Tup always had an answer: the lights, the recycled air, the bunk under the vent. He'd skipped two scans. Rex had let it go, because Tup kept his rifle clean and made his marks, and because Rex had had other things on his mind in those months. He had told himself a headache was a headache.

"Tup," he said on the channel. "You good?"

The hand came down. "Fine, sir. Just the glare."

"Stay low."

"Sir."

"Got it," Fives said, and the spoke-four seal came down with a long shriek of durasteel, cutting a battle droid neatly in half on the way.

Two spokes left. They took spoke five in the next turn of the station, and spoke one in the turn after, and Rex lost two troopers doing it. He would write their numbers down later. He always did.

Then the hub was quiet.

Not silent. Silent never happened. Smoke curled out of a burning sled. A wounded droid's vocabulator repeated half a word. Somewhere in the ducts, the station's air system coughed and caught. But nobody was shooting, and the troopers began to straighten up from behind their cover the way men do when they don't quite trust the quiet yet.

"Good work," Tiplar said, to everyone. She deactivated her blade and pushed a loose tendril back under its cord. "Very good work. Captain, my sister and I will hold here until the General—"

She stopped. She was looking past Rex, to the left.

Tup was standing at the spoke-three junction with his head bowed. His rifle hung from one hand. The other hand was pressed flat against his temple again, and he wasn't moving at all.

Tiplar went to him. She didn't hurry; there was no reason to hurry. She went the way she'd go to any trooper standing wrong after a fight, and Rex watched her do it and thought, *good*, because Tup listened to her. Tup liked her. He'd said once she was the only general who'd ever asked his name twice to make sure she had it right.

"Trooper." She crouched a little, to see under the brim of his helmet. "Did you take a knock? Let me look."

The sun came around.

Rex would remember afterward that he saw it clearly, even in the white. That the light behind the twins was so bright they were only shapes, but Tup was turned the other way and Rex could see him perfectly. He saw the hand come away from the helmet. He saw it come away smoothly, without hurry, the way a man lowers his hand after a salute. He saw the rifle come up the same way.

One shot.

It was not loud. There had been louder all day.

The white rolled past. In the grey that followed, Tiplar was lying on the deck at Tup's feet, and Tup was standing over her with his rifle at his shoulder and his head tilted, as if he were listening to something a long way off.

"It was the order," he said.

Nobody moved. Rex could not afterward have said how long that lasted. A breath. Two.

Then Tiplee's blade lit across the hub.

She did not cry out. She came across the deck in the silence like a thrown thing, and Rex was moving before he knew it—not toward Tup, but in front of him, between a Jedi General and his own trooper, his blaster pointed at the floor and his free hand up.

"General!" His voice cracked on it. "General, we have him. We *have* him."

Fives hit Tup from the side, low, the way they'd been taught in the cadet rings. Tup went down without resisting. The rifle skidded across the plates and Jesse stamped on it. Fives was on Tup's back with one knee, wrenching his arms behind him, and he was saying, "Tup, it's me, it's Fives, Tup, look at me—" and Tup's helmet was turned sideways against the deck, and Rex could hear him through the open channel, quite calm.

"It was the order. It was the order. It was the—"

Tiplee stopped an arm's length from Rex. Her blade was so close he could feel the hum of it in his teeth. Her face was not angry. That was what he'd remember. It wasn't anything at all.

Then she looked down at her sister, and the blade went out, and she knelt.

Rex lowered his hand. Behind him, Tup kept saying it, over and over, in the voice he used for roll call, until Kix got a sedative through the seal of his armor and the words slowed and blurred and finally stopped.

The sun came around again. Nobody bothered to duck.

* * *

They put Tup in the isolation room off the *Resolute*'s medbay, because the brig was full of captured tactical droids and nobody wanted to argue about where a clone belonged.

Six hours after the hub, he was strapped upright in a restraint chair behind a pane of transparisteel, out of his armor, in a grey undersuit with his head shaved for the electrode pads Kix hadn't been allowed to attach. His eyes were open. Every so often his lips moved. The room was soundproofed, and Rex was grateful for that, and ashamed of being grateful.

"No scans," Kix said. He was standing at the glass with a med scanner in his hand, holding it the way a man holds something he's been told to put down. "No samples. No neural workup. Not even a blood panel, Captain. I asked."

"Who said no?"

"Came down from the medical officer. Who got it from Fleet. Who got it from Kamino." Kix's jaw worked. "Patient's to be kept sedated and transferred as-is. Kaminoan specialists will conduct the examination. Field medics are not to—" he made the quote marks in the air with two fingers, which Rex had never seen him do "—*compromise the subject*."

"The subject," Rex said.

"That's what it says."

The *Resolute*'s legal liaison had come and gone already: a Judge Advocate lieutenant, a young human with a pale careful face, who'd taken statements in the corridor and used words like *preliminary* and *deliberate act* and *no indication of coercion at this time*. He'd said there would be a formal inquiry. He had also said, in the tone of a man reading a weather report, that the possibility of Separatist conditioning could not be excluded, and that the trooper's service record would be annotated pending findings.

Annotated. Rex had thought about that word for a long time after the lieutenant left. He knew what an annotation was. It was the first line of a verdict.

By the time the troopers' mess sat down for the evening meal, the word going around wasn't *annotated*. Rex had heard it in the lift and in the armory and in the line for caf. Tup had turned. Tup had been bought. Tup was a clanker in a clone suit. A sergeant from Bravo who had never met him said he'd always thought there was something off about that one. Rex had stopped and looked at the sergeant until the sergeant found something else to do.

It had taken less than a day. That was the part that sat in his stomach like a swallowed stone. Less than a day, and men who had shared a barracks with Tup for three years were saying *that one*.

He'd felt this before. He'd been standing in the back of a Senate chamber three months ago, helmet under his arm, while a clerk read out *guilty* four times, and he had felt exactly this: the floor not quite where he'd left it. The sense of something enormous and certain moving through the room, and everyone stepping aside to let it pass.

He'd stepped aside, that time. He'd hunted her through the lower levels on orders, and he'd stepped aside.

The medbay doors opened, and General Skywalker came in.

He was still in the scorched armor from the docking ring, a long black scar across one shoulder plate where something had come too close. He'd lost weight since Ordaine—since Narkis, maybe; he never said much about either—and in the medbay lights his face was all hollows. He stopped next to Rex at the glass and watched Tup without speaking.

"General Tiplee?" Rex asked finally.

"With her sister." Skywalker's voice was flat. "She won't leave. I didn't ask her to." His mechanical hand closed slowly on the edge of the observation console and opened again. Rex watched the knuckles move under the dark glove. "What happened, Rex? Not the report. I read the report."

So Rex told him. The hand on the temple. Three months of headaches, the skipped scans. The way the rifle came up—not like a man who'd snapped. Like a man doing a drill.

"He didn't know her, sir. Not really. He *liked* her." Rex heard his own voice go rough and pulled it back. "There's no reason in it. I've been turning it over for six hours and there's no reason in it anywhere."

"The Judge Advocate seems to have found one."

"The Judge Advocate found a word."

Skywalker looked at him then. And Rex saw that the General knew exactly what he meant, had known it before Rex said it, had been standing in the same Senate chamber with the same stone in his stomach.

"*Deliberate*," Skywalker said quietly. "I've heard that one before."

Neither of them said her name. They didn't need to.

"Sir." Fives was in the doorway. He'd been there for a while, Rex realized; he'd come off the hangar deck still half in armor, his dented helmet under his arm. He'd never had the dent hammered out of it. "Sir, permission to speak."

"Go ahead, Fives."

Fives came to the glass. He didn't look at Tup. He looked at Skywalker, very straight.

"I've known Tup since we were cadets, General. He apologized to a training droid once. Out loud. In front of the whole squad, and we never let him forget it." Fives swallowed. "Whatever that was in the hub, it wasn't him. Something's wrong in his head. Something *physical*. And they've just told our medic he's not allowed to look."

"Kamino will look," Skywalker said.

"Yes, sir. That's what I'm afraid of."

It came out too fast, and too loud, and the medical officer at the far station glanced up. Fives didn't take it back.

"They'll call it a defect," he said, lower. "Or a Separatist trick. Or they'll call it a rogue trooper and write him off and send the rest of us a nice memo about vigilance. I want to go with him, General. I want to be in the room. Somebody who knows him should be in the room."

Skywalker was quiet for a moment. Rex watched him look from Fives to the glass, and from the glass to Rex, and he wondered what the General was seeing. A trooper begging to stand next to the accused. Rex had a guess at whose face he was seeing instead.

"Medical transport leaves at oh-nine-hundred," Skywalker said. "You're the escort. I'll clear it with Fleet. If anyone argues, tell them to call me." He paused. "And Fives. Be polite to the Kaminoans."

"I'm always polite, sir."

"Be *more* polite."

The corner of Fives's mouth moved. It wasn't quite a smile. "Yes, sir."

Skywalker left. On his way out he put his hand on Rex's shoulder, briefly, the way he used to do in the old days after a bad fight, back when there had been three of them on the command deck instead of two. Then he was gone, and Rex was alone with Kix and Fives and the glass, and behind it Tup's lips were moving again.

Rex walked down to the morgue before he turned in. He didn't know why. He stood in the doorway and did not go in.

General Tiplee was sitting on a low stool beside the slab, her hands folded on her knees. She had loosened the cord from her sister's tendrils and set it on the slab beside her, neatly coiled. She didn't look up.

"Captain," she said. "Did he hate her?"

It was the first thing Rex had ever heard her say without her sister there to laugh at it.

"No, General," he said. "He liked her. She learned his name."

Tiplee was silent for a while. Then she nodded, once, as if that were worse, and Rex left her there.

* * *

The medical shuttle was Kaminoan, long and white and smooth as a riverstone, and it sat at the far end of the *Resolute*'s main hangar looking as though it had been set down there by mistake.

Rex got there at oh-eight-thirty. Fives was already at the foot of the ramp in full kit, checking the charge on his sidearm for what was probably the fifth time. Two Kaminoan medical attendants had wheeled Tup aboard in a sealed pod an hour ago. Rex had watched from the gantry. Through the pod's small window Tup's face had looked very young and very peaceful, and Rex had not gone down.

"Captain."

"Fives."

They stood together at the bottom of the ramp. Around them the hangar went on with its morning: a gunship being hosed down, a load lifter beeping in reverse, two pilots arguing over a cracked canopy. Normal noise. It was a long time since anything had felt normal to Rex. He wasn't sure he'd notice anymore.

"Report every twelve hours," Rex said. "Standard check-in. I want to know what they find."

"Yes, sir."

"I mean it. Not just the findings. I want to know who you talk to. Who's in the room. What they say when they think you're not listening."

Fives turned his head and looked at him properly. "You think I'll need that."

"I think a field medic was ordered not to take a blood sample from a trooper who just killed a Jedi General," Rex said. "I think that's an odd thing to order. And I've been keeping count of odd things lately."

Fives didn't answer right away. He looked down at the dented helmet in his hands, rubbed his thumb across the crease in the brow.

"You know what I keep thinking about?" he said. "The lower levels. All those nights after she was sentenced. Every stall we went into, every broker, every lead. And the one time we got close—"

"Pesk."

"Pesk." Fives's mouth flattened. "Dead before the General got there. Not a mark on him. Hands folded. Cleaned out." He turned the helmet over. "Somebody tidied up after her, Captain. Somebody made sure the story stayed the story. And now Tup goes strange in the head and a day later there's an order from Kamino that nobody's allowed to look inside it. Maybe it's nothing. Maybe that's just the way they do things."

"Maybe."

"But you've been keeping count."

"I have."

Up the ramp, one of the Kaminoan attendants appeared in the hatch, tall and grey-white and patient, and inclined her long neck toward Fives. Time.

Fives put his helmet on. Then he took it off again. He stood there with it in both hands, and for a moment he looked less like an ARC trooper than like the stubborn cadet who used to ask the instructors *why* until they assigned him extra laps for it.

"Captain," he said. "If something happens on Kamino. If it goes wrong—if they decide it's easier to write Tup off, and anyone who argues with them along with him—"

"Fives."

"Let me say it." His voice was low and very steady. "I watched them take the Commander apart. In that chamber, in front of everyone. And she stood there and took it, because she trusted them to get it right in the end. She trusted the system all the way down to the bottom." He shook his head slowly. "I won't. If it comes to that, I won't go quietly. Not the way she had to."

Rex looked at him for a long moment.

"She didn't go quietly," he said. "She went with her head up. There's a difference."

"Not to the people who sent her," Fives said. "Not on paper."

Rex had no answer to that. He had spent three months looking for one.

So he did the only thing he had. He came to attention and saluted, and Fives saluted back, crisp and correct, the way they had both been made to do it ten thousand times on Kamino. Then Fives put his helmet on, and this time he left it there, and he went up the ramp without looking back.

The hatch sealed. The shuttle lifted on a whisper of repulsors, turned its smooth white nose toward the shield, and slid out of the hangar into the dark. Rex watched until the engine glow folded into hyperspace and was gone.

Jesse was standing behind him when he turned around. Rex didn't know how long he'd been there.

"Do we really think it's nothing, sir?"

"No," Rex said, and walked past him.

* * *

The datapad was old—a cadet-issue model, scuffed at the corners, with a crack across one end of its screen. Rex had kept it at the bottom of his footlocker since Kamino for no reason he'd ever been able to explain to himself. It still held a charge. That night, with his door locked, he took the back plate off with the point of his knife, found the little network chip, and prised it out. He held it up to the light for a second. Then he crushed it under the heel of his boot and swept the pieces into the waste chute.

Nothing on it now could be pulled by Fleet, or by anyone else. Nothing could be pushed onto it either.

He sat on his bunk with the pad on his knee and the cursor blinking, and for a long while he didn't write anything. Writing it down made it real. Writing it down meant he was the kind of trooper who kept a file on his own side.

Then he thought of Tup's hand coming away from his temple, smooth as a salute, and he began.

He didn't write what he felt. He wasn't sure he could have. He wrote it the way he'd write an after-action report, numbers and times, because that was the only language he trusted to hold still.

*Day 0. Commander Tano sentenced. Order to strike her from 501st roster received approx. one hour after sentencing. Not confirmed. Still not confirmed.*

*Same night. CT-5385 (Tup) reports headache, right temple. Declines scan. First occurrence on record.*

He stopped. He went back and changed *on record* to *that I know of*, because that was the truth, and the truth was the whole point.

*Day 1. Fox mentions Force-dampening collar to be fitted in flight. New device. Nobody asked why it was ready so fast.*

*Six weeks approx. Dalo Pesk, Kesh Arcade stall 41. Lead found by Fives. Pesk dead before General S. arrived. No marks. Stall cleaned. Seven datapads and cam core missing.*

*Weeks 1–13. Tup reports headaches, right temple, recurring. Skips two scans. I let it go.*

He read that last line twice. He left it.

*Ringo Vinda. Tup kills General Tiplar. No warning. No motive. Repeats "It was the order" until sedated. Does not resist arrest.*

*Same day. Order: no scans, no samples, no neural workup by field medics. Subject to be transferred unexamined to Kamino.*

He wanted the time of that order. He pulled up the *Resolute*'s medbay log on his own terminal, found the directive from Kamino where Kix had shown it to him, and copied the timestamp across by hand. Then, because it was a habit, he pulled up his own incident report to note when it had been filed.

He looked at the two times side by side.

He looked again.

His report—the first report of the incident from anyone on the station, the one he'd dictated with Tiplar's blood still drying on his gauntlets—had cleared the *Resolute*'s comm queue for Fleet at fourteen-twelve.

The directive from Kamino ordering that CT-5385 be kept sealed and unexamined had been logged by the medbay at fourteen-oh-three.

Nine minutes. Kamino had known what to forbid nine minutes before anyone had told them there was anything to forbid.

Rex sat very still. Somewhere in the decks below, the ship's engines changed pitch as the *Resolute* came about. He checked the times a third time. They didn't change.

Slowly, carefully, he typed it in. Then he reached for his comlink and keyed the secure channel he'd assigned to Fives for the check-ins, though it was hours too early, though there was nothing he could tell him yet except *watch your back*.

The channel opened. A voice answered—not Fives. Smooth and recorded and courteous.

*This transport is under medical quarantine by order of the Kamino Cloning Facility. External communications are suspended for the duration of transit. Thank you for your understanding.*

The link closed.

Rex sat on his bunk with the dead comlink in one hand and the cracked datapad in the other, in a locked room on a Republic warship among three thousand of his brothers, and listened to the hull hum around him. For the first time in his life, he could not have said whether the thing he was afraid of was out there, past the hull, or somewhere in here with them.

At the top of a new page, he typed: *CT-5555. Departed 0900. Comms cut.*

Then he sat looking at it, and waited for the check-in he already knew wasn't coming.

## Chapter 13: What Fives Found

Tup died at three-forty in the morning, Tipoca City time, with the rain drumming on the recovery ward's dome and Fives holding his hand through a sterile glove that was supposed to keep them both clean.

There was no noise to it. That was the part Fives would keep. On Ringo Vinda there had been blaster fire and the sun coming round and Tiplee's blade lighting across the hub, and here there was only the soft chime of a monitor shifting from amber to a color that didn't have a name, and the rain. For the last hour Tup had been somewhere far away behind his own eyes. Near the end they had opened, clear for the first time since the station, and found Fives, and for a moment Fives had thought he was going to say something. Tup had frowned, the way he used to frown at a sabacc hand he didn't understand. Then the frown smoothed out, and his fingers loosened, and the monitor sang its one long note.

The Kaminoans had told him it was a growth. They had told him with great gentleness that it had been removed, that the removal had been successful, that the patient's neural tissue had simply been too compromised to survive it. Regrettable. They used that word four times. Then two assistants in grey came with a gurney and a sealed sample canister no bigger than a ration tin, and they took Tup, and they took the canister, and Nala Se turned her long neck toward Fives and suggested he rest.

Fives went to find the droid.

AZI-3 was where he'd left it, in the diagnostic bay at the end of the quarantine wing, polishing instruments that were already clean. It was a slender medical unit with a bulbous head and a voice that apologized for things before they happened. Two days ago, when Fives had first stood over Tup's bed and said *something is wrong in his head, I don't care what Kamino ordered*, it had been the only thing in Tipoca that hadn't told him to calm down.

It had also run the scan. Unauthorized, unlogged, while Nala Se was in conference with the Prime Minister. Fives had watched the image build line by line on the screen: Tup's skull, Tup's brain, and in the right temporal lobe, exactly where he'd been pressing his fingers since the night of the verdict, a small dark shape with filaments trailing from it like roots. Not ragged, the way the training holos showed tumors. Neat. Deliberate. *Grown*, AZI-3 had said, and then, *oh dear, I shouldn't have said that.*

Two hours after that scan, Tup had been taken into surgery. Seven hours after that, he was dead.

"He's gone," Fives said now.

The droid set down the forceps it was holding. "I know. I monitor the ward. I am very sorry, CT-5555."

"Fives."

"Fives. I am very sorry."

Fives climbed onto the examination table and lay back. The ceiling lights were too white. "Scan me."

"I am not authorized to—"

"You weren't authorized last time either. Don't log it."

AZI-3 hesitated for a long, humming second. Then the scanner arm swung over him, and the light ran down from his forehead to his chin, and Fives lay very still and listened to the rain and did not let himself think about anything at all.

The screen built its picture. Fives turned his head to look.

There it was. Right temporal lobe. The same small dark shape, the same delicate roots. Quiet. Not swollen like Tup's had been at the end, not inflamed, not anything. Just there, the way it had evidently been there his whole life.

"It's in me," he said.

"Yes." AZI-3's voice had gone very small. "I took the liberty—while the scan was running—of checking the embryonic records. The structure is introduced at an early developmental stage. It is in the template. It is in every one of you."

Every one of you. Rex. Jesse. Kix, who counted bacta patches when he was upset. Echo, if anything of Echo was left. Hardcase, buried on Umbara with this thing still curled in his skull. Three million brothers walking around with a stranger living behind their right eye.

"What does it do?"

"I don't know. I truly don't. It interfaces with the regions that govern decision and impulse. In your brother it was—" The droid stopped. "It had become very active."

Fives thought of Tup on the hub, the sun sliding round, the rifle coming up without any hurry at all. Tup saying the same words over and over afterward, flat, like a recording. *It was the order.* Like something had reached into him and pulled a lever.

"Take it out," he said.

"The procedure killed—"

"Tup was already sick. His was already running. Mine's asleep." He sat up. His hands were steady, which surprised him. "If I go back out that door with this still in me, they'll put me in a ward next to him by morning and call it a virus. You know they will. Take it out, and give it to me, and then at least one of us walks out of here knowing what it is."

The droid did not answer at once. Then it went to a cabinet and began laying out instruments on a tray, small precise ones, and said in a thin voice, "You will need to stay awake. I'm afraid it will be unpleasant."

It was unpleasant. Fives would not remember most of it afterward—only the cold of the anesthetic on the side of his head, a pressure that went on and on, the smell of something hot and clean, and a strange moment near the end when the room tilted and he felt, very clearly, a door somewhere inside him swing shut that he hadn't known was open. Then AZI-3 was holding up a sample vial to the light, and inside it, in a bead of clear fluid, was a grey-pink thing the size of a fingernail clipping, with filaments drifting from it like hair in water.

"That's it?" Fives said. His voice sounded strange to him. "That's all?"

"That's all."

He took the vial and held it in his palm and looked at it for a long while. He'd expected to feel something. Rage, maybe. Instead he felt like a man who had found the hand of a stranger resting on his shoulder and didn't know how long it had been there.

They came at dawn, and they came smiling.

Lama Su did most of the talking. The Prime Minister stood very tall in the doorway of the diagnostic bay, with Nala Se at his shoulder and two Kaminoan technicians behind, and explained in that unhurried, musical voice that an unauthorized procedure had been performed, that the droid would be wiped, that CT-5555 had clearly been exposed to the same parasitic agent that had damaged CT-5385, and that it was a credit to Kamino's diligence that the infection had been caught in time. The trooper would of course be confined for observation. The sample would of course be surrendered.

"It's not a parasite," Fives said. "It's in the template. Your droid checked."

"The droid is malfunctioning," Nala Se said.

"The droid's the only thing in this city that's told me the truth."

He had the vial in his fist and his back to the wall and no weapon, and he was working out which of the technicians to go through first when Master Shaak Ti arrived.

She came in quietly, the way she did everything, robes the color of the storm outside, and she looked at Fives, and at the vial, and at the bandage on the side of his head. She didn't tell him to calm down. That was the first thing he noticed. She asked him what he had found, and she listened while he told her, all of it, Tup and the scan and the embryo records, his voice going ragged in places. When he was done she turned to Lama Su and said, quite mildly, that she would like to see those records herself.

There followed a conversation conducted entirely in courtesy, in which nobody raised their voice and Fives understood maybe a third of what was really being said. At the end of it, Lama Su informed them that the Supreme Chancellor's office, having been made aware of the incident, had requested that the trooper be transported to Coruscant for examination by the Republic's own medical authorities. Nala Se would accompany him. Master Ti might observe.

"The Chancellor will hear you," Shaak Ti told Fives afterward, in the corridor, while the Kaminoans prepared the shuttle. "I'll see to that. You have my word."

Fives nodded, and thanked her, and meant it. He liked her. She'd looked at him like a man and not a malfunction.

But he had been in the barracks the night of the verdict. He'd thrown his helmet into a bulkhead hard enough to dent it, and he had never had the dent fixed, because every time he put the helmet on he wanted to remember. Commander Tano had been promised she'd be heard too. She'd been given a hearing, and counsel, and a tribunal with all the right furniture, and she'd walked into it believing that if she told the truth plainly enough the truth would hold her up.
