They'd put a collar on her and sent her to the ice. Rex had stood at Platform Six and watched the transport go, and come back to the barracks without a word.

You didn't hand the only copy of the truth to the system and trust it to give it back. She'd taught him that, without ever meaning to.

He went back to the diagnostic bay. The technicians hadn't come for AZI-3 yet. The droid was standing perfectly still in the corner, the way droids stood when they had been told what was going to happen to them.

"I need you to send something," Fives said. "Before they wipe you."

"I am not—"

"Authorized. I know."

They did it in nine minutes. Everything AZI-3 had: Tup's scan, Fives's scan, the embryonic template records, the droid's own analysis, high-resolution images of the chip in its vial. At the end, Fives recorded forty seconds of himself, bandage and all, speaking into the droid's optical sensor in a low fast voice. *Rex. It's in all of us. It's what made Tup do it. They're calling it a virus and they're taking me to the Chancellor. If I come back, good. If I don't, and they tell you I turned—don't you believe it. Don't take my word for any of this either. Get Kix. Look for yourself.*

AZI-3 folded it all into a routine medical telemetry packet addressed to the *Resolute*'s medbay, attention Captain Rex, sealed it with the cipher from the check-in channel Rex had given Fives at the shuttle ramp, and fired it off in a single compressed burst on the hospital's outbound relay, sandwiched between a growth-rate report and a requisition for nutrient gel.

"It's gone," the droid said. "I don't know whether it will arrive."

"It'll arrive." Fives put a hand on the droid's cold shoulder. "Thank you."

"I hope you are right about everything," AZI-3 said. "And I hope you are wrong about what they'll do to you."

* * *

On Coruscant they put him in a medical suite three floors under the Chancellor's offices, a white room with no window and a cot that smelled of disinfectant. Nala Se gave him an injection for the transit sickness, she said, though he hadn't been sick. Shaak Ti was asked to wait in the anteroom. Two Coruscant Guard troopers in red-marked armor stood outside the door. Fives sat on the cot with the vial in the breast pocket of his fatigues, the pocket buttoned, his hand over it like a man protecting a heartbeat, and waited.

The drug came on slowly. That was the clever thing about it. First his hands felt heavy, then his tongue. By the time the troopers came to walk him upstairs, the corridors had acquired a faint shimmer at the edges, as if everything were being seen through warm water, and he had to think about each step before he took it.

The Chancellor's office was red. He'd heard that from Rex, who'd heard it from General Skywalker, and it was still a shock: red carpet, red walls, red light through the tall windows at the end of the day. It was warm in there. Too warm. Fives felt sweat start under his collar.

Palpatine came around the desk to meet him. He was smaller than Fives had expected, and older, with a soft voice and a soft handshake, and he said Fives's number and then, kindly, his name, as if he'd taken the trouble to learn it. He thanked him for his service. He said he had heard about Ringo Vinda and was grieved. He said Fives must be very tired.

"Sir." Fives had to stop and line the words up. They kept sliding. "Sir, there's something—in us. In all of us. Kamino put it there. I've got it. I've got mine." He fumbled the pocket open and set the vial on the edge of the desk, and it rolled a little, and the grey thing inside it turned slowly in its fluid. "It made Tup kill a Jedi. I don't know who it answers to. But it isn't a virus, sir, it isn't, it was grown."

The Chancellor looked at the vial. He picked it up between two fingers and held it to the window, and the red light came through it.

"How extraordinary," he said. "And who else has had the privilege of seeing this?"

Something in Fives, some animal thing underneath the drug, went still.

He had told Shaak Ti. He had told the droid, and the droid was being wiped. He'd sent everything to Rex. The true answer was a list of names, and the question had been asked so gently, and he didn't want to give it.

"Sir," he said, "I'd like Master Ti to be here."

"Of course," said the Chancellor. He set the vial down on his side of the desk. Then he stepped back, quite calmly, and touched something on the desk's edge, and raised his voice for the first time. "Trooper—no. *No.* Put that down!"

Fives hadn't moved.

He was sure, afterward, that he hadn't moved—but the drug made the room lurch, and his hand was on the desk to steady himself, and the doors were opening behind him, and in the red window glass he saw his own reflection leaning over the desk at the Chancellor with his arm out, and it looked exactly like an attack. It looked exactly like a man going for the throat of the head of state.

The Chancellor's face, over his shoulder, was not afraid. That was the last clear thing Fives saw before the training took over. It was watching him the way you'd watch a game piece slide into the square you'd left open for it.

Then the troopers had him, and he did what a lifetime had made him do. One went into the desk; one went down with his wind gone. Fives came up with a blaster that wasn't his and a red-marked helmet rolling at his feet. Somewhere Shaak Ti was saying his name sharply. Somewhere an alarm had started. The vial was on the far side of the desk, in the Chancellor's reach and not his, and there were boots in the corridor, a lot of them.

He left it. He hated himself for that for the rest of the night, but he left it. He took the downed trooper's comlink off his belt, jammed it into his collar, and went out the side door at a staggering run.

* * *

The comlink told him what he was before he'd made it four levels down.

*All units, all units. Clone trooper CT-5555 has attempted to assassinate the Supreme Chancellor. Suspect is armed and considered deranged. Coruscant Guard has authority to use lethal force.*

He heard it in a maintenance lift, with his back against the wall and his eyes closed and the blaster cradled against his chest. Deranged. He'd been a traitor to Kamino for a night and now he was a madman to the whole Republic, and it had taken them about eight minutes. He thought of Commander Tano in the detention center, with three dead Guards outside her cell and her own sabers in the evidence locker, and he understood now what that had felt like from the inside. Not the fear. The speed. The way the story was already written before you'd even caught your breath.

He went down.

The drug was thinning, but slowly, and he moved through the lower levels in fits—a stretch of clear, cold sense, then a stretch where the neon swam and the walls leaned in. It was raining down here, the dirty runoff rain that fell from a thousand levels up and arrived warm and smelling of metal. Speeders hissed past overhead. A Toydarian vendor shouted at him for walking through his awning. He kept his head down and his arm across his chest and walked like a drunk, which wasn't hard.

He passed 79's without meaning to.

He came around a corner and there it was across the plaza, the light in the windows, the number over the door. Through the glass he could see a table of troopers with their helmets lined up beside their drinks—shore leave, off-duty, laughing at something one of them had said. One of them had a 501st marking. Fives didn't know him. He stood in the rain on the far side of the plaza and looked at them, three or four brothers, with that thing curled asleep behind every one of their right eyes, and none of them knew.

He could go in. He could stand on a table and tell them.

And they'd hear it on their comlinks inside a minute, *armed and deranged*, and they'd look at the bandage on his head and the stolen blaster in his hand, and they'd believe the comlink. He would have. Yesterday, he would have.

He turned away from the window and kept walking.

He knew where he was going, though he didn't let himself think about it until he was nearly there. The old munitions district. Rex had walked him through it street by street after the trial, the two of them in civilian coats, retracing the night Commander Tano had been run to ground: where she'd crossed the transit line, which alley Ventress had used, the warehouse with the nano-droid crates where they'd found her. Rex wanted to know if anything had been missed. Fives had wanted to know how a person ended up cornered. You learned a district fast when you were looking at it with that question.

Two blocks from her warehouse there was another one, older, an abandoned coolant refinery with half its roof gone and the floor of its main hall made of open grating, because underneath it was the sump—a drainage shaft that went down farther than anyone had ever bothered to measure. He and Rex had stood on that grating and dropped a bolt through it and listened, and never heard it land.

He went in through the loading bay. Rain came through the broken roof in long silver threads. He found a corner where he could see both doors, slid down the wall onto the grating, and set the blaster across his knees.

He didn't have the chip anymore. He didn't have anything, except a bandage, a blaster that wasn't his, and a burst of data somewhere in the fleet's medical traffic that might or might not ever be opened.

It would have to be enough. He sat and shook, and waited, and didn't know for what.

The footsteps, when they came, were only two sets.

"Fives."

He knew the voice before he knew he'd raised the blaster. He lowered it. Rex stood in the loading bay door, helmet under his arm, rain running off his pauldrons. Behind him, a darker shape, cloak soaked, hands empty: General Skywalker.

"Knew you'd come here," Rex said quietly. "You always liked this place. Gloomiest building in the district."

Fives laughed, or something did. It hurt his head. "Did you get it?"

"I got it." Rex came a few steps in, slow, careful on the grating. "I've got a medical file I can't read without Kix and forty seconds of you looking like death. I've played it eleven times."

"Then you know."

"I know you believe it."

"That's not—" Fives pushed himself up the wall to his feet. His legs held, mostly. "Rex. It's real. It's in your head right now. Right there." He touched his own temple, beside the bandage. "Tup didn't turn. Something turned him. And I went to the Chancellor, I did it properly, I did everything right, and he—"

"Careful," General Skywalker said.

It wasn't a threat. It was almost gentle. Fives looked at him and saw a man who hadn't slept in a long time, gloved hands half-closed, something stretched very thin across his face.

"Sir, I didn't touch him. I swear on Tup, I didn't lay a finger on him. They drugged me. Nala Se drugged me and he set it up, he *wanted* me to—"

"The Chancellor," Skywalker said, slowly, "is the only person in this whole government who lifted a finger for Ahsoka. The *only* one. Do you understand me? When the Council threw her out, when the Senate wanted her dead—"

"I know what he did for her, sir."

"Then don't tell me he—"

"I'm telling you what happened." Fives's voice cracked across the hall. "That's all I've got. That's all she had, too. Nobody believed her either."

Skywalker flinched. It was small—a hitch in his shoulders, as if the words had landed somewhere already bruised. For a moment nobody said anything, and the rain kept falling through the roof.

"Come in with us," Rex said. "Right now. Me and the General. Not Fox. We walk you in, we get Kix to look at those scans, we get Master Ti to say what she saw, we do it in the open where there are too many people watching for anything to—"

"That's what she did," Fives said. "She walked in."

Rex stopped.

"You remember. You were there. She told the truth, and she sat in a chair, and she waited for them to believe her. Where is she now, Rex?"

Rex didn't answer. He didn't have to. Fives watched it go through him—watched the captain's jaw set, and his eyes drop for a moment to the grating, and come back up with something in them that hadn't been there when he came in.

"Give me the blaster," Rex said. "Just the blaster. Then we'll talk about the rest. Brother. Please."

Fives looked down at the weapon in his hand. He didn't want it. He'd never wanted it. He began to turn it, to offer it grip-first—

Light flooded the hall.

They came through both doors at once, red-marked armor, a full squad, rifles up. Floodlights on the shoulders turned the rain into falling sparks. At the center of the line was Commander Fox, the red on his helmet very bright, his pistol already level.

"CT-5555," Fox said, loud and flat, the voice of a man reading from a card. "Drop the weapon."

"Fox, stand down," Skywalker snapped. "We have this—"

"With respect, General, you don't. I have orders from the Chancellor's office." The pistol didn't waver. "Drop it, trooper."

Fives's hand was already open. The blaster was turning in it, grip outward, falling. It was halfway to the grating when he saw Fox's finger begin to tighten. He saw it the way you see things in the last half-second of a fight, very clear and very slow: the finger, the barrel, the line from the barrel to the center of his own chest.

And he saw Rex move.

Rex didn't shout. He just went—one long lunge across the grating, helmet flying out of his hand, shoulder driving into Fox's outstretched arm a fraction of an instant before the shot.

The bolt took Fives high in the left shoulder instead of the heart.

It didn't hurt at first. It was more like being shoved very hard by someone he couldn't see. He went back a step, then another, and his boot came down on the grating at the edge of the hall where the rain had been eating it for decades, and the grating wasn't there.

It gave with a groan like an old ship. He felt it fold under him. His hand went out and closed on nothing.

The last thing he saw from the top of the world was Rex: on his knees on the grating with Fox's arm still pinned under him, helmet gone, face naked in the floodlight, staring straight at him. Rex's mouth was open. His hand was out across the gap, reaching. He was too far away. He'd always been going to be too far away.

Then the hall went up and away from Fives like a light pulled up a well, and he was falling into the dark.

* * *

He hit things.

Pipes, mostly. A cable that caught him across the chest and spun him and let him go. Something that rang like a bell when he struck it. Then a long nothing that went on far too long, and the cold of falling air, and the floodlights shrinking to a pale coin above him and then to a star and then to nothing.

Then water. Or something like water—thick, cold, stinking of coolant and rust—and he went under it and came up and went under it again, and his left arm wouldn't work at all, and he got his right arm over something hard and round and hung there in the black, breathing.

He didn't know how far down he was. Farther than the bolt he and Rex had dropped. Farther than anyone had ever bothered to measure.

Somewhere above him, faintly, a voice was shouting his name. Not his number. His name. The shaft took the sound and bent it and smeared it until it was only a shape, and then it stopped.

His shoulder had begun, at last, to hurt. He was very cold. The cold was coming into him through the wound, he thought, like water into a breached hull. He couldn't feel his left hand. He could feel the comlink jammed in his collar, though, pressed against his jaw, still somehow alive. It clicked, and hissed, and spoke.

*Fox to all units. Stand down the search. The fugitive is dead.*

A pause. Static. Somebody saying something Fives couldn't make out.

*Negative. He went down the refinery sump with a bolt in him. There's no bottom on our scanners. Log the body as unrecoverable.*

Another pause. Then, quieter, as if Fox had turned away from the pickup: *General Skywalker, I'm sorry. Captain, get off the edge. Captain—that's an order. Get him off the edge.*

The comlink clicked once more and went silent.

Fives hung in the dark and listened to the silence where it had been. Dead. Unrecoverable. Already in the log. He thought, from very far off, that he ought to be angry, and found he didn't have anything left to be angry with. He thought about Tup's frown at the end, puzzled, as if at a hand he didn't understand. He thought about a grey-pink thing turning slowly in a red window. He thought about Rex on his knees with his hand out. *I got it. I've played it eleven times.*

Rex had it. That was something. Rex had all of it.

The water moved against him, slow, pulling. His fingers were slipping on the pipe. He told them not to. He wasn't sure they heard.

Somewhere far above, the last of the floodlight went out, and the dark closed over the shaft like a hand, and Fives, CT-5555, ARC trooper of the 501st, officially deceased, let go of the one thought he had left and did not know, as the cold came up to meet him, whether he had let go of the pipe as well.

## Chapter 14: Narrow Paths

When the official report reached Anakin, Fives had been dead for nineteen hours. According to the report he had also been sick for weeks.

It said so in the second paragraph. A neurological infection, picked up during the Ringo Vinda engagement, the same one that had broken CT-5385. Aggressive and rare. Its symptoms were paranoia, fixation and sudden violence. The Kaminoan medical authority regretted that it hadn't been identified sooner. Every other trooper of the 501st had been screened and was clear. The matter was closed.

Anakin read it standing in the Chancellor's outer office, because he couldn't make himself sit, and when he reached the end he started again from the top. There was still grit under the nails of his left hand from the refinery grating. He had washed twice. It hadn't come out.

"General Skywalker." Dace Orrin stood at the inner door. He was rubbing his left wrist, the way a man touches an old injury that no longer hurts. "The Chancellor will see you now."

The office was red and too warm, as it always was. The Chancellor wasn't at his desk. He stood at the tall window with his hands folded behind him, watching the traffic lanes slide past in the grey morning, and he didn't turn around right away. When he did, Anakin saw that he looked older than he had a week ago. A faint yellow bruise showed on the back of one hand, where somebody had steadied him, or failed to.

"You've read it," Palpatine said.

"Yes, sir."

"I asked them to send it to you first, before the Holonet had it. You were there. You shouldn't have to hear it from a newsreader." He came away from the window and lowered himself into one of the low chairs by the fire-screen, and after a moment Anakin sat too, because it would have been strange not to. "Please tell me you've slept."

"Some."

"Liar," the Chancellor said kindly.

Anakin almost smiled. Then he didn't. He looked down at the datapad on his knee. *Aggressive. Rare.* A virus didn't need anyone to have been wrong. It was something that happened, like weather or a bad landing. It had happened to Tup, and Tup had been sick, everyone had seen it, the hand at his temple for days and the headaches he blamed on the lights. Then it had happened to Fives, who went to Kamino as an escort and came back as something the Coruscant Guard hunted through the lower levels with orders to fire. Nobody had chosen any of it. No one had failed to see it. It had just come.

He wanted that so badly his chest hurt.

"The Kaminoans tell me it attacks the parts of the mind that govern loyalty," Palpatine said. "I don't pretend to understand the medicine. But I keep thinking about the moment he came through that door." He looked at the door, a plain panel of dark wood, and Anakin looked with him. "I've had people come at me before, Anakin. It's rather a hazard of the office. They look at you the way a slicer looks at a lock: a problem in their way. He didn't look at me like that. He looked at me as though I had broken a promise to him personally." He was quiet a moment. "I suppose that's what the illness does. It finds the deepest loyalty a man has and turns it inside out. There's nothing quite so painful as when the loyal turn out to be false."

The fire-screen ticked as it warmed.

Anakin didn't move. Inside the glove, his mechanical fingers had closed on the edge of the datapad, and he heard the casing creak before he felt it. He made the fingers open one at a time.

Palpatine saw. Anakin watched him see it. The Chancellor's face changed, the warmth going out of it and something like dismay coming in.

"Oh," the Chancellor said. "Oh, my boy. Forgive me. That was thoughtless. I wasn't speaking of her." He leaned forward and put his bruised hand briefly on Anakin's sleeve. "You know where I stand on that matter. I sat in that chamber and listened to Tarkin ask for her life, and I couldn't allow it. I wouldn't have done what I did if I believed she was some kind of traitor. That was a clumsy remark from a tired old man, and I'm sorry for it."

"It's all right."

"It isn't. But you're generous to say so."

Anakin couldn't remember the last time anyone had apologized to him. He tried to think of the last time and couldn't find it. Not the Council. Not Obi-Wan. Ahsoka had, once, for something small, a lost hydrospanner on Christophsis, and he had told her to forget it, and now he couldn't forget it himself.

"Did he say anything?" Palpatine asked. "At the end. I only ask because the delusions can be persuasive. I'm told they often come with a whole story attached. I'd hate for anyone in your legion to carry it around afterward."

Anakin was back in the refinery hall: the floodlights, the rain coming through the broken roof, Fives with his hands open and empty, talking too fast. Something in their heads. Something that had been put there before any of them were born. Orders they didn't know they had. *General, please, you have to listen. You of all people. You know what it's like when they've already decided.*

Then Fox's arm coming up. Rex's shoulder knocking it sideways. Then the bolt, and the grating, and the dark.

"Nothing that made sense," Anakin said.

"No," said Palpatine. "I don't suppose it did."

He didn't mention Rex's lunge. He hadn't put it in his own statement, and he had read Fox's report twice and seen that Fox hadn't put it in his either. Two commanders had left the same three seconds out of the record without saying a word to each other. Anakin told himself it didn't matter. Fives was down a shaft with no bottom on the scanners, and nothing in a report would change that. It would only cost Rex.

That was the reason, and it was a good one. He didn't look too closely at whether it was the only one.

"Go and rest," Palpatine said as he rose. "And Anakin, have you had any word from Narkis?"

"Not since my visit."

"Then I'll make some quiet inquiries. I'd like to know she's warm." He smiled, and the smile was so tired and so plainly fond that Anakin felt it in his throat. "Someone ought to be asking."

* * *

The rebuilt hangar still smelled new. Anakin hated it for that. The durasteel was too clean and the deck plating too bright, and the memorial wall by the east doors was polished so well you could see yourself standing in front of the names. Tutso Mara. Five other Jedi. The clones and the workers, listed in smaller letters underneath. Every time he came in here he read them, and every time he caught his own face looking back over them.

He had his interceptor's port stabilizer open and was retorquing a coupling that didn't need it. R2 sat beside the strut, whistling in a low, disapproving way every time the hydrospanner went round again.

"I know," Anakin said. "I heard you."

R2 whistled again.

"Then go and tell someone who cares."

The droid rotated its dome away from him, rolled three meters, stopped, and stayed exactly there. Anakin put the spanner on the coupling and turned it until it wouldn't go any further.

He heard Obi-Wan before he saw him. Obi-Wan had a way of walking in hangars, careful of cables, as if he expected the deck to do something treacherous. It used to make Anakin laugh.

"Rex filed his report this morning," Obi-Wan said. "It was very short."

"Rex's reports are always short."

"Not this short." Obi-Wan stopped on the other side of the stabilizer. Through the open access panel Anakin could see the hem of his robe and one boot. "I'm sorry about Fives. I know what the 501st means to you."

"Do you." Anakin didn't look up. "That's good of you. Did the Council send you, or did you come on your own? I'd like to know which version of you I'm talking to."

"Anakin."

"Because I'd guess the Council's relieved. It's very neat. A sick clone, a sick trooper before him, a rare infection and a report from Kamino. Nobody's fault." The spanner slipped off the coupling and rang against the strut. "You must love that. A verdict where nobody has to be guilty. Maybe you could have used one of those three months ago."

He heard Obi-Wan breathe in, slow, the way he did before saying something he had decided to say whatever it cost him.

"I don't believe she did it," Obi-Wan said.

Anakin stood up so fast that he hit his head on the stabilizer housing. He didn't feel it. He came round the strut, and Obi-Wan was standing very still in his plain brown robe with his hands tucked into the sleeves, looking at him with no particular expression at all.

"Say that again."

"I don't believe Ahsoka bombed this hangar, or killed Letta Turmond, or those three troopers." Obi-Wan kept his voice low. Across the hangar, two techs had stopped work on a gunship and were finding reasons to stay near it. "I've read the tribunal transcript more times than I'd care to admit. It's an elegant case, Tarkin's best work. Every piece fits. That's what troubles me. In my experience the truth is untidy. It has loose ends and things that don't fit and witnesses who remember the wrong colors. Tarkin's case had none of that. Somebody built it."

The deck seemed to tilt a little under Anakin's boots, the way it had in the Dovrin gallery when the stack began to lean.

"When?" he said.

"When what?"

"When did you stop believing it?"

Obi-Wan was silent for a moment. "I'm not certain I ever fully did."

That was the worst thing he could have said. Anakin felt it go through him like the plasma had on Ordaine, a white line of heat from one side to the other. For a second he couldn't speak at all.

"You sat in the gallery," he said at last. "For three days. I saw you. Every time I turned around, there you were with your hands in your sleeves, exactly like that. And you never stood up. Not once."

"I wasn't called."

"You didn't need to be called. You're on the Council. You argued against expelling her, I was there for that too, and then you lost the vote and you just *let it happen*. You let them take her beads. You let them put her in front of Tarkin. And then you sat and watched him ask for her life, and the whole time you didn't believe a word of it?"

"Yes," Obi-Wan said.

He didn't argue or explain. He just said it, and the plainness of it made Anakin want to put his fist through the memorial wall.

"Then go and say it now," Anakin said. "Go and say it to Windu. Say it to the Senate. Say it to the Holonet, I don't care."

"Say what, exactly? That I have a feeling? That the evidence is too neat?" Obi-Wan's mouth tightened. "Tarkin would thank me for it. He'd have it read into the record as proof that the Jedi refuse to accept a lawful verdict on one of their own. It would cost her the last friends she has in the Senate, and it would not open a single door on Narkis. I've thought about it, Anakin. Believe me, I've thought of very little else."

"Then think less and *do* something."

"Like you?" Obi-Wan said quietly.

Anakin went still.

Obi-Wan didn't press it. He just looked at him for a long moment, the way he had looked at Anakin when he was nine and had broken something and was standing in front of the pieces. "If there's anything you know that I don't," Obi-Wan said, "anything at all, I would very much like to hear it. I'm not asking as a member of the Council."

Anakin remembered the masked figure on the catwalk above the Dovrin platform, the turn it had made coming out of his overhead strike. That pivot was taught in the Temple's third-year training halls to children not much bigger than the younglings who had once followed Ahsoka around on Ilum. He had seen it, and he had told Fox, and Tarkin, and the whole tribunal, that he hadn't recognized the style. He had kept his silence too. He had his reasons, and they had seemed good. That made it worse.

"I know she's on an ice moon wearing a collar," he said. "That's what I know."

He picked up the hydrospanner from the deck, ducked back under the stabilizer, and fitted it to a coupling that didn't need it. After a while Obi-Wan's boot went away. R2 rolled back, slowly, and settled beside the strut without a sound.

* * *

The ice was blue, and she was under it.

He knew it was Narkis, though he had only ever seen one grey room on Ring One. In the dream there were no rooms. There was the ice cut, terrace after terrace going down into blue twilight, and Ahsoka was beneath the surface of it the way a leaf is under a frozen pond. She lay on her back with her eyes open and her hands flat against the underside, as if she had been pushing.

The collar's status light glowed at her throat. That was how he found her. It was the only warm color anywhere, and it was blue too.

He went down on his knees and put his hands on the ice. The left one burned with cold. The right one felt nothing, and then it locked: the fingers curled and seized the way they had at Dovrin with the whole hab stack hanging off them, and he couldn't open them. He tried to reach for her through the Force, and the Force was there, it was everywhere, roaring, but where she should have been there was nothing at all. It was like reaching for a stair in the dark that isn't there.

She looked up at him through the ice. Her lips moved. He thought she said *Skyguy*. She had called him that only once since the hangar, in a grey room on Ring One, to make him promise.

Then the ice began to thicken. He could see it happen, a slow white cloud blooming inward from the edges, closing over her like a lid. Somewhere behind him a man was falling down a shaft and calling out, and Anakin couldn't turn around because if he looked away from her she would be gone, and he couldn't look at her either, because the light at her throat was going out.

It went out.

He woke with his right hand closed so hard on the sheet that the fabric had torn.

The bedroom at 500 Republica was dark except for the slow traffic lights crossing the ceiling. For a moment he didn't know where he was. Then Padmé's hand was on his chest, and her voice was low and close, saying his name, the name only she used.

"Ani. Ani. You're here."

"I know." His voice came out wrong. He tried again. "I know."

She found his right hand in the dark. She didn't try to pull it off the sheet. She worked her fingers in beside his and began to open the mechanical fingers one joint at a time, patiently, as if she'd done it before. She had.

"Was it Fives?"

"No." He stared at the ceiling. A speeder's running lights passed over it, blue, then gone. "It was her."

Padmé's hand stopped, then went on.

"She was under the ice," he said. "In the yard. I could see her. I could see the light on the collar, it's the only thing that shows down there, and I couldn't..." He stopped. "It went out."

"It was a dream."

"I've had dreams that weren't."

She didn't answer that. She knew what had happened on Tatooine. She was one of two people alive who did. She lay down against his shoulder instead, careful of the burn along his ribs, and for a while they listened to the city.

"I saw the Chancellor this morning," he said.

"I know. It was on the Holonet. 'Chancellor shaken but unharmed.'" She was choosing her words; he could hear her doing it. "How was he?"

"Kind." Anakin turned his head on the pillow. "He said a strange thing. He was talking about Fives, about how the illness turns loyalty inside out. And he said there was nothing as painful as when the loyal turn out to be false. And then he saw my face and he apologized. He said he hadn't meant her."

Padmé was quiet for a long time.

"He compared her to a trooper who tried to kill him," she said.

"He apologized."

"That isn't what I said."

"He's the one who kept her alive, Padmé. He's the only one in this whole city who's even asking whether she's warm." His voice had risen. He brought it down. "He's an old man. He'd been attacked. He said a careless thing."

"Yes," she said. "Maybe he did."

He knew that tone. It was the one she used in committee when she had stopped arguing and started taking notes. He thought about telling her the rest: the three seconds on the grating, Rex's shoulder in Fox's arm, the bolt going wide of where it should have hit and the dark swallowing what was left. Obi-Wan's *Like you?* The Soresu turn on the catwalk. He thought about it for so long that her breathing began to slow against his shoulder, and he still hadn't said anything.

"I'm going to ask for another permit," he said instead.

Her breathing changed. She wasn't asleep after all.

"She asked you not to come, Ani."

"She asked me not to do anything stupid, too."

"And what do you call this?"

He didn't have an answer. He lay with his hand open on the torn sheet and watched the lights cross the ceiling. Every so often a blue one went by, the same blue as the collar, and every time it vanished at the edge of the window he caught himself holding his breath until the next one came.

* * *

On Serenno it was raining, the soft, persistent rain that came down out of the hills every autumn. Count Dooku had liked it since he was a boy.

He sat in the long gallery with a glass of Serenno red he had not yet touched and watched the Republic talk to itself.

The Holonet feed was one of the Core's sober ones, which meant the anchor wore dark grey and paused before anything she considered important. Tonight the important thing was a Senate Judiciary subcommittee hearing on what the Republic was pleased to call *restraint devices for Force-sensitive detainees*. A senator from somewhere in the Mid Rim was asking, with commendable indignation, whether the devices had been adequately tested before their first long-term use. A Judiciary official was answering that they had, and that the subject was doing well.

Then they ran the file footage of the subject, as he had known they would.

It was the sentencing. He had watched it at the time, idly, in a dull week. The tribunal chamber, the senators in their tiers, the Chancellor in his high seat pronouncing death in a voice of great sorrow. Then came the pause, and the clemency, and the girl's face. She had stood through *death* without moving. At *life* she broke.

He froze the image there and sat looking at it while the rain ran down the tall windows.

Ahsoka Tano. Seventeen, or near it. Togruta. Trained since childhood by the Temple, and for the last several years by Anakin Skywalker personally, which meant she had learned to fight from the most dangerous and least disciplined swordsman the Order had produced in a generation. Dooku had crossed blades with Skywalker more often than he cared to count. Whatever the boy had poured into her, she had survived it, and the reports from a dozen campaigns suggested she had sharpened it. She had held a commander's rank since she was fourteen and had a legion's loyalty. Then the Order she had given her life to put her on a table for the Republic to carve.

He knew something about being handed back by the Jedi. He had at least walked out on his own.

Then the feed moved on to a clone trooper who had attacked the Chancellor, and some illness out of Kamino, and the anchor's careful voice wondering whether public confidence in the Grand Army had been shaken by two such incidents in so short a time. Dooku let it play without listening. Clones did not interest him. Clones were the Republic's to break.

The girl did interest him.

He had tried this twice before. Neither attempt was a thing he liked to remember. There had been Ventress, gifted and furious and devoted, whom he had been obliged to cast off at the moment she was becoming truly useful, because his Master had asked it and Dooku had not yet been ready to refuse him anything. Then there had been the Nightsister's brute, Savage, who was all strength and no mind, and who had told Dooku, more clearly than any failure in his life, that raw power was a poor foundation. What he needed was not a weapon. He had weapons, whole factories of them. He needed a student: a Force-user with the discipline of the Temple and none of its faith, someone who already knew that the Jedi would sacrifice anyone who became inconvenient, because she had been sacrificed herself.

He needed someone his Master did not know existed.

Dooku turned that thought over carefully, the way a man handles a blade he has just finished sharpening. Even in the privacy of his own mind there were things he had learned to think quietly. His Master saw a great deal. His Master took an interest in Skywalker that Dooku had never been invited to share and had never asked about. But a convicted girl on an ice moon at the edge of Wild Space, a girl the Republic itself had locked away and forgotten, was beneath anyone's notice. That was precisely her value.

She would hate him, of course, at first. Ventress had hated him at first, and so had he once hated the man who taught him. Hatred was not an obstacle. It was material. What mattered was what she would come to understand, given time and the right instruction: the Republic had needed her guilty, the Jedi had let it, and only one man in the galaxy had troubled to come for her.

He set down the untouched glass, rose, and went to the small console at the end of the gallery. It was not connected to any Separatist network or to his Master's channels. Its relays ran through a series of commercial beacons in Hutt space that had cost him a great deal and had never once been used.

The call took some time to connect. When it did, the hologram that rose over the console was blue and slightly unsteady. The broad brim of the hat came first, then the long narrow face under it. The breathing tubes at the cheeks hissed faintly in the transmission. In the blue of the holo Bane's eyes were only darker points of light, but Dooku knew their color well enough.

"Count," said Cad Bane. "Been a while. Figured you'd forgot my frequency."

"I forget very little, Bane."

"That a fact." Somewhere behind the bounty hunter a droid chattered and was ignored. "This a Confederacy job? 'Cause their rates went down last time I checked, and mine didn't."

"This is a private matter. It will remain one." Dooku touched a control, and beside Bane's flickering figure the frozen sentencing image rose: the girl's face, the collar not yet on her throat. "Ahsoka Tano. Convicted three months ago of the Temple hangar bombing and several murders. She is serving a life sentence in the Republic Penal Facility on Narkis. It is the ninth such facility, on an ice moon past the edge of the charted lanes. The inmates call it the Deep."

Bane looked at the image for a while. He chewed on something; Dooku had never wanted to know what.

"Skywalker's kid," he said. "Heard about that." He tipped his head. "Heard she got herself a fancy new necklace, too. Keeps the Jedi tricks in."

"It does. I'd prefer it stayed on until she reaches me. If you can obtain the warden's control along with her, so much the better. If not, my people can deal with it. You will have the Judiciary's schematics of Narkis within the hour, and the collar's full specification with them. The Senate entered most of it as exhibits when it debated the device's funding, and a Judiciary contractor was kind enough to sell me the pages it redacted, including the terminal in the warden's office that the collar answers to."

"Alive, then."

"Alive and unharmed. I want that understood. Not a mark on her that she didn't already have."

"Prison breaks cost extra," Bane said. "Republic prisons cost extra on top of extra. There's a clone detachment, Judiciary guards, a warden who don't take bribes, from what I hear, and a whole lotta ice between the landing pad and anywhere worth bein'." He spread his long hands. "Folks are gonna get hurt on that moon, Count."

"Republic folks."

"Just so we're clear on what you're buyin'."

"We are." Dooku named a figure. Bane's head tilted a few degrees, which in a Duros was very nearly astonishment. "Half when you accept. Half on delivery. There is one more thing. Among the prisoners there is a Confederate officer, Commander Merrick Tal of the Third Raxus Picket. He has been held for more than a year for an exchange the Republic never intended to make. Bring him as well."

"Sentimental."

"Practical. If anyone ever asks what you were doing on Narkis, you were hired to rescue a loyal officer of the Confederacy. That is a story everyone will believe, because it is the sort of thing everyone expects me to do."

Bane's mouth stretched slowly into something that might have been a smile. "And if anybody asks *you*?"

"No one asks me anything," Dooku said.

Bane considered him through the wavering blue. Then he touched the brim of his hat.

"Give me three weeks," Cad Bane said, and the hologram folded in on itself and was gone.

Dooku stood alone in the gallery. Outside, the rain had thickened, and the lights of the palace grounds blurred into soft gold against the dark. On the wall screen the girl's face was still frozen at the moment of *life*, her mouth just open, the whole of her caught between one verdict and the next.

He stood before the frozen image a while longer.

"Let us see," he said to no one, "what the Jedi have thrown away."

Somewhere past the charted lanes, under a dome of durasteel and a sky of ice, a prisoner with a grey collar at her throat had just become the most valuable thing on her moon. Nobody there knew it yet.

## Chapter 15: The Admiral's Visit

For twenty-one days the Force had been coming back to Ahsoka in pieces, and none of the pieces was big enough to hold.

It came while she was cutting ice, a pressure at the edge of her hearing, like voices in the next room through a durasteel wall. It came at night as she lay on her bunk in 4-17, a faint tug toward the warmth of Tess sleeping on the other side of the left wall. Once, in the mess, it came so clearly that she turned before the tray dropped behind her. Then it was gone again, and the tray clattered on the floor, and she could not have caught it if her life had depended on it.

That was the part nobody had warned her about. Total silence she had learned to live in. This was worse. It was like being dragged up toward the surface of a frozen lake, close enough to see the light, never close enough to breathe.

She had not understood any of that the morning Vorne stood at the foot of her cot in Ring Two with the black cylinder in her hand. Ahsoka had woken to the sight of it and gone very still. For one wild moment she had believed it was over: a pardon, a mistake found, Barriss's doing somehow.

"Healer Offee has filed a note," Vorne had said, in the same flat voice she used for quotas and meal times. "Prolonged dampening at full intensity, nerve inflammation, risk of seizure. Judiciary medical regulations oblige me to act on the written recommendation of an accredited healer." She had thumbed the cylinder twice. At Ahsoka's throat the status light had faded from hard blue to something paler, the color of the ice at the bottom of the cut. "This is a recalibration. Not a release. And not a kindness, Twenty-two fourteen. If you have a seizure in my facility, it goes on my record."

So that was all it was. The headaches eased. The ache in the tendons of her neck faded. And the whisper started.

* * *

On the day of the collapse the whole Deep smelled of solvent, because Admiral Tarkin was coming.

The inmates had learned it the usual way, from the guards' tempers. Floors that had not been scrubbed in years were scrubbed twice. The heat strips in the mess were repaired. Sergeant Holt had shaved so close that his jaw had a red rash along it, and he spent the morning shift on the terrace above Cut Five shouting at prisoners to stand up straight while they swung their picks.

"Two days," Tess said under her breath, setting her chisel against the next block. "Two days of this. Then he looks at us for half an hour and flies away, and we go back to being invisible."

"I've met him." Ahsoka levered her block free. Thirty-one. "He won't look at us at all. He'll look at the numbers."

"You've met the Admiral." Tess snorted. Her breath smoked in the blue air. "Of course you have. Is there anyone important in the galaxy you haven't annoyed?"

"Give me time."

Cut Five lay at the bottom of the Yard, deeper than the others, where the ice turned old and glassy and the refinery's pipes ran along the walls. Most of the pipes were cold. One of them, the thick return line wrapped in cracked grey insulation, was not. It carried coolant back from the tibanna stills after the coolant had spent the morning drinking their heat, and the ice around it was always slick, always weeping. The crews called it the Kettle and ate their ration bars with their backs against it on the days the guards were not looking.

Today the guards were looking. Commandant Vorne had come down onto the terrace herself, walking the length of the cut with Holt a respectful pace behind her, inspecting the shoring posts one by one. Her coat was buttoned to the throat. The collar control rode on her belt as it always did, a small black shape against grey wool.

Krasso was working two stations down. He had not spoken to Ahsoka since his fourteen days in the Well, beyond a nod in the mess that might have meant anything. Pell was on the sled line at the far end, hauling blocks with the bent-shouldered patience of a nineteen-year-old who had been told he would be doing it for thirty years.

Ahsoka was reaching for block thirty-two when the whisper changed.

It did not get louder. It sharpened, like a single thread pulled tight in a slack net. Something under the ice was straining. She did not know how she knew. She turned toward the Kettle before she had decided to.

"Tess."

"What?"

"Get away from the wall."

Tess had survived sixty-one years by knowing when not to ask questions. She stepped back. Two prisoners further along did not.

The return line split with a sound like a blaster bolt fired inside a barrel.

Steam erupted from the insulation in a white column, scalding, roaring, so thick that the far end of the cut vanished. Someone screamed. The prisoners near the wall threw themselves flat. Hot coolant sheeted down the ice, and where it ran the ice softened and sagged and opened in long dark seams.

Above them, the overhang groaned.

It was a shelf of old ice forty meters long that leaned out over the lower terraces like the brim of a hat. Ahsoka had looked at it every day for three months and never once thought about its weight. Now it was all she could think about. A crack ran along its base, fast as thought, and the whole shelf dropped a hand's breadth and stopped with a jolt that shook the floor.

"Out!" Holt bellowed from somewhere in the steam. "All crews out! Move!"

They moved. But the cut had only one ramp up to the terrace, and the ramp was at the far end, past the steam, under the shelf. Prisoners stumbled into each other in the white murk. A guard Ahsoka didn't know was trying to wave people forward with his stun baton and getting knocked aside for his trouble. The shelf dropped again, another hand's breadth. Chunks of ice the size of a crate began to rain down at the edges.

One of them landed on Krasso.

Not squarely; if it had, there would have been nothing to do. It caught his legs and pinned him face-down beside a shoring post, and the post, knocked from its footing, slanted across his back. He roared and clawed at the floor and could not move.

Tess had stopped halfway to the ramp. She was looking back at Ahsoka, and at the shelf above them, and Ahsoka saw in her face the arithmetic that everyone in the Deep did without thinking. There were twenty people under that ice. The ramp could take three at a time.

The overhang would not wait for that.

Ahsoka looked up at the terrace. Vorne stood at the rail, very straight, one hand already resting on her belt. Steam rolled up around her. She was looking down, not at the prisoners or at the shelf, but at Ahsoka, and her face was the face of someone reading a regulation she had never expected to need.

"Twenty-two fourteen." Her voice cut through the roar without ever being raised. "If I take it off, can you hold that?"

Ahsoka did not let herself think about what she was answering.

"Yes."

"Then do it."

The cylinder came off the belt. Vorne's thumb moved.

The blue light at Ahsoka's throat went dark, and the Force hit her like a wave hitting a seawall.

She had forgotten. That was the first thing she understood, on her knees on the wet ice with her hands spread: she had forgotten how much there was. Every life in the cut flared up at once, bright and close and terrified. Tess's heartbeat, hard and fast. The young guard's panic. Krasso's fury, hot as the steam. Pell somewhere far off at the sled line, his fear thin and high. And under all of it the ice itself, which was not dead the way she had always assumed, but full of strain and slow movement and old pressure. She could feel the shelf above her the way she could feel her own arm. She could feel exactly where it was going to break.

She reached up and caught it.

It was heavy. It was so heavy. Not the weight of a speeder or a droid, nothing she had trained for; it was the weight of a hillside, and for a terrible moment it pressed her down toward the floor and she thought she had lied to Vorne. Then she stopped trying to lift it and simply refused to let it fall. That was different. That she could do.

The shelf stopped moving.

The ice stopped raining. In the sudden quiet the steam hissed on, and twenty people under the overhang looked up at the shelf, then at the kneeling girl with her arms raised, and did not move.

"Go," Ahsoka said through her teeth. "*Go.*"

They went. Tess first, grabbing the nearest two prisoners by their coats and hauling them toward the ramp, her voice cracking orders over the hiss. Then everyone at once. Ahsoka felt each of them pass beneath the ice, felt the shelf shift a hair as their footsteps shook it, and adjusted. Her shoulders burned. Blood ran warm from her nose and over her lip.

Krasso was still down.

She could not let go of the shelf to go to him. She did not need to. She found the slanted shoring post with a part of herself she had not used in four months, and pushed. It swung away and slammed into the wall. The block across his legs was harder; it was wedged, and the shelf above her trembled as she split her attention. She lifted the block anyway, a finger's width, then two, and Krasso dragged himself out from under it on his elbows with a hiss of pain.

"Up," she said. "Up, Krasso, or I swear I'll drop it on you myself."

He got up. He limped. He did not look back at her until he reached the bottom of the ramp, and then he did, once, his yellow eyes wide.

Holt and the young guard came last, half carrying a prisoner who had been scalded across the arms. Then the cut was empty except for Ahsoka.

She could feel everyone on the terrace above. She could feel Vorne at the rail, watching, the cylinder in her hand. She could feel the guards' rifles. She could feel the dome over the Yard, and the hangar beyond it, and the landing pad, and a cargo hauler sitting on it with its engines cold. For one long, bright moment she knew with total certainty that she could put the shelf down, walk up that ramp, and no one in the Deep could stop her.

Somewhere far away, very faintly, she thought she felt someone she loved. She couldn't tell who. It was gone before she could reach for it.

She lowered the shelf.

She did it slowly, guiding it down onto the empty floor of the cut where the shoring had been, letting the ice settle against ice with a sound like a long exhale. Powder rose in a cloud and drifted, glittering, in the blue light. When it cleared, Cut Five was half its old height, and nobody was under it.

Ahsoka stood up. Her legs shook. She turned and looked up at the terrace and met Vorne's eyes.

Vorne pressed the cylinder.

The silence came back. Not the whisper; for a moment not even that. It came down like a lid on a box, and every life she had just been holding went out at once, Tess and Krasso and Pell and the young guard, all of them gone, as if she had walked out of a crowded room into a sealed vault. She staggered. Then the recalibrated collar settled, and the faint pressure returned, just beyond hearing, thinner than she remembered. Thinner than it had been that morning.

She had thought she knew what losing the Force felt like. She had done it once already, on the transport, and survived it. She had not known that losing it a second time, after she had just had it in her hands, would feel like learning the first time all over again.

"Four minutes and nine seconds," Vorne said into her comlink, to someone in Ring One. "Log it. I'll file the report myself."

* * *

They put the scalded prisoner in medical and everyone else back in their cells two hours early, which was the closest thing to a holiday the Deep had ever had.

Ahsoka lay on her bunk with a wad of gauze pressed under her nose and stared at the ceiling. The blood had stopped. The shaking had not. Every so often her hands twitched toward the ice they were no longer holding.

A tap on the left wall. Two short, one long. Tess's knock.

"Still alive in there?"

"Mostly."

"Good." A pause. Through the vent slot near the floor, Ahsoka heard the rustle of Tess sitting down with her back against the wall, close enough that their voices didn't need to carry. "That was something, kid. I've been in this rock nine years. I've seen people die in that cut. I never once saw the ice stop."

"It didn't stop. I stopped it." She closed her eyes. "Then I gave it back."

"Mm." Tess was quiet for a while. "You know what you just did, though. Besides the obvious."

"Saved your life?"

"Besides that." A dry laugh. "Rule four. Never show them what you can do. You just showed every guard on that terrace, and Holt, and the Commandant, and forty prisoners who'll be telling it in the mess tomorrow. By next week the story'll have you lifting the whole dome."

"I didn't have a choice."

"I know you didn't. I'm not scolding." Something shifted in Tess's voice, slower now, thoughtful in a way Ahsoka didn't quite like. "I'm saying you're worth something different than you were this morning. To a lot of people. That's all."

Ahsoka didn't answer. After a while Tess knocked once more, a soft single tap that meant goodnight, and the rustle of her coat moved away from the wall.

Later, when the lights had gone down to the dull blue of the night cycle, someone stopped outside 4-17. Not a guard. Guards walked heavier, and they didn't breathe like that, a slow rasp through a scaled throat.

"Krasso," Ahsoka said, without sitting up. "You're out of your cell."

"Holt is drunk on the Commandant's good mood." His voice was low, careful with the Basic. "The leg is not broken. The medic says so."

"I'm glad."

"You are not glad. You are polite." A long silence. "Twice now, Togruta. In Cut Three you could have let me fall. Today you could have let the ice keep me. Garnac would have let me."

"I'm not Garnac."

"No." He made a sound she couldn't read. "It was easier to hate you before. A debt this size is heavy. I do not know where to put it down."

"Then carry it," Ahsoka said. "That's what everyone else here does."

He was quiet so long she thought he had gone. Then the footsteps moved off down the walkway, uneven, favoring the left leg, and somewhere below, faint through the floor, she heard a door slam and Holt's voice raised in a laugh.

She turned over and pressed her forehead against the cold wall and reached, as she did every night, for the whisper. It was there. It was so faint. She could not tell anymore whether it was the Force or only her own memory of it, playing back like a recording she couldn't stop.

* * *

The Admiral's cruiser came down two days later in a storm of ice crystals, and an hour after that, two clone guards came to 4-17 and took Ahsoka up to Ring One.

They sat her on a bench outside the Commandant's office with her wrists in binders and stood on either side of her in silence. The corridor was warm. She had forgotten that too; Ring One was heated properly, for the staff, and after four months in the Yard the warmth made her skin prickle and her montrals ache.

The office door opened, and a guard led Merrick Tal out.

Until now he had been two things to her: a shape at the bottom of the shaft, saluting, and a voice through the vent in the Well. He was smaller than his voice. Thin, pale from fifteen months under lights that never went off, in the same high-collared tunic stripped of its insignia. He squinted at the corridor's ordinary light as though it were too dim to see by.

He saw her and stopped. The guard let him; this was a day when the guards were being careful about everything.

"The Jedi," he said. "The one who held up the mountain. I heard it through the floor. Holt couldn't stop talking."

"Former Jedi."

"Yes. They do insist on that, don't they." He glanced back at the closed door. "I'm being transferred. Coruscant-ward, the Admiral says, for an exchange that has been about to happen for fifteen months. In a few days, when the next Judiciary hull comes through." His mouth twisted. "Do you know, I think I'll miss the lights. One grows attached to anything."

"You don't believe them."

"I believe they've found a use for me," Merrick said. "That's not the same thing as an exchange." He looked at her a moment longer, at the collar and the binders and the gauze still taped under her nose. "Be careful in there, Tano. He's very good at telling the truth. It's the thing that makes him dangerous."

The guard touched his elbow, and he went.

Then it was her turn.

Vorne's office was as Ahsoka remembered it: grey, spare, with a desk squared to the wall and a single window overlooking the Yard. What she had not expected was that Vorne would not be in it. The Commandant stood outside the door as Ahsoka was brought through, and closed it behind her, and her face as the door slid shut showed nothing at all, which on Vorne might have meant anything.

Tarkin stood at the window with his hands clasped behind his back. He did not turn around.

"Sit," he said.

She sat. The clone guards had stayed in the corridor. It was the first time since the tribunal that she had been alone in a room with him, and she was surprised to find she wasn't afraid. She was too tired to be afraid. She just watched the narrow line of his shoulders under the olive-grey uniform and waited.

"I've read the Commandant's report," Tarkin said. "Four minutes and nine seconds. Twenty-three lives, by her count, including two of her own personnel. She tells me she reviewed the regulations governing emergency suspension of restraint before she came down to the cut that morning, as part of her preparation for my visit." He sounded faintly pleased. "That is why I recommended her for this post. Most wardens would have hesitated. Most wardens would have had to look it up."

"Is that why I'm here? So you can tell me she did well?"

"You're here because I wished to see you." He turned at last. His face was exactly as she remembered it across the tribunal floor: long, hollow-cheeked, the eyes pale and patient. He looked her over the way he might have looked over a damaged cargo manifest. "The Senate has held a hearing on your collar, you know. Several of the more sentimental members feel it constitutes cruelty. I expect the Commandant's report will be very useful to me."

"Because I saved people?"

"Because you saved people and then allowed it to be switched back on." He came around the desk and leaned against its edge, close enough to look down at her. "Your collar can be lifted when the Republic requires it, and replaced when the Republic is finished. It does exactly what it was designed to do. And the prisoner wearing it chose to stay. That is a far better argument for the program than any I could have made."

She thought of the moment at the bottom of the cut, the dome and the hangar and the landing pad and the cold hauler all laid out in her mind like a map. She had not told anyone about that moment. She felt, absurdly, as if he had read it off her face.

"I'm not your argument," she said.

"You are precisely my argument. You are the first Force-user to wear one of these for longer than a week. There will be others." He said it the way another man might read out a supply schedule. "Whatever you do here, Miss Tano, becomes evidence. I'd advise you to keep behaving well."

Ahsoka looked at her bound hands for a moment. Then she looked up at him.

"You know I didn't do it."

She had not meant to say it. It had been sitting in her for four months, under the ice and the quotas and the long nights, and it came out flat and tired and true.

Tarkin regarded her for a long moment. Then, to her surprise, he answered.

"I know that it doesn't matter," he said. "I'm not certain anyone could convince me you did, if you want the truth. I'm not certain anyone could convince me otherwise. I've never found it a very interesting question."

"Six Jedi died. Clones died. Three troopers in the detention level. And you don't find it *interesting*?"

"I find the consequences very interesting." He folded his arms. "For four days the Holonet showed the Jedi Temple burning. Every citizen on Coruscant watched the Republic's guardians fail to guard their own front door. The public needed someone to be answerable, and quickly, and visibly. The Order offered no one. Your former Master chased shadows in the lower levels. And you were on the footage, and your blades were in the troopers, and you ran." He gave a small shrug. "The Republic needed an answer. You were the most useful answer available. You still are."

"And whoever really did it is still out there."

"Very possibly."

"Doesn't that frighten you?"

"Not nearly as much as the alternative." His voice did not change. "If the real culprit walked into my office tomorrow with a full confession, I would have to explain to the Senate, the Holonet, and every grieving family in deep blue why the Republic sent the wrong girl to the ice. The verdict would be wrong. Every verdict after it would be questioned. It is far better for everyone, Miss Tano, that you remain correct."

She had imagined this conversation a hundred times. In the Well, in the cut, at night with her face to the wall. She had imagined shouting at him, or saying something clever and cutting that made him flinch. Now that it was here she found she could not say anything at all.

Because he was right. Not about her guilt; about what she was. She had spent four months thinking of herself as a prisoner, a person who had been wronged, someone with a name and a case and a history that could be untangled if the right people only looked. And the right people had looked, and they had decided that the name and the case and the history did not matter. What mattered was the shape she made on the Holonet. The Temple Bomber. The Jedi Who Fell. The first prisoner in the collar. The Senate could debate her and the Commandant could log her and the Admiral could use her, and none of them needed her to be anyone in particular for that to work.

She was a symbol. Symbols didn't get appeals.

"Thank you," she said finally. Her voice was very steady. "For being honest. I don't think anyone else has been."

For the first time Tarkin seemed faintly, genuinely surprised. Then the look smoothed away, and he pushed off the desk and went back to the window.

"Commandant," he said, not raising his voice. The door opened at once. "We're finished here. Return Twenty-two fourteen to her cell."

* * *

The cruiser lifted off at midday. The inmates watched it go from the Yard, leaning on their picks, and nobody pretended to work until it had vanished into the blue overhead. The heat strips in the mess went cold again that same evening. Holt's rash began to heal. The Deep, which had held its breath for three days, let it out.

Ahsoka didn't. She went back down to Cut Four, since Five was buried, and cut her forty blocks, and said almost nothing, and Tess watched her sideways the entire shift without asking a single question. It was the kindest thing anyone had done for her in days.

At shift change Tess caught her sleeve on the way to the tool shed.

"Come here. Quick. Before Holt does his count."

The tool shed was a prefab hut against the dome wall where the crews racked picks and chisels at the end of each shift. Tess went straight to the back, where a stack of broken sled runners leaned against the wall, and knelt, and pulled a loose panel off the bottom of the rack. Inside, wrapped in a heat-pack sleeve, was a small, ugly device: the cracked casing of an ice-density sounder, held together with wire and sealant, with a leak-detector coil soldered onto one end and a scratched readout the size of Ahsoka's palm.

"Tess. What is that?"

"Nine years," Tess said, "is a long time to go without knowing who's in your sky." She thumbed a switch. The readout flickered green. "Smuggler's habit. Sounder picks up the dome's own sensor pulses and bounces them. Coil filters the noise. It's terrible. It's also better than nothing, which is what they give us."

"If Vorne finds that—"

"Thirty days in the Well. I know. Look."

She tilted the readout. A rough circle in the center, the dome. A bright smear at the edge, which Tess tapped with a frostbitten finger: "That's the Admiral's cruiser, climbing out. Gone in a minute." Then she moved her finger to the opposite side of the screen, low down, near the curve where the moon's horizon would be.

Three faint marks. Close together. Not moving.

"They came in an hour after the cruiser landed," Tess said quietly. "Low and slow, around the far side, where the dome's sensors don't reach. If I hadn't been watching the cruiser I'd have missed them. No transponders, or none I can read. They're not Judiciary, they're not supply, and they're not the Admiral's escort, because his escort left with him." She looked up, and her face in the green glow was older than Ahsoka had ever seen it. "And they're still there."

"Pirates?"

"Pirates don't come to the Deep. There's nothing here to steal." Tess turned the device off, and the shed went dark around them except for the blue light from the door. "Except people."

Ahsoka thought of Merrick Tal saying *I believe they've found a use for me*. She thought of Tarkin saying *you still are*. She thought of the cut, four minutes and nine seconds, and forty prisoners telling it in the mess, and how many guards and supply pilots and hauler crews would have heard that story by now and carried it out of here to wherever they went next.

Somewhere beneath her thoughts, faint as frost on glass, the whisper of the Force went suddenly, perfectly still—the way a small animal goes still in tall grass when something large has started to move nearby.

## Chapter 16: Bane

The heat strips died at the start of third watch, and the cold came up through the floor of cell 4-17 like water filling a hold.

Ahsoka was already awake. She had not really slept in two nights, not since Tess had tilted that cracked green readout toward her in the tool shed and shown her three marks that did not move. Every shift since, Tess had checked again and given her the smallest shake of the head. *Still there.* And every night the whisper of the Force, thin as it was under the collar, had stayed flat and listening, as if it were holding its breath along with her.

Now the strips along the ceiling faded from orange to grey to nothing. The blue twilight of the corridor flickered once and went out. For three heartbeats the Deep was completely dark and completely silent, which in four months she had never known it to be.

Then the emergency lamps came on, a dull amber, and somewhere far below her the ice shook.

It wasn't a collapse. She knew the sound of a collapse now; it came from everywhere at once and had a long tail. This was short and flat and deliberate, and a second one followed it exactly as long after the first as it took to walk a corridor and set another charge.

"Told you," Tess said through the left wall. Her voice was very calm. "Didn't I tell you."

"You told me." Ahsoka was on her feet, coat on, boots sealed. She didn't remember doing any of it. "Where?"

"Ring One, from the feel of it. Maybe the Yard doors." A pause. "Kid. Whatever they're here for, it isn't the tibanna."

The alarm started, a rising two-note howl that bounced up the central shaft and set every prisoner on Ring Four shouting at once. Under it Ahsoka heard boots on the gantry, running, and a voice she knew giving orders in short barks. Sergeant Holt came around the curve of the ring with two guards behind him and a blaster rifle held across his chest, and he stopped in front of her cell.

He was breathing hard. Frost had already formed in his moustache.

"Twenty-two fourteen. Hands through the slot."

She put her hands through. The binders were cold enough to sting. "What's happening?"

"Commandant wants you in Ring One. Secure room." He keyed the door, and it rolled aside, and he took her by the upper arm the way he always did, not hard, just firm. "Somebody's cut through the outer hull of the Yard tunnel. Lost contact with the hangar crew. That's all I know and more than you need."

"Sergeant, it's three ships. They came in on the far side, low, two days ago—"

Holt stared at her. Then at the wall of Tess's cell. Then back.

"Two days," he said.

"I didn't have anyone to tell."

He let out a breath through his teeth and didn't argue. "Move."

They took the gantry at a jog. The lift was dead, so Holt turned them toward the service stair that ran up the inside of the shaft, and they were halfway to the landing when every cell door on Ring Four opened at once.

It was a single sound, forty locks releasing together, a clack like a giant cracking its knuckles. For one moment no one on the ring moved. Then the prisoners came out into the amber light, slowly at first and then not slowly at all.

"They've sliced the block," one of the guards said. He was young, younger than Holt by twenty years, and his voice cracked on it. "Sarge, they've got the whole block—"

"I can see that, Dannick." Holt put his back to the stair rail and his rifle up, not aimed at anyone yet. "Everybody stay where you are! Back in your cells and nobody gets hurt!"

Nobody went back. Nobody came at them, either. Most of the Deep's prisoners were too cold and too old and too tired to riot; they milled on the gantry, shouting questions down the shaft, and a few of the quicker ones started for the stairs below. Ahsoka saw Tess step out of 4-18 with her hands in her coat pockets, take one look at the stair, and walk the other way along the ring—toward the maintenance hatch, toward the tool shed, toward the scanner hidden behind its loose panel.

And she saw Krasso, three cells down, duck his notched brow under the doorframe and come out limping on his bad left leg, and look straight at her.

"Up," Holt said, and pushed her up the stair.

They reached the Ring Three landing. Smoke was rolling up the shaft from below now, thin and bitter, smelling of cut durasteel. The second guard went first through the hatch to Ring Two, and Ahsoka heard him say "Clear—" and then heard the sound of a single blaster shot, very close, and the word didn't finish.

The guard came back through the hatch backward, sat down against the landing wall as though his legs had simply decided they were done, and didn't move again.

Holt swore and shoved Ahsoka behind him so hard she hit the rail.

A figure stepped through the hatch into the amber light. Wide-brimmed hat. Long coat. A face the color of old jade, ridged and hairless, with red eyes that caught the lamp and gave nothing back, and two breathing tubes that ran from his cheeks down under his collar and made each breath a soft mechanical hiss.

He held a pistol in each hand. Neither of them was pointed at Ahsoka.

"Evening, Sergeant," Cad Bane said.

Holt fired. He was fast for a man his age, faster than Ahsoka would have guessed, and he didn't waste anything on words. Bane had already moved half a step to the side, the way a person moves to let someone pass in a doorway. The bolt scorched the hatch frame. Bane's left-hand pistol answered it once.

Holt went down on one knee. Then onto his side, on the grating, with his rifle under him and his eyes still open and surprised.

"No—" Ahsoka was on her knees beside him before she knew she'd moved, her bound hands on his chest. Under the thermal coat there was no rise and no fall. The frost in his moustache wasn't melting anymore.

Dannick, the young one, threw down his rifle and put his hands up and started to cry.

Bane looked at him, then at the rifle on the grating, then back at Dannick, and seemed to make a decision about how much trouble he was worth. "Smart," he said. "You stay smart. Sit down against that wall and count to a thousand." He turned the red eyes on Ahsoka. "And you're the reason I came all this way. You're smaller than the holos."

She hit him.

She hadn't planned to. There was no plan, there was only Holt's open eyes and the binders and a stair rail, and her body did what three years of Anakin's training and four months of ice had made it into. She came off the grating low, used the rail to swing her weight, and drove both bound fists up under his chin.

It should have put him down. It moved him back a step. Bane's head snapped up, the hat stayed on, and he made a sound through the tubes that might have been a grunt or a laugh.

She went for his right-hand pistol. She got her fingers on the barrel and twisted, and for a moment it was almost hers—and then something hit her in the ribs with no more fuss than a door closing, and she was against the wall with Bane's forearm across her collarbones and the barrel of the other pistol resting gently against her collar, just below the blue light.

"Now that," he said, "I didn't see coming. Somebody told me you'd be docile without your Jedi tricks." He leaned in. His breath through the tubes smelled faintly of machine oil. "Somebody was wrong. I'm going to remember that."

"You killed him." Her voice came out raw. "He was doing his job."

"So am I." He tipped his head toward the hatch. "Contract says I bring you back alive, in one piece, with that pretty necklace still switched on. It doesn't say anything about anybody else. You keep that in mind before you get creative again, because the next one who steps in front of you is going to be a prisoner, not a guard, and I'll still be on schedule."

From the hatch behind him came a wet, clicking voice. An Aqualish in patched grey armor ducked through, a heavy rifle in his hands and a coil of cable over one shoulder. "Ring Two's clear. The Separatist's in the Well, they say. Krinn's gone down after him with two of the boys."

"Then let's not keep the gentleman waiting." Bane stepped back, and the pistol came off her collar, and he gestured at the stair with it like a host showing a guest to the table. "After you, Commander. Oh—sorry. That's not right anymore, is it?"

She went up. There wasn't any choice. The Aqualish fell in behind her with his rifle, and Bane behind him, and on the landing below them Dannick sat against the wall beside Holt and the other dead guard, counting under his breath.

They were three steps from the Ring Two hatch when Krasso came up the shaft.

He didn't come up the stair. He came up the outside of it, hand over hand on the support struts, bad leg dragging, and he swung over the rail directly onto the Aqualish's back and bore him down onto the steps with a sound like two crates colliding. The rifle went off into the ceiling. Ice and dust rained down.

"*Go*," Krasso snarled at her, and that was the whole of it.

She went. Not up. Down—over the rail, the way Krasso had come, dropping to the Ring Three gantry and landing on her bound hands and rolling. The Ring Three prisoners scattered from her. She ran along the curve of the ring toward the far stair with her lungs burning in the cold and her ribs shrieking where Bane had hit them, and she heard two shots behind her, and a roar from Krasso that was all fury and still alive, and then a third sound she didn't know.

A hiss. A roar. Light.

Bane came over her head in a shallow arc on two jets of blue flame, coat flaring, and landed on the gantry ten meters in front of her as lightly as if he'd stepped off a curb.

"I'll admit it," he said, "I've had worse evenings."

She skidded and turned and he fired, not at her, past her, and the bolt took the gantry rail beside her hand and turned it white-hot. She flinched away from it. He was already there.

The stun bolt didn't hurt. That was the strangest thing. It was only a weight that settled over her whole body at once, and the amber lamps tipping sideways, and the grating coming up very slowly to meet her face.

* * *

She surfaced twice before she properly woke.

The first time she was being carried over someone's shoulder through the Ring One corridor, and the walls were scorched black in long streaks, and there was a guard lying across the threshold of the records office who did not look as if he'd ever get up again. Commandant Vorne stood at the end of the corridor in front of her own office door. Her uniform was still impeccable. Her sidearm was in her right hand and the collar control, the small black cylinder Ahsoka had watched hang from her belt for four months, was in her left.

"You will put the prisoner down," Vorne said. There was no tremor in it at all. "She is the property of the Republic Judiciary, and I am the—"

Bane shot her through the right shoulder. Vorne turned halfway around with the force of it and went down against the door, and the sidearm skittered away across the floor.

Bane crouched and took the cylinder out of her hand. She didn't let go of it easily; he had to bend her fingers back one at a time.

"It's keyed to the office terminal as well," Vorne said through her teeth. Her face was grey. "It won't do you any good to take it. I'll shut her down from my desk the moment you're gone."

"Appreciate the warning, Commandant." Bane stood, stepped past her, and put four shots through her open office door into something that sparked and then burned. "There. Now there's only the one." He tucked the cylinder into an inner pocket of his coat and patted it. "I'd get that shoulder looked at. Cold place like this, you don't want it setting wrong."

Vorne looked at Ahsoka. Not at Bane. Her iron-grey hair had come loose on one side, and there was something in her face that Ahsoka had never seen there in a hundred and eighteen days, and it took her a long, swimming moment to recognize it as fear—not for herself.
