"Twenty-two fourteen," Vorne said. Then, quieter, as if it cost her something: "Tano."

Then the dark came back.

The second time she surfaced, it was to cold so total it felt like being struck. She was outside. She had not been outside the dome since the day she arrived. The sky over the Yard was black and enormous and full of stars, and the wind off the ice field cut through her thermals like they were paper. Three ships squatted on the landing pad with their ramps down and their running lights dark: two blunt grey haulers with their identification plates scraped off, and a third, longer and meaner, all angles and dark hull.

Men were carrying crates up the haulers' ramps. Loot, she thought dimly. Medical stores. Tibanna canisters. Anything that would make it look like pirates and not a contract.

And between two of Bane's crew, being walked up the ramp of the nearest hauler with his hands bound in front of him and a hood held in one man's fist, ready to go over his head, was a thin man in a high-collared tunic stripped of its insignia.

Merrick Tal turned his head. He had not seen open sky in fifteen months; she watched him look up at it, just for a moment, before he looked at her.

"It seems we are both useful after all," he called across the pad. His voice was steady. "Remember what I told you, Tano. Someone always pays."

"Merrick—"

The hood went over his head. The crew pushed him up the ramp, and the hauler swallowed him, and its ramp began to rise.

She tried to turn toward it. Her body wouldn't do it. Whoever was carrying her shifted her weight with a grunt, and in the jolt her eyes swung across the pad, across the crates stacked at the foot of the dark ship's ramp—a pallet of grey insulated heat-pack cases, Narkis stores, the kind she had watched Tess trade a hundred times—and for a single heartbeat she was certain she saw a figure with cropped white hair crouched in the shadow behind them.

Then the stars went out again.

* * *

When she woke for real, she was sitting up.

She was on a narrow durasteel bench in a holding cage at the back of a ship's hold, and the binders had been replaced with heavy magnetic cuffs locked to a ring set into the deck between her boots. Her ribs ached in a long hot line. Her mouth tasted of metal. The deck under her hummed with the deep, steady vibration of a hyperdrive, and through it, very faintly, she could feel the ship's age: the uneven pulse of a motivator that had been repaired more times than it had been serviced.

The collar was still on. She knew without touching it. The world was still muffled, and the faint whisper she had lived on since Vorne turned it down was still there, and still silent.

"She's awake! She's awake, she's awake." A small skeletal droid on spindly legs clattered up to the bars of the cage and peered at her with two round photoreceptors. "Hello! I am Todo 360, I am in charge of you. Are you going to hit anyone? The boss says you hit him. Nobody hits him. I'm not supposed to open the door."

"I wasn't going to ask you to," Ahsoka said.

"Good. Because I wouldn't. Probably." Todo bobbed. "Would you like water? I am also in charge of water."

"Todo." Bane's voice came from the front of the hold. "Go count something."

The droid went, muttering. Bane walked down the hold toward her without hurry. He'd taken the hat off; it hung on a hook by the hatch, and without it his head looked longer and stranger, the ridges over his eyes deeper. He was turning Vorne's collar control over and over in his long fingers, the way a gambler turns a coin he hasn't decided to bet yet.

"Sleep well?" he said.

"How many?"

He knew exactly what she meant. That was the worst part. He didn't pretend not to. "Didn't count. Five or six. A few more if your lizard friend makes a habit of jumping on people."

"Is he alive?"

"He was when I left him. Vosh wasn't happy about it." He leaned against the bars. "I'll tell you something for free, since I've got time and you've got nowhere to be. Ninety percent of this job is getting people to stop fighting before they get hurt. Your sergeant didn't stop. He made a choice. I respected it."

"You murdered him."

"I *ended* him. Murder's a word for courts." The red eyes considered her. "You'd know more about that than me. You've got a conviction. I just have a reputation."

Behind him a console on the bulkhead chimed. He glanced at it, and something in his posture altered very slightly—not respect, exactly, but the attention a professional gives to the person signing his payment.

"Speaking of which," he said, "you've got a call."

He keyed the console. Blue light spilled across the hold and resolved into a tall figure, standing very straight, with a cape over his shoulders and a neat white beard and hands folded before him. Even flickering and translucent, he filled the hold as if he had built it.

Ahsoka had seen Count Dooku once in person, from a distance, across a battlefield. She had seen his face on a thousand briefings. She had never had him look at her the way he was looking now: with interest, and something that could almost have passed for sympathy.

"Miss Tano," Dooku said. "I am glad to see you unharmed. I gave strict instructions."

"Your instructions killed six people tonight."

"Mr. Bane's methods are his own." Dooku did not glance at Bane. Bane did not appear to mind. "I would not have chosen them. But I did not have the luxury of asking politely, and neither did you. You were not going to leave that place by any other door."

"I was serving a sentence."

"For a crime you did not commit." He let that sit a moment. "Oh, I know you didn't. Half the Senate knows it, if they are honest with themselves late at night, which they rarely are. The Republic needed a face for its grief, and yours was conveniently nearby. The Council needed to show the Senate that the Order would sacrifice one of its own, and you were conveniently small." His voice was beautifully reasonable. It was the voice of a man explaining a long-settled thing to a promising student. "They tried you in a room you once guarded. They took your beads with their own hands. And then they locked you in the ice with a collar on your throat and congratulated themselves on their mercy."

Ahsoka said nothing. Her hands, in the cuffs, had closed into fists, and she made them open again.

"I left the Order myself," Dooku went on. "Not as you did. I walked out of the Temple when I saw what it had become, and I have never regretted it. You were not given that choice. I am giving you one now. Come to Serenno. Not as a prisoner—as a guest, and in time as something more. I can teach you things the Jedi were afraid to teach. I can take that collar off. And I can give you the one thing the Republic never will." He paused, and when he spoke again it was softer. "The chance to make them understand what they did."

For a moment—only a moment—she felt the pull of it. Not the offer. The *anger*. It was real, and he had found it without effort, the way a healer finds a broken bone by pressing exactly where it hurts. Tarkin in the inspection room, saying guilt had never mattered. Windu's hand at her montral. The Council chamber, and Plo Koon looking at the floor.

And then Holt sitting down against the wall. And Merrick's hood going over his head. And four minutes and nine seconds in Cut Five, with the whole weight of the ice in her hands and the door to the stars right there, and choosing to set it down.

"No," she said.

Dooku's eyebrows rose a fraction. "You haven't heard the terms."

"I don't need to. You're right about some of it." It came out steadier than she expected. "They got it wrong. They got *me* wrong. But you're offering me the same thing they did—you've decided what I am, and you want me to agree. I've done four months of being whatever somebody else needed. I'm not starting over with you."

"That is pride talking. It does you credit, and it will not keep you warm."

"Neither did the ice. I lived."

Dooku studied her for a long moment. Then he smiled, very slightly, as though she had confirmed something he'd hoped to find.

"You'll reconsider," he said. "People generally do, once they have been on Serenno a while and the Republic has finished telling the galaxy what you are. Mr. Bane. Proceed as arranged."

The hologram folded into itself and was gone.

Bane switched off the console. In the sudden quiet the hyperdrive hum seemed louder.

"Well," he said, "that's his problem, not mine. I get paid on delivery, not on enthusiasm. You can sulk all the way to Serenno for all it matters to my account." He tucked the collar control back inside his coat, against his chest, and gave the pocket a pat. "But I'll give you some advice, since you turned his down. Don't try anything on my ship. I like my ship."

"And if I do?"

"Contract says alive." He took his hat off the hook and settled it on his head. "Alive covers a lot of ground."

She counted.

Not the way Dannick had been counting. She counted the way Tess had taught her to count blocks on the terrace: steadily, without hope, so that hope couldn't run out. Bane's crew—only two aboard, from the voices: Bane himself, and the Aqualish called Vosh, whose rasping complaints about his back came down the corridor at intervals. The others had gone with the haulers. With Merrick. Todo clattered in and out of the hold, counting crates aloud and losing his place.

She had counted to nineteen hundred when the lid of a heat-pack case at the bottom of the stacked Narkis loot lifted two centimeters and stopped.

Ahsoka went absolutely still.

The lid rose further. A frostbitten hand came out, two of its fingers stiff and dark. Then a cropped white head. Tess Varro unfolded herself out of a crate the size of a footlocker one joint at a time, wincing at every one of them, and sat back on her heels on the deck and looked around the hold with the expression of someone who had just woken up in a cheap hotel.

"Stars," she whispered. "I'm too old to be cargo."

"Tess—" Ahsoka kept her voice to nothing. "How did you—"

"Same way I always get anywhere. I walked in while everybody was looking at something else." She crept to the cage and crouched beside the bars, and up close Ahsoka could see how blue her lips were, how badly her hands were shaking. "They were loading anything that looked like it was worth stealing. I looked worth stealing."

"You could have stayed. Tess, you could have walked out of that block with every door open and nobody—"

"And gone where? Out on the ice in a thermal coat?" Tess's mouth twisted. "There's no road out of the Deep. There's ships, and there's dying. I picked a ship." She was already digging in her coat. Out came the cracked sounder, and then the leak-detector coil, its wires bent into a crude loop. "Hold still. Mag-lock's a mag-lock. Same as the tool shed."

She pressed the coil against the cuff housing and thumbed the sounder. It whined, a thin needle of sound. Nothing happened. She adjusted something with her thumbnail and tried again. Ahsoka watched the corridor hatch and listened to Todo, two compartments forward, explaining something to Vosh at great length.

The cuffs clicked and fell open.

Ahsoka caught them before they hit the deck. Tess let out a long breath.

"Cage door's on the same circuit," Tess said, and did it again, faster.

Then they were out, crouched together behind the stacked crates, and Ahsoka's mind was moving faster than it had in months—not the Force, she didn't have the Force, but everything else. Three years of Anakin. Three years of watching him crawl into the guts of every ship they'd ever flown, swearing at couplings, explaining things to her she'd pretended not to listen to.

"That hyperdrive," she breathed. "Hear it? The motivator's out of true. He's been running it hard and patching it." She pointed down the hold, past the cage, to a low access panel near the deck. "Main power feed to the motivator runs back along the keel. If we pull the regulator coupling, the safeties will drop us out of hyperspace before the drive tears itself apart."

"And then we're sitting in the dark with a bounty hunter."

"And then we find the escape pods. Every ship that size has two." She was already moving. "Watch the hatch."

The access panel was held with four bolts, and she had no tools. Tess silently handed her a flat steel spike from the inside of her boot—a guard would have given her thirty days in the Well for that—and Ahsoka used it as a lever and then as a driver, and the panel came away. Inside was a tangle of conduit, heat shimmering off it. She found the regulator by the warning stencils, the same ones on every hyperdrive she'd ever seen, and got both hands on the coupling and thought of Anakin, for one sharp instant, lying under a fighter in the hangar with grease to the elbows, saying *the trick is not to care what it costs you*.

Beside the panel, a cargo repeater scrolled the nav plot in dull amber so the loaders could see how long the run would be. The lane bent out past the Florrum system. She knew exactly who lived on Florrum, and what he would do for a profit, and what he might do for her. She kept her hands on the coupling and watched the marker crawl along the plot until it sat level with the system, because if the safeties were going to drop them somewhere, it would be next to Hondo.

She pulled.

The coupling came free with a shriek of sparks. The hum of the deck changed pitch, climbed, stuttered. Every light in the hold went red. Somewhere forward an alarm began to whoop, and the whole ship lurched as if something enormous had grabbed it by the tail—and then the long streaked light beyond the hold's single viewport snapped back into stars.

And a planet. Close. Huge, and sickly yellow, its sky hazed with sulfur, its surface cracked with dark canyons.

She knew that yellow. She had fought for her life down there, with a crate of younglings behind her and a pirate who couldn't decide whether to sell her or applaud her.

"Florrum," she said.

"*Intruder! Intruder!*" Todo came clattering through the hatch, arms waving. "The prisoner is out! The prisoner is out and there are *two* of her!"

"Pods," Tess said.

They ran. Through the forward hatch, down a cramped corridor washed in red, Todo screeching behind them. Ahsoka saw the pod hatch ahead, recessed into the port bulkhead, with its yellow-and-black frame—and saw Bane come around the corner at the far end of the corridor at the same moment, hat low, a pistol already rising.

He didn't fire at her. He fired at the pod controls, and missed only because Vosh blundered into him from behind. The bolt scorched the bulkhead a hand's breadth from the release lever.

Ahsoka hit the lever. The hatch cycled open. Tess dove in. Ahsoka turned in the doorway, and for one half-second she and Bane were looking straight at each other down the length of the corridor. She saw the outline of the collar control under his coat, over his heart. Six meters. It might as well have been six systems.

He saw her see it. He touched the brim of his hat.

She threw herself into the pod and slammed the inner seal, and the launch charge went off with a bang that knocked the breath out of her, and the *Justifier* fell away above them.

The pod's comm crackled almost at once.

"That was my ship," Bane's voice said, perfectly calm. "I told you I liked my ship." The breathing tubes hissed. "You know what's down there? Pirates. Bad weather. Things that eat pirates. I'll give you a day or two to discover how much you miss my company. Then I'll come and collect. I always collect."

Tess reached past her and turned the comm off.

Then Florrum's atmosphere caught them, and the pod began to scream.

There was nothing to do but hold on. Ahsoka strapped Tess into the second crash couch with hands that wouldn't stop shaking, and strapped herself in, and the viewport went orange and then white with heat, and the whole pod bucked and spun until she couldn't tell which way was the planet. The retros fired late and not enough. A canyon wall flashed past the viewport, close enough to see the striations in the rock. A plume of steam—a geyser—enveloped them, and the pod struck something and bounced and struck again and skidded, on and on, a long grinding roar of rock against durasteel that went through her teeth.

Then it stopped.

Silence. A tick of cooling metal. The smell of hot plating and, seeping in through some crack in the hull, the sharp rotten-egg reek of sulfur.

"Tess?"

A cough beside her. "Still here. Don't ask me what's broken. I'll let you know when I find out."

Ahsoka popped her harness and fell against the side of the pod, which was now the floor. She got the hatch open by kicking it. Yellow light poured in, thick and hot after four months of blue, and she climbed up into it and stood on the warped hull of the pod at the bottom of a red-walled canyon. A geyser hissed somewhere upstream. The sky was the color of an old bruise healing. The air burned her throat.

It was the first real warmth she had felt since the day they put her on the transport, and she stood in it with her eyes closed and let it.

Then she lifted her hand to her throat, and her fingers found the grey metal and the small hard bump of the status light, still glowing blue, and the Force beneath it still a whisper too faint to use.

Bane had the only control. And Bane was coming.

Below her, Tess was hauling herself out of the hatch. As she came up, her shaking hand closed around something small she'd taken from the pod's emergency locker—or from somewhere—and slipped it into her coat before Ahsoka could see what it was.

"Power cell," Tess said, catching her look. "For the sounder. We'll want to know who's coming." She squinted up at the yellow sky. "Kid. Where in all the hells are we?"

"Somewhere I know," Ahsoka said. "Which isn't the same as somewhere safe."

* * *

*HOLONET NEWS — CORE BULLETIN*

*CONVICTED TEMPLE BOMBER FREED IN SEPARATIST RAID; BELIEVED TO HAVE JOINED COUNT DOOKU*

*NARKIS-NINE PENAL FACILITY — Former Jedi Padawan Ahsoka Tano, convicted four months ago of conspiracy in the bombing of the Jedi Temple and the murders of four people, was removed from Republic custody overnight in an armed assault on the remote ice-moon prison. Six members of the facility staff were killed. Facility Commandant Ilsa Vorne was wounded and is expected to recover. A captured Separatist officer awaiting exchange was also taken.*

*Judiciary sources say the attackers appeared to know the prison's layout in detail, and that Tano offered no resistance to her rescuers. Admiral Wilhuff Tarkin, who prosecuted the case, said the raid "confirms beyond any remaining doubt where her loyalties lie," and urged the Senate to expand the Force-restraint program without delay.*

*The Jedi Council has not commented.*

# Part Three: The Hunt

## Chapter 17: Fugitive

The Council had put her prison number on the holoprojector.

NK9-2214, in square Judiciary lettering, floated above the center of the chamber beside an intake image from Narkis: Ahsoka in grey thermals against a grey wall, chin up, the collar's status light a hard blue dot at the base of her throat. Somebody had picked that image. Somebody had decided this was what the Jedi Council should be looking at while it decided what to do with her.

Anakin stood in the circle where petitioners stood, and he looked at it, and he did not let himself look at anything else.

His second request for a visitation permit had come back four days before the raid, stamped *pending review* by the Chancellery and nothing else. He had filed it twice more by hand. Nobody had answered those either.

"Six members of the facility staff are dead," Mace Windu said. The morning came in low and gold through the tall windows behind him and put his face in shadow. "The Commandant was shot. She identified the leader of the raid as Cad Bane. A Separatist officer held for exchange was taken in the same assault. The attackers knew where the power couplings were, where the cells were, and which door to open." He let the list sit a moment. "Bane works for whoever pays him. This time, it is reasonable to believe that was Count Dooku."

"Reasonable," Anakin said.

"Reasonable." Windu didn't blink. "We have to assume she is with Dooku now. Whether she went willingly is a question we can't answer from here. Until we can, she is a fugitive from a Republic sentence and a potential asset to the Separatist command."

"She's a seventeen-year-old in a Force collar."

"She is a trained combatant who knows our fleet movements, our codes as of four months ago, and the habits of every officer in the 501st." Ki-Adi-Mundi folded his long hands. "If Dooku has her, he has all of that."

"If Dooku has her," Anakin said, "then she's a prisoner who got stolen from one cell and put in a nicer one. That's not the same thing as joining him."

Plo Koon had turned his masked face a little toward the windows. Shaak Ti was very still. Yoda sat low in his chair with both hands on his stick and his ears drawn back, and he had not said a word since Anakin came in, which in Anakin's experience was never a good sign.

Windu went on as if Anakin had not spoken, which was worse than if he'd argued.

"She is to be recaptured," Windu said. "Alive, if it can be done. If she resists with force, she will be met with force."

"Say what you mean."

The chamber went quiet in the particular way it did when someone had broken a rule nobody had written down.

"I'll say exactly what I mean, Skywalker." Windu leaned forward. "If it comes to her life or the life of a Jedi or a trooper in the field, the Knight in command is authorized to end it. The Council will not send people after a fugitive and tell them to die rather than defend themselves."

Anakin heard something tick behind him. One of the glow-panels set into the floor had begun to buzz, its light stuttering. The intake image wavered and pulled sideways in the projector, her face stretching, snapping back.

"You expelled her so a court could kill her," he said. His voice came out level, which surprised him. "The court didn't manage it. The Chancellor didn't let it. So now you'll do it yourselves, and you'll call it a field decision so nobody in this room has to vote on it twice."

"Anakin." Obi-Wan, from his chair to Windu's left. Not loud.

"No. She held up a thousand tons of ice so twenty-three people could walk out of a mine. It's in the Commandant's report, I've read it, so has everyone here. She could have run that day. She *put it down.* And now some bounty hunter drags her out of her cell in the middle of the night and you're telling me she went with him because she wanted to?"

The glow-panel cracked. A thin line ran across it, and the buzzing stopped, and it went dark.

Nobody looked at it. That was the Jedi way. You noticed everything and acknowledged nothing until it was convenient.

"Heard you, we have," Yoda said at last, quietly. "Wrong, you may be. Right, you may be. Know, none of us do. That is the trouble, hm? Always, the trouble."

"Then let me go and find out."

"That," Windu said, "is exactly what you'll do."

Anakin had a whole second argument loaded and ready, and it took him a moment to realize he wouldn't get to use it.

"You and Master Kenobi," Windu said. "The 501st is released from rotation for the duration. You'll begin at Narkis and follow whatever the Commandant's people can give you." He paused. "You know how she thinks better than anyone in this Order. That's the reason. The other reason is that I would rather you were hunting her where Master Kenobi can see you than somewhere he can't."

"How generous."

"It wasn't meant to be."

At least that was honest. Anakin almost respected it.

"One more thing." Windu's mouth thinned, and for the first time that morning he looked like a man reading out words he hadn't written. "The Chancellor's office has recommended that Master Unduli and her Padawan be attached to the search. Offee treated Tano at Narkis. She filed the medical note on the collar's nerve damage. If the collar has been tampered with, or if Tano is injured, she may need a healer who already knows her condition." He set the datapad down. "The Council has agreed."

He didn't say *the Council has been asked to agree*, but it was in the way he set the datapad down, square to the edge of the armrest, as if putting it down very carefully would keep it from being somebody else's idea.

Anakin found he didn't mind. Barriss had been to Narkis. Barriss had seen her, touched her, had come back and written the note that got the collar turned down. He'd heard about that later, from a Judiciary summary, and had sat with it in the dark at 500 Republica for an hour, grateful to a Padawan he barely knew. If Ahsoka was hurt, Barriss would be the one she'd trust to put hands on her.

"Fine," he said.

"Fine," Windu agreed, and the holo with her number on it went out.

Obi-Wan caught up with him afterward in the long corridor, where the light came in bars between the columns and the tourists' gallery two levels up was closed for the morning.

"You don't believe she went to Dooku," Anakin said, without turning.

"No."

"Then why didn't you say that in there?"

"I did. At some length. Before you arrived." Obi-Wan fell into step beside him. "It was very persuasive. You saw how well it went."

Anakin almost laughed. It caught somewhere around his ribs, near the stiff place the Ordaine burn had left, and turned into something else.

"They authorized killing her, Master."

"They authorized a Knight in the field to defend his life." Obi-Wan's voice was mild, and very tired. "I know how it sounds. I don't like it any better than you do. But the Knight in command of this search is going to be you, or me, and I don't intend to kill her. Do you?"

"That's not funny."

"No," Obi-Wan said. "It wasn't meant to be either. We seem to be having a morning of that."

* * *

The rebuilt hangar still smelled of new sealant under the fuel and the ozone. The memorial alcove on the eastern wall had candles in it again; somebody kept them lit, and Anakin had never once seen who. He'd stopped looking at it months ago. He looked at it now, at the little flames standing straight in the still air, and thought about the six names cut into the stone above them, and the one name that would never be there, that had been put on a prison number instead.

The 501st was loading a pair of gunships for the transfer up to the *Resolute*. Rex stood at the foot of the near ramp with a datapad, checking off crates, and he looked up when Anakin came in, and Anakin saw him register Anakin's face and decide not to ask.

"General."

"Rex. We're on it."

"Heard, sir. The men heard too." Rex lowered the datapad. "Holonet's running the Tarkin quote every hour."

"I know."

"Jesse put his fist through a locker door."

"Is he all right?"

"The locker isn't." Rex paused. "Sir. The orders we got say *recapture*."

"That's what they say."

Rex looked at him a moment longer through the visor, and then he nodded once, and went back to his crates. Something had changed in Rex since the refinery. Anakin hadn't worked out what. The captain was as precise as ever, the reports clean, the men squared away. But there was a stillness in him now, a held-back quality, as if he'd put part of himself in a pocket and buttoned it shut.

Anakin didn't ask. He had his own pockets.

Luminara Unduli came across the deck a few minutes later, her dark robes hardly moving, Barriss a step behind her and to the left, the way Padawans walked when they had been taught well. Barriss had her hood down. She looked rested, which surprised him. The last time Anakin had seen her up close, at the Temple, months ago, she'd had the hollowed look of someone who'd given up sleeping as a bad habit. Now her face was smooth and her eyes were clear and her hands, folded in her sleeves, were still.

"Skywalker." Luminara inclined her head. "We are glad to help, if we can."

"Thank you, Master." He turned to Barriss. "You saw her. At Narkis."

"Yes." Barriss's voice was quiet and steady. "Once, in the infirmary. She'd hurt her shin pulling an inmate out of a chute. And there was the shoulder, from before."

"How was she?"

Barriss considered it. She was the kind of person who considered things, Anakin remembered; Ahsoka used to say it about her with exasperated fondness, that Barriss would think about whether to sit down for so long that the chair would leave.

"Thin," Barriss said. "Tired. Stubborn. She asked about the Temple. She asked about you." A small pause. "She was trying very hard not to hate anyone."

Anakin's throat closed. He made it open again.

"The collar," he said. "If it's still on her."

"Headaches. Pain down the neck and into the shoulder. If it's been damaged in the raid it could be worse—seizures, possibly." Barriss's gaze stayed on his. "If it's still on her, she will need it off. Carefully. It should not be cut by someone who doesn't know where the leads run."

"Then you'll be there when we find her."

"Yes," Barriss said. "I'll be there."

Luminara was looking at her Padawan. Not at Anakin; at Barriss, with a kind of stillness Anakin didn't understand and didn't have time to understand, as if Luminara were listening for something under the words and not finding it. Then she turned her attention to the gunships and the moment was gone.

The hangar's main doors opened again.

Admiral Tarkin walked in as if the deck had been laid for him personally. He had eight people with him, and they were not Coruscant Guard and they were not Republic Navy. They wore close-fitted grey armor with no unit markings, and they moved in the loose, unhurried way of people who were used to being the most dangerous thing in a room and didn't need to prove it. The woman at their head was in her forties, lean, black hair cut close to the skull, a pale old scar running down through her left eyebrow and into the lid. She carried nothing in her hands. The trooper behind her carried a small hard case with a Judiciary seal.

"General Skywalker." Tarkin stopped at a precise conversational distance. "Master Unduli. I won't keep you. As a courtesy, I wished to inform the Jedi personally that the Senate Judiciary will be conducting its own recovery operation. Major Teva Rusk, Judiciary Special Recovery. Her team will operate independently of yours."

Rusk nodded once. She looked at Anakin the way Anakin looked at an engine he'd been told was faulty: thoroughly, without opinion, already thinking about where to put the spanner.

"Independently," Anakin said.

"The prisoner was taken from Judiciary custody. Six Judiciary personnel died in the taking. The Senate considers her recovery a Judiciary matter." Tarkin's mouth moved slightly. "The Jedi Council, I understand, considers it a Jedi one. Two searches will be twice as likely to succeed. I'm sure we can all agree on the value of efficiency."

"What's in the case?"

Tarkin glanced back, almost lazily. "A replacement collar, General. The one she wears is controlled by a single device, which the bounty hunter now holds. Should the original be removed or fail, she cannot be left unrestrained. Not with the Senate's eyes on the restraint program." He let the next words come out cleanly, one at a time. "She is, after all, the program's most prominent demonstration."

The case was small. That was the thing Anakin couldn't stop looking at. It was no bigger than a field medkit. You could carry it in one hand.

Beside him, Barriss had gone very still. Anakin felt it more than saw it, a tightening in the Force at his elbow, and when he glanced at her she was looking at the case too, her face bloodless. She had treated the damage that thing did. She had felt it under her hands. He thought, with a rush of something like kinship, that she was the only other person in this hangar who knew what was inside that box and hated it as much as he did.

"She's not a demonstration," Anakin said. "She's a person."

"In my experience, General, those are not mutually exclusive." Tarkin turned to go, then paused. "Major Rusk's rules of engagement are set by the Judiciary, not by your Council. I mention it only so there are no misunderstandings in the field. Her people are very good at what they do. They are not burdened with the scruples that so often slow the Jedi down."

"Is that a threat, Admiral?"

"It's a description." Tarkin smiled thinly. "Good hunting."

He left. Rusk lingered a heartbeat longer, long enough to look at Rex, at the captain's sidearm, at the 501st's markings on the gunship, at the candles in the alcove. Then she followed him out, and her people went with her, and the hangar seemed larger afterward and colder.

Rex had come up beside him without Anakin noticing.

"Sir," Rex said, low, "permission to say something."

"Go ahead."

"I'd like to find her before they do."

Anakin looked at the doors where the grey armor had gone.

"Get in line," he said.

* * *

The Chancellor's office was too warm. It was always too warm. Anakin had stopped noticing it after the first month and only noticed it now because the day had been so cold—the hangar, the Council chamber, the sealed little case.

Palpatine stood at the window with his back to the room, watching the evening traffic slide past in its lanes, red and white and red. He didn't turn when Anakin came in. He said, "Sit, my boy, please," to the glass, and Anakin sat.

"I tried," Palpatine said.

Anakin waited.

"When I heard what the Council intended, I sent a message to Master Windu. I asked him, in the plainest terms I'm permitted to use with the Jedi, to reconsider the language. To insist on her capture alive and nothing else." He turned from the window at last. He looked older in the evening light than he did in the Senate. The yellow bruise Anakin had seen on the back of his hand weeks ago had faded to nothing; Anakin found himself looking for it anyway. "Master Windu replied, very courteously, that the internal decisions of the Order are not subject to review by the Chancellor's office. He is quite right, of course. The Senate and the Jedi have their separate spheres. That's how it should be." He sat down across the desk, slowly, an old man's sitting. "I simply wish, this once, that it were otherwise."

"You recommended Barriss Offee."

"I did. Was that wrong?" Palpatine looked genuinely worried. "She went to Narkis with the healer circuit, and when she came back her report was the only document in that entire file that treated Ahsoka as someone who could be hurt. I thought—" He spread his hands. "I thought if the worst happened, she should have a friend nearby who could do something about it. The Council would never think of that. They think of assets."

"No," Anakin said. "It wasn't wrong. It was right."

"I'm glad." Palpatine let a breath go. "And the Admiral?"

"You knew."

"I learned an hour after you did, I'm afraid. Wilhuff doesn't ask my permission for things he can get from the Judiciary committee." A small, rueful shake of the head. "He believes very deeply in his collar program. More deeply than in most people, I sometimes think. I've asked him to be restrained. I don't know that he will be."

Anakin's hands were on his knees. He found that the right one had closed, and he opened it, and the glove creaked over the metal knuckles.

"They're going to kill her," he said. It was the first time he'd let himself say it out loud. "One way or another. The Council will tell itself it was self-defense and Tarkin won't bother telling himself anything. And everyone will say it was a tragedy, and light a candle, and move on."

Palpatine was quiet a long moment.

"Anakin," he said. "Look at me."

Anakin looked at him.

"I could not stop the Council's order. I could not stop the Admiral. I am the Chancellor of the Republic, and today I discovered—not for the first time—how little that is worth when the people with weapons have made up their minds." His voice was low and even, a voice for telling someone something true. "But there is one person in this search who loves her. One person who knows her well enough to find her before the others do. Who would not, I think, stand back and watch while someone else decided she was too dangerous to bring home."

The traffic lanes went on sliding past the window. Red, white, red.

"Find her first," Palpatine said. "Before Master Windu's Knights, before the Admiral's people. And when you find her, don't take her to the Temple. Don't hand her to the Judiciary. Bring her to me."

"To you."

"I kept her alive once, when no one else would. Let me do it again." Palpatine reached across the desk and laid his hand over Anakin's closed one, the warm ordinary weight of it, an old man's hand on metal. "I can't promise you what happens after. I can promise she'll be safe in this building while we find out. No one will put her in a cell on the edge of nowhere. No one will put her down for resisting arrest. You have my word."

Anakin's eyes stung. He was embarrassed by it, and then too tired to be embarrassed.

He thought of the warehouse. He thought of the Dovrin platform, of the hab stack groaning on its pylon, of a pale mask on a catwalk turned toward him, watching him choose. He'd held up twenty stories of durasteel so nobody would die, and nobody had, and it had cost her everything. He'd been living inside that arithmetic for five months. Every night he did the sum again, and every night it came out the same.

He was not going to stop and save anyone else this time.

The thought arrived whole and he didn't push it away. It was steadying. It was the first solid thing he'd stood on all day.

"I'll find her," he said. "Before any of them. And if anyone tries to stop me—"

"Anakin." Gently. "I don't need to know the rest."

Anakin stopped. He nodded. He stood, and Palpatine stood with him, and walked him to the door, a hand on his shoulder, the way he had since Anakin was a boy and the Chancellor was only a senator who took an interest.

"Do give my regards to Master Kenobi," Palpatine said at the door. "He has a difficult task too. I don't envy him."

"He'll be fine."

"I'm sure he will," Palpatine said, and smiled, and the door slid shut between them.

In the turbolift going down, Anakin leaned his forehead against the cool panel and closed his eyes and let the car fall. Somewhere out there she was wearing a collar that a bounty hunter held the key to, and there were three hunting parties coming after her, and only one of them was going to bring her home.

It would be his. He'd make sure of that. Whatever it took.

He didn't call Padmé. He told himself it was because it was late.

* * *

The barracks had gone quiet by the middle watch, the kind of quiet that wasn't sleep. Somewhere down the row Jesse was still awake; Rex could hear him not moving. The 501st shipped up to the *Resolute* at dawn. Nobody had said much at evening mess. Nobody had needed to.

Rex sat on the edge of his bunk in his blacks with the cracked cadet datapad on his knee, the one with the network chip dug out, the one that held everything he'd written down since Ringo Vinda. Tup's headaches. The Kamino order logged nine minutes before his own report. Pesk. The refinery. The edge of the shaft and the grating and Fox's arm under his own, too late by half a second. He'd written that part the night it happened and had not been able to read it back since.

He was adding a line about Major Rusk—*scar left brow, no unit markings, eight personnel, case w/ Judiciary seal*—when his comlink chimed.

Not the command channel. Not the 501st net. The tone was the flat double note of a city-band relay, and the routing header on the little display said it had come in through the Coruscant Guard's maintenance traffic: an automated equipment-fault ping from a Guard-issue comlink, reporting a low power cell. Nineteen digits of serial and status code. The kind of thing that came in by the thousand every night and got filtered out by the thousand. Rex's personal unit shouldn't have received it at all.

He nearly deleted it.

His thumb was on the key when he looked at the status code again.

Nineteen digits. Fault class, cell level, a unit serial, a timestamp. Except the timestamp was wrong—it was twelve hours and some minutes off local, and the cell level read as a percentage no power cell ever reported, and the fault class was one that hadn't been used since the Guard switched suppliers two years ago. Garbage. Somebody's corrupted unit spitting out nonsense into the city net.

Or somebody who knew that nonsense was the only thing nobody would bother to read.

Rex set the comlink down on the bunk very carefully, as if it might go off.

He knew the shape of that string. He'd built half of it himself, on Kamino, in the long wet nights before their first deployment, two cadets bored out of their skulls inventing a code nobody else would want: the check-in cipher. Twelve-hour offsets for the timestamp. Cell level as a running count. The dead fault class as the signature, because, Fives had said, *who checks the dead ones?* They'd used it on Ringo Vinda. They'd used it for the medical burst that had come in through the *Resolute*'s medbay, the one Rex had watched eleven times.

He pulled the datapad closer. His hands were steady. He was very aware of his hands being steady, the way you were aware of your footing on a bad slope.

He worked the cipher out by rote, digit against digit. It didn't take long. It had never been a very good code. It had only ever needed to be good enough for two people.

Unit serial: a Guard comlink, registered to a trooper of Fox's. A comlink that had gone missing off a belt in the underlevels on the night an ARC trooper had run from the Chancellor's office past 79's and into an abandoned refinery.

Cell level: a count. Not of hours. Of check-ins. The number of twelve-hour check-ins a man would have missed, if he'd been ordered to check in every twelve hours, and had fallen down a drainage shaft, and had spent some long time after that unable to reach a comlink, or to lift one.

Rex counted it twice. It was right. It was exactly right.

And the signature, the dead fault class, carried the one tag they'd agreed on the day they made the code, the one Fives had insisted on and Rex had called childish and agreed to anyway: the ARC's own number, in the cipher, with the last digit flipped to show the sender was hurt.

*Five-five-five-four.*

Down the row, Jesse turned over on his bunk. A gunship engine ran up somewhere outside and spooled down again. Rex sat in the dark with a cracked datapad on one knee and a stolen signal on the other, and he did not breathe for what felt like a long time.

Then he picked up the comlink, and opened a reply on the same dead fault class, and his thumb found the old acknowledgment code without his having to think, as if it had been waiting under his skin.

Fives was alive.

## Chapter 18: Pirates

The first speeder bike came over the canyon rim trailing black smoke, and Ahsoka knew three things before its skids touched the rock: there were more behind it, the rider had already seen them, and she had not felt a single one of them coming.

Four months ago she would have known about the bike before she heard it. She would have known the rider's mood, whether his finger was on the trigger, whether he was the kind who shot first. Now she had only her ears and her eyes and the stiff grey collar under her jaw, and the whisper beneath it told her nothing at all.

"Down," she said, and pulled Tess behind a spur of red stone.

They had been walking since first light, following the canyon floor away from the crash because it was the only direction that wasn't straight up. The air stank of sulfur. Every so often a geyser somewhere ahead coughed and hissed, and the ground shivered under their boots. Tess had been limping for the last hour and pretending she wasn't. Her breath came in short, careful gasps, though the air was warm; four months of ice had taught both of them to breathe like it was still cold.

A second bike crested the rim. A third. Then the low, grinding hum of something heavier, and a battered cargo skiff slid into view above the canyon, its hull patched in six different colors of plate, a pair of rotary cannons swinging lazily on its bow.

"Friends of yours?" Tess said.

"Maybe," Ahsoka said. "Depends what kind of day he's having."

The bikes dropped into the canyon and circled them, close enough that grit stung her face. The riders were Weequay, leathery and grinning, in mismatched armor and goggles, rifles slung forward. One of them shouted something in a dialect she half knew. She stood up slowly with her hands open and stepped out from the rock so they could see her, and so Tess could stay behind it.

The skiff came down last. A figure stood at the rail with one boot up on the gunwale like a man posing for a holo he'd commissioned himself: long coat, goggles pushed up on a patterned head-wrap, a face like weathered stone split by an enormous, disbelieving smile.

Hondo Ohnaka spread his arms wide.

"No," he said. "No, no. I refuse. This is a trick of the fumes. I have been breathing the geysers for too long and now I am seeing ghosts. Little Jedi?" He leaned so far over the rail that one of his crew grabbed the back of his coat. "Is that you, or is it a very sad impostor?"

"It's me, Hondo."

"It *is* you!" He vaulted the rail, landed badly, made a show of it being on purpose, and strode toward her with his hands still spread. He stopped an arm's length away and looked her over the way a dealer looks over a speeder he suspects has been in a crash. "Look at you. Grey. Everything grey. Who dressed you? And so thin. They don't feed people where you have been, I think." His gaze dropped to her throat, and the smile changed shape without leaving his face. "Ah. And that is not jewelry."

"No," she said.

"No." He clicked his tongue. "You know, it is a very strange thing. Only this morning I am sitting in my chair, drinking something I stole, and the Holonet tells me that the famous Temple bomber has been rescued by her friends the Separatists and is now sitting at Count Dooku's right hand, eating his little cakes. And I think, well, good for her, she always had spirit. And then my scouts tell me a pod has come down in the Red Narrows, and I come to see what I can take off it, and—" He gestured at her with both hands, as though presenting her to an audience. "Here is the Temple bomber, in my canyon, eating no cakes at all."

"I didn't bomb the Temple."

"I did not ask." He held up a finger. "But I notice that if you were with Dooku, you would not be here. So someone has lost you. And when someone loses a thing, someone else is looking for it, and when someone is looking for a thing, the thing has a price." He began counting on his fingers, brows lifting with each one. "The Republic wants you back. They have not even posted a number yet, which frankly I find insulting on your behalf. Count Dooku, apparently, wanted you in the first place. And whoever put you in that pod and then failed to keep you in it is now very embarrassed and very angry, and embarrassed people pay the best of all." He looked at his three raised fingers, then raised a fourth with great ceremony. "Then there is the fourth party."

"Who's the fourth party?"

"Me!" He beamed. "I am the one who has you."

"You're thinking about selling me."

"Of course I am thinking about it. I am a pirate. I think about selling everything. My boots. This canyon. My crew, frequently." Behind him, one of the riders made a rude gesture, and Hondo waved it off without looking. "Thinking is free. Selling is a matter of timing, and I never sell before I know what a thing is worth. Before that, it is just a guest."

Tess stepped out from behind the rock. Two rifles swung toward her. She looked at them the way she looked at guards who got too close in the mess line, which was to say like they were a weather condition.

Hondo's eyebrows went up. "And who is this? Your grandmother?"

"Her neighbor," Tess said. "Next cell over. Four months. And before you start counting your fingers again, I'm worth nothing to anybody, so save yourself the arithmetic."

"Everyone is worth something," Hondo said generously. "Some people are only worth very little."

"Bane put us in that pod," Ahsoka said.

Hondo went still.

It lasted less than a heartbeat. Then the smile came back, wider and with more teeth in it. "Cad Bane."

"He's the one who lost me. He said he'd be back for me in a day or two."

"In *my* canyon. Without asking." Hondo laid a hand on his chest as if wounded. "This changes things. Now it is personal, and personal is always more expensive." He turned and snapped his fingers at the skiff. "Water! Blankets! Something to eat that is not still moving! Our guests have come a long way." Then, quieter, to her, almost an afterthought: "You are shaking, little Jedi."

She looked down. She was. She hadn't noticed.

He took off his long coat and put it around her shoulders. It smelled of engine grease and sweet smoke and something fermented. She did not say anything, because if she said anything she might do something embarrassing, and he did not say anything either, and she was more grateful for the silence than for the coat.

"This is a loan," he said, walking her toward the skiff. "I will want it back."

* * *

Hondo's base had been rebuilt since the last time she'd seen it—or rather, it had been patched, which was not the same thing. New plating sat over old scorch marks. A gun emplacement Grievous had flattened had been replaced by two smaller ones, both painted a cheerful orange for reasons nobody explained. Pirates lounged on crates and argued over sabacc in the shade of the landing pad. Everything smelled of fuel and sulfur and cooking oil.

They fed her first. She ate a bowl of something spiced and brown with her hands trembling, then made herself slow down when her stomach cramped. Tess ate beside her with the steady, unhurried efficiency of someone who had eaten every meal for nine years as if it might be taken away.

Then Hondo brought them down a ramp into a workshop that ran the length of a hangar bay, crowded with half-stripped swoops, droid torsos hung from hooks like smoked meat, and a long bench under a ring of work lamps.

The mechanic's name was Juko. He was a Weequay older than Hondo, his skin creased deep as dry riverbed, with a jeweler's loupe strapped over his right eye on a band of cracked leather and fingers stained black to the second knuckle. He wore a heavy welding apron scorched through in places and chewed slowly on something that he never swallowed. He did not say hello. He looked at the collar, then at her, then back at the collar, and held out his hand for her to sit.

She lay back on the bench with a rolled tarp under her neck. The lamps were hot on her face.

Juko ran a hand-scanner around the collar twice, peering through the loupe at the readout. His chewing stopped.

"Judiciary," he said. "New. Ugly work. Good work, but ugly."

"Can you get it off?" Ahsoka asked.

"Off, yes." He tapped the band near the seam, just beneath the root of her left lekku, and she felt the vibration in her teeth. "There is a charge cell inside. Here. Runs both ways around. If someone cuts the band without the proper key, the cell empties into you."

"Into me," she repeated.

"Into the neck." He tapped his own, thoughtfully. "Maybe you sleep for a day. Maybe your heart forgets what it is for. Depends on the charge. This one—" he squinted at the scanner, "—this one is full."

Tess, standing at the foot of the bench, said, "Hells."

"The key's in Bane's coat," Ahsoka said. "The warden's terminal was keyed to it too, and he shot that to pieces."

"Then no key." Juko shrugged. "So we drain the cell first. Small hole, here, to reach the line. A clip to ground it. Then I cut both sides, quickly, while it is cold, before it notices." He said it like the collar was an animal.

"And if the clip slips?" Tess said.

Juko considered the question with real care. "Then she will have a very bad afternoon."

From the doorway, Hondo said, "He has done this before. Many times. Almost never on necks, but the principle is the same."

"That's not helping, Hondo."

"I am not trying to help, I am trying to reassure. They are different arts." He came in and dragged a stool over, sat on it backward, and folded his arms on the backrest so his chin rested on them. "If it helps, I will not let him kill you. You are far too valuable dead—no. Alive. I meant alive. You are far too valuable alive."

She almost laughed. It came out as a breath.

Tess pulled up a crate and sat by her head. After a moment Ahsoka felt Tess's left hand find hers—the one with the two frostbitten fingers, stiff and dark to the second joint—and grip it hard.

"Don't break my good one," Tess said. "I only have the one."

Juko packed cold gel around the band, so cold it bit, and set a thin bar of chilled metal against her throat to keep the heat off her skin. Then he lifted a drill no thicker than a stylus.

"Do not move," he said. "Do not swallow if you can help it."

The drill whined. She felt it as pressure first, then a high thin vibration in the bones behind her eyes. She fixed her gaze on the ring of lamps and counted them. Eight. One flickered. She counted them again.

The whine changed pitch.

The status light under her jaw, the small hard blue bump she had felt every day since the transport, flickered. Then it turned amber.

A tone sounded from somewhere inside the collar—two short pulses, thin and polite, like a door chime.

"Juko," Hondo said, not theatrical at all.

"I know." The mechanic's hands did not speed up. A tool clicked. "I know. Hold."

Something bit down at the base of her skull.

It was not pain at first. It was white—a white sheet thrown over the world, every lamp blooming at once, her whole spine snapping straight like a cable pulled taut. She heard Tess swear from very far away. Her hand clenched so hard on Tess's fingers that something in them popped. Her heels drummed once against the bench and she could not make them stop.

"*Hold her*," Juko said, and hands—Hondo's, she thought, rings and all—pressed her shoulders flat.

Then a clip snapped home, and the white drained out of the world as fast as it had come.

She was lying on the bench breathing in short tearing gasps, tasting metal. The amber light was dark. Somewhere a cooling fan ticked.

"Grounded," Juko said. He did not sound relieved. He sounded like a man noting the weather. "The cell is empty now. Mostly. You are fine?"

"I don't know," she said. Her voice came out cracked. "Ask me later."

"Later is good. Later means alive." He lifted a cutter with a blade like a silver fingernail. "Now we cut. This part is only loud."

It was loud. The cutter screamed against the band on the left side and she felt heat bloom through the gel, sharp and sudden, and something stung beneath her jaw like a spark had landed there. She hissed. Juko muttered an apology to the collar, not to her. He switched sides. The cutter screamed again. She stared at the lamps. Tess's fingers were still in hers, and Tess had not let go even after the pop.

Then Juko set down the cutter, slid two flat bars into the cuts, and pried.

The collar opened like a jaw.

For half a breath, nothing happened. She lay there with cold air on the raw skin of her neck, waiting, afraid to want anything.

And then the Force came back.

Not a whisper. Not an echo. It came the way morning comes over a ridgeline, all at once and everywhere, sunlight through a door someone had kicked open. It poured into her through every crack she hadn't known she had. She felt the stone of the base around her, ancient and warm and patient. She felt the geysers beneath the canyon, slow pulses of heat like the breath of something sleeping. She felt Juko beside her, careful and dry as old leather, already thinking about the next job. She felt Hondo, a big bright untidy blaze of greed and curiosity and an affection he would have denied at blasterpoint. She felt every pirate in the yard above, every swearing card player, every bored lookout, the small quick heartbeat of some creature nesting in the hangar beams.

She felt herself. She had forgotten how that felt—to be a shape in the light instead of a hole in the dark.

She did not decide to cry. It simply happened, the way breathing had happened in the Well. Her shoulders shook and the tears ran sideways off her face into the tarp, and she put her free hand over her eyes and could not stop, and for once she didn't try.

Nobody said anything for a while.

Then Hondo cleared his throat loudly and stood up. "The fumes in here are terrible," he announced. "Juko, you must do something about the ventilation. My eyes are very irritated." He wiped them with the heel of his hand and glared at a pirate in the doorway until the pirate found something else to look at.

When she could sit up, Juko smeared burn gel across her neck. Where the collar had been, the skin was red and rubbed raw all the way around, a ring the width of two fingers. Under the left side of her jaw was a small round burn the size of a credit chip where the cutter's spark had landed. He pressed a patch over it with his thumb.

"Scar, maybe," he said. "Small one."

"I don't mind."

She reached for Tess with the Force, without meaning to, the way you'd turn your head toward a familiar voice. What she found surprised her. Tess was knotted up tight, a fist of a person, and underneath the relief there was something heavy turning over and over, like a coin being worried between fingers in a pocket. Fear, Ahsoka thought. Of course it was fear. Bane was coming, and Tess had nothing to fight him with but a cracked sounder and a sharp tongue.

"I hurt your hand," Ahsoka said.

Tess flexed it. One of the frostbitten fingers was already swelling. "Not the first time somebody's broken something of mine." She managed a crooked smile. "Worth it. You look like somebody turned the lights on in you, kid."

Hondo had picked the collar up off the bench with two fingers, the way someone might lift a dead thing by the tail. He turned it over in the lamplight, studying the seam, the dark status light, the neat black hole Juko had drilled.

"This," he said, "I keep."

Ahsoka looked at it in his hand. Four months of her life, broken open in a pirate's workshop.

"Why?"

"Because somebody will want to know how it works. Somebody always does." He tucked it into his belt. "The Republic, to get it back. The Separatists, to build their own. A third person I have not thought of yet, who will pay the most of all."

"Don't ever put that on anyone."

He looked genuinely offended. "On whom? I have no Jedi." Then he looked at her, the raw ring at her throat, and something in his face went briefly quiet. "Except, it seems, one. And she is a guest."

* * *

The trophy room was at the top of the base, behind a door with three locks, two of which Hondo opened with keys and one of which he opened by kicking it in a particular place.

It was not a large room. It was crammed with things: a Mandalorian helmet with a blaster hole through the visor, a crate of crystal statuettes from some looted palace, a painting of a Hutt that looked like it had been used for target practice, and, along one wall in a glass case lined with dark red cloth, lightsaber hilts.

She stopped in front of the case. There were seven of them. Each one was laid on its own little cushion like a jewel in a shop window.

"You've been busy," she said.

"I am a collector," Hondo said with dignity. "It is a hobby, and like all hobbies, it is expensive and occasionally gets people killed." He unlocked the case and stood back, hands clasped behind him. "Choose one. No—I will choose. You would pick the most valuable, because you have good taste, and then I would be sad."

He reached past two ornate hilts and lifted out a third.

It was a plain thing: a brushed steel body, a ridged black grip worn shiny where a hand had held it for years, an emitter shroud with a small dent on one side. Near the pommel was a rough patch of metal where something—a mark, a name, a symbol—had been filed away.

He held it out to her across both palms.

"This," he said, his voice dropping into the rich, dramatic register of a man who had told a story many times, "I took from a Jedi Master. A terrible one. Huge. Very old, very fierce. We fought for an entire day atop the great geyser at Kesh Falls, and at the end, as the steam rose around us, he knelt and handed me his blade, and he said, 'Hondo Ohnaka, I have never met your equal.' And then he wept."

"He wept."

"Copiously."

She took the hilt.

It was heavier than her own main blade and longer in the grip. She turned it over, feeling the balance, and then, carefully, she reached into it with the Force the way she would have reached into her own crystal on a long night in the field.

The crystal was there. It was quiet, and it was blue, and it had been asleep a long time. There was nothing in it of a duel on a geyser, nothing of rage or surrender. What she felt instead was something almost gentle: long hours of stillness, a patient hand, the faint echo of a single person who had carried it for many years and had put it down, at the end, without any violence at all. Whoever had owned this had not lost it to Hondo. Whoever had owned this had probably never met Hondo.

She looked up at him. He looked back at her, beaming, innocent as a child.

"A terrible Jedi Master," she said.

"The worst."

"Huge."

"Enormous." He held up a hand well above his own head. Then he seemed to consider something and lowered it a little. "Well—big in spirit. Physically, he was actually quite small. Very small. That is what made it so frightening."

"Hondo."

"What?"

"You bought this from a salvage dealer."

He put a hand over his heart. "You wound me. I would never pay for a lightsaber." He paused. "I traded for it. A very fair trade. Some of the people involved are still alive."

She turned away so he wouldn't see her smile, thumbed the activator, and the blade sprang out with a snap-hiss that she felt in her chest.

Blue light filled the little room, washing over the cracked painting and the shot-through helmet and Hondo's delighted face. The hum was a fraction lower than her own sabers, and the color was wrong—every instinct she had expected green—but it was alive, and the crystal stirred a little under her attention, like something rolling over in its sleep.

She shifted her grip, flipping the blade into a reverse hold, and took two slow steps through a form she'd practiced a thousand times. The weight pulled at her wrist. A single blade felt lopsided after years of two. She would have to learn it again.

That was all right. She had learned harder things lately.

"Blue," Hondo said, admiring. "It suits you. Very dignified. Very respectable. Nobody will ever believe a girl carrying a blue saber blew up a temple."

She deactivated it. The room went dim and orange again.

"Why are you doing this?"

"Doing what?"

"You could sell me to anyone. You said so yourself. Bane's coming. You could just hand me over when he lands and spare yourself the trouble."

Hondo pursed his lips and looked at the ceiling for a while, as if the answer might be written on it.

"Bane," he said at last, "would not pay me. Bane would shoot me, take you, and steal my good chair on the way out. This I know from experience. The Republic would pay, but slowly, with forms. Count Dooku—" he wobbled a hand, "—Count Dooku and I have a history, and it was not a friendly history, and he still owes me for a ransom that went very badly for everyone." He shrugged expansively. "And you. You are a guest. You brought me a mystery and a reason to annoy Cad Bane, and that is worth something too."

"That's not a real answer."

"It is the answer I am giving." He waved at the saber in her hand. "That is a loan. I will want it back, with interest. The interest is that you do not die, because the dead are terrible at returning things."

She looked at him a moment longer, and through the Force she felt it again: under all that noise and gold-plated nonsense, a stubborn, unreasonable warmth that he would have sold his own mother rather than admit to.

"Thank you, Hondo."

"Yes, yes." He was already ushering her toward the door. "Do not thank me. Thanking is how people start expecting things."

On the stair down, they passed Tess coming up.

She'd changed out of her prison greys into a stained pirate's jacket two sizes too big, and she had a cup of something steaming in her good hand. Her swollen finger had been splinted to its neighbor with a strip of tape.

"Ohnaka," she said. "That big dish on the north tower. That reach off-world?"

"It reaches everywhere," Hondo said. "For a price."

"What's the price?"

He looked at her with fresh interest. "Who are you calling, grandmother?"

"Nobody yet." Tess sipped her drink. "Just like to know where the exits are. Old habit. Prison does that." Her free hand was in her jacket pocket. Ahsoka noticed it there and thought nothing of it; Tess had always kept one hand hidden, in the cut, in the mess, in the cell. It was where she kept whatever she was trading that week.

"There are no exits on Florrum," Hondo said cheerfully. "Only very long walks."

* * *

By evening the sky had gone the color of old brass, and the geysers out in the canyons threw up plumes that caught the last of the light and turned it orange.

Ahsoka sat cross-legged on the edge of the landing pad with the borrowed saber across her knees and her eyes closed.

She was not meditating, exactly. She was just listening. She had spent all those months in the ice learning to sit with nothing, to find her breath when there was no Force to lean on, and now that it was back she found she did not want to rush into it. She let it come to her a little at a time. The warm stone. The pirates arguing over cards behind her. The engines cooling. A lookout up on the north tower humming badly to himself.

The raw ring around her neck stung in the evening wind. She touched the patch under her jaw and let the sting be what it was.

Footsteps behind her, uneven. Tess lowered herself onto a crate nearby with a grunt.

"Hondo's people say the last ship through here was three days ago," Tess said. "Smugglers. Gone again."

"Bane said a day or two."

"Bane says a lot of things. He likes to hear himself drawl." Tess pulled her jacket tighter, though the evening was warm. "What'll you do, kid? Now you've got your little torch back."

Ahsoka opened her eyes and looked out at the canyon.

"Find out who did it," she said. "Who killed Letta. Who killed those three troopers with my sabers. Who was in that warehouse." She turned the hilt over in her hands. "The Republic convicted me because they needed someone. Tarkin as good as told me. If I can find whoever it really was, then maybe they don't get to need me anymore."

"And then what? You go back to your Temple?"

The question landed somewhere she hadn't expected. She thought of the Council chamber. Windu's hand at her montral, lifting away her beads. Plo Koon looking at the floor.

"I don't know," she said.

Tess was quiet for a while. When she spoke again her voice was rougher. "You know what I've learned in nine years of being guilty? Nobody gets cleared. Not in this galaxy. You get bought out, or you get forgotten, or you get lucky. That's the list. There's no fourth thing."

"There was a fourth thing in the ice," Ahsoka said. "Four minutes. I could have walked out."

"I know. I was there." Tess looked at her strangely. "You didn't."

"No."

"That's the difference between you and me, kid." Tess's hand moved in her pocket. "I'd have walked."

Ahsoka reached for her through the Force, gently, not prying. She found that same tight knot, the same coin turning over and over. It felt sadder than it had in the workshop. She took it for fear, and for the particular weariness of an old woman who had been locked up for nine years and was now loose in a galaxy that had moved on without her.

"You did walk," Ahsoka said. "You walked out of Bane's hold. You opened my cuffs. I wouldn't be here without you."

Tess didn't answer. She drank from her cup and looked away, out at the geysers, and her jaw worked as if she were chewing on something that wouldn't go down.

Then the Force *changed*.

It was not loud. It was a single cold note threaded through the warm evening, like a blade drawn slowly from a sheath somewhere just out of sight. Ahsoka's head came up. The hair along her lekku prickled. She knew that feeling. She had felt it in a lower-level alley with the rain coming down, beside her in the dark of a stairwell, across the width of a burning warehouse. Sharp, bitter, and wound tight around something hurt.

She was on her feet before she knew she'd moved, the borrowed hilt in her hand.

"Kid?" Tess said.

Up on the north tower, the humming stopped. The lookout shouted something. Then another voice in the yard, and another, and the clatter of rifles being snatched up off sabacc tables.

Down at the end of the landing pad, where the canyon road came up through the outer gate, a single figure was walking out of the dusk.

Cloaked. Hooded. Unhurried. She didn't run and she didn't raise her hands. She walked up the middle of the road as though Hondo's base was a public street and the dozen blasters swinging toward her were somebody else's problem. A pirate stepped into her path and she simply looked at him, and he stepped back out of it.

Hondo appeared on the balcony above the hangar in a borrowed coat that didn't fit him, a pistol in each hand. "Whoever you are," he called down, "you are trespassing, and I charge for that!"

The figure stopped where the floodlights began. Pale hands rose and pushed the hood back.

Bald head, ghost-white skin. Dark tattoo lines down the chin. The purple bruise Ahsoka remembered from the night of the warehouse was long gone, but the eyes were exactly the same: cold, amused, and very tired.

At her belt hung two empty clips where a pair of curved hilts used to be.

Asajj Ventress swept her gaze across the circle of pirates, past Hondo on his balcony, past Tess on her crate, and let it come to rest on Ahsoka standing at the edge of the pad with a stranger's blue saber in her fist.

"I heard," Ventress said, in a voice like silk dragged over a knife, "that there was a Jedi for sale on Florrum." Her lip curled. "I see the merchandise has been damaged."

Ahsoka's thumb found the activator.

"I'm not for sale," she said.

"Everyone's for sale, little girl." Ventress smiled, and it did not reach anywhere near her eyes. "The only question is who's buying, and what they've already paid."

## Chapter 19: Old Wounds

The blue blade snapped on between them with a sound like a held breath let go, and Ventress, who had walked unarmed into a pirate nest on purpose, found that her first feeling was not fear but offense. Somebody had handed Tano a lightsaber that sang like a lullaby. It hummed in her fist, placid and blue and completely wrong for her, and she held it in that backward grip as though the hilt had insulted her personally.

"Put that away before you hurt yourself," Ventress said. "It doesn't even like you."

"It doesn't have to like me." Tano didn't lower it. There was a raw ring around her throat, angry pink against the orange, and a small burn under the left side of her jaw. Collar scars. Fresh ones. "What do you want?"

"At the moment? A chair. Something to drink that won't strip the paint off my teeth." Ventress let her gaze drift up to the balcony. "And for your host to stop pointing two pistols at me. One is a threat. Two is just showing off."

"Two," Hondo called down, "is called being prepared! You are the one who walked into my house with no weapons at all. Either you are very stupid or you are very dangerous, and I have heard about you, so I know which." He beamed. "Also, I am told you owe me money."

"I owe you nothing."

"Not yet. But the night is young."

The pirates laughed, a few of them, nervously. None of them lowered their rifles. Ventress could feel each of them in the Force like coals in a grate, little heats of greed and fear and drink. The old woman on the crate was another kind of heat, steadier, banked low. And Tano —

Tano was a bonfire somebody had just finished putting out and relighting. The Force poured off her in uneven pulses, too much and then too little, the way blood comes back into a numb hand. Ventress had felt that once in herself, after Dooku had thrown her away and she'd lain half-dead on a ship deck finding out she was still alive. She did not care for the memory, or for recognizing it in someone else.

"You didn't answer me," Tano said.

"I want," Ventress said, "the same thing you want, little girl. I want the creature who wore my face in that warehouse." She touched the empty clips on her belt with two fingers, lightly, the way one touches a bruise to see if it still hurts. It did. "And I want my sabers back. You can keep the Jedi lullaby."

The blade wavered. Only a little. But Ventress had spent her life watching for the moment an opponent's guard thinned, and she saw it.

"Hondo," Tano said, without looking away. "Is there somewhere we can talk?"

"There is everywhere you can talk. It is my base. Every room in it is available, at very reasonable rates." He holstered one pistol, considered, and kept the other. "Juko! Take our new guest's cloak. Check it for knives. Then check it again, because she will have hidden them better the first time."

* * *

There had been four knives. Juko found three. Ventress let him keep them as a professional courtesy.

Hondo's idea of a private room was a storage bay off the hangar stacked with crates of stolen ration bars and a single flickering lamp. Someone had dragged in two chairs and a table that stood on three legs and an old fuel canister. Tano sat with the borrowed saber on the table in front of her, unlit, one hand never more than a few centimeters from it. The old woman, Tess, had followed them in uninvited and settled on a crate by the door with her bandaged hand in her lap, and Tano hadn't told her to go. Ventress noted that, and filed it.

"Start with Coruscant," Tano said.

So Ventress did, though she gave it in pieces and kept the worst of it for herself.

She had not gone to Ord Mantell. The flight plan she'd filed with the Marrow Lark's captain had been a gift to Skywalker, who would waste three days on it, and she hoped he had enjoyed every one. Grell had dropped her at a fuel stop in the Bith systems with a hold full of illegal spice and a firm request never to see her again. From there she had worked. Collected bounties. Slept badly. Bought information with the credits and spent the information on more questions.

Because she had spent her whole life being the thing in the dark, and in that warehouse something had come out of a darker dark than hers and put her on the floor. It had moved like a Jedi. Not like Kenobi — no wit in it, no flourish — but with that same infuriating serenity, the whole body turning around a still center. Only the center hadn't been still. That was what had stayed with her through the months, waking her at odd hours in odd ports: the feel of it in the Force, the half second before the blow landed. Calm, yes. Cool water. And beneath the water something gone soft and wrong, like a pool in a garden with a dead thing at the bottom.

"A Jedi," Tano said quietly.

"A Jedi." Ventress leaned back until the chair creaked. "Or something raised by them. You can wash the training out of your hands, but you can't wash it out of your spine. I should know."

Tano looked at the table. Her lekku had gone very still.

"Before I left Coruscant," Ventress went on, "I found the man who sold the nano-droids. It took me two nights and one broken arm — his bodyguard's, not mine. Rodian. Dalo Pesk. He kept a stall in the Kesh Arcade. Forty-one, with a shutter that screamed."

"Pesk," Tano said. The name meant nothing to her; Ventress watched it mean nothing.

"He remembered the buyer, because she paid in clean credits and Pesk had never seen a clean credit before in his life. Hooded. Spoke very little. Came twice. The first time in gloves. The second time she'd forgotten them, or didn't think a Rodian salvage dealer was worth hiding from." Ventress turned her own hand over on the table, white and long-fingered in the bad light. "Green hands, he said. Green skin, with markings across the backs of them. A pattern. Neat, he said, like somebody had done it with a ruler."

"Lots of species have —"

"Do you know, that's exactly what I told him. He said all you soft-skinned types look the same to him anyway." Ventress's mouth twisted. "He had a cam over the stall. A recording. He wanted two thousand for it, and I didn't have two thousand, and before I could go and take two thousand from somebody who didn't need it, your Skywalker kicked the door in on half the Undercroft looking for me. I had a choice between a datacard and my neck." She shrugged. "When I could risk Coruscant again, weeks later, the stall was shut and Pesk was dead. No mark on him, they said in the arcade. Hands folded in his lap like a good boy. Cam gone. Datapads gone."

"Somebody cleaned up," Tess said from the door.

"Somebody very tidy." Ventress didn't look at her. She was looking at Tano.

Because something had happened in Tano's face — or not in her face, which was holding perfectly level, but beneath it, where Ventress could feel it. It was like watching someone step onto ice and feel it shift. A small, sharp, involuntary recoil. Not from Ventress. From a thought. Tano's eyes had gone, for the space of a heartbeat, somewhere far away, to some memory with green hands in it — and then she flinched from it so hard Ventress felt the snap in the Force, the way one feels a door slammed two rooms over.

Tano picked up the borrowed saber and put it down again a centimeter to the left.

"What about the mask?" she said. Her voice was steady. Too steady. "The mask was yours. Your face. How did they get it?"

Ventress let the silence go on long enough to make clear she had noticed.

"Anyone with eyes and a Holonet feed knows my face," she said at last. "I'm famous. It's a burden." She tilted her head. "Which brings me to something you told me. That night, in the lower levels, when you were so desperate and so very trusting. You said you had contacted a friend. A friend in the Temple. And your friend sent us to that warehouse, and at that warehouse someone was waiting who knew exactly where to stand."

"No," Tano said.

"I haven't asked anything."

"You're going to. The answer's no." She met Ventress's gaze, and there was the Jedi in her, all the way down — that stubborn, brilliant, idiotic loyalty that made them so easy to kill. "My friend's in danger because of me. Someone is killing everyone who knew anything about those nano-droids. Pesk. Letta. If my friend's alive it's because they're inside the Temple where nobody can reach them. I'm not putting their name in your mouth."

"How noble."

"It's not noble. It's just what I'm doing." Tano's hand closed on the saber hilt. "So. You want your sabers. You want the person who took them. What do you need from me?"

There it was. Ventress almost smiled.

"The sabers are on Coruscant," she said. "I can feel them. Not well — it's like the ache of a pulled tooth, the place where something used to be — but I can feel the direction. Coruscant.
