And not in some gutter. Somewhere quiet. Somewhere old." She spread her hands. "I need a way into the Jedi Temple that doesn't involve fighting my way through every robed hypocrite in it. You grew up there. You know where the walls are thin."

Tano was quiet a long moment.

"And what I need," she said, "is a witness."

Ventress laughed out loud. It surprised them both.

"A witness. Me. In front of a Republic court." She wiped one eye with a knuckle. "Oh, you've grown a sense of humor in that ice box. Very good."

"I'm serious. You're the only person alive who saw the masked figure take your sabers. The tribunal said it was my accomplice. You know it wasn't. You know it knocked you out." Tano leaned forward. "I'm not asking you to like it. I'm asking you to say what happened."

"To Tarkin."

"To anyone who'll listen. If it comes to that."

"And if it comes to that, they'll arrest me on the spot."

"Probably." Tano held out her hand across the three-legged table. "Better have the real thief standing next to you when they do."

Ventress looked at the hand. Orange, ringed in old calluses, a fresh ice-burn across the palm. Not a Jedi's hand anymore, quite. Something rougher.

She thought of the half second in the warehouse. The dead thing at the bottom of the pool.

She took it.

"If you get sentimental at any point," she said, "I'm leaving."

"Same," said Tano.

* * *

Ventress did not sleep. She rarely did in places where she'd been searched, and Hondo's base had a hundred noises she didn't know yet — the boom of the geysers out in the canyons, the drip of condensation from the hangar ribs, pirates singing a song about a Hutt that seemed to have no last verse. She found a ledge on the outer wall above the landing pad, out of the floodlights, and sat there with her knees drawn up and watched the sulfur sky go from yellow to rust-brown to a sickly, starless black.

Below her, Tano had fallen asleep sitting up against a crate with the borrowed saber across her knees. Hondo, on his way past with a bottle, stopped, considered her with his head on one side, and laid a blanket over her shoulders with a delicacy that would have ruined his reputation if Ventress had told anyone. Which she might. Later. When it was useful.

It was nearly the third hour when she saw the old woman.

Tess came out of the barracks door in her borrowed pirate's jacket and crossed the yard without hurrying, the way people do when they very much want to hurry and know it would look wrong. She went to the base of the north tower, where the big comm dish sat hunched against the dark like a beggar's bowl, and began to climb the service ladder. She climbed one-handed. The bandaged hand stayed in her jacket pocket the whole way up.

Ventress watched her the way she might watch a lizard on a wall. Idly. Without much intention.

At the top Tess crouched beside the dish's housing for a long time. When she came down, her pocket hung differently.

None of my business, Ventress told herself. The old woman was Tano's pet, not hers. Perhaps she was sending a message to some grandchild on some miserable little world. Perhaps she was stealing copper from the dish. Pirates' bases were full of people stealing from each other; it was practically the local currency.

But she noticed the small light at the base of the dish afterward — a pinprick, amber, that hadn't been there before. It blinked. Then it waited. Then it blinked again.

She watched it blink forty times before she climbed down to wake Tano. By then the sky over the eastern canyons had begun, very faintly, to scream.

* * *

The Justifier came in low and fast out of the sunrise, its engines a raw shriek off the canyon walls, and the first thing it did was shoot the north tower.

The dish went over in a cascade of sparks and wire. The amber light went with it. Ventress, already halfway across the yard with Tano stumbling awake behind her, thought: of course. Never leave the doorbell for the next visitor.

"Hondo!" Tano shouted.

"I see it, I see it!" From somewhere in the hangar came the voice of a man very offended to have been woken. "Who invited Bane? I did not invite Bane! Somebody has very bad manners and it is not me!"

Pirates spilled out of every door, half dressed, rifles in hand. The ship swung around over the pad and its ramp dropped while it was still a meter off the ground. Two figures came down first — the Aqualish, Vosh, with a heavy repeater, and a lean grey-skinned crewman with a shock-net launcher. Behind them, a third figure stepped off the ramp into the air and didn't fall. Rocket boots flared blue. A wide-brimmed hat. A long coat flapping like a carrion bird's wing.

"Mornin'," said Cad Bane, drifting down onto the landing pad. "Didn't mean to wake the household. I've come for my property, and then I'll be on my way."

"I am not your property," Tano said. The blue saber came to life in her hand.

"Wasn't talkin' to you, Commander. Not first, anyhow." Bane's red eyes moved over the pirates, the ruined tower, the yard — and stopped, for half a heartbeat, on Tess standing in the barracks doorway. He touched the brim of his hat to her.

Tess didn't move. But Ventress felt her in the Force, and all that banked, steady heat in her went suddenly cold and small.

Ah, Ventress thought. There you are.

Then Vosh opened up with the repeater, and there was no more time to think about anything.

Ventress had no sabers. She had three fewer knives than she'd come with. What she had was a pirate base full of loose objects and a lifetime's practice at making them hurt. A rifle came out of a dead-drunk Weequay's hands and into hers; she didn't bother firing it, only swung it by the barrel into Krinn's jaw as he raised the net launcher. The net went off into the air and came down over two of Hondo's men, who began cursing each other. She reached into the Force and found the repeater in Vosh's hands — heavy, hot, its power pack humming — and wrenched it up and sideways, so that his next burst stitched a line across the Justifier's own hull. He bellowed. She threw a crate of ration bars at his head. It was undignified. It worked.

Across the pad, Tano was moving toward Bane.

She wasn't good with the borrowed saber yet. Ventress could see it — the blade was a fraction too heavy for her, its balance wrong for the reverse grip, and the first pistol bolt she deflected went wild into a fuel tank instead of back at its sender. But she was fast. And she had the Force back, and she was using it like someone who had been starving for months and found a table laid. She leapt. Bane's rocket boots kicked him up and away; she followed him into the air off a stack of cargo, higher than any of the pirates could believe, and her blade sheared the tip off his hat.

"Now that," Bane drawled, landing on the hangar roof, "was a good hat."

He fired twice. She blocked both. He fired at the pirates below instead, and she had to throw herself down in front of them, deflecting, and he laughed.

"That's the trouble with you lot," he called. "Always somebody to save."

He reached into his coat. Ventress, who had been killing people for a long time, knew the gesture of a man reaching for something that wasn't a weapon and mattered more. A small black cylinder came out in his long fingers. He pointed it at Tano, quite casually, and pressed it.

Nothing happened.

Tano straightened slowly. She touched the raw ring around her neck with two fingers, the way Ventress had touched her empty clips the night before.

Bane looked at the cylinder. He looked at her bare throat. And for the first time, Ventress saw him do arithmetic he didn't like.

"Huh," he said, and put the control back in his inner pocket. "Well. Worth a try."

"Hondo," Ventress said, not loudly. "That gun on the hangar roof. The big ugly one. Does it work?"

"Does it work?" Hondo appeared at her elbow out of nowhere, dragging a pirate's bandolier over one shoulder, outraged. "It is the finest gun I have ever stolen! It works magnificently, when it is pointed at something!"

"Point it at his ship."

Hondo looked at her. Then he grinned with every tooth he had, and ran.

Bane saw it too late. He'd been watching Tano, and Tano had seen Ventress's thought arrive and understood it — Ventress felt that too, the uncomfortable brush of another mind meeting hers in the Force, quick and clear. Tano went at him again, harder, driving him off the roof and back toward the pad, and Bane, for all his drawl, was retreating. His boots flared. He hung above the yard, a pistol in each hand, firing down at the blue blade that kept finding every bolt.

Ventress felt for the ground.

Florrum was a world built over its own fury. Under the landing pad, under the hangar foundations, the geysers ran through the rock in hot pressured veins, and Hondo's people had capped the nearest one years ago to feed the base's boilers. She could feel the cap. She could feel the furious old water beneath it, pushing, pushing, the way she had once felt rage beneath her own ribs.

She opened it.

The pad's edge burst upward in a column of boiling, sulfur-yellow steam, straight up into the air where Bane hovered. He screamed — a short, ugly sound, the first honest thing she'd heard out of him — and his boots stuttered and his coat smoked and he dropped out of the cloud trailing vapor, one arm wrapped across his face. He hit the deck of his own ramp hard. On the hangar roof, Hondo's great ugly gun spoke once, twice, and the Justifier's port engine housing came apart in a bright orange flower.

"Vosh!" Bane rasped. "Get us up!"

The Aqualish was already dragging Krinn up the ramp. The ship lurched skyward, listing, black smoke pouring from its side. Bane got to his knees on the closing ramp. His face was blistered down one side and his breathing tubes were hissing. He found Tano in the yard with his eyes and held her there.

Then he looked past her, to the barracks doorway, and fired.

The bolt took Tess low in the side. She folded down onto the threshold without a sound.

"Never pay twice for the same goods," Bane called down, his voice thick and raw. "Tell your friend, Jedi. Next time I come, I'll bring the collar to fit the key."

The ramp sealed. The Justifier shrieked away over the canyons, trailing smoke, until it was a dark speck against the yellow, and then nothing.

Pirates were cheering. Hondo, on the roof, was hugging his gun.

Ventress was watching Tano, who had already dropped the saber and was running toward Tess in the doorway.

* * *

It was not a killing wound. Juko said so, with his loupe pushed up on his forehead and his black-stained fingers packing the burn with something that smelled like engine grease and probably was. It had cooked through flesh and missed everything important. She would live, if she lay still and the fever didn't take her. Bane, Ventress suspected, had missed on purpose. Dead traitors made no point. Live ones did.

Tess lay on a cot in the infirmary — a corner of the barracks with more bottles than bandages — with her white hair plastered to her forehead and her bad hand on her chest. Tano sat beside her on an upturned crate. Ventress leaned in the doorway and did not offer to help.

"You don't have to say it," Tess said. Her voice was very thin. "I know you know."

"Say it anyway," said Tano.

Tess closed her eyes.

"The power cell," she said. "From the crates on his ship, at Narkis. It wasn't a power cell. It was one of his homing tags — he puts them on cargo so he can find it again if somebody steals it. I knew what it was when I saw it. I've been in the trade forty years." A breath, shallow and careful. "Its range was rubbish. Needed a big dish. So I asked you about the dish. And you told me."

Tano didn't move.

"He'd left a channel open on it. In case any of his goods wanted to sell themselves back. Funny man." Tess's mouth twitched, though it wasn't a smile. "I called him last night. Told him where you were. He wired the first half before he even got here." Her good hand fumbled at her jacket. A credit chit came out, scuffed and ordinary. "There's enough on here to buy a ship. A small one. And a name that isn't on any Judiciary list. That's what he promised. A name."

"You were going to let him take me," Tano said.

"I was going to let him take you," Tess agreed. "Yes."

The room was very quiet. Out in the yard, the pirates were still celebrating; somebody had found the Hutt song again.

"Sixty-one," Tess said at last. "I'm sixty-one, kid. I was going to die in that ice. I'd made my peace with it, near enough. And then you came, and you held up a mountain with your mind, and I saw what you were worth and I thought —" She stopped. "I'm not going to say I'm sorry. I'd be lying, and you'd know. I'm sorry I got shot. That's about as far as I go."

Ventress waited for the Jedi to come out — the forgiveness, the soft speech about second chances. She had heard it from Kenobi often enough to recite it.

It didn't come.

Tano looked at the credit chit in Tess's hand for a long time. Then she reached out and folded Tess's fingers — the broken one, the two frostbitten ones, all of them — closed around it.

"Keep it," she said. "You earned it. That's what you'd tell me, isn't it? Never let anyone pay you less than you're worth."

Tess's eyes opened. They were wet.

"Hondo will look after you until you can stand," Tano said. "He'll charge you for it. You can afford it now." She stood. "Then buy your ship. Buy your name. Go somewhere warm."

"Kid —"

"Goodbye, Tess."

She walked past Ventress in the doorway without stopping. Her face was perfectly composed. Her lekku were not; they had drawn in tight against her shoulders like something bracing against cold, and in the Force she felt like a bruise somebody was still pressing.

Ventress followed her out. She told herself it was because she wanted to see what the girl did next. That was nearly true.

Hondo's comm room sat beneath the wreck of the north tower, half its panels dark from the blast. Hondo met them at the hatch, rubbing his hands.

"A great victory," he said. "Bane runs away crying, my gun is a hero, and only some of my men are injured. I will be generous and not charge you for the dish." He paused. "I will charge you for the geyser cap."

"Does the short-range array still reach the relay at the edge of the system?" Tano asked.

"Does it — of course it does. It is a very fine array. Why?"

"I need to send something. Very small."

Hondo looked at her face and, for once, said nothing clever. He stood aside.

Tano sat down at the console. Ventress watched her fingers move — not typing a message, nothing so foolish, but building a string of numbers, short bursts and pauses, a pattern that meant nothing at all to Ventress's eye. It had the shape of something drilled in. Something from a different life.

"What is that?" Ventress said.

"Christophsis. My first week." Tano didn't look up. "The 501st had a code for an officer cut off from her unit. Position, condition, whether she could still fight. Rex made me memorize it in case I ever got myself lost." A breath that wasn't a laugh. "He said with my sense of direction it was only a matter of time."

"Your clone captain. The one who's hunting you."

"The one they've ordered to hunt me." She hesitated over the last sequence. "If anyone in the army still knows that code, it's him. And if he's the one who answers, it'll be because he wants to."

"And if it's not him who answers?"

"Then I'll have told the Republic exactly where I am." Tano's finger hovered over the transmit key. "And we'll leave tonight anyway."

She sent it.

The array clicked and hummed and fell quiet. Somewhere out past the yellow sky, a burst of numbers went out into the dark toward a man who still called her Commander.

Tano sat looking at the dead console with her hands in her lap. Ventress watched her. She was thinking — she couldn't help it — about the storage bay the night before. The lamp. The table on its three legs. The moment she'd said green hands, and that brilliant, stubborn, loyal mind had reached for something and slammed the door on it so hard the whole Force rang.

A friend in the Temple, Ventress thought. Someone who knew where we would be.

Someone with green hands.

She didn't know the name. Tano did — and wouldn't let herself.

Ventress settled her shoulders against the hatch frame and said nothing. There was no hurry. She had been hunting a ghost with green hands for months. It had never occurred to her until now that the girl might have trusted the ghost with her life, and thanked it for the trouble.

## Chapter 20: The Dead Man

The dead man wanted to meet at the bottom of the hole he'd died in.

Rex read the second ping three times to be sure. It had come in at 0210 the night before, on the same false channel as the first: a Coruscant Guard power-cell fault, a class of fault that hadn't existed for two years, reported from a substation grid that didn't exist either. Run through the old check-in cipher, the grid became a place, and the place became a joke only two men in the galaxy would get. *Outflow chamber, Seventh Refinery. Where the shaft comes out. 0300 tomorrow. Bring a medkit and don't bring anybody.* Signed the same way as before. 5554. Still hurt.

He sat on the edge of his bunk aboard the *Resolute* with the cracked cadet datapad on his knee and the deck humming under his boots, and he did not play the video again. Eleven times was enough. He knew it the way he knew his own rifle: forty seconds, a white room on Kamino, a medical droid's flat voice, Fives strapped to a table and awake, talking through his teeth while something no bigger than a fingernail was lifted out of his skull. *Rex. If they tell you I'm crazy, look at this first.* The rest of what Fives had sent was scans and embryo charts and columns of Kaminoan medical shorthand that might as well have been written in Huttese. Kix could read it. Rex couldn't. And he hadn't decided yet whether he had the right to put that weight on Kix.

The *Resolute* should have been in hyperspace eighteen hours ago.

She was still sitting in her cradle at the fleet yards because Major Teva Rusk's team had arrived at dawn with a sealed case the Judiciary refused to let anyone else touch, and the Judiciary's paperwork had to be stamped by the Admiralty, and the Admiralty's stamp had to be countersigned by an office that answered to Admiral Tarkin, and Admiral Tarkin, it turned out, was at a dinner. Rex had watched General Skywalker take that news in the hangar with his jaw locked and his mechanical hand closing slowly on nothing. Then the General had turned around and walked away without a word, which was worse than shouting.

Rex wasn't sorry about the delay. He was ashamed of not being sorry, and he decided he could live with both.

He was pulling on the plain brown work jacket he kept at the bottom of his locker — no rank, no colors, the kind of thing a clone wore on a maintenance detail — when the door chimed.

He shoved the datapad under his pillow. "Open."

General Skywalker stood in the doorway in his dark tunic, no cloak, eyes shot through with red. He looked at the jacket. "Going somewhere, Captain?"

"Supply depot on the lower ring, sir. The med stores came up short. Kix wants more fever shots for the boys before Narkis." It wasn't even entirely a lie. Rex had signed for twelve injectors that afternoon. One of them was already in his jacket.

"At two in the morning."

"Depot clerks are friendlier at two in the morning. They want you gone."

That got the corner of the General's mouth, barely. He leaned on the frame. "Tarkin's office says we'll have clearance by noon. Noon. She could be halfway to Serenno by noon."

"If she was going to Serenno, sir."

"I know she's not going to Serenno." It came out too fast. The General looked at the floor and visibly put something away. "Windu thinks she is. The Holonet thinks she is. Rusk has a box with a collar in it, and she's going to walk up to Ahsoka holding it like a gift." The metal fingers flexed. "I keep trying to sleep and I keep seeing that refinery. Fives going over the edge. I was right there, Rex. I was right there and I let Fox—" He stopped. "Don't tell me that's not what happened."

Rex had spent nineteen days not telling him anything, and he found it was a muscle like any other. It got stronger with use. It also ached.

"It happened fast, sir," he said.

"Everything does, lately." The General straightened off the frame. "The Chancellor wants me to bring her to him. Not to Tarkin. Not to the Council. To him. He says that's the only way he can protect her." He said it like a man holding a rope out over a drop, wanting someone to tell him it would hold. "That's something, isn't it?"

Rex thought of the video. A white room. A Chancellor who'd taken Fives's chip and kept it.

"It's something," he said.

The General nodded as if that had been an answer, and looked at him a moment longer than he needed to. "Get your fever shots. Don't let the clerk talk you into the cheap ones."

When the door slid shut, Rex let out a breath he'd been holding since the chime.

He was reaching for the datapad again when his wrist comm gave a single, odd click. Not the Guard channel. Not the fleet net. Something older.

He knew the shape of it before he'd finished decoding it, the way you know a voice in the dark. He had written that code himself on a shipping crate on Christophsis, with a fourteen-year-old Padawan rolling her eyes at him from the other side, and he had made her repeat it until she could do it with her eyes shut. *Officer cut off from unit. Position follows. Condition: able to fight. Not alone.* The position was a relay mark in the Outer Rim, and the relay mark sat in the Florrum system, which was pirate country and nowhere near Serenno.

The signal had come up the old 501st field band, the one nobody had used since they'd changed the codes after Umbara. Nobody watched it anymore. Nobody but him. The header stamp told him the rest: the code had crawled in from the Outer Rim through public relays, one cheap booster to the next, and arrived most of a day late.

Rex sat on the bunk with the jacket half on and looked at the little line of characters until his eyes stung.

Then he copied it to the cracked datapad, scrubbed it from his comm's buffer, and went down to the lower levels to see a dead man.

* * *

The Seventh Refinery had been abandoned for longer than Rex had been alive, which wasn't saying much, and it showed every year of it.

He came in from the south side, away from the street where Fives had run that night, through a gap in a fence somebody had cut a long time ago and nobody had bothered to mend. Rain came down the inside of the great dead stacks in sheets. Everything smelled of rust and old sulfur and the sweet rot of standing water. Two blocks east, past a wall of shuttered hab blocks, stood the warehouse where all of this had started for Commander Tano. He didn't look that way. He didn't have to. He could feel it there the way you feel a wound under a bandage.

The outflow chamber was at the bottom of a long ramp of cracked ferrocrete. The drainage shaft emptied into it from far above through a mouth as wide as a gunship, and the water came out of that mouth in a steady grey curtain and roared into a channel that ran off into the dark. Rex stood at the edge of the light from his hand lamp and listened to it. He thought of how long a man would fall before he hit that water. He thought of whether he'd want to be awake for it.

"You're late," said a voice behind him.

Rex didn't turn around fast. He'd had too many years of not turning around fast. He turned slowly, lamp held low, and there he was, sitting on a rusted valve housing with his back to the wall like a man who had picked that wall very carefully.

Fives.

He was thin. That was the first thing. Not lean the way they'd all been lean, but scraped down, the bones of his face standing out under skin that had gone grey and slick with fever. Somebody had scraped his head down to stubble, badly, without a mirror. He wore a laborer's coat two sizes too large, stained along one side, and his left arm was tucked inside it against his chest. The five inked at his temple was still there. So was the grin, sort of. It looked like it hurt him to do it.

"It's two minutes past," Rex said. His voice did something on the last word.

"Two minutes is late. You used to make us run laps for two minutes."

"You used to deserve it."

Fives laughed, and the laugh turned into a cough, and the cough went on long enough that Rex crossed the space between them without deciding to and caught him by the good shoulder before he slid off the housing. Fives's body under the coat was hot as a cook plate.

"Easy," Rex said. "Easy. Sit."

"I'm sitting."

"Sit harder."

He set the lamp on the ground, pulled open the medkit and took out the first fever injector. Fives watched him with eyes that were too bright. When Rex pressed the injector to his neck, Fives didn't flinch. He just closed his eyes and let his head knock back against the wall.

"I wasn't sure," he said. "When I sent the first ping. I wasn't sure you'd come. Or that you'd come alone."

"I almost didn't."

"Which part?"

Rex didn't answer that. He pulled back the coat. Underneath, Fives had packed the shoulder wound with Coruscant Guard field dressings — stolen, from the look of the stenciled packets — and bound them in place with what had once been a shirt. The dressings were brown and stiff. The smell, when Rex peeled the edge back, made him set his jaw.

"How long since you changed these?"

"Three days. Four. I ran out." Fives's eyes stayed shut. "Lifted them off a Guard patrol kit two nights after. Comlink I already had, off a belt the night I ran. Figured they owed me."

"You're lucky you've still got the arm."

"I'm lucky I've still got the head." Fives opened one eye. "You saw me go. Didn't you."

"I saw you go."

"Fox told them I was dead."

"Fox told them your body was unrecoverable." Rex cut away the old binding and started cleaning, which gave his hands something to do. "Which, as far as he knew, was true. Nobody went down after you. I was ordered back from the edge."

"And you went."

"I went."

Fives didn't say anything to that. He didn't need to. The water roared on behind them.

"Tell me how," Rex said.

So Fives told him, in pieces, between the sting of the antiseptic and the slap of the bacta patch going on. He hadn't fallen all the way, not at first. There were rungs inside the shaft, a maintenance ladder half rusted off the wall, and he'd hit one and hung there with his good hand for a while — he didn't know how long — before his grip went. After that he didn't remember. He'd come to on a sludge shelf just above where the water came out, in the dark, half in the current, with his shoulder on fire and his helmet gone. It had taken him most of a day to crawl up to where they were sitting now. He'd lived since on what he could take: water off the stacks, ration bars out of a vending cabinet two levels up that took a kick in the right place, a blanket off a line. He'd moved every night. He'd listened to the Guard channel on the stolen comlink until the battery went low, and then he'd only listened once a day.

"I heard them say my number," he said. "On the Guard channel. Logging it closed. CT-5555, deceased, recovery not attempted." He gave that grin again. It was even worse this time. "That's a strange thing, hearing yourself filed."

Rex pressed the last patch down and held it with the heel of his hand. "Ahsoka knows what that's like."

Fives went very still.

"Tell me," Rex said quietly, "the rest."

Fives told him the rest.

He told it badly, out of order, with the fever going down and his hands starting to shake as it went, and Rex let him. He didn't interrupt. He'd learned in the field that when a man came back from something, the worst thing you could do was put the questions in the order you wanted them.

The chips were in all of them. Fives said it twice, as if he didn't trust it to stay said. Every clone, from the embryo up — grown in, not put in, the way a bone was grown in. AZI-3 had scanned Tup and found it, and scanned Fives and found it, and in the Kaminoan files the droid had pulled there were records showing it on the earliest charts. Lama Su called it a precaution. Nala Se called it nothing at all. The official story on Kamino was a parasite, and then a virus, and Rex said, "A virus from Ringo Vinda. That's what the report said." And Fives laughed until he coughed again.

"It's not a virus," Fives said. "It's not a tumor. It's a switch, Rex. AZI-3 showed me the structure. It's built to be turned on. And when Tup's switched on, he did what it told him. He kept saying it — *it was the order.* You heard him. Over and over, like a recording. He didn't hate her. He didn't hate anyone. Something reached in and moved his hand."

Rex heard Tiplee asking him on the *Resolute* — *Did he hate her?* — and his own silence in answer.

"So there's an order," Rex said.

"There's an order." Fives's good hand closed on his knee. "I don't know what it is. I don't know when. Tup's went off early — something wrong with his, AZI-3 thought. The rest of ours are waiting. Yours. Mine, before they cut it out. Jesse's. Kix's. Every man in the 501st and every man in every legion in the Republic. Sitting in our heads, waiting for somebody to say the right words."

"Who?"

"I don't know." Fives looked at him. "I know who took my chip, though. I know whose desk it went to. I was in the room. I was drugged out of my head and I watched him hold it up to the light like a jewel and put it in his pocket. And then I was the man who'd attacked the Chancellor."

The water roared. A loose sheet of metal somewhere high up the stacks banged in the wind, and banged again.

Rex realized he'd sat down on the wet ferrocrete at some point. He didn't remember doing it.

He waited for the part of himself that had been built to do this — to weigh a report, find the hole in it, send it back up the chain — to tell him Fives was sick. Fever, a fall, a man alone in the dark for nineteen days hearing his own death on the comm. Anyone would come out of that seeing switches in the walls. That was the reasonable thing. That was what the reports would say. That was what Fox would say, and the Judge Advocate, and Kamino, and the Chancellor in his warm red room.

And he'd heard reasonable before. He'd stood in a tribunal gallery and listened to reasonable for two days, a whole chamber of it, footage and forensics and an admiral's level voice, and every piece of it had been reasonable, and at the end of it a girl he'd have died for was led away to a moon made of ice. He had stood there in his armor. He had not said a word. The silence had felt like discipline at the time.

He wasn't going to stand in that gallery twice.

"I believe you," Rex said.

Fives blinked. It was such a small thing, and his whole face changed with it. He looked down quickly.

"Just like that," he said to the floor.

"Not just like that. I watched your video eleven times."

"Eleven."

"I stopped counting after eleven. That's not the same as stopping." Rex rubbed his face. "I can't read the rest of it. The files. The charts. I need Kix for that."

"Then get Kix."

"Not yet."

"Rex—"

"Not yet." He said it in his captain's voice and was sorry the moment it came out, and didn't take it back. "Kix is the best medic in this army, and he's also the man who's going to have to look every one of the boys in the eye afterward knowing what's in their heads. When I put that on him, it's going to be because I've got something for him to do about it. Not just something to carry."

Fives stared at him. Then his gaze went, slowly and deliberately, to the side of Rex's head. Just above the ear.

"You've got one," he said. "Right now. You know that."

"I know that."

"And you're going to walk back up there and stand next to General Skywalker with it."

"Yes."

"Rex, if that order comes while you're—"

"Then you'll have been right, and I'll have been a fool, and you can say so at my funeral." Rex held his eyes. "But if a captain in the 501st asks the medics for a brain scan three weeks after one of his men was declared a traitor and another was declared a lunatic, how long do you think it takes before somebody in an office notices? If I start acting different, they'll look. Right now they're not looking at me. That's the only thing I've got worth anything."

Fives's jaw worked. For a moment Rex saw him at the shuttle hatch on the *Resolute*, a lifetime ago, promising he wouldn't go quietly. "You knocked Fox's arm," he said. "I saw that. Right before I went. You knocked his arm."

"I know what I did."

"Does the General?"

"He saw it. He left it out of his report." Rex paused. "So did Fox."

Fives made a sound that might have been the start of a laugh. "Everybody's lying for everybody."

"Seems that way."

"Does Skywalker know I'm alive?"

"No."

"Are you going to tell him?"

Rex thought of the General in his doorway with his red eyes. *The Chancellor wants me to bring her to him.* He thought of the metal hand closing on nothing.

"No," he said. "Not now. Maybe not for a long time."

Fives studied him a long while. Then he nodded, once, as if Rex had passed a test he hadn't known he was taking.

Rex reached into his jacket and took out the cracked datapad, and turned it so the screen faced Fives.

"There's something else," he said.

Fives read the code twice. Rex watched him do it. The second time, his lips moved.

"That's Christophsis," Fives said. "That's the cut-off code. That's — Rex, that's *her*. She's out?"

"She's out."

So Rex told him that too: the raid on Narkis, the dead guards, Cad Bane, the Holonet bulletin that had the Temple bomber joining Count Dooku, the Council's orders, Windu's authorization of lethal force. Fives listened with his mouth open, then shut, then tight.

"Lethal force," he said.

"In the field. If necessary."

"And Skywalker's on it."

"The General's on it. General Kenobi. Master Unduli and her Padawan. And a Judiciary major who's bringing a new collar along for the trip, in case anybody forgets what she's for." Rex tapped the screen. "That came in last thing before I left, on the old band. Position's Florrum. She says she can fight, and she's not alone."

"Who's with her?"

"Don't know. Code doesn't have a box for it."

Fives looked at the code a while longer. Then he looked up, and Rex already knew what he was going to say, because it was what Rex had been thinking since the click on his wrist.

"I'm going to her."

"You can barely sit up."

"I'm sitting up fine. I'm sitting up harder, even." Fives leaned forward, and the effort of it showed. "Think about it. Who else in this galaxy is going to listen to me? Kix? You just told me you won't put it on him. The General? He's carrying the Chancellor's messages around like a holy relic. The Council?" His voice went harsh. "The Council heard *her* out and handed her to Tarkin. What do you think they'll do with a dead clone who says the Chancellor has a pocket full of switches?"

"I know."

"She'll believe me. She's the only one who will. She's been where I am." Fives's hand was shaking again, but not from the fever now. "And whoever she's with, she needs somebody at her back who isn't going to sell her the first time a better price comes along. Pirate country, Rex. You know what Florrum's like."

"I know what Florrum's like." Rex was quiet for a breath. "I'd thought of it."

"Then why are you looking at me like that?"

"Because I'd thought of it before you said it," Rex said, "and I don't like that I'm sending you."

Fives sat back against the wall. "You're not sending me. I'm going. You're just paying for it."

"Am I."

"You've got three years of stipend sitting on a chip you've never spent on anything but caf."

That was true, and Rex was annoyed that Fives remembered it.

"And you?" Fives said. "What do you do?"

"Stay." Rex said it plainly. "I stay inside. I stand where they've put me. I keep the General between his worst idea and his next one if I can. I watch Rusk. I watch what comes down the chain about Florrum before it gets there. If anyone gets close to either of you, I want to know before they do." He met Fives's eyes. "She gave me an order before they sent her to Narkis. Look after him. I haven't been relieved of it."

"And what about us? Who's looking after us?"

"I am," Rex said. "From the inside. It's the only side I've got."

Fives was quiet for a long moment. Then he held out his good hand.

Rex took it, and they stayed like that, two men with the same face in a drainage chamber under the city, until the banging metal high above went still.

* * *

Berth Nine in the Undercroft smelled of coolant and fried tubers and was busier at four in the morning than most of the upper city was at noon.

Rex had read General Skywalker's report on the place weeks ago — the Pantoran berth-master, Ventress's freighter, the long nasty message she'd left — and he had memorized the layout the way he memorized anything he might someday have to fight through. He hadn't expected to walk into it in a work jacket with Fives leaning on his shoulder under a pulled-down cap, coughing into his collar, playing a deckhand with a hangover. It was not a hard part for Fives to play.

The Pantoran berth-master looked at Rex's face, and then at Fives's face, and then at Rex's face again. Her expression didn't change. In the Undercroft, Rex suspected, two men who looked alike were not the strangest thing that had walked in before breakfast.

"Outer Rim," Rex said. "Florrum, or close enough to walk."

"Nobody walks on Florrum. They get carried." She tapped her datapad. "Gatch is going out in two hours. Scrap hauler, the *Gravel Saint*. He sells to the pirates on the Florrum markets every few weeks. Two stops on the way, Ord Torvin and a fuel dump off the Lanteeb lane. Two days, give or take what the engine feels like."

"Is he careful?"

"He's quiet. Out here that's better." She looked at Fives, swaying slightly. "He won't take a corpse."

"He's not a corpse," Rex said. "He's just been dead a while."

That earned him the faintest twitch from the Pantoran, which he chose to count as a smile.

Gatch turned out to be an old Weequay with a ruined left ear and hands like knotted rope, who listened to the berth-master, looked at Fives, looked at the credit chip in Rex's hand, and named a price that took most of three years of stipend in a single bite. Rex paid it without arguing. Gatch counted it twice, then jerked his chin at a space between two scrap bins in the *Gravel Saint*'s hold, where someone had laid an old blast blanket over a stack of sheet metal.

"Bunk," Gatch said. That was the whole conversation.

Rex walked Fives up the ramp and got him down onto the blanket. He tucked the medkit under Fives's good arm: six more fever shots, the rest of the bacta, a roll of clean binding, a handful of ration bars. Fives had the stolen Guard comlink in his coat. Rex had spent ten minutes in the Undercroft market buying a battery for it from a Chadra-Fan who sold, as far as Rex could tell, nothing that hadn't been stolen.

"Comlink's got charge now. Don't waste it on the Guard channel." Rex crouched beside him. "I'm sending her a reply from the public relay on the way out, not from the *Resolute*. Old band. It'll say hold position, contact arriving in two days, friendly. When you make planetfall, you raise her on the same band with the 501st ident. Not before."

"And when she asks how she knows I'm friendly?"

Rex thought about it. Then, very nearly, he smiled.

"Tell her Rex says her sense of direction is still terrible."

Fives grinned up at him, and for a second the fever and the bones and the cap were gone and it was just Fives, the way he'd been on Kamino in their first year, before there'd been anything to be afraid of. "She's going to hate that."

"She'll know it's me."

The last thing out of Rex's kit bag was wrapped in a shirt. He'd found it after the fall, wedged against the grating at the lip of the refinery shaft where it had stopped rolling, and he'd carried it since without deciding why. Fives unwrapped it one-handed and turned it over, and his thumb found the dent over the brow before his eyes did. "You never fixed it," Fives said, and Rex said there hadn't been time, which was true and not the reason. "Keep it on you," he said. "You'll want something to talk to out there."

The engines coughed somewhere forward. Gatch shouted something in his own language that didn't sound like a farewell. Rex stood.

"Fives."

"Captain."

"Don't let her do anything stupid."

"Have you met her?"

"That's why I said it."

He went down the ramp and didn't look back, because a man in a work jacket at a smuggler's berth who looked back was a man someone would remember. He heard the ramp come up behind him. He heard the old hauler lift out of its cradle with a grinding complaint, and then the roar of it going up the shaft, and then nothing but the Undercroft again, coolant and tubers and voices.

At a public relay booth three levels up, under a flickering advertisement for Rodian fight-holos, he punched in the old 501st band and keyed the reply by hand. *Hold position. Go to ground. Contact arriving two days. Friendly.* He hesitated over the last sequence, the one that meant *acknowledged, Commander*, and then sent it with it.

Then he wiped the booth's buffer, paid the booth for nothing, and went to find twelve fever shots' worth of an excuse.

* * *

The *Resolute* got her clearance at 1140. By 1200 she was climbing out of the fleet yards through a sky the color of old durasteel, and Rex was standing on the forward observation deck in full armor, helmet under his arm, the cracked datapad buttoned into the pouch at his belt where it had always been.

General Skywalker came up beside him without a sound. He'd slept, maybe, an hour. It didn't look like it had helped.

"Get your fever shots?"

"Twelve, sir. Clerk tried to give me the cheap ones."

"You didn't let him."

"No, sir."

They watched Coruscant fall away under them, the towers turning to a gold scatter, the scatter to a curve. Rex thought of a scrap hauler six hours ahead of them on a very different lane, and a man asleep between two bins in its hold, and held his face still.

"Rex." The General didn't look at him. His voice was odd. Quiet. Not the brittle tone he'd been using for weeks. "Something happened last night."

Rex's heart did one hard, cold thing. "Sir?"

"About two in the morning. After I left you." The General's mechanical hand was resting on the rail, and the fingers were moving slightly, the way they did when he was listening for something. "I was lying there. Not sleeping. And for a second — less than a second — I *felt* her."

Rex said nothing. He was very good at saying nothing now.

"Not like on Narkis. On Narkis there was just — nothing. A wall. That collar. I sat across a table from her and it was like she wasn't in the room." The General finally turned his head. His eyes were bright and wet and fierce. "But last night it was like a door opening a long way off. Somebody turning on a light in a house across a canyon. She's not wearing it anymore, Rex. That collar's off. She's out there and she's *herself* again."

"Do you know where?"

"No. Not yet. It was too far, too fast." His hand closed slowly on the rail, and the durasteel creaked under it. "But I felt her once. I'll feel her again. And the next time I do, I'm not going to need Tarkin, or Rusk, or anybody's clearance."

He pushed off the rail and walked toward the lift, and called back over his shoulder, almost cheerful, the way he'd sounded in the old days before Rex had ever learned the word *tribunal*.

"Set a course for Narkis, Captain. We'll start where they took her. But I don't think we'll be there long."

The lift doors closed on him.

Rex stood alone at the glass, with Ahsoka's position buttoned against his hip and Fives's two-day head start already burning away behind them, and understood that he was no longer racing Tarkin, or Rusk, or the Council.

He was racing the General.

## Chapter 21: Green Hands

Rex's message had said two days, and the second day was nearly gone.

Ahsoka sat cross-legged on the rusted roof of the ore crusher with the borrowed saber across her knees and the Force running through her like meltwater through a cracked dam, and she was not thinking about the warehouse. She was carefully, deliberately not thinking about it. It took more concentration than any kata she had ever learned.

Below her the outpost baked in the sulfur light. Hondo called it Number Nine — "because, my friend, the first eight exploded" — and had charged her for the directions. It was a dead place: a winch tower leaning over a shaft somebody had capped with plate durasteel, a line of ore hoppers rusting on a buckled rail, a bunkhouse whose roof had given up on its eastern half. The canyon walls rose red on both sides, close enough that the sky was only a yellow seam overhead. Every hour or so a geyser somewhere up the canyon coughed, a smell like old eggs came drifting down the rail line, and the hoppers ticked as they cooled or warmed.

She could feel all of it. That was the trouble. For four months the collar had held her in a silence so total she'd forgotten what the galaxy sounded like, and now it was all back at once — the stone's slow weight, the tiny lives in the cracks, the heat-pulse of the geysers, the hard bright knot of Ventress on the bunkhouse step — and every bit of it pulled at her attention except the one thing she would not look at.

Each time she sank toward stillness, the thought rose to meet her. Each time, she stood up inside herself and walked away from it.

A pebble struck the roof beside her knee.

"You're doing it again," Ventress called up.

Ahsoka opened her eyes. Ventress had the long rifle Hondo had lent them — he'd itemized that, too — propped across her thighs, and she was cleaning her one knife on her sleeve with the absent care of someone who had nothing else to clean.

"Doing what?"

"Flinching. You go quiet, and quieter, and then you jerk back like you've put your hand on a hot pipe." Ventress tilted her head. "It's very loud."

"Then stop listening."

"I'd love to. It's a small canyon."

Ahsoka unfolded her legs and climbed down the crusher's side ladder, the rungs gritty with yellow dust. Her neck still felt strange without the collar's weight, the raw ring of skin there tender under the scarf Hondo had thrown at her on their way out. She caught herself reaching for it and dropped her hand.

"Your clone is late," Ventress said.

"He said two days. It's still the second day."

"For another hour." Ventress sighted down the canyon along the rail line, more out of habit than interest. "If it's the Republic who answers, they'll come in loud. If it's Bane, they'll come in quiet. I would like to know which kind of night I'm having."

"It's Rex."

"You sound sure."

"I am sure."

"You were sure about the smuggler, too," Ventress said.

That landed, and Ventress knew it had landed; she didn't bother to look pleased. Ahsoka sat on an overturned ore bucket across from her and studied her own hands. Tess had looked her in the eye over a blaster wound and kept the credit chit. There had been no apology in it and no lie, either, and Ahsoka found she couldn't hate her, which bothered her more than hating would have.

"Tess was afraid," she said. "People do things when they're afraid."

"People do things when they want to. Fear is just the excuse they bring along." Ventress slid the knife into her boot. "You keep doing that, by the way. Deciding why people hurt you. Always the kindest reason on the shelf."

"Is this the part of the deal where you lecture me?"

"The deal," Ventress said, "is that I get into the Temple, and you get a witness. A witness to what, exactly? I've told you what I saw. A hooded buyer with green hands. A dead Rodian. A fighter who knew where to wait for us." Her voice didn't rise. It got very precise instead, and colder. "You have spent two days on that roof pretending you don't know what that adds up to."

"I don't."

"Your friend in the Temple—"

"Is not part of the deal."

The words came out harder than she meant. Somewhere up the canyon the geyser coughed. Ventress watched her for a long moment with that pale, unreadable face — the bruise from the warehouse had faded months ago, but Ahsoka could still picture it there — and then she shrugged, as if the whole subject bored her.

"As you like," she said. "It's your neck."

It wasn't fair, and it wasn't finished, and they both knew it. Ahsoka looked away down the rail line toward the canyon mouth, where the walls opened onto the flats, and because she was looking — because the Force was open and she was straining away from the thing in her own head — she felt him before she heard him.

A presence, small and far off and moving fast. Hot with fever. Stubborn as a rivet. Achingly, impossibly familiar, the way the 501st had felt from the command deck on a good day: a hundred bright, separate lights that all burned the same color.

She was on her feet before she knew it.

"Speeder," Ventress said a moment later, and the rifle came up.

* * *

It was one of Hondo's skiff-bikes, with one of Hondo's younger boys driving it far too fast, and it stopped in a spray of yellow grit thirty meters short of the bunkhouse, as if the boy didn't care to get any closer to two women with weapons. The passenger climbed off the back. He did it badly, favoring his whole left side, and stood swaying in the dust while the bike wheeled and screamed away down the canyon.

He wore a spacer's coat too big for him, and a hood, and his left arm was bound across his chest inside the coat. A medkit hung on his right hip, a Guard-issue comlink clipped beside it.

Ventress's rifle tracked his chest. "That's close enough."

The man lifted his good hand, slow, and pushed back the hood.

His hair, which she had only ever seen regulation-short, had grown out ragged and dark. There were hollows under his cheekbones that had not been there the last time she'd seen him, and his skin had a sheen that wasn't sweat from the heat. At his right temple, small and blunt, someone had inked a single numeral: a five.

He looked past the rifle at Ahsoka, and the corner of his mouth twitched.

"Rex says her sense of direction is still terrible," he said.

Something in her chest came loose. It was so stupid. It was such a Rex thing to say, and such a Fives thing to repeat, standing half-dead in the sulfur dust with a gun on him, that she laughed — a short, cracked sound that surprised her as much as anyone.

"Fives."

"Commander." He tried to straighten into something like attention and listed sharply to the left instead. "Sorry. Long trip. Captain of that hauler flies like he's got a personal grudge against the stars."

She was across the yard before Ventress could object, and she caught him under his right arm as his knees went. He was too light. He'd always been all shoulders and opinions; now she could feel his ribs through the coat.

"You know this one?" Ventress hadn't lowered the rifle.

"501st," Ahsoka said. "He's ours."

"He's on fire."

He was. Even through the coat the heat came off him, and in the Force his presence had a ragged, smoldering edge, like a bright light seen through smoke. "Inside," she said. "Help me."

Ventress looked at Fives for one more long second, then slung the rifle and took his other side, careful of the bound arm — more careful than Ahsoka would have expected.

They got him onto the bunk in the half of the bunkhouse that still had a roof. Ahsoka peeled the coat back. The binding beneath was stiff and discolored, and when she cut it away the shoulder underneath was angry: a blaster wound, high on the left side, that had closed wrong and swollen hot and dark around the edges.

"Fox," Fives said, watching her face. "Coruscant Guard. Good shot. I'd have told him so, but I was busy falling."

"Fox shot you?"

"Officially, Commander, I'm dead. It's a whole story."

"Then you'd better tell it." She had Rex's medkit open on the floor: bacta, binding, a row of six fever injectors with one already gone. Inside the lid, on a strip of tape, Rex's blocky handwriting said ONE EVERY TWELVE HOURS. DON'T BE A HERO. "And hold still."

She wasn't a healer. She'd never had the patience for it. Healing in the Force was a slow listening, a coaxing, and she had always been better at pushing things out of the way. But she laid her palm a finger's width above the wound and closed her eyes, and she listened.

The heat was a knot of wrongness under the skin, a dull, stubborn fire the body was losing to. She couldn't burn it out. She could hold the pain away — fold it back like a blanket so his breath eased — and she could steady the body's own fight, lend it a little of her strength, the way you'd brace a door someone else was trying to hold shut. When she opened her eyes, the swelling looked no different, but his jaw had unclenched.

"Huh," Fives said. "That's better than the shots."

"Talk," she said, and reached for the bacta.

He talked.

He told it plainly, the way clones told things in debrief, and it was worse for that. Ringo Vinda: a rotating hub, a sun-glare, and Tup — sweet, earnest Tup, who'd once asked her in all seriousness whether Togruta montrals could hear through walls — putting his blaster to General Tiplar and pulling the trigger. Tup saying it was the order, over and over, like a droid stuck on a loop. Kamino forbidding scans. A medical droid that scanned him anyway. A growth in Tup's brain that wasn't a growth. Tup dying on the table when they took it out.

"So I had them check me," Fives said. "Same thing. Same place. AZI pulled it out while I was awake." He tapped his temple, just under the inked five. "Every clone, Commander. From the tank. Before we've even got eyes. They grow it into us."

Ahsoka's hands had stopped on the binding. She made them start again.

"What does it do?"

"Nothing. Until somebody tells it to." He watched the broken roof. "Tup didn't hate her. He didn't hate anybody. Something in his head got told to kill a Jedi, so he did, and then he couldn't tell you why. Just kept saying it was an order."

"An order from whom?"

"That's the part." Fives's voice dropped. "The Kaminoans were going to bury it. Call it a virus. General Shaak Ti got me a hearing with the Chancellor instead. I thought — he's the top. If anybody can stop it, he can." He breathed for a while. "Somebody drugged me on the way in. Next thing I know there's a guard saying I attacked him. The Chancellor. And he's holding my chip in a little vial, and he doesn't give it back."

From the doorway, where she had been leaning with her arms folded, Ventress made a soft, unpleasant sound.

"How wonderful," she said. "A secret worm in every soldier's skull and a politician holding the jar. Was there thunder? Did he laugh into the dark?"

"Ventress."

"I'm only asking for the details. It's a very good story."

"It's not a story." Fives turned his head on the bunk to look at her, and something in his flat, fevered voice made Ventress's mouth close. "I ran. Down through the lower levels, past 79's, to an old refinery Rex and I used to know. Rex found me. General Skywalker found me. I told them. Fox fired anyway, and Rex knocked his arm or I'd be dead, and I went down a drainage shaft and the Republic wrote me off." He shut his eyes. "Rex believes me. He didn't, at first. Then he thought about your trial, Commander. How he stood there and let it happen. He said he wasn't doing that twice."

Ahsoka couldn't speak. She tied off the binding because her hands knew how.

Rex. Rex at her cell door, his salute stalling halfway and then completing. Rex teaching her the Christophsis codes in a mess hall at three in the morning, patient, deadpan, making her repeat them until she could tap them out half asleep. There was something in his head. There had always been something in his head. It had been there while he taught her.

And Jesse, and Kix. And the small clone detachment at Narkis, who had stood in the Yard tunnel and watched her haul ice. Every one of them.

And the Chancellor, who had said *death* in that warm, regretful voice and then, with everyone watching, said *life* — and she had told herself it was a mercy, or tried to.

"Does Anakin know?" she said.

"No." Fives's eyes opened. "Rex wants to tell him. Can't figure how. The General's —" He hesitated. "Close to the Chancellor, these days. Rex says he talks about him a lot."

The doorframe creaked. Ventress was gone. Through the gap in the wall Ahsoka could see her out on the rail, her back to the bunkhouse, her pale head bowed a little. In the Force she had gone very still and very tight, like a wire pulled to its limit.

Ahsoka thought she understood that, a little. Ventress knew what it was to be made into a blade by someone who praised you while he did it.

She gave Fives the second injector from Rex's kit. He grimaced, and then the fever's grip loosened enough for him to look almost like himself.

"Whoever did it to you," he said drowsily. "The hangar. The troopers outside your cell. Rex and I walked that night over and over. Never fit. Whoever killed those three walked right into the Guard's own building and walked right back out. Rex always said it had to be someone they'd wave through without blinking." His eyes drifted shut again. "Someone they'd never think to look at twice."

"Sleep, Fives."

"Yes, Commander."

He was asleep before she'd finished packing the kit.

She sat on the floor beside the bunk for a long time afterward, her back against the cold wall, her palms resting on her knees. They still felt warm from the healing. The bacta had left them faintly sticky, and the smell of it — green and sharp and clean — was the smell of every medcenter she'd ever been patched up in.

It was the smell of Narkis, too. Of the infirmary on Ring Two, and Barriss's hands on her split shin, warm, steady, so careful. Barriss bent over her the way she had just bent over Fives, listening for the wrongness under the skin.

And afterward — Ahsoka remembered it exactly — Barriss's hands had shaken.

She'd thought it was the cold.

* * *

Night on Florrum was never fully dark. The geysers lit the low cloud from beneath, and the canyon walls held a dull orange glow like the inside of a forge gone to embers. Ahsoka went out past the hoppers and sat at the edge of the capped shaft, where the plate durasteel still held a little of the day's heat, and she let herself look.

It was the first time in two days. It was the first time in four months. She had been walking away from this since the Well — since Merrick Tal's voice through the vent, telling her that whoever had really done it knew exactly where she was. She'd heard him, and asked the question — *who knew?* — and every time the answer had begun to take shape, she'd stood up inside herself and left the room.

She didn't stand up now. She made herself sit.

Who knew where she would go?

She had been running. The lower levels, her own face on every public screen, Anakin and Rex and Fox all somewhere above her, and Ventress — of all people — at her side. She had needed to know where the nano-droids came from, and she hadn't trusted anyone. Except one. She had opened her comlink in a stinking service corridor and called the one person in the Temple she was sure of, and a voice she loved had told her: an old munitions warehouse, down in the lower levels. Go and see.

And when they went and saw, someone was waiting.

Ventress had said it in Hondo's storeroom. Not followed. *Waited.*

There are a hundred ways, Ahsoka told herself. The channel could have been monitored. Barriss could have told someone — Luminara, a Council member, anyone she thought would help.

But she'd begged Barriss to tell no one. And Ahsoka, in her turn, had promised never to name the friend who sent her there — not to Tarkin, not to Padmé, not to anyone — and she had kept that promise in a tribunal chamber with her life on the table, because she had believed her friend would be in danger if she didn't.

She'd protected Barriss. From the Republic. With her own neck.

Next.

Who had visited her cell?

Anakin. Padmé. Fox and his Guards. And Barriss, before the trial, to heal her wrists — no. Before that. The first time had been before the escape. Barriss had come down to the detention level with her hood up and sat outside the energy field and been so gentle, so sorry, and had said in that soft way of hers that the Council was already deciding what to do with Ahsoka, that no one was coming. And then she'd gone, and some time later a keycard had appeared where no keycard should be.

Ahsoka had thought it was Anakin. She'd been so sure it was Anakin, breaking the rules for her the way he always did, that she hadn't asked him until the day she was expelled, and when she finally did, he had looked at her with honest confusion and said no.

She had let it go. A sympathetic Guard, she'd decided. Someone in the building.

*Someone they'd wave through without blinking.*

Three troopers dead outside her cell, cut down with her own sabers — the sabers Fox had taken from her at the door of Tarkin's base and that had never been hers again. Someone had walked in, gotten hold of them, killed three men with them, and left her a keycard and an open door. Someone had made that corridor look exactly like what a desperate Padawan would do. And she, desperate, had done it. She had walked out among the bodies and run, because staying felt like dying and running felt like a chance.

It had never been a chance. It had been the second half of the trap. The visit had been the first.

Her lekku felt tight; she realized she was hunched like something freezing. She made herself straighten.

Who could fight her like that?

The warehouse. The masked figure with Ventress's red blades. She'd fought it among the crates and lost, and she'd told herself at the time that it was the dark, and the shock, and an unfamiliar style. But it hadn't been unfamiliar. That was what had bothered her in the Well, on the nights she let herself remember the fight at all. It had defended like water. Patient. Circling. Never overextending, never giving her a single thing to hit, just waiting for the opening she always gave in the end.

Anakin had seen it on the Dovrin platform. He'd come to her cell and told her, low so the Guards wouldn't hear: a Soresu turn. Temple training.

*You always give me one,* Barriss had said once, laughing, in a practice salle the year after Geonosis, when Ahsoka had come in too fast for the third time and ended up flat on her back. *An opening. Every single time. I only have to wait for it.*

Ahsoka put her hands over her face.

Not yet, she thought. That's three things. Three things isn't proof. Half the Order learned Soresu. Luminara taught it — Barriss had learned it from her, yes, but so had others. The Temple was full of patient duelists.

And green hands. A hooded buyer at Dalo Pesk's stall, paying for nano-droids. Green, Ventress had said, and patterned. As if it were a detail, a scrap of description to hand over and forget.

There are Mirialans all over the Core, Ahsoka thought. There are — Luminara is — there are other species with—

She heard how it sounded. She stopped.

That was the trouble with the thought she'd spent four months not thinking. She was a good investigator. Anakin had made her one, and Obi-Wan, and Rex; they had taught her to look at a scene and see what was missing, to ask who had access, who knew, who gained. If anyone else had brought her these things — a comlink call, a keycard, a defensive form, a pair of hands — she'd have seen it in a heartbeat. She'd have been proud of herself.

She'd have been sure.

And there was still the last thing. The thing that wasn't evidence at all.

Narkis, day ninety-two. Barriss in her healer's robes, come all the way out to the edge of Wild Space on a prison circuit nobody wanted, sitting by her cot. They had talked about Geonosis — the tunnels, the worm, Barriss's hands at her throat with something else looking out through her eyes, and Ahsoka dragging her back from it. They had almost laughed about it, the way you could about a thing you'd both survived. And Barriss had said, so quietly, that she lit candles every morning. Four. The three troopers Ahsoka had asked her to remember, and one more.

Ahsoka had thought — had let herself think, warm with it, grateful — that the fourth was for Letta Turmond. For the woman who'd died in front of her, accused and terrified, insisting that a Jedi was behind it all. A candle for someone no one in the Temple would ever mourn. She'd loved Barriss for it.

But the Temple had its own candles for the six Jedi lost in the hangar. Everyone lit for them. Barriss's four were her own — unmarked, private. Three she had been asked for.

The fourth, no one had asked for.

Either it was for Letta, whose throat had closed before she could say which Jedi, or it was for the hangar worker who'd carried a bomb he never knew he had — the one person in this whole story more innocent than Ahsoka herself. Either way, it was a candle for someone Barriss had no reason to mourn.

Unless she'd killed them.

People didn't light candles every morning for strangers. They lit them for the ones they couldn't stop seeing.

Ahsoka sat on the lip of the capped shaft with the warm durasteel under her and the orange cloud overhead, and the thing she had been holding the door against for four months walked in and sat down beside her, and it had Barriss's face.

She didn't make a sound. She had imagined, once or twice in the Well, how it would feel to learn who'd done it. She'd imagined rage, the clean hot kind, something she could stand on. There wasn't any. There was only a hollowing, as if someone had reached in and taken out everything behind her ribs and left the shape.

Barriss had sat outside her cell and been kind.

Barriss had sent her to the warehouse.

Barriss had fought her among the crates with Ventress's red blades, and won, and left her there for the Guard to find, and gone home, and lit a candle.

Barriss had come to the tribunal. Ahsoka had looked up at the Jedi gallery once during her own testimony, searching for one friendly face, and found her there.

Barriss had come to Narkis. Barriss had held her shin in both hands and closed the wound and eased the burning under the collar and asked how she was sleeping. Barriss had listened to her talk about trying not to hate the Council, and nodded. And when Ahsoka had warned her — had actually warned her, frightened for her, *be careful, someone is cleaning up behind this* — Barriss had gone grey, and thanked her, and left with her hands shaking.

Not the cold. Never the cold.

A geyser went up somewhere with a long hiss, and for a moment the canyon walls flared bright. Ahsoka pressed the heels of her hands against her eyes until colors bloomed behind them, and her shoulders shook, once, twice, and then she held still. She'd learned stillness in the Well. She'd learned you could carry a weight that ought to break you, if you stopped trying to set it down and let your bones take it.

She had saved Barriss's life on Geonosis. She'd always thought that was the end of that story: the worm, the tunnels, her own hand pulling her friend out of the dark. And for one sick, hopeful moment, sitting there, she reached for it — maybe something had been in Barriss's head again. A worm. A chip. Something that made you do what you'd never do, the way Fives said it had been with Tup. Maybe—

A worm didn't light candles. A chip didn't come back to visit the cell, or fly to the edge of Wild Space to heal the person it had buried there. Whatever Barriss had done, she had chosen it. And every morning since, kneeling in front of four small flames, she had chosen it again.

"You've finally looked," Ventress said behind her.

Ahsoka didn't turn. She hadn't heard her come; she hadn't been listening for anything. Ventress came around the edge of the shaft cover and lowered herself onto the warm plate an arm's length away, rifle across her knees, and looked out at the glowing canyon instead of at her. It was almost tactful.

"I felt it from the bunkhouse," Ventress said. "Like a building coming down. Very quietly."

"Don't."

"I'm not mocking you." A pause. "For once."

They sat. The geyser subsided. Far up the rail line something small scuttled over the stones and went still.

"Say it," Ventress said.

Ahsoka's throat closed. She had kept the promise in front of the Republic. She had kept it in front of Anakin while a steel cup crumpled on the table between them. She'd kept it alone in the Well for ten days. It turned out that letting it go was harder than any of that.

"Barriss," she said. "Barriss Offee. Master Luminara's Padawan." Her voice came out level and strange, like someone else's. "She's a healer. She's one of the best duelists in the Temple. She's my friend."

Ventress turned the name over. "The one who sent you to the warehouse."

"Yes."

"The one with the green hands."

"Mirialan," Ahsoka said. "The backs of her hands are patterned. Little diamonds. I used to—" She stopped. She had used to think they were beautiful. "Yes."

Ventress let out a long breath through her nose. "Well," she said. "There's our ghost."

"I can't prove it."

"You know it."

"I know it," Ahsoka said. "And what I have is your word about a hooded buyer — the word of a bounty hunter and former Separatist assassin, which any tribunal in the Republic will weigh against the word of a Jedi healer from the Temple clinic. And a dead Rodian with no marks on him and no recording." Her mouth twisted. "That's all. It's less than Tarkin had on me."

"Tarkin had footage."

"Tarkin had footage of my back, and my hand moving, and a woman dying." She laughed, a short dry sound with nothing in it. "And it was enough."

Ventress was quiet a moment. "So what do you want? Proof?"

"I want—" Ahsoka shut her eyes. What did she want? She wanted Barriss to have said it. She wanted to go back to that cell on the detention level and hear her say it there, while there was still time for it to matter. She wanted back the years when Barriss had only been Barriss. "Something that can't be argued with. Something she can't explain away and the Council can't look past."

"Like my sabers," Ventress said.

Ahsoka opened her eyes.

"She took them," Ventress said. "She used them on you. Then what? Melt them down? Drop them in a reactor?"

"No." The answer came from somewhere below thought; she knew it the way she knew Barriss's guard. "No. She'd never destroy them. Kyber's alive. She'd feel the crystals every second she held them — she couldn't crush them any more than she could crush a bird in her hand. She'd hide them. Somewhere no one would ever look, and she'd tell herself she'd deal with it tomorrow." Her voice cracked on that, and she pushed through. "Somewhere close. Somewhere she could reach. Under the Temple, probably. Down in the Temple's depths — the old foundations, the crawlways nobody's walked in—"

Ventress went rigid.

Ahsoka felt it before she saw it: the hard bright knot of Ventress's presence drawing suddenly tight, the way a hunting animal's whole body goes still at a sound in the grass. Ventress's head had come up. Her pale hands had closed on the rifle stock hard enough to make it creak.

"Say that again," Ventress said.

"The Temple's depths. The foundations. Under—"

"There." Barely a breath. Ventress's eyes were unfocused, looking at nothing; the Force around her had narrowed to a needle. "Oh, *there* you are."

"Ventress?"

"For months," Ventress said, very low, "they've been a whisper. A pull. Somewhere on Coruscant, that much and no more — like trying to hear your own name called across a city." She drew in a slow breath and let it out through her teeth. "And you say *depths*, and they turn over. Like a sleeper hearing its name. Down. Under a great deal of stone." Her lips drew back from her teeth in something that was not quite a smile. "Under your precious Temple."

The hunger in her was so naked it was almost hard to look at. Ahsoka looked anyway.

"That's not proof either," she said.

"It's my sabers in the cellar of the Jedi Temple," Ventress said. "Where a bounty hunter couldn't have put them and a Jedi could. If that isn't proof, little Jedi, it will certainly start a conversation."

Ahsoka said nothing. She thought of the Temple at dawn, the long light through the high windows, Barriss kneeling in front of four small flames. She thought of Barriss going down in the night to check on the things she'd hidden, the way you'd keep touching a bruise.

She had been gone long enough that she'd stopped calling the Temple home. She hadn't understood until now that some part of her had still been waiting to be called back to it. That part was quiet now. It had stopped waiting.

"Go and sit with your clone," Ventress said, and stood. "I'll take the watch. You're no use to me like this."

It was, Ahsoka understood after she'd gone, the kindest thing Ventress knew how to say.

* * *

She didn't sleep. She sat by Fives's bunk through the last hours of the night, listening to him breathe — rough, but steady — and gave him the third injector when the fever climbed again toward dawn. Twice he muttered something with Rex's name in it. Once he said Tup's.

When the cloud over the canyon began to turn from orange to the dirty gold of a Florrum morning, she went out to the crusher roof again and sat cross-legged, and laid the borrowed saber across her knees, and opened herself to the Force because she didn't know what else to do with her hands.

It was a mistake.

He was there.

Not near, not yet. But there — at the far rim of everything, where a few nights ago there had been only a faint pressure, like weather on the other side of a mountain. Now it had a shape. A storm, coiled and burning and grieving, so familiar it hurt to touch. Yesterday it had been somewhere out past the stars. This morning it was closer. Much closer.

And it was turning toward her, the way a compass needle swings and settles.

She felt the instant he felt her. A jolt across the bond, raw and astonished, like someone grabbing for a hand in the dark — and then, terribly, joy.

She slammed herself shut. Too late. She knew it was too late.

Ahsoka was on her feet on the roof with the hilt in her fist and her heart slamming against her ribs. Below, Ventress had already stepped out of the bunkhouse doorway with the rifle in her hands, her face tipped up toward the yellow seam of sky.

"What is it?" Ventress said. "What did you just do?"

Ahsoka didn't answer. She was looking up too, past the canyon rim, into the gold, where nothing showed yet.

"Skyguy," she whispered, and it hurt exactly as much as she'd known it would.

## Chapter 22: The Canyon

For one heartbeat, before she slammed the door on him, Anakin Skywalker was happy.

It hit him over the open airspeeder like the sun clearing a ridge. Ahsoka. Not the faint pressure he'd felt days ago in his bunk on the *Resolute*, thin as a held breath at the far end of the galaxy. Her, whole and loud and close, the quick bright stubborn shape of her that he had felt every waking hour for three years and had not felt once since a technician sealed a grey collar around her neck. And she felt him. He knew it the way he knew his own pulse. The startle. The reach. And then gladness, so plain and so young that it went through his chest like a blade.

Then a wall where she had been.

The speeder dipped. Obi-Wan's hand closed hard on the edge of the dash.

"Anakin."

"I've got it." He hauled the nose up. A rib of red rock went past close enough to scrape paint, and the slipstream threw grit across the windscreen. His mechanical hand had locked on the yoke. He made the fingers open, one at a time, the way he'd had to on the Dovrin platform with twenty stories of hab stack on his back. "I've got it."

"You felt her."

"She felt me." He heard his voice crack on it and didn't care. "She was glad, Obi-Wan. For a second. Before she remembered."

Obi-Wan said nothing to that, which was kinder than anything he could have said.

Below them Florrum's canyons opened and closed like the folds of a burned hand. The sky was the color of old brass, dirty gold, streaked with geyser steam. The air stank of sulfur even at this speed. Anakin banked east, toward the place where she had been, and kept his eyes on the rock because if he looked at Obi-Wan he might say something he couldn't take back.

It had been thirty-one hours since the intercept reached the *Resolute*. Tarkin's office had sent it "as a courtesy," six hours after it went to Major Rusk: a cargo homing tag of the kind Cad Bane liked to hide in shipments he meant to collect, pinging off-world through a pirate comm dish on Florrum, and then, abruptly, dead. Anakin had turned the cruiser around in the Narkis approach lanes before the message finished playing. He'd pushed the hyperdrive past anything the chief engineer liked. They had reached orbit in the dark.

Hondo's dish had been a blackened ruin on its tower. Hondo himself had come out to meet the gunships wearing a dressing gown and an expression of deep personal injury, and had spent forty minutes explaining, in a great many words, that he knew nothing, had seen nothing, and was in fact the victim here. Anakin had left Rex and two platoons to search the base. Rex had looked at him a beat too long when he gave the order, and Anakin had put it down to the same thing everyone was carrying: the lethal-force authorization, signed by Windu, sitting in every trooper's orders like a live grenade.

That was why he'd taken an airspeeder and only Obi-Wan. No one with that order in their pocket was getting anywhere near her.

Up in orbit, Barriss had asked to come down. She'd been waiting in the hangar with her healer's satchel already on her shoulder, very still, very composed. *If she's hurt—*

*If she's hurt,* he'd told her, *I'll bring her to you.* Luminara had laid a hand on her Padawan's arm and kept her aboard, and Barriss had nodded and said nothing, and Anakin had been grateful that somebody besides him still cared.

The comlink on his wrist chirped.

"Sir." Rex. "Judiciary corvette just dropped out of hyperspace. Major Rusk is asking for your coordinates. She's asking in the Admiral's name."

Anakin thought of the case Rusk carried. Sealed, grey, the size of a dinner tray. A spare collar, already calibrated.

"Tell her we're still searching the base."

A pause. "She'll know that isn't true, sir."

"Then tell her slowly."

"Slowly, sir." Another pause, shorter. "Good luck, General."

Obi-Wan was watching him. Anakin dropped the speeder into a side canyon, then another, following the ache where Ahsoka had been, and five minutes later the walls fell back and he saw the outpost.

* * *

It clung to the canyon wall on rusted stilts: a crusher house with a sagging roof, a dead conveyor running down to a cluster of prefab huts, a bunkhouse with its door shut. Someone had painted a large, cheerful *9* on the crusher's flank. A geyser vent at the base of the slope breathed out a lazy yellow plume and sucked it back.

There was a figure on the crusher roof. Not Ahsoka. Too tall, too pale, the hood thrown back from a bald head.

"Well," said Obi-Wan. "There's a face I'd hoped to see only in my nightmares, where at least I'm expecting it."

Ventress raised a rifle.
