The first bolt took the windscreen. Anakin threw the speeder sideways and set it down hard in the scree, and Obi-Wan was already out, his blade blue and humming, turning the second and third bolts into the dirt. Anakin went over the side the other way, boots skidding on loose stone, and reached out with everything he had.

She wasn't in the outpost.

She was below it, and moving. Down a slot canyon that cut off south-east from the geyser vent, fast and sure-footed, and she wasn't hiding now. She'd opened herself again, deliberately, a lantern held up in the dark. *Here. This way. Follow me.*

Away from the outpost.

The wind turned, and over the sulfur he caught something else, sharp and clinical, drifting from the bunkhouse's open vent. Bacta. A lot of it, recently. Someone was hurt. His stomach dropped — and then steadied, because the presence in the canyon below was running too fast to be wounded. Ventress, then. He filed it and let it go.

Obi-Wan reached out with his free hand and the rifle tore from Ventress's grip, cartwheeled off the roof, and smashed against the conveyor housing. She let it go without a glance. She dropped from the roof to the top of the conveyor and from the conveyor to the ground, light as ash, and came up with a knife in her hand. Long, plain, single-edged. Not a saber. Not even close.

"Kenobi," she said. "They still let you out without a leash."

"And they've let you out without your lightsabers. I had heard." Obi-Wan's blade dipped politely toward the knife. "I'd offer my condolences, but I don't think either of us would believe them."

"Keep talking. I've been wanting something to cut for a week."

"With that? I've seen better cutlery in a Senate canteen."

"It's sized for small men."

Anakin was already moving toward the slot canyon. Ventress pivoted to block him, and her free hand came up, and a stack of rusted ore carts beside the conveyor slid screaming across the yard into his path. He went over them with a jump that wrenched the Ordaine burn along his ribs. Behind him he heard the snap of Obi-Wan's blade meeting nothing, and Ventress's laugh.

"Anakin!" Obi-Wan called. "Go. Our old friend and I have a great deal to catch up on."

"He'll be dull company," Ventress said. "He always was."

"And yet you keep coming back for more."

Anakin didn't hear her answer. He was over the lip of the slope and sliding down loose scree toward the geyser vent, and then into the narrow red throat of the canyon, and the sound of the yard fell away behind the rock.

* * *

The canyon twisted and narrowed until he could have touched both walls at once, then opened suddenly into a pocket of pale mineral crust, flat as a floor, crazed with white cracks where some old geyser had died. A warm wind breathed up through the cracks. The sky was a single gold seam far overhead.

She was standing in the middle of the crust with her hands empty.

Anakin stopped at the edge of it. Ten paces. He'd closed bigger gaps than that in one jump. He didn't move.

She was thinner. That was the first thing, and it was stupid that it was the first thing, but he couldn't stop seeing it — the hollows under her cheekbones, the way the patched pirate's jacket hung off her shoulders over prison greys. There was a raw ring around her neck where the collar had been, pink and angry against her skin, and a small burn under the left line of her jaw. No beads hung from her montral. He'd known that. Windu had taken them himself. Knowing it was different from seeing the empty place.

A saber hung at her belt. Brushed steel, worn black grip, a dented emitter. Not hers.

"Snips," he said.

For a moment she didn't answer. Her montrals tilted, just slightly, the way they did when she was listening to something underneath what was said.

"You found me," she said.

"You let me."

"I didn't let you. I was careless." Her mouth moved, not quite a smile. "I let you find *this* part."

He looked at the hilt on her belt because he couldn't keep looking at her neck. "Whose is that?"

"Hondo's. He says he won it from a Jedi Master." A breath. "He's told me how four times. It's a different Master every time."

The laugh came out of him before he could stop it, short and painful, and her eyes went bright and she looked away, up at the seam of sky, and blinked hard. He took a step onto the crust.

She took a step back.

It was a small thing. One step on a flat of cracked white stone. It hurt worse than the plasma burn on Ordaine. He stopped where he was.

"Are you hurt?" he said. "I smelled bacta at the outpost."

"It's not mine."

She said it flatly, a door closing, and he let it close.

"Ahsoka." He didn't know what to do with his hands. The left one opened and closed. The right one, the metal one, had found its way to his belt and was resting just above the saber hilt, and he hadn't told it to. He saw her see it. He saw her own hand stay exactly where it was, loose at her side, nowhere near the borrowed steel. Something in him went cold and ashamed, and he moved his hand away and hooked his thumb in his belt instead.

"I had them," he said. "On the platform. The one in the mask. I was this close, and the stack started to go, and I stopped." The words came out in the wrong order, too fast, the way they had in his head for five months. "I could have caught them. If I'd caught them, none of this—"

"Fox told me. Nobody died."

"*You* did. Near enough."

"Not yet." She looked at him then, straight on, and her voice gentled. "If you'd kept running, there'd be a mother and a kid under that stack. I've had a long time to think about it. I wouldn't trade them. You wouldn't either. That's the whole problem with you, Skyguy — you can't help stopping."

It wasn't the name. It was how quietly she said it, like setting down something that might break. He had to look at the rock for a moment.

Then he made himself say what he'd come to say.

"Come back with me." He took a breath. "Listen to me. Just listen, before you say no. The Chancellor asked me to bring you to him. Him, not the Council. He's been talking about an amnesty since before the visit. If you come in to *me*, if you come in on your own, he can pardon you. He's the only one who can. He's the only one who's lifted a finger for you since this started."

She didn't say anything. He pressed on, because if he stopped he'd hear how it sounded.

"Rusk's corvette is in atmosphere right now. She's got a collar in a case, Snips. A new one. Windu signed lethal force. Do you understand what that means? Every trooper on this planet has an order that says if you lift that saber they can put you down. I can't stop that. Obi-Wan can't stop that. But the Chancellor—"

"The Chancellor already made his choice once."

She said it quietly. The warm wind breathed up through the cracks between them.

"He saved your life."

"He sat above me and said *death*." Her voice didn't rise. Somehow that was worse. "I heard him say it. I was still standing there with that word in me when he said the rest. And then he gave me the rest of my life in the ice, and everybody in that room stood up and called it mercy. And you thanked him."

"He didn't have to do anything."

"He could have done *anything*, Anakin. That's what I'm telling you. He could have said there wasn't enough proof. He could have sent it back. He's the Chancellor. He looked at everything there was to look at, and he decided what my life was worth, and he picked a number." She touched the raw ring on her neck, once, as if to check it was still only skin. "I wore his mercy for a hundred and eighteen days. I know exactly what it weighs."

He wanted to tell her she didn't understand, that she hadn't been in that red office, that she hadn't seen the old man's face when Anakin begged. But she had been in the ice. He hadn't.

"A pardon isn't the same as innocent," she said. "A pardon is somebody important deciding to be kind to a guilty person. I'm not guilty. I won't let him be kind to me."

"Then prove it." It came out hoarse. "Let me help you prove it. That's all I've wanted, from the first night. Let me *help*."

"I'm going to prove it."

And there it was. Not in her words. In the Force, where she'd stopped hiding from him: a hard, cold, settled certainty, like a stone at the bottom of clear water. Grief all around it. He knew that feeling. He'd felt it in himself, kneeling in the sand of a Tusken camp. The feeling of knowing exactly who.

"You know," he said. "You know who did it."

She didn't deny it. She didn't need to.

"Tell me."

"No."

"Ahsoka—"

"No."

"*Tell* me." He took two steps before he knew he'd moved. "Give me a name, and I'll end this tonight. Tonight. You'll never have to run again, you'll never see the inside of a cell again, you'll—"

The gravel around his boots was rattling.

He heard it before he understood it. A fine dry chatter, like rain on a hull. Pebbles along the rim of the crust were lifting, shivering, a finger's width off the ground. A crack under his left foot spat a puff of warm steam and widened with a sound like snapping bone. Along the canyon wall, a fist-sized stone pulled loose and hung in the air beside his head.

Ahsoka was looking at the stones. Not at him. At the stones.

He let them fall. They clattered down all at once, and the canyon was very quiet.

"At Narkis," she said, "you crushed a cup and didn't notice. Vorne noticed. I noticed. I had a collar on and I couldn't feel a thing, and I still noticed." She drew a breath that shook a little at the top. "This morning I felt you before the sun came up. All of you. And there's something in you that wasn't there when they took me away. It was small at Narkis. It isn't small now."

"That's not—" He stopped. He didn't know what it wasn't.

"If I give you a name," she said, "you won't take it to the Council. You won't take it to Master Kenobi. You won't take it to a court. You'll take it to *them*. Alone. Tonight, like you said." Her eyes were wet and steady. "And then whatever's left after, you'd have to live in it. For the rest of your life. I've seen what you do when you're carrying something you can't put down. I won't hand you this one."

"You're protecting them." He couldn't believe it. He *could*; that was the worst part. "After what they did to you, you're protecting them."

"I'm protecting you."

The wind moved her lekku. She didn't move at all.

"Both," she said, more quietly. "Both of you. Somebody has to. You were never going to let it be you."

He had nothing. Five months of things he had planned to say, and nothing.

Somewhere up at the rim, far off, he heard the high thin whine of a repulsor engine that wasn't a pirate's. His comlink chirped against his wrist.

"Sir." Rex's voice was very level. "Major Rusk stopped asking. She's tracking your speeder's transponder. Judiciary gunship inbound to your position. I make it twelve minutes. Maybe ten."

Anakin looked at Ahsoka. She'd heard. Her chin lifted a fraction, the way it used to lift on the flight deck before a drop, when she was afraid and had decided not to be.

He thought of the spare collar in its grey case. He thought of the Chancellor's hand on his shoulder in the red office, the warmth of it, and the voice saying *bring her to me*. He'd promised. He'd told himself, standing in the Temple hangar, that this time he wouldn't stop for anything. That this time he'd run the bomber down and not look back.

He had stopped anyway. He'd been standing still for five minutes.

"Rusk will come down the Narrows from the north," he heard himself say. "It's the only cut wide enough for a gunship. Go south-east. Keep the walls high."

"I know."

"Of course you know." He had to swallow. "How are you getting off-planet?"

"Better if you don't know."

"Hondo."

A flicker at the corner of her mouth. "He's calling it a loan. I think I'll be paying it back until I'm a hundred."

"He'll charge you interest on the interest."

"He already has."

She crossed the crust then, the ten paces he hadn't, and before he understood what she was doing she'd put her arms around him and her forehead against his chest, right over the healed burn, hard enough to hurt. He felt her in the Force all at once and all the way through, the way he used to every day without thinking about it — tired, frightened, furious, and so stubbornly, impossibly *her* — and he got his arms around her and held on. His metal hand was against her back. He was careful with it. He was always careful with it, with her.

"Don't be grateful to him on my account," she said into his tunic. "Please. Don't let anyone own you. Not the Council. Not him. Promise me that one."

"Snips—"

"Promise."

He didn't. He couldn't make his mouth do it. She felt that too; he knew she did. She stepped back, and her hand brushed the hilt at her belt — not to draw it, only to settle it — and his own saber stayed dark on his hip, and hers stayed dark on hers.

"I'll prove it," she said. "In front of everybody. And then nobody will ever have to pardon me for anything."

"And then you'll come home?"

She looked at him for a long moment. Her eyes were very clear.

"Go on, Skyguy," she said. "They'll be looking for you."

Then she turned and ran, light and quick over the white crust and into the south-east slot, and the red rock took her. He felt her for twenty heartbeats more. Then the wall went up again, gently this time, and there was only the canyon, the warm breath of the cracks, and the whine of the gunship growing louder overhead.

Twice now, he thought. Twice he had stood still and let the one he was chasing get away. The first time had cost her everything.

He had no idea yet what this one would cost. He stood there until he could make his face do what he needed it to, and then he climbed back toward the outpost.

* * *

Obi-Wan was sitting on an overturned ore cart in the middle of the yard, his cloak torn at the hem and a long smear of red dust down one side of his face. A heap of crushed ore lay beside him where none had been before. The crusher's discharge chute hung open above it, still dribbling gravel.

"She dropped most of a hopper on me," Obi-Wan said, before Anakin could ask. "Then she left, by way of the roof, with a remark about my beard I'd prefer not to have entered into the record."

"Which way?"

"Up. Over. Gone." Obi-Wan dabbed at the dust on his cheek and considered his fingers. "She's slower without her sabers. Not as slow as I'd like."

The bunkhouse door stood open now. Anakin went to it and looked in. Bunks stripped to the frames. A scorched patch on the floor where someone had burned something small and quickly. The bacta smell was still there, fainter, under the sulfur. Nothing else. Whoever had been hurt had been well looked after and was gone.

He stood in the doorway longer than he needed to. Then he came back across the yard.

"Well?" said Obi-Wan.

"She wasn't there." It came out steadier than he'd expected. "She knew I was coming. She opened up just enough to drag me down three side canyons and lost me in the vents. I never got closer than a kilometer."

Obi-Wan looked at him.

It was not a long look, by Obi-Wan's standards. It took in Anakin's face, and his hands, and the pale mineral crust caked white on his boots — the only white ground Anakin had crossed all morning, a kilometer and a half down the south-east slot. Anakin had felt Obi-Wan's attention in the Force while he stood on that crust: steady, distant, waiting for the flare and roar of two blades meeting. It had never come. Obi-Wan would have felt nothing at all. Nothing but quiet, and then a grief so sharp it must have reached him even through the fight.

"Ah," said Obi-Wan. "That's unfortunate."

"Yes."

"Three side canyons."

"At least."

Obi-Wan nodded slowly, as if Anakin had said something reasonable about the weather. He stood, brushed off his knees, and looked up at the gold seam of sky, where the Judiciary gunship was dropping toward them, grey and unmarked, its engines kicking a storm of grit across the yard.

"For the report, then," he said.

"For the report."

And that was all. Anakin waited for the rest of it — the sigh, the *Anakin*, the quiet question he'd asked on Coruscant that had cut deeper than any blade. It didn't come. Obi-Wan simply let it lie there between them, a lie with both their names on it now, and turned to face the gunship.

Rusk came down the ramp first, in grey armor without insignia, the pale scar through her left brow pulling her eyelid very slightly askew. Behind her a trooper carried the grey case. Anakin made himself not look at it.

"General Skywalker." Her voice was pleasant and exact. "You went off-transponder for thirty-eight minutes."

"I was chasing a fugitive through a canyon system. It doesn't have great reception."

"And?"

"She led me away from the outpost and lost me in the geyser vents. Ventress was here. She's gone too."

Rusk's eyes went to Obi-Wan. "General Kenobi. You were here."

"I was rather occupied with Ventress, Major," Obi-Wan said. "General Skywalker has given his account."

"So he has." She let that sit a moment. Then she touched her ear and listened, and something in her face sharpened. "My sensor officer tells me an unregistered vessel lifted from these canyons nineteen minutes ago. South-east of here. Ore-scow profile, one engine running hot. It made orbit and jumped before the *Resolute* could get a tractor on it."

"Florrum's full of pirates," Anakin said.

"It is." Rusk turned her head and looked south-east, along the line of the canyon, as though she could see the white crust and the place where two people had stood. "The jump vector was Core-ward, General. Toward the Corellian Run." Her gaze came back to him, unhurried. "I'll be reporting that to the Admiral. I expect he'll pass it to the Chancellor's office. They both like to know where things are going."

She went back up the ramp. The trooper with the case followed her.

Anakin stood in the settling grit with Obi-Wan silent at his side and stared at the gold sky, at nothing, at the place where the scow must have climbed and vanished.

Core-ward. She wasn't running away. She was going *back*. Back to Coruscant, to the city that had already decided she was guilty, to the Senate and the Temple and the Chancellor's red office. Back to whoever it was she'd stood on that white crust and refused to name — someone she'd rather protect than save herself with. Someone she still loved.

She was going to prove it in front of everybody, she'd said.

He had just made very sure she would do it alone.

## Chapter 23: The Order

Six hours out of Florrum, the medbay console chimed with a follow-up request concerning a Chancellery aide's left wrist, and Barriss Offee, who had healed that wrist herself months ago and knew there was nothing left in it to follow up, felt her hands go perfectly still.

The duty droid rolled toward the console. "A routine query, Healer. I can answer it from the file."

"No," Barriss said. "I'll take it. Isolation two."

She did not hurry. That was the first discipline she had learned in the clinic as a youngling, carrying trays: the one who runs spills. She walked past the three troopers asleep under scald dressings from the geyser field, past the cot where Master Kenobi had sat an hour earlier, grey with ore dust from the crown of his head to his boots, declining everything but a cold pack for his shoulder. *I'm told I was buried,* he had said, mildly, while she scanned him. *Which seems an exaggeration, since here I am.* He had looked at her a moment longer than the scan required. Everyone was looking at everyone a moment too long on this ship.

Isolation two was a white box with one cot and a smell of antiseptic so strong it had a taste. She sealed the door, sat on the edge of the cot, and keyed the query open.

It was not Dace Orrin.

The Chancellor resolved out of the blue static a hand's height tall, seated in one of his deep chairs with his fingers folded on his knee, as if she had dropped by for tea and he were glad of it. Even in miniature, even in monochrome, she could feel the warmth of that red room on her face.

"Barriss. Forgive the subterfuge. I find a wrist is the most discreet thing in the galaxy. No one ever asks about a wrist." He tilted his head. "You look tired, my dear."

"Chancellor."

"I won't keep you. I only wanted you to hear something from me before you heard it from the Admiral, who has a way of making news sound like a sentence." A small, rueful smile. "Major Rusk tells us the vessel that left Florrum jumped Core-ward. She believes it will make for Coruscant. With one engine running as hot as the Major describes, she estimates three days."

Barriss said nothing. She had known since the hangar deck, since she'd watched General Skywalker come up the ramp with white crust caked on his boots and a face like a shut door. She'd known since before that, honestly — since the dawn she'd felt Ahsoka in the Force for the first time in five months, bright and whole and free of the collar, and had sat in this medbay with her hands pressed flat on her knees, making herself small.

"She isn't running," the Chancellor said. "I think we must both admit that now. A girl who runs goes outward, toward the Rim. A girl who comes home has something to say." He let a breath pass. "And I think, from what Anakin did not tell me in his report, that she knows to whom she wishes to say it."

"Anakin told you —"

"Anakin told me she lost him in the vents. Anakin is a very poor liar to the people who love him." The hologram's hands opened, closed again. "He believes she knows who did it. He believes it's someone she cares for. He said that much without meaning to."

The antiseptic taste had reached the back of her throat. She swallowed it.

"Let me be plain with you," he said, "because you have always been plain with me, and I value it. If young Tano proves her innocence, she does not merely prove that she did not do a thing. She proves that someone else did. A verdict cannot be set aside into empty air. The Senate will want the chair filled. There will be a second trial."

"Yes."

"I sealed what I had, Barriss. My little camera. The gallery. Those later recordings from the detention level I told you I had no need to watch. I could keep them sealed because there was a verdict standing in front of them, solid as a wall. Nobody asks for footage of a closed case." His voice was very gentle. "In a second trial, everything is asked for. Admirals file requests. Senators subpoena. Clerks find things in drawers. I could protect you from a question nobody was asking. I can't protect you from one the whole Republic is asking at once."

She heard herself say, "Then there's nothing you can do."

"Not then. No." He looked down at his folded hands, as though they disappointed him. "The only shelter I can offer you is a world in which there is no second trial. I'm afraid that is the only world in which my help is worth anything at all."

The cot creaked as she shifted. In the next room a trooper muttered in his sleep and was quiet.

"And how," Barriss said carefully, "would there be no second trial?"

The Chancellor smiled at her with real fondness, as one smiles at a clever student who has asked the question one hoped for.

"I'm a politician, my dear. I only know the ways that involve committees." He sighed. "But you were a healer long before you were anything else. A healer knows better than anyone that not every patient can be saved. That a wound left open will poison everything around it. That sometimes the hardest mercy is the one done quickly, by a steady hand, before the suffering spreads."

She did not answer. She could not have.

"And there is Anakin to consider," he went on, softer still. "You've seen what he is when she's in danger. You saw the cup at Narkis — oh yes, the Commandant wrote it up; a steel cup crushed like paper. If Ahsoka sets a name before him, I do not believe there is anyone in this galaxy who could stand between Anakin Skywalker and the owner of that name. Not his Council. Certainly not an old man in a red office." He shook his head. "I would grieve that. For him as much as anyone."

"What do you want me to do?"

"Want?" He seemed faintly surprised, then sorry for her. "Nothing, Barriss. Nothing you don't already want for yourself. I only wished you to understand where things stand, so that whatever comes, you'll have chosen it with your eyes open. That is all friendship ever really is, I think. Clear sight, shared." The hologram flickered as the relay shifted. "Rest, if you can. You'll be home before she is."

The blue light folded down to a point and went out.

Barriss sat on the edge of the cot in the white room for a long time.

He had not said it. She went back over every word, the way she went back over a scan she didn't trust, and he had not said it once. He had talked about committees and wounds and mercy and friendship. He had talked about Anakin. He had told her what he could not do.

She understood him perfectly.

She looked down at her hands, waiting for them to shake. They did not. They lay on her knees, green and patterned and steady as a surgeon's, and that frightened her more than anything he had said.

* * *

The Temple clinic at night was the only place on Coruscant that still felt like the Order she had joined.

Barriss had been home two days. She'd taken the late rotation both nights, because the clinic at the late rotation asked nothing of anyone. A Padawan with a sprained knee from the training salle. An old Master whose lungs rattled when it rained above the Temple, which was always. Racks of bacta sleeves to count and date and turn so the oldest came forward. The lights dimmed to amber. The air faintly sweet with kolto. Work that had an end to it.

She was turning the bacta racks when Luminara came in.

Her master did not announce herself. She never had. She came across the clinic floor in her dark robes with her hands folded into her sleeves, sat down on the end of an empty cot, and watched Barriss work, the way she had watched her a thousand times in the gardens and the archives and the salles, with that stillness that had once felt like shelter.

"You should be asleep, Master," Barriss said.

"So should you." Luminara's eyes went to the rack. "You've dated that sleeve twice."

Barriss looked. She had. She peeled the second label off with her thumbnail and set the sleeve back in its slot.

"Master Kenobi gave his report to the Council this morning," Luminara said. "Ventress fought him with a knife. A single knife, and the Force, and a great deal of falling ore." She paused. "Not her own blades. It seems she never recovered them. Master Kenobi found that curious. So did Master Windu, though he didn't say so."

"She's a bounty hunter," Barriss said. "She could have lost them anywhere."

"Yes. She could." Luminara let the words rest. "You stayed aboard at Florrum."

"The medbay needed a healer. There were burns from the vents."

"There were. Three, and minor, and Commander Rex's medic could have managed them blindfolded." Her voice held no accusation. That was the worst of it. It was only careful, the way a hand is careful on a bandage it suspects is hiding something worse. "I asked you once about a bruise on your arm. Do you remember? The day after the Dovrin platform."

Barriss's left forearm, under her sleeve, remembered. The mark had faded months ago. The shape of it hadn't.

"I remember."

"You told me it was a crate. Then you told me it was sparring. Then you wouldn't let me heal it." Luminara looked down at her own folded hands, then up again. "I told myself a great many things after that. That you were grieving. That the war was in you the way it is in all of us now. That a Padawan is allowed a door or two her master doesn't open." She drew a slow breath. "I am not asking about the bruise tonight."

The clinic was very quiet. Somewhere at the far end a monitor kept its patient time.

"Barriss," her master said, gently and directly, "is there something you need to tell me?"

There it was. Five months of it, laid on the cot between them like a tray of instruments, every one of them clean and ready. All she had to do was pick one up.

She thought: *The warehouse. The mask. Letta's throat under my hand from across a corridor. The three troopers outside the cells — I lit candles for them because she asked me to. She asked me to.* She thought of Ahsoka at Narkis, holding up her healed shin and smiling at her like a sister. She thought of a red room and a gentle voice saying *a steady hand.* And she thought of Luminara on a witness stand in a second trial, being asked under oath what she had suspected and when, and having to answer.

"No, Master," Barriss said. "There's nothing."

She heard how it sounded. Calm. Courteous. A good Padawan's answer, given in a good Padawan's voice. She had become very good at that voice.

Luminara held her gaze, and Barriss watched her master know.

It was not dramatic. Nothing in Luminara's face moved that a stranger would have seen. But something behind it settled, the way a stone settles to the bottom of still water and the surface closes over it, and Barriss felt it in the Force as plainly as a hand withdrawn.

She waited for the rest. For Luminara to rise, take her wrist, say *No. Sit down. We are not leaving this room.* For her to call Master Windu. For her to do anything at all that would take the choice out of Barriss's hands and put it somewhere else.

Luminara stood. She smoothed her robes.

"Then go and sleep, Barriss," she said. "Leave the racks. They'll still be here at dawn." She crossed to the door, and stopped with her hand on the frame, not turning around. "I'll be in my quarters. If that changes."

And she went.

Barriss stood among the bacta sleeves with the amber light on her hands and understood that she had just been given the most terrible thing her master had to give: her own freedom. No one was going to stop her. No one was going to drag the truth out of her for her own good. Luminara had looked straight into the closed door and decided, as a Jedi Master decides, that she would not break it down — that if it opened, it had to open from inside.

Barriss had wanted, more than she had wanted anything in all those months, for someone to break it down.

She left the racks. She did not go to sleep.

* * *

Crawlway C-31 had not changed. That was the thing about the Temple's underside: nothing down there ever changed. The upper Temple was rebuilt, repainted, re-dedicated, wreathed in candles for the hangar dead. Three levels below the eastern foundation, it was still the same old dark, the same weeping durasteel, the same smell of cold stone and stale lubricant that had been there when the first Jedi laid the first block.

She went down on the third night, when the clinic rotation ended and the corridors had emptied.

The route was in her body. Through the service door behind the old water-reclamation pumps. Down the ladder with the third rung bent. Along the conduit tray on her hands and knees, the glow rod in her teeth, the stone close enough on either side to brush her shoulders. Past the junction where the air went suddenly warm from some forgotten heat exchanger and then cold again. She counted the panels as she had counted them five months ago, with her own saber in one hand and Ventress's in her satchel and her heart going so hard she had thought the whole Temple would hear it.

Tonight her heart was quiet. She noted that, as a healer notes a symptom.

The panel was where she had left it, its edge fused to the frame in a ragged seam where she had run her blade along it. She knelt, lit her saber, and the blue light filled the crawlway like water filling a cup. She cut along the old seam slowly, not wanting to touch what was inside, and the panel tipped toward her with a scrape of hot metal and leaned against her knees.

The bundle sat in the cavity behind it, exactly where she had put it.

The surgical drape had gone grey with dust and stiff with damp. She drew it out with both hands, and even through the cloth she felt them — the two curved hilts lying side by side, heavy and wrong, their bled crystals humming just beneath her senses like a held breath that had never been let go. Five months in the dark had not quieted them. If anything they felt more awake, the way a wound left untended grows hot.

She laid the bundle across her knees and unfolded the drape.

The mask was on top. Bone-white, slanted eye-slits, the curve of the brow catching the blue of her saber. She had forgotten how light it was. She had worn it for barely an hour in all, and in her memory it weighed as much as the hab stack at Dovrin had weighed on Anakin Skywalker's outstretched hand.

Underneath lay the sabers. She did not touch them yet.

She had come down here once before to destroy them. She remembered kneeling in this exact spot with the drape open and her hand over the hilts, reaching for the crystals to crush them, and finding she could not. She had told herself then that it was weakness — that some coward in her wanted to keep a proof, a way back, a door. She had sealed them in the wall and walked away and lit four candles every dawn after, and never once let herself ask what the door was for.

Now she knew what they were for, or what someone wished them to be for. Ventress's blades, found beside a body, would tell a story the whole Republic already wanted to believe: the fugitive and her Separatist accomplice, falling out in the lower levels. A sad end. No second trial. A steady hand.

He had not said it. He had not needed to. He had only reminded her where she'd hidden the knife.

Barriss closed her hand around the first hilt. The crystal's hunger came up her arm like cold water. She lifted it, and the second, and laid them in her satchel side by side, and wrapped the drape over them so the curves didn't show. She held the mask a moment longer. Then she put it in too.

She sealed nothing behind her. She left the panel leaning against the wall with its seam still glowing faintly orange, cooling in the dark, and went back along the conduit tray on her hands and knees with the satchel dragging at her shoulder and the glow rod in her teeth.

* * *

The comlink chimed as she came up the ladder.

Not her Temple comlink. That one sat in the clinic charging cradle where she'd left it, logged and traceable. This was the other one — the old, scuffed, civilian unit she had carried in her inner pocket since the warehouse without ever switching it off, for the same reason she had never managed to crush two kyber crystals. It had one channel set on it. A private frequency she had given Ahsoka long ago, years before any of this, laughing: *In case you ever need a healer and don't want the whole Temple knowing what you've done to yourself.* Ahsoka had used it exactly once. From the lower levels, on the run, with Ventress beside her, asking her friend in the Temple where the nano-droids had come from.

Barriss had answered that call. She had given her an address.

She hung on the ladder with one hand, three levels below the eastern foundation, and stared at the little amber light blinking against the dark of her robe.

It was a text message. No voice. No image. No signature. She brought it up with her thumb.

*I know. Meet me where you left me.*

That was all.

She read it four times. The words did not change. She found herself checking the signal path the way she would have checked a stranger's pulse — and there was no relay tag, no Holonet routing. A short-range bounce off the city grid. Whoever had sent it was on Coruscant. Was here. Already.

Rusk had said three days. It had been three days.

Barriss climbed the rest of the ladder because her body knew how. She stepped out through the service door behind the reclamation pumps and stood in the dim corridor with the satchel pulling at her shoulder and the comlink still lit in her palm.

*Where you left me.*

An old munitions warehouse in the lower levels, its roof half gone, its floor stacked with nano-droid crates that Barriss herself had seen arranged. A girl on her back among them, unconscious, with a stolen Guard baton lying out of reach where it had been knocked from her hand. The Coruscant Guard's lights already sweeping the windows. Barriss had stood over her for perhaps three heartbeats before she ran — long enough to see that Ahsoka was breathing, not long enough to do anything about it.

*I know.* Not *I think.* Not *Tell me it isn't you.* Ahsoka had never in her life been able to say a hard thing without softening it with a joke, and she had not softened this.

There was no time in the message. There didn't need to be. Ahsoka would be there now, or soon, and she would wait. She had waited in ice for four months. She could wait in the dark for a night.

Barriss walked.

She went back up through the Temple by the servants' ways, the satchel against her hip. At this hour the halls belonged to the night-shift droids and a few insomniac Knights pacing the colonnades. Nobody stopped her. Why would they? A healer with a healer's bag, coming from the lower levels at night; there was always someone down there who needed something.

At the junction below her quarters she stopped, without quite meaning to, and looked down the long corridor toward Luminara's door.

There was a line of light under it. Her master was awake.

*I'll be in my quarters. If that changes.*

She stood there long enough for the light to become the only thing she could see. Then she turned the other way.

In her own room she did not turn on the lamp. She took off her outer robe and put on the dark hooded cloak she had worn to the Chancellor's office that first afternoon, the one that hid the face. She clipped her own saber to her belt, where it had always been, where its blue crystal sat calm and familiar and faintly reproachful against her side. She checked the satchel once — the drape, the two curved hilts beneath it, the white mask on top — and buckled it closed. She put the old comlink back in her inner pocket, still on.

On the shelf by the window stood the four candles she lit every dawn, burned down to stubs. She looked at them for a long time. She had never named them to anyone. Jackar. CT-2261. CT-4408. CT-9170. A man she had killed through his wife's hands, and three troopers she had killed with Ahsoka's blades, and then lit candles for at Ahsoka's request.

There was no candle for Letta. Ahsoka had guessed wrong about that, kindly. There had never been room on the shelf.

Barriss pulled up her hood.

She took the old maintenance lift down from the eastern wing, the one the clinic used for deliveries, and stepped out into the rain at the base of the Temple's long shadow. Below her the city fell away level after level into neon and steam, toward the old munitions district and a building with half a roof, and a girl who had once pulled her out of her own mind on Geonosis and asked nothing for it.

She had three blades with her. One was her own. Two had been made to kill. She had a white mask that could make her anyone, and a face underneath it that the Chancellor had saved in a single frame.

He had given her a world with no second trial. Luminara had given her a door she would have to open herself. And Ahsoka, from somewhere in that rain, had given her five words and an address she already knew.

Barriss went down into the lower levels, and she did not know — truly did not know, for the first time since the hangar burned — which of the three blades she meant to light.

## Chapter 24: Senator Amidala's Guests

There was a Dathomirian in Padmé's reading chair, and she was eating the last of the pallies.

Padmé stood in the doorway of her own sitting room with her cloak half off one shoulder and the day's datapads under her arm, and for a long moment neither of them spoke. Asajj Ventress peeled the fruit with a short, ugly knife, letting one long curl of skin drop onto the carpet Padmé's mother had sent from Theed. The lights were at their evening setting. The veranda doors stood open to the traffic lanes, and the wind that came through them smelled of rain and hot metal.

"Your security," Ventress said, "is decorative."

Padmé did not reach for the blaster in the side-table drawer. Ventress would be faster, and the knife had gone still the instant Padmé's hand twitched. "The building has a Guard detail," she said. "Doubled, since Narkis."

"Yes. We counted them." Ventress tipped her head toward the veranda. "The one with the shoulder knows their rotation. Apparently it hasn't changed in three years. Your Republic is very loyal to its habits."

A second shape stepped out of the shadow by the veranda doors, and Padmé forgot about the blaster.

Ahsoka was taller. That was the first absurd thing Padmé noticed: five months, and the girl she had sat beside at the defense table had grown. She wore a patched spacer's jacket over grey thermals. A raw pink ring circled her neck where something had been fastened for a long time, and a small healing burn sat under her jaw. The saber at her belt wasn't hers. Her face was thinner, older, very still, and she held herself the way people did when they expected to be sent away.

"I'm sorry," Ahsoka said. "I didn't know where else—"

Padmé dropped the datapads. She crossed the room, took Ahsoka by both shoulders and pulled her in, and felt the girl go rigid and then not rigid at all. Ahsoka smelled of engine coolant and sulfur and someone else's coat. The tips of her montrals brushed Padmé's temple; they hadn't, before.

"You're alive," Padmé said into her shoulder. It came out foolish. She didn't care.

"Mostly." Ahsoka's voice was muffled. "You need to hear the rest before you decide anything. You can still call the Guard. I'd understand."

"Don't be ridiculous."

"She means it, ma'am," said a third voice, and a clone trooper limped in from the veranda.

He wore mismatched civilian layers, his left arm bound against his chest under the jacket, and he had the glassy, hollowed look of a man running a fever and ignoring it. A numeral five was inked at his right temple. Padmé looked at it, and at him, and the floor seemed to tilt a little under her.

She knew that mark. Not from the Holonet, which had shown only a grainy security still and a designation. She knew it because Anakin had sat on the edge of their bed at three in the morning, six weeks ago, and told her about the trooper with the five inked at his temple who had tried to kill the Chancellor and gone down a refinery shaft. *He used to be one of the best men I had.* He had turned his mechanical hand over and over in the lamplight as though checking whether it was still his.

"You're dead," Padmé said.

"Yes, ma'am." The trooper drew himself to something like attention, which seemed to cost him. "CT-5555. Fives. Officially deceased, recovery not attempted. I'd be grateful if the paperwork stayed that way."

"You attacked the Chancellor."

"No, ma'am. I was drugged, and then I was in a room with him, and then I was a traitor." His jaw worked. "I can tell you the rest. You might not want it."

Ventress bit into the pallie. "Oh, she'll want it. They always want it. Then they never sleep again."

Padmé looked at the three of them standing on her carpet — a convicted bomber, a Separatist assassin, a dead man. Far below, a transport sounded in the lanes. Tomorrow at ninth hour the Judiciary oversight subcommittee would hear Tarkin's people propose a fourfold expansion of the collar program, and she had meant to stand and say that a girl named Ahsoka Tano had been made to wear one for a hundred and eighteen days. On her desk, under the subcommittee briefing, lay a courteous notice from the Admiral's office reminding former counsel of their legal obligation to report any contact from the escaped prisoner.

"Sit down," she told Fives, "before you fall down. Ahsoka, close the veranda. And you." She looked at Ventress. "That was the last pallie."

"I know," Ventress said. "I checked."

* * *

Padmé made tea because her hands needed work. She scalded the side of her right hand on the kettle while Fives was explaining, in a flat report voice, what the Kaminoans had grown inside every clone's skull and what the Chancellor had kept in a small vial after it was cut out of his. She didn't feel the burn until Fives stopped talking and nodded at it.

He opened a battered medkit one-handed. A strip of tape across the lid read ONE EVERY TWELVE HOURS. DON'T BE A HERO. He pressed a square of bacta onto her skin with the care of a man who had been patched more often than he had patched anyone else, and the cool, sharp smell of it spread through the kitchen.

"Rex's," he said, seeing her read the lid.

"Rex knows you're alive."

"Rex knows a lot of things he isn't saying." Fives snapped the kit shut. "That's how he stays alive."

She didn't ask what the thing in the troopers' heads was for. She found she did not want to know tonight, and that cowardice frightened her nearly as much as his story had. Something grown into every one of the men who guarded this building, the Senate, the Chancellor's own door. A report blaming a virus. A vial kept in a desk. It was too large and it had no edges. She put it where she put the worst intelligence briefings, in a locked room at the back of her mind, and turned to the thing she could hold.

How they had reached her door she had already pieced together between Fives's sentences: Hondo's scow coming in under someone else's transponder code, a berth in the Undercroft where, Ventress said, nobody asked questions she hadn't already paid for, freight lifts, and the gap in her own Guard's rotation. What she could not piece together was why.

"Why Coruscant?" she asked. "Every Guard officer on the planet has your face, Ahsoka."

Ahsoka sat at the kitchen table, both hands around a cup she hadn't touched. "Because I know who did it."

Padmé set the kettle down.

At the trial she had asked a dozen times, a dozen ways. In the cell. At the defense table. In the last hour before the verdict, when there had been nothing left to lose. *Who sent you to the warehouse?* And Ahsoka had looked at her with that stubborn, shuttered face and said, *I promised.* Padmé had built a defense around the hole where that name should have been, and watched Tarkin pour the whole Republic's fear into it.

"Tell me," she said.

Ahsoka's lekku shifted, a small involuntary movement, as though something cold had touched them. "Barriss," she said. "Barriss Offee."

For a moment the name meant nothing. Then it meant too much at once. A hooded Mirialan in the Jedi gallery, every day of the trial, at Master Unduli's side, green hands folded in her lap. During Ahsoka's testimony Padmé had glanced up at that bowed head and thought, *There is one person in this room suffering as much as I am.* She had been grateful for her.

"No," Padmé said. "She's a healer. She went to Narkis. Anakin said she went to Narkis to—"

"She treated me." Ahsoka's voice was very level. "My shin. My shoulder. The nerves in my neck. Her hands shook the whole time, and I thought it was because she was kind." She turned the cup a quarter turn on the table. "She's the one who called me to the warehouse. She visited my cell the day before the keycard. She bought the nano-droids from Dalo Pesk, and Pesk's dead. She was in the mask on the Dovrin platform. Anakin saw her fight. He told me it was Temple forms."

Padmé put her palm flat on the counter. The bacta patch was cool against the stone. "Letta Turmond," she said. "The footage. Your hand was raised."

"Mine was raised because Letta was choking. Hers was raised somewhere else."

"Stars." It was barely a word. Padmé thought of that silent footage played and replayed in the tribunal chamber, Tarkin's dry voice slowing it frame by frame, the senators leaning in. Six Jedi in the hangar. Three troopers cut down with Ahsoka's own blades. And a friend in the gallery, watching the Republic decide whether to kill the girl she had put there. "She sat there," Padmé said. "She sat there and listened to him ask for your death."

"And then she lit candles," said Ventress from the doorway. "Isn't that touching."

"Ventress." Ahsoka didn't look up.

"I'm agreeing with the Senator. It's monstrous. I'm simply impressed."

Padmé made herself breathe, and then made herself think like counsel, because that was the only use she could be. "What proof do you have?"

"Her." Ahsoka tilted her head toward Ventress. "A dead dealer who described green, patterned hands to her and wanted two thousand credits for a recording nobody's found. A fighting style I couldn't place until I let myself. The way Barriss's hand shook." At last she lifted the cup, then set it down untouched. "A Separatist assassin's word and my memory. You've stood in that chamber. You know what that's worth."

Padmé knew exactly what it was worth. It was worth Tarkin's thin smile.

* * *

She did not go to the subcommittee. She sent a written statement opposing the expansion and a message pleading illness, and spent the next day with the shutters drawn, her staff on leave and Threepio dispatched to the Naboo embassy to inventory a ceremonial archive that would occupy him for a week. By noon her dining table had become a war room.

Fives had brought the recorder. He set it in the middle of the table among the teacups: a phase-two trooper's helmet, scuffed, its white gone grey at the edges, with a deep dent above the brow that someone had never bothered to hammer out.

"Mine," he said. "Rex found it at the top of the shaft after I went down. Carried it around for weeks without telling anybody, then handed it to me at Berth Nine wrapped in a shirt." Something moved in his face and was put away. "I put it into a wall the day they took her off the roster. He kept it."

"And it records," Padmé said.

"Every bucket records. Command uses it for after-action reviews and courts-martial." He tapped a seam beneath the left cheek guard. "Hardware timestamp, serial burned into the frame, sealed data chain. You can copy what's on it. You can't change it without the seal showing. Kix explained it to us once when Jesse wanted to erase something embarrassing." A faint ghost of a grin. "Jesse lost."

"Audio range?"

"Good enough across an open floor, if nobody's shouting over a saber."

Padmé turned the helmet toward her. The visor looked back at her, black and blank. "A sealed recording," she said slowly, "of a Jedi confessing in her own words. A recording made by a dead man, held by a fugitive, in the company of a Separatist." She looked up. "Tarkin would call it staged before the first frame finished playing."

"Which is why you're coming," Ahsoka said, and then, a moment too late, "if you'll come."

Padmé had already understood. A Senator of the Republic, standing in that warehouse, seeing with her own eyes and swearing to it afterward — a Senator's sworn statement went into the Senate record the moment she rose to give it, before any office could seal it, before anyone could decide the footage was a forgery. Without her, the helmet was a curiosity. With her, it was a second trial.

"Of course I'll come," she said.

"No," Ahsoka said. "Not of course. Listen to the whole plan first."

The warehouse was the one in the old munitions district, the building with half a roof where Ahsoka had been found among the nano-droid crates. Ahsoka would wait on the floor, in the open, where it had begun. Fives would take the old crane gantry along the north wall, helmet recording, with a line of sight down onto the floor and a stair behind him to the service lane. Ventress would hold the loading doors at the south end, where a person running would run.

"And my sabers," Ventress said, "come home to me."

"After," Ahsoka said. "Not before she's said it. Not while she's still talking."

"And if your friend lights them at you before she's said anything?"

"Then I'll deal with her."

"Will you." Ventress's smile was thin and curved like the hilts she'd lost. "You couldn't say her name for a week."

"I've said it now." Ahsoka's voice didn't rise. "She walks out of that warehouse alive. That's the condition. You agreed on Florrum."

"I agreed to a great many things on Florrum. Most of them involved that Weequay." But Ventress let it lie, and Padmé saw that whatever passed between those two, Ahsoka had won this part of it a while ago.

Padmé herself would come last. Ahsoka was firm: she would not travel with them, and she would not set foot on the warehouse floor. She would come down by public lift and transit after second hour, cloaked and alone, and climb the service stair to Fives in the gantry. If a red blade lit, she would not move toward it. If Fives said run, she would run.

"I've been shot at before," Padmé said.

"I know. By people who wanted you alive for a ransom." Ahsoka met her eyes. "Barriss doesn't want anything from you. That makes you nothing to her. Please."

Padmé gave her a pair of short-range comlinks from her household security case, old Naboo issue, off every Senate network, and Fives showed her two clicks and a pause — *sent* — and three clicks — *in position*. Ventress watched this with the expression of a woman watching children play at soldiers. Then Fives went to lie down on the couch with the last of Rex's fever shots in him and his eyes closed, and Ventress went out onto the veranda to glare at the city, and Padmé and Ahsoka were alone at the table with the helmet between them.

"There's one more thing," Ahsoka said. "Please don't tell Anakin I'm here."

Padmé had known it was coming. It still landed like a hand on her chest.

"He's been looking for you for five months," she said. "He's barely slept. Ahsoka, he—"

"He found me." Ahsoka looked at her own hands. "On Florrum. In a canyon. He told me how to get past the Judiciary team, and then he let me go."

Padmé didn't move. Anakin had come back to Coruscant with the Resolute, two days ago now, and had sent her exactly one message: *Back. Can't come yet. Debriefs.* Nothing about a canyon. Nothing about letting anyone go. She thought of him lying to a Council that already distrusted him, and of the Chancellor's patient, interested face, and a coldness climbed her spine.

"When I told him I knew who it was," Ahsoka went on, carefully, "and wouldn't say, the stones came up off the ground around him. He didn't even notice. If he knows it's Barriss, he won't stand in a gantry and wait for a confession. He'll—" She stopped. She did not finish it. Padmé was grateful, and ashamed of being grateful, because she had once sat in a garage on Tatooine and listened to Anakin finish that sentence himself, and she had never repeated it to a living soul.

"Afterward," Ahsoka said. "When it's on the record and she's in custody and nobody can make it disappear. Then he can know everything. Then he can be angry at me. I'd rather he was angry at me than—" Again she stopped. Then, choosing each word as if setting her feet down on ice: "He'll come back here eventually, won't he. When he's on Coruscant. He comes to see you sometimes. To talk. About the war."

The kitchen was very quiet. Padmé heard the couch creak as Fives shifted in his sleep. Ahsoka was not looking at her; Ahsoka was looking with great attention at the dent in the helmet, and her face had the particular stillness of someone handing over a gift wrapped so that neither of them would ever have to open it.

"Sometimes," Padmé said.

Ahsoka nodded once, as if that settled something, and did not ask anything more.

"I won't tell him," Padmé said. Saying it felt like signing a document she hadn't read all the way through.

"Thank you."

"Don't." Padmé stood and took the empty cups to the sink so that she didn't have to look at the girl's grateful face. "Don't thank me for this."

* * *

They left at dusk, over the veranda rail and into a hired air-taxi that Fives had summoned to a public platform two levels down, by the same gap in the Guard rotation they had used to come in. Ahsoka would send her message from the shadow of the Temple. Barriss's old comlink only answered on short range; it had to be close. Then the three of them would go down into the lower levels and wait.

At the rail Ahsoka turned back. The rain had started, a fine haze that blurred the lane lights to smears. "Second hour," she said. "Not before."

"Second hour," Padmé agreed.

Ventress was already gone. Fives sketched her a salute with his good hand and dropped out of sight. Ahsoka lingered one more beat, as if she meant to say something, and then she was gone too, and Padmé was alone in an apartment that still smelled of tea and bacta and coolant.

She cleaned. She was methodical about it, as she had been methodical before every hard vote of her life. Four cups washed and shelved. The pallie peel picked out of the Theed carpet strand by strand. The couch cushions turned over, the fever-shot wrapper burned in the kitchen incinerator, the dining chairs squared to the table. She left the bacta patch on her hand, because the burn was real.

She was sitting in the dark with a datapad she wasn't reading when the door chimed its private tone and Anakin came in.

He looked terrible. That was what she noticed first, before the relief, before the fear. His hair was wet from the landing platform, his tunic creased as though he'd slept in it for three days, which he probably had. The glove on his mechanical hand was new, and the knuckles of it were already scuffed. He stopped just inside the door and looked at her as if she were the one solid thing in a room built of smoke.

"I couldn't stay over there another night," he said.

"Then don't." She went to him, and he held on hard enough to hurt.

He wasn't hungry. She heated soup anyway, and he ate it standing at the counter, and halfway through he lifted his head and went still.

"Bacta," he said.

Her heart did something complicated. She held up her right hand. "The kettle. Last night. I'm an idiot."

He took her hand and turned it over, very gently, with the mechanical one. He studied the patch longer than a kettle burn deserved, and something passed behind his face that she couldn't read — a memory, she thought; something from somewhere else. Then he raised her hand and pressed his lips to the edge of the patch.

"Be careful," he said. "One of us has to be."

He talked, after. Not much, and not in order. The debriefs. The Council's long silences. Master Windu asking the same question four different ways. The Chancellor, who had been kind, who had told him that the offer stood: bring her in and she would be safe, she would be heard, he would see to it personally. Anakin said it as if it were the only rope left over a long drop.

"She's here," he said. "On Coruscant. I can't feel her. She's hiding herself, she's gotten good at it. But I know she's here." He looked at Padmé across the counter. "Has she come to you?"

The question was so simple. He asked it the way he'd ask whether she had eaten. He was too tired even to be suspicious; he was only hoping.

"No," Padmé said.

It was the first time. She had lied to the Senate about where she slept; she had lied to her family about why she didn't come home to Naboo more often; she had lied to the whole galaxy by omission every day for three years, for him. She had never once looked at him and lied *to* him about something that mattered. It went out of her mouth smooth and quiet and believable, like a coin dropping into deep water, and she felt it land somewhere inside her and stay.

"If she does," Anakin said, "you'll tell me."

"Of course."

Then the household comlink in her pocket clicked twice against her hip, and paused.

*Sent.*

Anakin glanced at the sound.

"Bail," she said. "About the subcommittee. He's furious I missed it."

That one was easier. That was the worst thing about it. The second lie slid out after the first as if the first had greased the way, and she understood in that moment how people became the kind of people they had never meant to be. Not with one great choice. With the second small one, and the way it didn't hurt as much.

He believed her. Of course he believed her. He finished the soup and let her lead him to bed and lay down beside her in his clothes, one arm heavy across her waist, and she listened to his breathing roughen and slow and deepen. She watched the chrono on the wall. She counted the minutes past midnight the way she had once counted votes.

At half past first hour, three clicks against her hip. *In position.*

At second hour she slid out from under his arm a fraction at a time. He murmured something without words and turned his face into the pillow. She dressed in the sitting room in the dark — plain trousers, plain boots, the hooded rain cloak she wore when she didn't want to be a Senator — and left her Senate comlink on the counter where it would be found if anyone looked, and clipped the small blaster from the side-table drawer to her belt under the cloak. At the door she stopped and looked back toward the bedroom.

Then she went out, and down, into the rain.

* * *

Anakin had not been asleep. Not really. He had been somewhere underneath it, where he'd lived for five months — a grey place where he could hear everything and rest from none of it.

He heard the door seal.

For a while he lay still, because he was so tired that the sound didn't mean anything yet. Then he reached across the bed and found the sheet cool, and the meaning arrived all at once.

He sat up. The apartment was dark. He reached out with the Force through the rooms, through the walls, the way he'd done ten thousand times without thinking, and found them empty. No Padmé. No Threepio. Only the rain on the veranda glass and a faint, sharp smell still hanging in the kitchen.

Bacta.

He had smelled bacta in a canyon on Florrum, in a stripped bunkhouse with a burn mark on the floor and nobody in it. He had told himself it meant nothing then. He told himself it meant nothing now — a kettle, a careless hand — and the telling didn't take.

He went to the window. Coruscant poured away beneath him, light over light, down into the haze where the lanes thinned out and the old districts lay dark and wet and forgotten. He hadn't let himself reach for her in days. Every time he had, there had been nothing — a closed door, a careful silence.

He reached now.

And she was there.

Not hidden. Not shielded. Far below him, in the deep wet dark of the lower levels, Ahsoka burned like a single lit candle — bright, steady, wide open, and waiting for someone.

Not for him. He knew the shape of her waiting for him. This was something else.

Anakin's mechanical hand closed slowly on the window frame until the metal sill creaked. Padmé's lie came back to him in her own calm voice — *No* — and with it, colder, the understanding that his wife and his Padawan had both looked at him and decided he could not be trusted with the truth.

He reached for his boots.

## Chapter 25: Where It Began

The comlink on Ahsoka's wrist clicked three times, and somewhere above her in the dark a Senator of the Republic and a man the Republic had declared dead settled in to watch her best friend confess to murder.

If her friend came.

Ahsoka let her hand fall back to her knee. The old munitions warehouse had not improved in five months. Rain still came through the roof in long silver threads, through holes punched by something heavy a long time ago, and it still pooled on the floor in the same low places, and the same smell still hung over everything — wet rust, dead machine oil, the faint chemical sourness that never quite leaves a building where explosives were once stored. The crates were gone. The Guard had taken every one of them as evidence, and where they had stood the grime of the floor gave way to pale, clean rectangles, a row of them, like the outlines of graves. A strip of evidence tape had snapped and lay soaking in a puddle near her knee. On the support pillar to her left, at about the height of her throat, there was a long black scorch where a red blade had missed her the first time.

She had been found right about here. On her side, between two of those crates, with Fox's lamp in her eyes and the rain on her face and no idea yet how completely she had been finished.

She breathed, and let the memory go by.

High in the north end of the building a crane gantry hung from its rusted rail, a cab of grey durasteel and broken windows forty meters above the floor. She did not look at it. She did not need to. Fives was up there with his left arm strapped to his chest and his helmet propped on the rail in front of him, the dent over its brow pointed down at her like an eyebrow raised in doubt. They had put a scrap of black tape over the little recording light so it wouldn't show. Padmé was beside him. Ahsoka could feel them both in the Force as two small warm points, one stubborn and feverish, one steady and very frightened and refusing to be. She drew a thin veil over them, the way she'd once learned to hide younglings in a cave, and left it there.

Herself she did not hide. Barriss would need to feel her here, alone and waiting, before she came through any door. Whoever else in the city might reach down tonight and feel it too, Ahsoka had decided, would have to be a problem for afterward.

By the south loading doors, Ventress was a cold knot of impatience in the shadows.

"Your friend," Ventress had said, an hour ago, "is late."

"She'll come."

"You keep saying that as if it were a kindness."

Ahsoka had not answered, because it wasn't.

She closed her eyes now and went down into the stillness she'd found in the Well, the one that hadn't needed the Force at all — just the breath going in and the breath going out and the knowledge that the next one would come. The burn under her left jaw itched. The raw ring around her neck where the collar had sat for four months ached in the wet. Hondo's saber lay across her thighs, heavy and plain, the worn black ridges of its grip cool under her fingers.

She waited.

* * *

She felt Barriss before she heard her.

It came the way Ventress had described it on Florrum, in a storeroom that smelled of ore dust: calm water, very clear, very deep, and at the bottom something that had stopped moving. Ahsoka had wanted to argue with that description when she heard it. She didn't now. It came toward her through the lower levels like a cold current through warm sea, unhurried, and stopped outside the narrow personnel door in the west wall — the same door, Ahsoka realized, that she had come through herself five months ago, with Ventress at her back and a friend's directions in her head.

The door scraped open. Rain blew in.

Barriss stepped through with her hood up and her healer's satchel over one shoulder. Her own lightsaber hung at her belt where it always had. She shut the door behind her with care, the way she did everything, and stood with her back to it, dripping.

Neither of them spoke.

Then Barriss opened the satchel. Ahsoka watched her lift away a fold of pale cloth — a surgical drape, Ahsoka saw, still creased from long storage — and take out something white. She turned it once in her hands. Then she raised it and fitted it over her face.

The bone-white mask. The slanted eye-slits. The hood falling forward around it.

And there it was again, the figure from among the crates, the shape that had come out of the dark with a red blade in each hand. Ahsoka's body knew it before her mind did. Her shoulders rose; her grip shifted on the saber hilt; her heart kicked hard against her ribs, the way it had in this room when she had still believed she was fighting a stranger.

"Is that who I'm talking to?" Ahsoka said. Her voice came out level. She was proud of that, distantly.

The mask regarded her.

"I thought it would be easier," Barriss said. The voice behind it was flattened and strange, but it was hers. "If you were speaking to this. I thought it might be easier for both of us."

"It isn't."

"No." A long breath, audible even through the mask. "No, I can see that."

She reached up and took it off.

Her face was thinner than it had been on Narkis. The diamond markings across her cheeks stood out darker than Ahsoka remembered, the way paint stands out on a wall that has lost its color. There were shadows under her eyes, but her eyes were very steady. Barriss set the mask down on the corner of a fallen beam beside the door, face up, so that it stared at the roof with its empty slits and filled slowly with rain.

"Ventress is at the south doors," Barriss said. "I can feel her from here."

"She wanted to come."

"Of course she did." Something almost like humor touched her mouth and died there. "She'll want them back."

"She'll want a lot of things." Ahsoka stood up. Her knees were stiff from kneeling. She did not ignite the saber; she held it loosely, emitter down, along her forearm. "Tell me."

"You already know. Your message said so."

"I want to hear you say it."

Barriss studied her for a while. Rain fell between them in a curtain from one of the roof holes, catching what little light there was, and Ahsoka thought: *She doesn't know about the gantry. She thinks it's just us, and Ventress, and nobody would ever believe Ventress.* She kept her eyes on Barriss and did not let her thoughts rise.

"Where would you like me to begin?" Barriss said.

"The hangar."

"The hangar." Barriss nodded slowly, as if accepting a diagnosis. "I bought the nano-droids from a Rodian in the Kesh Arcade. He didn't ask what I wanted them for. People down here don't. I gave them to Letta Turmond, and Letta gave them to her husband in his lunch tin, and Jackar Bowmani carried them into the Temple hangar the way he carried in everything else, every morning, for eleven years." She paused. "He never knew. You should know that. He thought she'd packed him a meal."

Ahsoka's hand tightened on the hilt.

"Six Jedi," she said. "Tutso Mara. Clones. Workers. People who fixed lifts."

"I know the number."

"Why?"

And Barriss, who had always been the quiet one, who had sat at the back of every class and asked the question nobody else had thought of, lifted her chin.

"Because nobody was looking," she said. "Ahsoka, when did you last see a Jedi heal someone who wasn't a soldier? When did you last see one of us sit with a farmer, or a child, or a dying man who wasn't wearing armor we'd given him? People used to send for us when they wanted a war stopped. Now we have ranks. We have *fleets*. We read casualty reports over breakfast and call the numbers acceptable. We take boys who were grown in jars ten years ago and we walk them into droid fire and we say the Force is with them." Her voice had not risen. It had not needed to. "I have stood in the clinic and put the same troopers back together three times so they could be sent out to be broken a fourth. I have watched Masters plan campaigns the way the archivists catalog star charts. The Order has become a weapon that the Senate keeps in a drawer, and I thought — I truly thought — that if the war came home, into our own hangar, into the one place we believed was ours, then perhaps the Jedi would look up and see what they had become."

"And did they?"

"They mourned. They lit candles. Then they went back to the war." Barriss's mouth twisted. "And they expelled you."

The rain hissed in the puddles.

"Letta," Ahsoka said.

Barriss closed her eyes for a moment. When she opened them, some of that terrible steadiness had gone.

"She was going to tell you it was a Jedi. She was going to say my name — she'd have had to, sooner or later, and she'd asked for you because she thought you might believe her. I was in the upper service passage over the cells. I hadn't planned it. I want you to understand that I hadn't planned it." She raised her right hand a little, looked at it, let it fall. "It was very easy. That was what I couldn't forgive afterward. Not that I did it. That it was easy."

"And then you watched them arrest me for it."

"Yes."

"And the three troopers." Ahsoka heard her own voice go thin and careful, like ice in the yard on Narkis that would hold you right up until it didn't. "CT-2261. CT-4408. CT-9170."

Barriss flinched at the numbers. Not much. Enough.

"I took your sabers out of the evidence locker," she said. "I used them because they had to be yours. I left the keycard in your cell because I knew you would run. I knew that if you ran, you would call the only friend you thought you had left in the Temple, and I would tell you where to go." She glanced around the warehouse, the pale squares on the floor, the scorched pillar. "Here. I sent you here. I had the crates moved in the night before. I wore that." A small motion of her head toward the mask in the rain. "I took Ventress's sabers from her while she was on the floor, and I fought you with them, and I left you lying among the crates for the Guard to find."

Ahsoka found that she was shaking. Not visibly — or she hoped not visibly — but deep inside, a fine continuous tremor like a ship's hull in the last second before a jump. The Force pressed close around her, hot and dark at the edges, offering itself. She could feel exactly how easy it would be. She could feel the shape of Barriss's throat in it.

She breathed in. She breathed out. The next breath came.

"There's more," Barriss said quietly. "You'll want all of it. The night before the vote, Anakin found Ventress in the old district. He was close — very close. She'd begun to tell him things. I came down on them with the mask on and threw her off a ledge, and I cut a pylon with the droids I had left, and a whole hab stack began to fall." She swallowed. "He could have caught me. I was on a catwalk across the platform. He looked right at me. And then he turned around and held up a building with his hands so that a mother and her child could get out from under it. I stood there and watched him choose."

Ahsoka had known it. Anakin had told her himself, in the canyon on Florrum, with the stones lifting around his boots. But she had only ever heard it from his side. She had never pictured the mask up there in the dust, watching him choose, and counting on it. She thought of Anakin in the visiting room on Narkis, thinner, angrier, a steel cup folding in on itself in front of him. Of how he'd never been able to look at her for long.

"Don't," she said.

"You asked for all of it."

"I know what I asked for." She made herself lift her eyes. "Then you came to see me. Before the trial. You healed my wrists."

"Yes."

"I thanked you."

"Yes." Barriss's voice cracked, finally, just a little, at the bottom. "You promised you would never name the friend who sent you here. You asked me to light candles for the troopers. You said you couldn't, so would I."

"And on Narkis." Ahsoka could feel the place on her shin where the gash had closed under those green, patterned, steady hands. "You came all that way. You fixed my shoulder. You put your hands on my collar and made the pain stop, and you wrote that note, and they turned it down so I wouldn't seize." She heard herself laugh, a short ugly sound she didn't recognize. "I told you to be careful. I told you there was someone cleaning up, and I was *afraid for you*."

"I know."

"Was that guilt? All of it? Every time you touched me?"

Barriss was quiet a long moment.

"The note was the only honest thing I did all year," she said. "The rest — I don't know anymore, Ahsoka. I don't know which parts of me were lying. I'm not sure there's anyone left in here who could tell you."

Ahsoka turned away from her. She did it before she could stop herself, and then she stood with her back half-turned and stared at the black scorch on the pillar until the edges of it blurred.

"The fourth candle," she said to the pillar.

She heard Barriss breathe in.

"I asked you to light three. For the troopers. You lit four. I lay in my cell on Narkis and thought about that fourth candle for days — I thought you'd lit it for Letta. Because you were kind. Because you felt sorry for a woman everyone hated." She turned back. "Who was it for?"

"Jackar Bowmani," Barriss said.

The rain filled the silence.

"He fixed lift motors. He had a little scar on his thumb from a coupling that slipped. He used to hum while he worked; I used to hear him from the clinic walkway." She looked down at her hands. "I've tried to light one for Letta. I can't. My hands won't do it. She knew, you see. She chose. Jackar didn't choose anything. I chose for him."

Ahsoka's eyes stung. She hated that they stung. She had been certain, on Florrum, sitting in that dead outpost with the bacta smell in her nose and the pieces falling together one after another, that once she knew everything she would feel something clean — fury or grief, one or the other, something she could hold by the hilt. Instead she felt everything at once, and none of it had a handle.

"Come back with me," she said.

Barriss stared at her.

"I mean it. Come in. Tell them what you told me. Tell the Council, tell Master Luminara, tell the tribunal. You were brave enough to do all this; be brave enough to say it." Her voice was rising now; she let it. "I *lost everything*, Barriss. I stood in that chamber and listened to the Chancellor say *death*, and I believed him, and for one breath I was dead — do you understand that? I was dead, standing up, and then he said *life* and I didn't even know if that was better. They put a collar on me so I couldn't feel the Force. I broke ice for four months. There are people in that prison who still think I blew up the Temple. My own Master can't look at me. And all you have to do is *say it*."

"Who would hear it?"

"Everyone."

"No." Barriss shook her head slowly. "Ventress? A Separatist assassin with a price on her head, swearing a Jedi stole her sabers? And you — a convicted bomber, escaped in a Separatist raid, believed to have joined Count Dooku? They wouldn't even let you finish the sentence." She drew a breath. "And it doesn't matter, because I wasn't sent here to talk."

The word *sent* went into Ahsoka like a needle.

"Sent," she said.

"Someone knows." Barriss's voice dropped. "Someone with a great deal of power has a recording of my hand in that passage over the cells, at the very second Letta died. Clearer than the one they showed at your trial. He's had it for months." She stopped, and Ahsoka saw her catch the word *he* and hold it, too late, and decide to let it stand. "They have been very kind to me. That's how it's done, did you know? You'd be amazed how far kindness reaches. Further than threats. A threat you can refuse."

"Who?"

"If I told you, you'd do something brave and stupid about it, and they'd kill you, and it would all have been for nothing. Again." Barriss lifted her chin. "If you are cleared, there will be a second trial. A second trial is the one thing that recording cannot survive — and neither can I, and neither can the person who has been keeping it. So I was told, very gently, in a medbay, by someone who never once used the word, that it would be best for everyone if you never testified."

Ahsoka looked at her. At the hood, the rain on its shoulders. At the satchel.

"And you came."

"I came."

"To kill me."

"I came," Barriss said, "not knowing which blade I'd light. I walked all the way down here in the rain, past Master Luminara's door — her light was on, she was waiting up for me — and I still didn't know. I thought perhaps when I saw you I would know."

"Do you?"
