Barriss reached into the satchel. The surgical drape came away and fell to the wet floor, and in her hands were two curved hilts, dark and elegant and wrong. She held them a moment without igniting them, as if weighing them. Her own saber stayed at her belt, untouched.

"These seem more honest," she said. "Don't you think?"

From the south doors, Ventress's presence in the Force flared like a struck match. Ahsoka lifted her free hand, palm out, without looking — *stay there* — and felt Ventress's fury hang a moment, coiled and furious, and then hold.

"You don't have to do this," Ahsoka said.

"That," Barriss said, "is what everyone kept telling me about the war."

The red blades snapped alive.

* * *

Ahsoka thumbed the blue blade on as Barriss came at her, and the warehouse filled with color.

Barriss had always been the better student of forms. Ahsoka had known that since the first time they sparred, the year after Geonosis: Barriss in the training salle with her blade drawn in close and level, every parry the minimum, giving nothing away, waiting for you to tire yourself out against her. But Ventress's sabers weren't made for that. They were curved at the hilt, built to swing out wide and come back hooking, and Barriss fought with them the way someone speaks a language learned from books — correct, precise, and with every word a half-beat late.

Ahsoka took the first strike high on the blue blade, turned the second low, and felt the jolt of it all the way up her arm into the sore place in her shoulder. Hondo's saber was heavier than hers. Five months heavier. There was no shoto in her other hand, nothing to catch the second red blade but air and the Force, and she spun out of its path and felt it pass so close the heat dried the rain on her cheek.

They broke apart. Circled.

Rain came down through the roof holes in columns, and every time a red blade swept through one it went up in a hiss of steam. The pale crate-squares on the floor slid past under Ahsoka's boots. She kept her saber in reverse grip, tight along her forearm, and watched Barriss's feet, because Barriss's face was giving her nothing.

Barriss came again — a feint high, a real cut low, the second blade looping in behind it. Ahsoka dropped under the first, jumped the second, and slashed backhand as she landed. Barriss caught it on both red blades crossed. For a moment they were close enough to see each other's breath. The blades ground together, blue between the red, throwing sparks that died in the puddles.

"You're slower," Barriss said, almost gently.

"I've been busy," said Ahsoka, and kicked her in the knee.

Barriss staggered back, recovered, and reached out with her left hand. Above them something groaned. Ahsoka looked up in time to see the old crane hook, a great rusted thing on a slack chain, come swinging down out of the dark toward her head. She threw herself flat. The hook went over her with a sound like a door slamming in the sky and crashed into the scorched pillar, and the pillar rang like a bell, and plaster and rust came down in a gritty rain.

She rolled to her feet in the debris and pushed.

A fallen section of the old mezzanine railing — three meters of bent durasteel — tore itself up off the floor and flew at Barriss end over end. Barriss cut it in half without looking at it. The two pieces clanged away to either side of her and skidded into the dark.

"You were always good at that," Ahsoka said, breathing hard.

"So were you."

They came together again, and this time neither of them held anything back.

It was fast after that, fast and loud and all light. Ahsoka went up a stack of broken shelving and Barriss followed, both red blades scything at her ankles; the shelving gave way under them and they rode it down into the shallows by the old loading ramp, blades still moving, water exploding up white around them. Barriss pressed her back across the ramp with a relentless overlapping rhythm, red on the left, red on the right, red on the left, and Ahsoka took every blow on Hondo's blade and felt her wrist begin to burn. A cut came in faster than she could turn; she twisted away and it skated across her left forearm, through the sleeve of her coat, and the pain was a white flash and a smell of scorched cloth. She hissed and kept moving.

She could feel Barriss through the Force now, the way you always can in a real fight. Not the calm water. Under it. Something churning and exhausted and desperate, a current going two ways at once.

*She doesn't want this either,* Ahsoka thought. *She just doesn't know how to stop.*

Barriss feinted left. Ahsoka bit. It was a good feint — the best she'd ever thrown at her — and Ahsoka's blue blade swung wide after it, and Barriss's right-hand saber came around and up in one smooth curve toward the open side of Ahsoka's neck, toward the raw ring where the collar had been.

And stopped.

It stopped a hand's width from her skin. Ahsoka felt the heat of it against the burn under her jaw. She saw Barriss's face above the red light — her eyes wide, her mouth open, as if she'd heard someone call her name from very far away. For one breath, one only, the blade hung there and did not move.

Ahsoka didn't wait to understand it.

She dropped. The red blade passed over her montrals. She reached out with her left hand and the Force together and *pulled*, and the curved hilt tore out of Barriss's right hand and went spinning away across the warehouse, its blade guttering out as it flew, and clattered somewhere near the foot of the loading ramp. Barriss swung the left-hand saber down at her. Ahsoka met it with the blue blade, caught it in a bind, turned her wrist the way Anakin had drilled into her a thousand times on a hundred decks — *don't fight the blade, Snips, fight the hand* — and wrenched.

The second hilt flew. It struck the fallen crane hook with a crack, went dark, and dropped into the water at its base.

Barriss stood empty-handed in the rain.

Ahsoka kicked her feet out from under her.

She went down onto her knees in a puddle, hard. Ahsoka was over her in the same motion, the blue blade turned and lowered so that its humming edge sat just beneath Barriss's chin, close enough that the rain falling between them burst into steam. With her left hand — her burned arm protesting — she reached and called Barriss's own saber off her belt. It slapped into her palm. She hooked it onto her own belt without taking her eyes off Barriss.

For a long moment the only sounds were the rain, and the blade, and the two of them breathing.

Barriss's hood had fallen back. Water ran down her face in lines across the diamond markings, and Ahsoka could not tell which of it was rain. She was looking past the blue blade at Ahsoka, and there was nothing on her face now at all — no defense, no argument, nothing calm. Just tiredness, all the way down.

"Do it," Barriss said.

Ahsoka didn't move.

"Please." Her voice was very small. "You have every right. You know you do. No one would ever blame you — not Ventress, not Anakin, not the Council. Not anyone." She swallowed, and the movement brought her throat a little closer to the blade, and she didn't pull away. "I can't go back up there. I can't light those candles again tomorrow morning. Please, Ahsoka. Finish it."

The Force was very loud. It was all around Ahsoka, inside her, roaring like the wind across the ice yard on Narkis at shift change, and in the middle of it she could feel exactly how simple it would be. One small turn of the wrist. She had lost everything she was to this woman; the Order, her rank, her Master's trust, her name, four months of the Force. She'd had the word *death* said over her in a full chamber. Six Jedi and a man who hummed while he fixed lift motors and three troopers who'd never even seen it coming. Letta, alone in a room with no one to help her breathe.

She thought about the silent footage. The tribunal had watched it frame by frame — a woman dying in a cell, and Ahsoka Tano standing in front of her doing nothing to stop it. They had looked at that and seen someone who would kill a helpless person because it was convenient. Tarkin had said it in his clean, dry voice. Nine senators had believed it, and the rest of the Republic after them.

They'd been wrong.

They'd been wrong, and she had spent four months in the ice holding onto that one fact like a heat-pack in her coat. If she turned her wrist now, here, in this room, with a woman on her knees before her who couldn't fight back — then they would have been right after all. Not about the hangar. Not about Letta. About *her*.

"No," Ahsoka said.

Barriss closed her eyes.

"They called me a murderer," Ahsoka said. Her voice was shaking now, and she let it. "They looked at me and decided I was someone who would kill a person who couldn't fight back. You *made* them think that. You built it piece by piece. And I'm not going to finish it for you." She lifted the blade away from Barriss's throat, a handspan, then another. "I won't become what they convicted. Not for you. Not for anyone."

She thumbed the switch. The blue blade folded down into the hilt and was gone, and the dark came back into the warehouse all at once, grey and wet and quiet.

Barriss made a sound — not a word — and bent forward over her knees until her forehead almost touched the water. Her shoulders shook. Ahsoka stood over her, the cold hilt in her hand, and found that she was crying too, without noise, and that she couldn't have said for which of them.

Over by the south doors, Ventress had stepped out of the shadows. She stood a few paces into the room, her gaze moving from the dark hilt by the crane hook to the other one lying at the foot of the ramp, and back. She didn't go to them. She looked at Ahsoka instead, a long unreadable look, and stayed where she was.

High overhead, in the cab of the crane gantry, the little taped-over light was still recording.

It was over. Ahsoka let the breath go out of her, the long breath she felt she'd been holding since the night they'd found her lying on this floor. It was over, and it was true, and somebody had heard it.

Then the Force changed temperature.

It came down from somewhere above them like the first breath of air from an opened airlock on Narkis — a cold so sudden and complete that the hair on Ahsoka's arms rose and the burn on her forearm went numb. The rain seemed to slow. Across the floor, Ventress's head snapped toward the west door.

Footsteps rang on the old iron stair outside. Not hurrying. Not careful either.

And Ahsoka knew them. She knew that tread the way she knew her own heartbeat — had followed it down a hundred gangways and across a hundred battlefields, had listened for it every day for four months on a moon past the end of every map. She knew the presence coming down the stair behind it, too, bright and familiar and dear.

Only now it was burning black.

## Chapter 26: The Dark Between Stars

The mask was lying face-up on a beam just inside the west door, as if someone had set it there to keep it out of the wet.

Anakin knew it before he knew anything else in the room. Bone-white. Slanted slits for eyes. The last time he had seen it, it had been forty meters above him, looking down from a catwalk through dust and the shriek of failing durasteel while he held twenty stories of other people's lives on the end of his arm. It hadn't moved, that night. It hadn't run. It had stood there and watched him choose.

He had followed Padmé to get here. He had lain in the dark at 500 Republica with his eyes closed and heard her moving about the sitting room without lighting a lamp, listened to the door sigh shut behind her at second hour, and he had counted to two hundred before he reached for his boots. Two lift cars behind her down the public shaft. Three transit carriages behind her on the Dovrin Line, the train rattling past the old platform under its cage of scaffolding — he had not looked at it — and then the lower terminal, and the crowds, and her plain grey cloak gone among a thousand others.

It hadn't mattered. Ahsoka was down here somewhere, burning like a lamp left on in a flooded room, and he had only needed to walk toward the light.

Except there had been something next to her. A second presence, quiet and careful and folded in on itself, that he knew from the Resolute's medbay and couldn't make sense of. He had still been trying to make sense of it when red light began to flicker in the high broken windows of the warehouse ahead, and he heard the hum, and he ran.

By the time he reached the iron stair it was quiet. Only rain, and water running somewhere, and Ahsoka's voice below him, low and wrecked: *Not for anyone.*

Now he stood inside the west door with the mask at his elbow, and the room told him the rest.

Black water an inch deep across the floor. A red hilt lying at the foot of the loading ramp, another one half-sunk beside a fallen crane hook — curved hilts, Ventress's; he'd have known them in his sleep. Ventress herself, a few paces in from the south doors, a pale shape gone very still, staring at him. And in the middle of the floor, Ahsoka, the pirate's blue hilt cold in her hand and another that wasn't hers hooked on her belt, a fresh burn shining on her forearm. Standing over someone.

Barriss Offee was on her knees in the water. Her hood had fallen back. The diamond markings on her face were stark in the grey light, and her hands lay open on her thighs, green and patterned and empty.

He didn't think it. It arrived, all of it at once, the way a hull breach arrives — not a sound but a sudden absence of everything that had been holding the air in.

The Soresu turn on the catwalk, tight and economical, the Temple's oldest defensive form. Done by someone who'd learned it from a master who'd learned it from a master. A healer's hands. The healer who had gone to Narkis and come back and stood beside him in the hangar telling him, so gently, how Ahsoka had looked — thinner, but steady, she asks after you. The healer who had gone white at the sight of Rusk's collar case while he stood there thinking *she understands, at least one of them understands.* Who had sat in the gallery every day of the trial. Who had been aboard his ship. Who had been at Florrum, a deck away, while he lied to Obi-Wan about the vents.

Someone Ahsoka loved. He'd had that much in the canyon, and it had nearly killed him not to have the rest.

He had the rest now.

And under all of it, the thing he could not get his mind around, the thing that kept turning over and showing him its other side: Dovrin. The stack tilting. The Gran mother with her child, the Ithorian clutching his ribs, the nosebleed, his fingers locking around nothing. He had held it, and she had stood on the catwalk and watched him hold it, and then she had walked away. And because he had held it, she had been free to walk. And because she had walked, Ahsoka had gone into the ice for four months with a grey collar around her neck.

He had chosen. Every life on that platform, against one person in a mask. He had been proud of it, in some small hidden place — the one thing that whole night he'd done right.

Barriss lifted her head and looked at him.

"Master Skywalker," she said. Her voice was hoarse, scraped thin by whatever she had already said tonight. "You held it up. I stood on the catwalk and I watched you do it. I knew you would. It's the best thing about you." Her mouth moved, not a smile. "I was counting on it."

Ahsoka said, "Barriss, *don't* —"

"I let her pay for it," Barriss said, still to him. "I'm sorry."

He did not decide anything. Later he would try to find the moment and there would be no moment, only his right hand coming up — the mechanical one, inside its dark glove — and closing.

Barriss's hands flew to her throat.

She came up out of the water the way a puppet comes up when someone finally lifts the strings. Knees, then feet, the black water running off the hem of her robe, her boots scraping the floor and then not touching it. He drew her up until her face was level with his own. Her heels kicked once against nothing. Her fingers clawed at the air beneath her jaw where there was no hand to pull away.

It was easy. That was the thing he would remember longest. Holding the stack had nearly split him in half; this was nothing. This was the simplest thing he had ever done. The Force ran through him cold and clean and enormous, and every night of the last five months came down his arm with it — the tribunal rail denting under his fist, the red office, *life*, the transport lifting off from Platform Six without him, the steel cup folding in on itself in a room on Narkis, Windu's face, the beads. Ahsoka's collar light dying in his dream. All of it had been waiting for somewhere to go, and here at last was somewhere.

*Let her know it,* something in him said. *Let her know exactly what it was like for the woman on the bench.*

Barriss stopped clawing. Her hands fell away from her throat and hung at her sides, and she looked at him over the invisible fist, and she let him.

Somewhere above, high in the dark of the crane gantry, he felt Padmé — a bright spike of horror, so sharp and so close that for a heartbeat it nearly reached him. Something moved up there in the dark of the gantry, a shape, a hand, and was gone. He didn't care. He didn't care about anything.

"Anakin."

Ahsoka walked into his line.

She didn't ignite anything. She jammed the pirate's hilt onto her belt, tore Barriss's free of it and let it fall into the water at her feet, and stepped between them with her hands open, so that his raised fist was pointed at her face, and she put her palm flat against his knuckles. He could feel the warmth of it through the glove, through the metal, which wasn't possible.

"Get out of the way, Snips."

"No."

"She did it." His voice came out of him strange, flattened, like a voice on a bad comm. "All of it. The hangar. Letta. Those three troopers — with *your* blades. You were in a cell, you were in the *ice* —"

"I know what she did. I was here when she said it." Her montrals were trembling. She didn't look at Barriss. She looked only at him. "Look at your hand. Look at what it's doing."

"It's doing what someone should have done five months ago."

"This is how it started," Ahsoka said. "Do you understand me? This is exactly how it started. A woman held up off the ground with her hands at her throat, and someone standing where it looked like they weren't touching her at all. That's what she did to Letta. That's the very first thing she did. Everything else came after that." Her voice cracked down the middle and she let it. "You don't get to be the second person in this room who does it."

He felt her then — not her hand, *her* — reaching into him the way she had at dawn on Florrum, except that on Florrum she'd been glad. She wasn't glad now. Her presence pressed against his, bright and familiar and dear, and it was shaking, and what it was shaking with was fear.

Not of Barriss. Of him.

He had seen that once before, he realized, and hadn't let himself see it. Across a steel table on Narkis, with a cup crumpling between them. She had looked at him exactly like this.

"Skyguy," she said, very quietly. "Please."

It went into him like a blade between the plates.

His hand opened.

Barriss dropped. She hit the water on her knees and folded over and dragged in air with a sound like tearing cloth, coughing, one hand braced flat on the flooded floor. Alive. Her shoulders heaved. Nobody went to her.

Anakin looked down at his right hand. The mechanical fingers were perfectly steady inside the glove, the way they always were. It was the rest of him that was shaking — his knees, his breath, something in the back of his skull that felt like it had been struck and was still ringing.

Ahsoka's palm was still resting on his knuckles. She took it away slowly, as if she wasn't sure what would happen when she did.

Over by the south doors Ventress let out a long breath through her teeth.

"Well," she said. "Now I've seen everything. A Jedi with a temper." Her voice was its usual venom, but there was something under it that Anakin, if he'd been capable of noticing anything, might have called recognition. "Careful, Skywalker. For a moment there you looked like family."

He didn't answer her. High in the gantry, nothing moved.

* * *

Obi-Wan came in through the south loading doors with his cloak soaked black and his hand on his hilt, and stopped.

He took in the room the way he took in a battlefield — once, entirely, with no change at all in his face. Ventress. The red hilts. Ahsoka. Anakin. Barriss, on her knees, coughing into the water.

"Ah," he said. "I see we've all had the same idea about where to spend the evening."

Luminara Unduli came in behind him, and did not say anything at all.

She had found the crawlway. Anakin understood it from the grey dust on her sleeves, the cut-metal smell clinging to her robes; somewhere beneath the Temple's eastern foundation there was a hole, and an empty space behind it, and she had stood looking into it and then gone to find Obi-Wan. She had followed her own Padawan's trail down through the rain into the lower levels, knowing at every step what she would find at the end of it. And here it was.

She didn't go to Barriss. She stood just inside the doors with her hands folded in her sleeves, perfectly composed, and only her face gave her away — the stillness of it, like a pool after something heavy has gone under.

Barriss looked up and saw her master, and for the first time that night she bowed her head as though she couldn't hold it up.

Obi-Wan crossed the floor. He stopped a pace from Barriss and looked down at her, and Anakin watched his eyes go to her throat, to the marks there already darkening above the collar of her robe, and stay.

"What happened to your neck?" Obi-Wan said.

Barriss raised her head. Her voice came out in pieces. "I fell."

Obi-Wan looked at her for a long moment. Then he turned his head, not hurrying, and looked at Anakin.

Anakin said nothing. He was very good at saying nothing to Obi-Wan by now. He'd had five months of practice — the Soresu turn, the vents, the canyon. He found that this silence weighed more than all the others put together, and he held it anyway.

Obi-Wan opened his mouth.

Light slammed through the high windows.

Not lightning. Searchlights — three of them, white and hard, crossing and recrossing the flooded floor so that every shadow in the warehouse leapt up and spun. The whine of gunship repulsors filled the air, then the crash of boots on the iron stair outside the west door. An amplified voice rolled over everything, flat and familiar.

"*Ahsoka Tano. This is the Coruscant Guard. By order of the Office of the Supreme Chancellor, you are to lie face down with your hands visible. Any resistance will be met with lethal force.*"

The west door burst inward. Red armor poured through it — a dozen troopers, two dozen, rifles up, fanning out along the walls with the smooth hard precision of men who had done this many times. Fox came last, down the stair and into the light, his helmet turning as he took in the floor.

Anakin had his saber lit before he knew he'd reached for it. He stepped in front of Ahsoka, the blue blade low and humming between her and the rifles, and felt her go still behind him.

"Stand down, Commander."

"General Skywalker." Fox's voice through the helmet speaker didn't change. "Step away from the prisoner, sir."

"She's not resisting."

"My orders are to fire if she does."

"Your orders." Anakin heard his own voice go thin and bright, the way it went on a bridge when a ship was coming apart around him. "Whose orders? Who even told you she was here?"

"The Chancellor's office received an anonymous report an hour ago, General. Fugitive Tano sighted in the old munitions district, armed, possibly with Separatist assistance." Fox's rifle didn't move. "Authorization to fire came down with it."

The Chancellor would never — Anakin felt the thought begin and stop. Of course he wouldn't. Someone in that office had taken a tip at the third hour of the night and pushed it down the chain the way it was written. The Chancellor was asleep. The Chancellor would be horrified.

"She's not armed," he said.

There was a saber on Ahsoka's hip, the pirate's blue one, and every trooper in the room could see it.

What happened next happened in about four seconds. Ahsoka lifted her hands away from her body, open, slow. One of the troopers along the north wall — young, judging by how he held his rifle — saw the movement and flinched, and his finger closed. The bolt came screaming across the floor at the center of Ahsoka's chest, and Anakin's blade was already there; it cracked off the blue and buried itself in a pillar in a burst of sparks. Two more rifles came up. Fox roared, "*Hold fire!* Hold your *fire*!" Somewhere a trooper was shouting about a hostile at the south doors.

Anakin turned his head in time to see Ventress move.

She had both hands out. The hilt at the foot of the ramp leapt off the floor and smacked into her left palm; the one by the crane hook tore itself out of the water in a long silver spray and found her right. She didn't light them. She turned and went for the south doors in three long strides, low and fast, the searchlights sliding off her pale head —

And Obi-Wan was standing in the doorway.

Anakin saw her stop. Saw the two of them look at each other at a distance of perhaps an arm's length, Ventress with her sabers in her fists, Obi-Wan with his hand resting on his own hilt and not drawing it.

Then Obi-Wan stepped one pace to the side, turned his head, and looked thoughtfully up at the roof, as though he'd heard something interesting in the rafters.

Ventress went past him and out into the rain, and was gone.

Anakin stared. In the canyon on Florrum he had stood very much like that and watched Ahsoka go. He knew exactly what that looked like from the inside. He had not known it was a thing Obi-Wan could do.

"South door!" a trooper shouted. "Commander, one went out the south —"

"Let her go." Fox's helmet hadn't turned away from Ahsoka. "Tano's the order. Tano first."

"Commander Fox."

The voice came from behind him, not loud. Fox turned anyway. They all did.

Barriss had gotten to her feet.

She was standing in the middle of the floor where the searchlights crossed, soaked to the waist, one hand at her bruised throat. The light made her face very white around the markings. She took a step toward the rifles, and then another, until she was standing between the Guard and Ahsoka — between the Guard and Anakin's blade — and she lowered her hand.

"You're here for the person who killed CT-2261, CT-4408, and CT-9170," she said. "Outside the detention cells at the Military Operations Center. You're here for the person who bombed the Temple hangar. Who killed Letta Turmond in your own custody." Her voice was raw, but it carried; it carried all the way up into the dark of the gantry. "You've come to the right place. You've just aimed at the wrong person."

Fox didn't move. Behind the T of his visor, Anakin could feel him — a clone's presence, stubborn and plain as a wall — go very still.

"Identify yourself," Fox said.

"Barriss Offee. Padawan to Master Luminara Unduli of the Jedi Order." She didn't look at Luminara. "I supplied Letta Turmond with the nano-droids that her husband carried into the hangar without knowing what he carried. I bought them from a broker named Dalo Pesk in the Kesh Arcade. When Letta asked to speak to Ahsoka Tano, I was in the upper service passage above her cell, and I closed her throat from there so she couldn't say my name. Your three troopers I killed with Ahsoka's own sabers, which I took from the evidence locker. I left her the keycard. I sent her here, to this building, and I was waiting for her when she came. And on the Dovrin Line —" Her voice faltered for the first time. "On the Dovrin Line, the night before the Council voted, I was the one in the mask. I brought the platform down so that General Skywalker would have to choose between me and everyone standing on it. He chose them."

The rain hammered on the roof. Nobody in red armor moved.

"Ahsoka Tano has spent four months in the ice," Barriss said, "for things I did with my own hands. Write it down, Commander. Write that it was a Jedi."

Obi-Wan's voice came quiet and level out of the dark by the south doors. "Was there anyone else, Barriss?"

Anakin saw it — a flicker, there and gone. Her gaze went past Fox to the high windows, where the gunship's searchlights hung white in the rain. Then it came back.

"No one held my hand when I did any of it," she said. "Every choice I made was mine."

"Commander Fox." A different voice now, from above — clear, carrying, trained in a larger room than this one. Padmé was coming down the service stair from the gantry with one hand on the rail, the hood of her plain grey cloak thrown back. "Senator Amidala of Naboo. I'm counsel of record for Ahsoka Tano and I am a witness to everything said in this building tonight. Please tell your men to lower their weapons."

Fox's visor stayed on her through two slow breaths. Then he lifted one hand, and the rifles came down — not all the way, but down.

Anakin's eyes went up to the gantry behind her. No one else was on the stair. The platform above was empty, and at its far end a service-lane door was swinging shut on its closer, slow and soft, the way a door does a moment after someone has gone through it. She had come down alone. He noted it the way he'd note a loose panel on a ship he wasn't flying, and could not have said why it stayed with him.

Padmé reached the floor and walked straight across the water to him, and held out something small and dark between two fingers. A data chip — sealed, Anakin saw, the kind with a tamper strip across the seam. Military format. Her hand was not quite steady.

"A sealed, timestamped recording," she said. "Tamper-evident. Everything she confessed to Ahsoka before you arrived, and everything she has just said to you. I'll be presenting it to the tribunal myself. I'd like it logged in front of Master Kenobi and Master Unduli so there can be no question later of where it's been."

Fox took the chip. Obi-Wan came forward and looked at it and nodded once.

*Everything,* Anakin thought.

He looked up past her at the gantry, where something had moved while his hand was closed. Nothing moved there now; there was nothing up there at all anymore, only the black skeleton of the crane against the black of the roof. Fox was already sending two troopers up the service stair to sweep it.

Padmé turned from Fox, and for one instant her eyes met Anakin's across the flooded floor.

Then she looked away, and he understood that there was a piece of tonight that wasn't on that chip, and that she had decided that it wouldn't be, and that she had decided it for him.

"Gantry's clear, sir," a trooper called down. "Nobody up here."

"I was up there," Padmé said, without looking up. "Alone."

Ahsoka stepped out from behind Anakin's blade.

She walked through the water to where Barriss's saber lay where she'd dropped it, bent and picked it up, and shook it once. Then she carried it across the room to Luminara.

Luminara looked at the hilt as though she had never seen it before. Ahsoka held it out in both hands. After a moment Luminara took it the same way, both hands, like something that might break if she gripped it wrongly, and held it against her chest, and closed her eyes.

Neither of them said anything. Anakin thought there probably wasn't anything to say.

* * *

The rain was coming down sideways on the loading platform outside, driven by the gunships' repulsor wash. Two of them squatted at the platform's edge with their bay doors open and their interior lights glowing a sick red. Troopers stood in a loose ring at the edges with their rifles slung, watching the dark.

They put Barriss in binders first. Fox did it himself. She held her wrists out to him without being asked, and he closed the cuffs on them with a care that surprised Anakin, until he remembered the names she'd said. CT-2261. CT-4408. CT-9170. Fox's men. Maybe Fox had known them. Maybe a man could put binders on the person who'd killed his brothers more gently than anyone else, because he'd already imagined it so many times there was nothing left in the act.

"Military Operations Center," Fox said to the trooper beside him. "Detention level. Secure cell. Nobody in or out without my authorization."

Detention level. Anakin's stomach turned over. The same corridors. The same cells. Somewhere in that building there was a room where Letta Turmond had died against a wall, while Ahsoka stood three paces away and took the blame for it.

Luminara stepped forward. "I'll go with her."

"No, General." Fox didn't say it unkindly. "Not tonight."

Luminara stopped. She still held Barriss's saber in both hands against her robe. Barriss held her master's eyes, and then said something Anakin couldn't hear over the rain, and Luminara's face did not move at all, and Barriss was walked away up the ramp of the farther gunship.

Fox turned to Ahsoka. He had a second set of binders in his hand.

Ahsoka looked at them. Then she turned away from Fox, deliberately, and unclipped the pirate's battered blue saber from her belt and held it out — not to Fox. To Anakin.

He took it. It was heavier than hers had been.

Then she held out her wrists to him.

He stood there in the rain with her surrender held out in front of him and couldn't make his hands work. The fresh burn on her forearm. The thin pale ring at the base of her neck where the collar had been, still raw at the edges. He thought: *the last time someone held out their wrists to me like this, I let her go.* He thought: *I don't know how to do this.*

"Do it properly," Ahsoka said quietly. "They'll want to see it done properly. All of them."

Fox held out the binders. Anakin took them.

He closed them around her wrists as gently as he had ever done anything. Barriss had healed those wrists, he remembered, in a cell before the trial; Ahsoka had told Padmé, and Padmé had told him, and he had thought what a kindness. The cuffs locked with a small, final click.

"Dovrin," he said. It was all he could get out. "Snips. It was her. If I'd gone after her instead —"

"There'd be a Gran woman and her child under twenty stories of hab block," Ahsoka said. "And an Ithorian. And a lot of other people whose names you never learned." She looked at the binders, not at him. "You don't get to carry that one. It isn't yours."

"Then whose is it?"

She didn't answer. The rain ran off her montrals and down the stripes of her lekku. Over her shoulder he could see Obi-Wan standing at the edge of the platform, talking quietly with Luminara, and not quite looking at Anakin, in the particular way Obi-Wan had of not quite looking at a thing he could not stop thinking about. Every so often his gaze dropped — briefly, without seeming to — to Anakin's right hand.

Fox's comlink chimed. He lifted his wrist, listened, and turned to Anakin.

"Message from the Chancellor's office, General." The helmet tilted, as though Fox himself found the words slightly odd. "The Chancellor has been informed. He asks that you escort the prisoner to the Military Operations Center personally. And he asked me to tell you —" A beat. "That he never doubted you, sir. Not for a moment. And that he's grateful it was you who found her."

Something opened in Anakin's chest — warm, enormous, unbearable after the cold of the last hour. He had broken his word to the Chancellor in a canyon on Florrum, and the Chancellor had never known, and here was the Chancellor anyway, reaching down through the rain to tell him he'd done right. That it would be all right. That someone, at least, still saw him clearly.

He felt his throat go tight. "Tell him —" He stopped. "Tell him thank you."

Beside him, Ahsoka had gone completely still. Not tense. Just still, the way she'd stood at the sentencing when the word *death* fell on her, before the word *life* came after it. She was looking at Fox's comlink.

On the ramp of the farther gunship, Barriss had stopped too.

The trooper at her elbow tugged at her arm. She didn't move. She had heard it — Anakin saw that she had heard it — and she was looking back over her shoulder through the rain, not at Anakin, but at Ahsoka. Her face, in the red light of the bay, was the face of someone watching a door close that she had been meaning to walk through.

"Ahsoka," Barriss called. Her ruined voice barely carried. "Come and see me. Before they put me in front of anyone."

Ahsoka's head came up.

"There's one more thing that's true," Barriss said. "I couldn't say it in there."

The trooper pulled her inside. The bay door ground shut on her, and the gunship lifted, and swung away into the rain with its searchlights dimming until there was only the red glow of its engines climbing between the towers, and then not even that.

"What did she mean?" Anakin said.

Ahsoka watched the place where the lights had been. She didn't answer him. And for the first time since a fourteen-year-old with a shoto and no manners had walked down a shuttle ramp on Christophsis and announced herself as his Padawan, Anakin looked at her face and could not read a single thing on it.

## Chapter 27: The Second Tribunal

The chip was the size of Padmé's thumbnail, and there was a hole in it three minutes and forty-one seconds long.

She had known about the hole since the warehouse, since the moment her own finger found the switch on the side of Fives's helmet and pressed it while Barriss Offee's boots left the floor. She had known about it through the gunship ride, through the long wet hour at the Military Operations Center while Fox logged the chip under Obi-Wan's eyes, through a night in which she did not sleep and Anakin, lying an arm's length away, did not either. But knowing a thing and watching it projected six meters high above the floor of the Senate tribunal chamber were different kinds of knowing. The timestamp bar ran along the bottom of the holo in clean white numerals, and at the point where it should have counted smoothly onward it simply jumped, the way a heart skips, and every person in the room could see it.

"The defense notes the interruption," Tarkin said, before she could. He stood at the prosecution table with his hands folded behind his back, as composed as he had been five months ago, when this same room had condemned a seventeen-year-old girl. "Three minutes and forty-one seconds. In a recording the defense describes as tamper-evident."

"Tamper-evident," Padmé said, "not tamper-proof. The seal shows no breach. The court's own technician has confirmed it." She kept her voice level and her right hand, with its bacta patch, flat on the table where it would not shake. "The confession is complete before the interruption. The prisoner's surrender is complete after it. Nothing in those minutes bears on the charges before this tribunal."

"The Admiral will forgive me." On the high dais, Palpatine leaned forward, his face lit gently from below by the holo's glow. "Senator Amidala is quite right. A recorder dropped in a ruined warehouse, in the middle of the night, among lightsabers — I think we may be grateful we have any of it at all. The gap is of no consequence. Proceed."

Tarkin inclined his head and sat.

Padmé did not let herself look at Anakin. He was in the first row behind the defense table, close enough that she could hear him breathe out when the Chancellor spoke, and she knew without turning what his face was doing. She looked instead at the senators' box: Riyo Chuchi, very upright, blue hands clasped; Mon Mothma, unreadable; Bail Organa, who met her eyes for half a second with an expression she couldn't name and then looked down at his datapad. She looked at the Jedi gallery, where Obi-Wan sat with Plo Koon on one side and Luminara Unduli on the other, and where Luminara had not moved since the session opened. She looked at the evidence table, where two sealed lightsaber hilts, numbered 417 and 418, lay in the same plastoid tray as before.

She looked last at Ahsoka.

Ahsoka sat beside her in grey detention clothes, unbound for the session at Padmé's insistence. The collar was gone, but the place where it had been was still a raw, healing ring around her throat, and there was a small, shiny burn under the line of her left jaw. She had folded her hands in her lap. She had not moved them since she sat down.

Padmé had asked, the night before, whether Ahsoka wanted to go to Barriss first — Barriss had asked for it, shouting it through the rain. Fox had refused on Tarkin's standing order: no contact between the accused and the witness before testimony. Ahsoka had only nodded, as though she had expected nothing else. *After,* the order said. After was a word Padmé had come to distrust.

"The tribunal calls Barriss Offee," said the clerk.

* * *

They brought her in through the same side door Ahsoka had used. Two Coruscant Guard troopers walked her to the witness stand, and Fox walked behind them, his helmet turning to watch the gallery as much as the prisoner. Barriss wore detention grey, too. Someone had given her a high collar, and it did not hide the bruises. They climbed from her jaw down into the fabric, dark as thumbprints, and when she was asked to state her name, the voice that came out of the amplifier was scraped thin, half breath.

"Barriss Offee. Padawan of Master Luminara Unduli." A pause. "I suppose that last part is for the Council to decide now."

In the Jedi gallery, Luminara's hands closed in her lap.

The families of the hangar dead had come. They sat together in the lower gallery in deep blue, as they had every day of the first trial, and Padmé saw a Zabrak woman among them press her fist against her mouth when Barriss turned to face the room. They had watched Ahsoka from those seats for two days and hated her. Padmé wondered what they did with that now, where it went.

Tarkin rose. "The prosecution has reviewed the recording entered by the defense and does not contest its authenticity." Each word was laid down separately, like a coin he resented paying. "The witness has asked to make a statement in her own words. The prosecution does not object."

"Then let her speak," said Palpatine.

Barriss put both hands on the rail of the stand. Her fingers were steady. Padmé, who had seen them shake in the warehouse, noticed that more than anything else.

"I have already told the Commander of the Guard what I did," Barriss said. "I've told it to a recorder in the dark. I will tell it to you, because you are the ones who condemned her, and you should have to hear it.

"I bought the nano-droids. I chose the hangar. I found Letta Turmond, who hated the war and had good reason to, and I gave her a way to make it cost something, and she gave it to her husband without telling him what it was. Jackar Bowmani carried my bomb into the Temple because he loved his wife. Six Jedi died. Clone troopers died, and workers whose names nobody in this room has ever said aloud. I did that.

"When Letta was taken, I stood in the upper service passage of the Military Operations Center and closed her throat through the Force before she could say who I was. She died looking at Ahsoka Tano. I meant her to.

"I took Ahsoka's lightsabers from the evidence locker and killed three troopers of the Coruscant Guard outside her cell." She said their numbers — two-two-six-one, four-four-oh-eight, nine-one-seven-oh — one after another, in the thin ruined voice, and the troopers on either side of her did not move at all. "I left her a keycard and let her think a friend had left it. Then I told her where to go and I waited for her there, wearing a mask, and I made certain she would be found."

She breathed. The amplifier caught the rasp of it.

"And when she was found, I came here. I sat in that gallery." She looked up at it, at the Jedi seats where Luminara sat motionless. "Every day. I watched Admiral Tarkin play the footage of the woman I killed and call it her crime. I watched this tribunal decide. Later I went to Narkis and healed the wounds she took in a prison I had put her in, and she thanked me." For the first time her voice slipped. She caught it. "That is what I am. I don't want anyone in this room to make it smaller.

"But I will not let you make it simpler, either."

Padmé felt the room change. It was not a sound; it was the absence of one, the small shuffles and murmurs of a chamber full of people all ceasing together.

"I was trained as a healer," Barriss said. "The first thing a healer learns is to count. How many are breathing. How many can wait. How many we have already lost. I was taught to count the living, and when the war came, the Order gave me the dead instead, and asked me to count those. Geonosis — twice. A dozen field stations no one in this chamber could find on a chart. I have stood in field hospitals where the clones were laid in rows, and I have watched Jedi Masters walk those rows with their hands behind their backs and their faces calm, and then go back to the Temple and light a candle and accept the next command.

"We swore to serve life. That was the oath. Somewhere — I could not tell you the day — we became officers in someone else's war, and we never once stood in the Council chamber and asked whether we should. We asked only how to win it. We told ourselves the clones were men and then we spent them like ammunition. We told ourselves we served the Republic and we let the Republic decide what serving meant.

"I wanted the Temple to feel what the battlefields feel. I thought if the war came home to them, if they had to sweep their own dead off the hangar floor, they would wake. That was my reasoning, and I give it to you whole, because I still cannot find the place where it fails." She turned her head, slowly, until she was looking at the Jedi gallery. "I can only find the place where I did. It was not the reasoning. It was the bodies. It was Jackar, who didn't know. It was Letta, who did and still deserved a trial. It was three men at a cell door who had done nothing but stand where they were told.

"And it was her." She did not look at the defense table. "Because the Order I wanted to wake did wake, for an afternoon — long enough to expel her. They needed it to be someone. She was nearest. They gave her to you without a hearing of their own, and you" — now she did look at the senators' box, and Riyo Chuchi's chin rose a fraction — "you needed it to be someone too. A Jedi bomber was an answer the whole Republic could understand. Nobody here wanted the harder answer. I counted on that. I was right to.

"Don't let anyone tell you some darkness crept into me while I slept. Nothing crept. I walked there, one reasonable step at a time, and every step had a good argument under it. That is what I would like the Order to understand, if they understand anything from this. It was not a shadow that came for me. It was a war, and a Temple that kept saying yes to it.

"I am guilty of every charge laid against Ahsoka Tano. She is guilty of none of them. That's all."

She let go of the rail.

Padmé noticed, then, what Barriss had not done. In all that time she had looked at the Jedi gallery and the senators' box and the families in blue and, once, very briefly, at the ceiling. She had not looked once at the dais. Not once. As if it were a light too bright to face.

Palpatine, on the dais, had listened with his chin on his folded hands and an expression of grave sorrow. "Thank you, Padawan Offee," he said. "That cannot have been easy."

Barriss said nothing.

"The witness will remain in the custody of the Guard," Palpatine went on. "The matter of her sentence is grave, and this body has learned, I think, the cost of deciding a life in haste. Sentencing is deferred to a future session, at a date to be fixed with the Judiciary and the Jedi Council." He let that sit. "We will not make the same mistake twice."

Barriss was led out. At the side door, she stopped — the trooper let her — and for one breath she looked at Ahsoka, and Ahsoka looked back. Neither of them did anything with her face. Then the door closed.

Under the table, Padmé felt Ahsoka's knee press, very slightly, against hers. It was the only sign she gave.

* * *

Tarkin did not wait for the recess.

"The prosecution accepts that the original convictions cannot stand on the evidence now before the tribunal," he said, rising, and there was a soft wave of sound through the chamber, relief and outrage mixed. He let it pass. "The prosecution is not finished. There remain the events of the past six weeks. On the night of the Narkis raid, six Judiciary personnel were killed, including Sergeant Aron Holt, who died on a stair in Ring Four while escorting the accused. The accused left Narkis that night aboard a vessel commanded by a known agent of Count Dooku. She did not return to Republic custody. She evaded a lawful recovery operation for the better part of a month, in the company of Asajj Ventress — a Separatist assassin who, I note, is not in this room. Whatever the defendant was five months ago, Chancellor, these are new acts. They are hers."

It was clever. Padmé had expected nothing less. He had lost the war he'd been fighting, so he had simply walked onto another field and planted his flag there, and half the senators in the box were already looking at Ahsoka with the old wariness, because it was easier than looking at what they had just heard.

"Senator Amidala?" said Palpatine.

Padmé rose. "The defense has two statements to enter regarding the Narkis raid. The first is a sworn holo deposition from Commandant Ilsa Vorne, Warden of Narkis-Nine. The Admiral will know her. He recommended her for the post."

Tarkin's mouth thinned by a hair.

The holo bloomed over the floor: Vorne in her uniform, impeccable except that the right sleeve hung empty and the shoulder beneath it was bound flat against her chest. She sat very straight. Her iron-grey hair was pinned exactly as Padmé remembered it from the image on Ahsoka's file. She gave her name, rank, and post in a voice that sounded as though it had never once been raised.

"On the night in question," Vorne said, "power to the Rings failed at twenty-two forty. I was in my office. I observed, from the office monitor and then in person, the Duros bounty hunter known as Cad Bane enter Ring Four with three accomplices. Sergeant Holt was moving prisoner NK9-2214 to the secure room per standing procedure. Bane shot him on the Ring Four stair. The prisoner was in binders and wearing an active dampening collar. She engaged Bane unarmed. She was stunned. I saw her carried past my door unconscious. Bane then shot me and took the collar control from my hand. The prisoner did not kill Sergeant Holt. She did not kill any member of my staff. Every one of my dead is accounted for by Bane's crew."

She paused, as if consulting an internal list.

"For the record of this tribunal, I will add one further fact. Four days before the raid, during a coolant rupture in Cut Five, I released the prisoner's collar on my own authority. For four minutes and nine seconds she had full use of the Force, and an open yard, and a perimeter fence she could have cleared without effort. She used the time to hold an ice shelf while twenty-three people got out from under it. Then she put it down and stood still while I re-engaged the collar. I have been Warden of Narkis for nine years. I have not seen a prisoner do that." The faintest pause. "I make no judgment. I state it because it is true, and the tribunal should have it."

She looked down, once, at something out of frame. "And one correction to my own file. The prisoner's posting to Terrace Eleven, Cut Three, in her first week came to my desk as a transfer order carrying an off-world authorization code. I signed it. I have not since been able to trace that code, nor learn why a camera on that terrace was allowed to stay frozen for two years without once reaching my desk. I should have asked sooner."

Padmé did not look up from her datapad. She wrote the word *code* and underlined it twice.

The holo went dark.

"The second statement," Padmé said, into the quiet, "is from Guard Corporal Dannick, the only member of the Ring Four detail to survive the raid. It is shorter. He states that he surrendered on the Ring Three landing, and that the prisoner, bound and collared, went at Bane with her bare hands over Sergeant Holt's body. He believes that is the only reason Bane did not trouble to shoot him. He adds that an inmate of Ring Four, a Trandoshan named Krasso, took a bolt tackling one of Bane's crew on the stair so that she could run. Krasso survived, wounded, and has given his own statement that Twenty-two fourteen never raised a hand against staff. Krasso asked that it be recorded that he had paid something on a debt." She set the datapad on the clerk's tray. "The defense submits that a prisoner who is stunned, bound, collared, and carried off by Separatist mercenaries has not escaped. She has been abducted. The defense further submits that a woman wrongly condemned to die in the ice, hunted under an order of lethal force issued in the field, who ran from that hunt rather than into the arms of Count Dooku — who refused Count Dooku to his face — and who then walked back into the custody of this Republic of her own will, with the true culprit's confession in her hands, has committed no crime this tribunal should be proud to name."

She sat down. Her right hand was shaking now. She put it under the table.

Tarkin rose again, but slower. "The defendant's associations remain —"

"Wilhuff."

Palpatine had not raised his voice. He did not need to. He had risen from the high chair, and the whole chamber turned to him the way plants turn in a window. He came forward to the edge of the dais, and his robes caught the light, and he looked down at the defense table with an expression of such tired, fatherly sorrow that Padmé, who had sat across from him in committee for years, felt it work on her even as she watched it.

"I think we have heard enough," he said. "I think this Republic has asked enough of this young woman."

Padmé's stomach tightened. She had a motion prepared. It was on the datapad at her elbow: a motion to vacate the convictions in their entirety, on the record, as unfounded. A finding that Ahsoka Tano had not done these things. She had written it at third hour that morning, while Anakin pretended to sleep.

"Five months ago," Palpatine said, "I stood in this room and exercised the powers of my office to spare a life. I told myself at the time it was mercy. I see now it was not enough. A great wrong was done here, in the Republic's name, and I presided over it. That error is mine to answer for, and I will answer for it." He lifted one hand. "By the emergency authority vested in this office, I grant Ahsoka Tano a full and unconditional pardon — for the offenses of which she was convicted before this tribunal, and for any and all acts arising from her abduction from Narkis and her return. No further charge will be brought. She leaves this chamber free."

*A pardon.* Padmé sat very still. A pardon was forgiveness. A pardon said *you did a thing, and I, in my grace, release you from it.* It did not say *you did nothing.* It could not be appealed, and it could not be refused, and it belonged entirely to the man who gave it. Her motion lay on the table beside her and was, in that moment, worth exactly as much as the datapad it was written on.

She half rose anyway. Bail Organa, in the senators' box, caught her eye and gave the smallest shake of his head. *Not now. Not against this.*

Palpatine had not finished.

"And I would be remiss," he said, "if I did not say before this chamber what I have said privately. One man never stopped believing in her. When the Order let her go, when this Senate condemned her, when the Holonet named her a traitor, one man went into the dark after her — and brought her home." His gaze moved past Padmé and settled, warm as a lamp, on the first row. "Knight Skywalker. The Republic is fortunate in your loyalty. I am fortunate in it. I hope this day repays a little of what it has cost you."

Somebody began to clap. Padmé never saw who. It started in the upper tiers and rolled down through the delegation pods, and then the senators' box was on its feet, Chuchi first, Mothma a moment after, Bail last; and the lower galleries took it up, and the families in blue, some of them weeping; and the sound filled the great round chamber and went on and on, a whole Senate applauding itself for undoing what it had done.

Padmé turned, finally, and looked at her husband.

Anakin was standing. She did not think he knew he had stood up. His face was turned up toward the dais, and it was lit — there was no other word — lit the way it had been the first time she ever saw him, nine years old in a junk dealer's shop, before he knew the size of the galaxy or anything it would take from him. All the exhaustion of the last months was gone out of it. His mechanical hand had found the rail in front of him and was gripping it, and the metal under his glove made a small, tortured sound, and he didn't notice that either. He looked at Palpatine as though the Chancellor had reached down and handed him back the sun.

He had let Ahsoka go in a canyon on Florrum. Padmé knew that. Palpatine had just thanked him, before the entire Republic, for bringing her in.

And she had switched off the recorder so that no one would ever see what else he had done in that warehouse. She had done it for him. She had done it, she understood now with a cold, slow clarity, for exactly the same reason Palpatine had just waved the gap away. The two of them had protected the same thing. She did not know what that made her.

She looked at Ahsoka.

Ahsoka had not stood. Around her the chamber roared and stamped; the clerk was trying, uselessly, to restore order; up in the Jedi gallery Plo Koon had bowed his head, and Obi-Wan was looking not at the dais but at Anakin, with an expression Padmé recognized because she had worn it in a mirror that morning. Ahsoka sat at the defense table with her hands folded in her lap and her eyes on the evidence tray where her lightsabers lay sealed. She did not smile. She did not weep. She did not move at all — not her hands, not her lekku, not the healing ring at her throat. She sat as perfectly still as a prisoner in a cell who has learned that stillness is the only thing in the room that belongs to her.

Padmé reached over and put her hand on Ahsoka's folded ones. They were cold.

"You're free," Padmé said, under the noise.

Ahsoka's eyes moved to her. Something passed behind them, too fast to catch.

"Am I?" she said.

* * *

It took an hour for the chamber to empty, and Padmé spent most of it being congratulated by people who had voted to convict.

She said the right things. She shook the hands. She let a junior senator from Kuat tell her it had been a privilege to witness, and she thanked him, and did not ask where he had been five months ago. Anakin had gone down with Ahsoka and Fox to the detention level for the release processing; there would be forms, a medical review, a formal return of personal effects that did not include her sabers. *Evidence in an open matter,* the clerk had said, apologetic. *The Offee sentencing.* Ahsoka had only nodded.

Padmé went to find her datapad, which she had left on the defense table in the confusion, and found the floor empty and the side passage behind the dais standing open, and she heard voices.

She should have turned around. She did not. She stood very still in the shadow of the arch with the useless motion in her hand.

"— a disappointing afternoon for you, I'm afraid," Palpatine was saying. His voice was easy, almost amused. "You'll forgive me for cutting you off."

"I understood it was coming." Tarkin, dry as ever. "I'd have preferred to have been told when. Major Rusk's recovery team was stood down an hour ago, and the collar case has gone back to inventory. A closed file is a tidy thing."

"If you had been told, my friend, the Senate would have seen it on your face. They needed to see a prosecutor who believed in what he was doing. And you did, didn't you?" A pause, a rustle of heavy fabric. "You served the Republic's need for an answer when it needed one. Today it needed a different one. There's no shame in that. A firm hand is not less valuable because it occasionally has to be withdrawn."

"The collar program —"

"Will survive this. The Senate will feel it has been very generous today. Generous assemblies are seldom vigilant ones." Something that might have been a soft laugh. "When this war ends, Wilhuff, the Outer Rim will want governing. Not administering — governing. I have been giving some thought to whose hand that should be. I find I keep returning to one name."

A silence.

"I'm honored, Chancellor."

"Don't be honored. Be patient. And do send Commandant Vorne my thanks for her deposition. Such a precise woman. I'd like to keep an eye on her career."

Padmé took one step back out of the arch, and then another, and the hem of her gown whispered on the stone.

The voices stopped.

She made herself turn and walk, not quickly, toward the defense table, and pick up her datapad from where it lay, and tuck it under her arm. When she looked up, Palpatine was standing in the archway behind the dais. Tarkin was not with him. She hadn't heard him go.

"Senator Amidala." The Chancellor smiled down at her, warm, a little weary, exactly the face he had worn on the dais. "I'd hoped to catch you. That was splendid work. Truly. Naboo should be very proud."

"Thank you, Chancellor." Her voice came out steady. She was grateful to it.

"I'm afraid I rather stepped on your motion." His eyes went, very briefly, to the datapad under her arm, and she had the sudden impossible certainty that he knew exactly what was written on it. "Forgive an old man his sense of occasion. The result is the same, I think. She is free, and she is safe, and that is what we all wanted."

"It isn't quite the same," Padmé said.

He tilted his head, pleasantly, as though she'd made an interesting point in committee. "No? Well. We can talk about the distinction some other time." He began to turn, then paused. "Oh — the release processing will run late, I'm told. The Judiciary is very thorough. I've asked young Skywalker to stay and collect her personally, however long it takes. It seemed fitting, after today." His smile deepened, fond. "He's earned that much."

He went back through the archway. The door slid shut behind him.

Padmé stood alone on the tribunal floor, under the great dim ring of empty pods, and found that her hand had gone to her throat.

Her comlink buzzed. Anakin's code. She thumbed it on.

"They're saying it'll be hours," he said. His voice was still bright at the edges, still carrying the afternoon in it. "Some medical thing about the collar burns. I'm going to wait. Don't wait up." A pause, and the brightness faltered for a moment, and she heard the man underneath it, the one who had not slept. "Padmé. She asked them for something. Before they'll let her out."

"What?"

"Ten minutes on the cell level," Anakin said. "Fox says it's the same block. Same row as hers was." He breathed out. "She wants to see Barriss. Tonight."

Padmé looked at the closed archway where the Chancellor had stood.

*There's one more thing that's true,* Barriss had called through the rain. *I couldn't say it in there.*

And she had not. Not one word of it, in a room full of people, with her eyes on everything except the dais.

"Ani," Padmé said, and heard her own voice come out strange. "Stay with her. Whatever happens tonight. Don't leave the building until she does."

"I wasn't planning to," he said, puzzled. "Why?"

She did not have an answer that she could say aloud. She had only the memory of a warm, fond voice saying *he's earned that much,* and of a woman in detention grey who would not look at the dais — and somewhere far below her feet, in a cell she had once failed to argue a girl out of, a confession that wasn't finished yet.

## Chapter 28: Mirror

The last time Ahsoka had walked this corridor, she had been on the other side of the energy screens, and the girl waiting at the end of it had come with a healer's kit, warm hands, and a lie. Now Ahsoka was the visitor. Commander Fox walked half a step ahead of her, exactly the way he had walked half a step ahead of her when she wore binders, and he had not said a word since the lift.

Her wrists were bare. She kept noticing them.

Detention level, Military Operations Center. The same pale-grey panels, the same faint ozone bite from the screens, the same hum that settled into the back of the teeth after a while and never left. The overheads were dimmed for the night cycle. Somewhere farther down a prisoner was snoring, a long ugly sawing sound, and Ahsoka found she was grateful for it. It was the only thing on the level that sounded like a person.

Fox stopped at the eighth cell on the left. Hers had been the eleventh. She had counted the doors once, on the way to her own tribunal, because counting was something to do with her mind.

"Ten minutes," Fox said, and tapped his wrist unit. "I'm logging the start. I'll be at the junction." He paused. "Ten. Not eleven."

"I heard you the first time, Commander."

His helmet gave her back her own face, small and orange and very tired. Then he walked away, boots even on the deck, and she turned to the screen.

Barriss was sitting on the bench with her back against the wall and her hands open on her knees. Someone had taken her hood and her robes and given her detention grey, and without the hood she looked younger than she had any right to look. Her dark hair was pulled back. The diamond markings on her face were the same as always.

Her throat was not.

The bruise went all the way around, a wide band of purple shading to green at the edges, darker under the jaw where the pressure had been worst. Ahsoka had watched it being made. She had stood in the rain and the dust and watched Anakin lift Barriss off the floor with nothing but his hand closed in the air, and for a moment, looking at the bruise, she was back there, with her heart going like a blaster on full auto.

Barriss stood. It took her a while.

"You came," she said.

It was barely a voice. It came out scraped and breathy, like something pulled over gravel, and she had to stop after the two words and swallow, and the swallow visibly hurt.

"You asked," Ahsoka said.

They looked at each other through the faint blue shimmer. There was a bench on Ahsoka's side, too. She remembered Barriss sitting on it once, kneeling beside her really, cradling her chafed wrists and closing them over with the Force. She remembered how warm it had felt. She remembered saying *thank you.*

"I could do something about your throat," Ahsoka said. "Not much. I'm not you. But I eased a blaster wound not long ago, and it didn't kill anybody."

"No." Barriss touched the bruise, very lightly, the way you touch a sleeping animal. "Leave it. It's the only honest thing anyone's put on me in months."

"That isn't funny."

"It wasn't meant to be."

Ahsoka sat down on the visitor's bench. After a moment Barriss sat too, on the edge of hers this time, leaning forward, and the screen between them was the width of a held breath.

"Six Jedi," Ahsoka said. She had not planned to start here. It came out anyway. "Tutso Mara. The workers in the hangar. The clones in the hangar. Jackar. Letta." Her voice wanted to climb and she would not let it. "Twenty-two sixty-one. Forty-four oh-eight. Ninety-one seventy."

Barriss's lips moved with the last three numbers. She did not need to hear them. She had been saying them every morning for half a year.

"I asked you to light candles for them," Ahsoka said. "Do you remember? Right here. You were holding my wrists."

"I lit them."

"I know you did. That's the part I can't get past." Ahsoka pressed the heels of her hands into her knees. "You lit candles for the men you killed with my sabers, and then you came to the Deep and fixed my shin, and you went home. I wore that collar for four months, Barriss. I couldn't feel anything. Not the Force, not Anakin, not *you.* I thought it had left me. I thought maybe I'd never had it the way I believed I did. And you sat across from me and let me thank you."

"Yes," Barriss whispered.

"Don't just say yes."

"What would you like me to say?" Barriss's eyes were dry and very steady. "That I had reasons? I gave the reasons yesterday, in front of the whole Senate, and I still believe half of them. This war is turning us into something. The Order is a weapon that's forgotten it was ever anything else. I was right about that." She stopped to breathe. "I was wrong about everything I did about it. Both of those are true. I've stopped expecting one to cancel the other."

Ahsoka looked away, down the corridor, to where Fox's silhouette stood at the junction under the one bright light. Her montrals ached the way they did before weather. She had imagined this conversation for days and in none of the versions had Barriss been calm.

"You said there was one more thing," Ahsoka said. "That you couldn't say in there."

"Yes."

"I've got about six minutes."

Barriss folded her hands. When she spoke she spoke slowly, picking each word up and setting it down like something that might go off.

"He knew," she said. "The Chancellor. From the beginning."

The hum of the screen seemed to get louder.

"There was a camera in the upper service passage," Barriss went on. "An old maintenance unit. Auxiliary. Nobody had looked at its feed in years. He had. He invited me to his office to heal an aide's wrist, and afterward he gave me tea, and he played it for me. Me, at the end of the passage. My hand coming up." She lifted her hand an inch off her knee, and let it drop. "The exact second Letta stopped breathing."

"When," Ahsoka said. Her own voice sounded far off.

"After your sentencing. But he told me he'd had it sealed since the beginning. Those were his words. *Since the very beginning, my dear.* He'd watched it before your trial ever opened." Barriss swallowed, winced. "He sat on that dais and let Tarkin play the other footage. The silent one, with you in the room. And mine was in his drawer the whole time."

"No."

"He had a still of my face from the gallery, too. The moment he said *death.* He said I looked like someone who needed a friend." Something moved at the corner of Barriss's mouth that was not a smile. "So I became his friend. He wanted to know what the Council was afraid of. Mostly, it turns out, they're afraid of Anakin. Master Luminara told me things in confidence, and I carried them up to that red room and drank his tea and told him."

Ahsoka's hands had gone cold. She pushed them flat against the bench.

"He protected me," Barriss said. "That's how he put it. He was protecting the Republic from another scandal, and protecting you, because if the truth came out the Senate would want blood and you might get caught in the spray. He's very good. You feel cared for. You feel *chosen.*" She drew a breath that rasped all the way down. "And when it looked like you'd be cleared, he called me on the Resolute and talked about second trials, and steady hands, and how angry Anakin had become. He never said *kill her.* He didn't need to. He sent me to the warehouse to make sure you never testified."

She was quiet a moment. "The Rodian who sold me the nano-droids saw my hands. I never went back to him. I lay awake for weeks deciding what to do about him, and then I heard he'd been found in his stall with his hands folded and everything wiped clean, and I thought it was luck."

Ahsoka thought of Ventress turning one white hand over on a table in bad light, describing exactly that. *Someone is killing everyone who knew anything,* she had said herself, that night. She had never once asked who.

"You said *he,*" Ahsoka said slowly. "At the warehouse. You said *he'd* sent you, and then you shut your mouth."

"Yes."

"And yesterday, in front of everyone, with Padmé right there, with the Chancellor right *there* — you said nothing."

"Who would they have believed?" Barriss asked. "The Temple bomber, or the man who'd just pardoned you? It would have been my last lie, as far as anyone in that chamber was concerned. A traitor trying to drag down the Republic on her way out." She looked down at her open hands. "And I was afraid. Even then. Even now. He watched me the whole time I spoke. I didn't look back. I couldn't."

Ahsoka stood up. She didn't know why. She walked two steps toward the junction and two steps back and stopped in front of the screen.

It made a terrible kind of sense. The *he* in the warehouse. The Chancellery courier that had carried Barriss to Narkis, white and red against the ice. The way the mercy had come down from the dais exactly when it would land hardest — *death,* and then the pause, and then *life* — and how Anakin had looked at that man afterward, as though he'd hung every star.

It also made a terrible kind of sense the other way. Barriss had lied to her face for months, warmly, with steady eyes. Barriss had stood up yesterday and condemned the Order, the war, and the Senate in one breath. What better parting shot, for someone who believed the whole Republic was rotten, than to put one last seed of poison into the only person who'd shown her mercy? Point it at the top. Let it grow.

Ahsoka reached for the Force and laid it against Barriss the way she would test the ice before stepping on it.

Exhaustion. Grief. Fear like a held note. Something underneath that might have been truth, or might have been someone who had learned to lie so well she no longer felt the seam.

She couldn't tell. Months ago she would have known. Months ago she had *trusted* what she felt, and what she'd felt was a friend.

"Why tell me?" she said. "If nobody would believe you."

"Because you'll be out of here by morning." Barriss raised her eyes. "And because he asked Anakin to stay here tonight. Didn't he? To collect you personally."

Ahsoka said nothing.

"Of course he did," Barriss whispered. "That's what he does. He puts people exactly where he wants them, and lets them be grateful for it."

"Don't," Ahsoka said. "Don't talk about Anakin."

"Then I'll say one thing and stop. Don't tell him. Not yet. He won't believe you, and if he does —" she touched her throat again "— you've seen what he does with things he can't bear."

Down at the junction, Fox shifted his weight. Ahsoka heard it more than saw it.

"I'm not forgiving you," she said. "I want you to know that. I don't know if I'm ever going to."

"I know."

"I don't know if I believe you."

"You shouldn't," Barriss said. "I wouldn't."

"Time," Fox called. His voice carried flat down the corridor.

Ahsoka didn't move. Barriss was looking at her the way she had looked across the warehouse with a red blade at Ahsoka's throat and the opening clean, and had not taken it.

"Will you light them?" Barriss asked. "The candles. I can't from here. Someone should."

There was a long moment in which Ahsoka could not have said a single word without her voice breaking, so she didn't try. When she could, she said, "Ask me tomorrow."

Something in Barriss's face eased, very slightly, like a fist letting go of one finger.

"Tomorrow," she agreed.

Ahsoka walked to the junction without looking back, and Fox logged the stop, and she noticed the time on his wrist unit only because he angled it so she would. Twelve minutes. He'd given her two he hadn't mentioned.

* * *

The medical bay was one level up and smelled of bacta and floor sealant. A droid with a long, patient face had her lie back on the table and tipped her chin up with one cold finger, and then it began painting something onto the raw ring around her neck that stung and then went numb.

"The burn under your left jaw has partially reopened," the droid said. "You have been clenching it."

"Sorry."

"It is not my jaw."

She stared up at the ceiling panels while it worked. The light in this room was brighter than in the cells, and it made the inside of her eyelids glow red when she closed them, so she kept them open.

*Since the very beginning, my dear.*

She tried to remember the Chancellor's face from her own tribunal. She had looked at him exactly twice: once when he took his seat, and once at the end, when he said *death.* She remembered that he'd seemed sad. She remembered thinking that at least *someone* up there was sorry. And then the pause, so long, and the word *life* dropping into it, and the way her whole body had broken around that word like a dam giving way. She had been grateful. She had sat in the transport on the way to Narkis with a collar coming down over her head and *still* been grateful, a little, in the part of her that didn't want to die.

If Barriss was telling the truth, that pause had been chosen. The sadness had been chosen. The mercy had been a leash, and the hand holding it had known all along that she was innocent.

If Barriss was lying, then Ahsoka was lying on a table thinking these things about the man who'd just set her free, because a murderer had asked her to.

She reached for the Force again, not toward anything, just to feel it there. It came, the way it had come every time since Juko cut the collar off on Florrum, quick and whole and almost too much. It gave her the droid's small electric attention. It gave her the building, layer on layer of clerks and guards and sleeping prisoners. It gave her, one floor above, the familiar banked heat of Anakin, waiting.

It did not give her an answer.

"Treatment complete," the droid said. "Zero-three-oh-seven. Do not scratch."

She sat up. The numbness had spread down into her collarbone. She thanked the droid, which did not reply, and took the lift to the waiting room on level two.

Anakin was standing at the narrow window with his back to the door and a cup of caf going cold on the ledge beside him. The traffic lanes outside made long slow streaks of light across the glass. He turned when she came in, and his whole face changed, the tiredness falling out of it all at once.

"There you are," he said. "They told me medical would take twenty minutes. That was forty minutes ago. I was about to go down there and start disassembling droids."

"It was mostly paperwork," she said. "And a lecture about my jaw."

"Your jaw's fine. Your jaw's always been fine. It's the things that come *out* of it." But he was looking at the fresh salve on her neck, and his mechanical hand flexed once inside the dark glove, and he didn't make the next joke. "How was it? Down there."

She had been ready for the question. She still wasn't ready.

"She wanted to say things she couldn't say in front of the Senate," Ahsoka said.

"Like what?"

The words were right there. *She says the Chancellor knew. She says he sat on that dais knowing I was innocent. She says he sent her to make sure I never got the chance to prove it.* She looked at Anakin's face, worn thin by months of searching and one night of almost killing someone, and lit from the inside by the afternoon in the Senate, by a full pardon, by the Chancellor's hand on his shoulder and his name said warmly in front of everyone. He had spent half a year being grateful to that man. It was holding him together.

*You've seen what he does with things he can't bear.*
