"Mostly that she was sorry," Ahsoka said. It was not a lie. It was not the truth either. It sat in her mouth like a stone.

Anakin's jaw set. "Good," he said, hard. Then, more quietly, "Good. She should be."

"Can we go?"

"Yes. Stars, yes." He picked up the cold caf, looked at it, put it down again. "Obi-Wan got you a guest room at the Temple. Before you say anything — it's one night, it's a bed, nobody's going to ask you to bow to anyone. The Chancellor offered rooms at the Chancellery, but I thought —" He shrugged, a little sheepish. "I thought you'd had enough of being somebody's guest."

"You thought right."

They went down together. At the main doors a Guard trooper scanned Anakin's code and then hers, and the door log chirped twice, and the night air hit her face — wet, metallic, smelling of a hundred thousand speeders — and she stood on the platform outside the Military Operations Center and breathed it in like she'd been underwater.

She was free. It was zero-three-thirty-one, and she was free, and she had never in her life felt less safe.

* * *

Obi-Wan came to the guest room door a little after dawn. Ahsoka had slept perhaps two hours, badly. She opened the door before he could knock, because she had felt him coming down the corridor, and she had felt that something was very wrong with him.

He was dressed and composed and his beard was neat. His eyes were not.

"Ahsoka," he said. "I'm sorry to wake you."

"You didn't."

"No. I suppose not." He held himself very still. "Barriss Offee was found dead in her cell this morning, at the zero-six-hundred check."

She heard the words. For a moment they did nothing at all. Then her hand found the doorframe on its own.

"How," she said.

"Walk with me," said Obi-Wan. "The Council has asked for you. You were the last person to see her."

The Council chamber at this hour was full of cold early light. Most of the seats were filled, a few by holograms. Yoda sat with his eyes closed and both hands on his stick. Plo Koon looked at the floor, which seemed to be where he looked whenever she entered this room now. Mace Windu did not look at the floor. Anakin was already there, standing at the edge of the circle, still in yesterday's clothes; when she came in his eyes found her and held on.

Commander Fox's image stood in the center of the chamber, blue and flickering, and gave his report in the same flat voice he used for everything.

The prisoner had been found on her bench at the zero-six-hundred check, sitting against the wall. The medical examiner had found her windpipe crushed. No marks of hands, no ligature, no sign of a struggle. The field across her cell door had never dropped; its own power-draw record, which ran on a separate circuit, showed no break all night. The night guard on the junction had seen nothing and heard nothing. "Consistent," Fox said, "with strangulation through the Force."

"The logs," Windu said. "All of them?"

"No, sir." Fox paused, the tiniest hitch. "There's a gap in the detention-level records. Cameras, door sensors, the junction guard's channel. Zero-two-forty-one to zero-two-fifty-two. Eleven minutes. Every system on the level, at once. The techs are calling it a power-regulation fault. They have not found the fault."

Eleven minutes. *Ten. Not eleven.* Ahsoka's stomach turned over slowly.

"The former Padawan Tano's visit," Ki-Adi-Mundi said.

"Ended at zero-zero-fifty-eight, sir. Twelve minutes, supervised. She was in medical from zero-two-twenty-five to zero-three-oh-seven. The droid's treatment log covers her." Fox's helmet turned, very slightly. "General Skywalker was logged into the building at nineteen-ten. Logged out at zero-three-thirty-one, with her. Between those times he was in the level-two waiting area."

"Alone," Windu said.

"Alone, sir."

Nobody said anything. Nobody needed to. Ahsoka watched it happen, the way you watch a crack travel through ice: Ki-Adi-Mundi's eyes going to Anakin and away. Shaak Ti's hologram turning her head. Saesee Tiin, unreadable, still somehow leaning. Plo Koon's mask lifting from the floor at last — to Anakin, and then, briefly, to her. Obi-Wan, beside her, not looking at Anakin at all, and that was somehow the worst of them.

Windu said it anyway.

"She came out of that warehouse with bruises around her throat," he said. "She told Master Kenobi she'd had a fall."

"I know what she said," Anakin said. His voice was quiet and very dangerous.

"Then you understand why this Council has to ask."

"Ask, then."

"Mace," Obi-Wan said.

Windu's eyes didn't move from Anakin. "Where were you between zero-two-forty-one and zero-two-fifty-two, Skywalker?"

"In a chair," Anakin said. "In a room. Waiting for her. Like I was asked to."

"By whom?"

"The Chancellor."

Something passed around the chamber, too faint to name. Yoda opened his eyes.

"Enough, for now," Yoda said. "Grief is in this room, and fear. Neither one sees well." He looked at Ahsoka. "The prisoner. Said anything to you, did she?"

Every face turned to her. Anakin's, too.

*He won't believe you. And if he does —*

"She said she was sorry," Ahsoka said. "She asked me to light candles for the people she killed."

It was true. She hated how easily it came out.

Yoda held her eyes for a long moment, and she had the uncomfortable feeling that he heard the shape of the thing she had left out, if not the thing itself. Then he nodded, slow, and closed his eyes again.

When the Council released her, Anakin had already gone. She went out into the corridor and found it nearly empty, the long windows full of morning, and halfway down, on a stone bench under one of them, Luminara Unduli sat alone with a lightsaber hilt in her lap.

Barriss's hilt. Ahsoka had handed it over herself, in the warehouse.

She didn't mean to stop. She stopped.

Luminara looked up. Her face was composed the way the surface of deep water is composed. Only her hands gave her away; they were holding the hilt as if it might be taken from her.

"She asked to see you," Luminara said. "Not me."

"I'm sorry."

"Don't be. She was right to." Luminara turned the hilt over once. "Did she say anything about me?"

Ahsoka thought of the long whispered confession, of *Master Luminara told me things in confidence.* None of that was hers to hand over in a corridor.

"She said you knew," Ahsoka said. "Before anyone. That you were waiting for her to tell you."

Luminara closed her eyes.

"I left my door lit for her," she said. "The night she went down there. I told her I would be in my quarters, if that changed." Her thumb moved along the hilt. "I left it lit again last night. I don't know why. Habit, I suppose."

Ahsoka stood there a moment longer. There was nothing to say. She went on down the corridor, and behind her Luminara did not move.

* * *

She found Anakin where she'd known she would, in the rebuilt hangar, in the bay where the bomb had gone off. The new floor panels were a slightly different grey from the old ones. You only noticed if you knew. He was sitting on an empty munitions crate with his elbows on his knees and his gloved hands hanging between them, staring at the seam.

She sat down on the crate beside him. It creaked. For a while neither of them said anything, and the hangar went on around them, techs and droids and the high echoing clatter of a gunship being serviced three bays down.

"Did you?" she said.

She'd thought about how to ask it all the way down from the Council chamber. In the end there wasn't a gentler way, and he'd have hated a gentler way.

He didn't flinch. He didn't get angry, which frightened her more than anger would have. He turned his head and looked at her, and his eyes were red-rimmed and very, very tired.

"No," he said.

She waited.

"I sat in that room for eight hours," he said. "And I thought about her. I'm not going to tell you I didn't. I thought about the warehouse. I thought about how it felt, holding her up there." His right hand closed slowly, metal under the leather, and opened again. "It felt *easy,* Snips. That's the part I keep — it was the easiest thing I've ever done. And I sat in that chair and I thought, she's two floors down. And I didn't go. I drank terrible caf and I watched the traffic and I waited for you." His voice cracked straight down the middle. "I didn't touch her. I swear it. I swear it on you."

She reached out with the Force, the way she'd reached for Barriss the night before, and laid it against him.

There was so much there. Grief like a wound left open to the air. Shame, old and new. Fear — of himself, of the Council, of her looking at him the way the Council had. Rage, banked low and always, the way it had been since she'd known him. And underneath all of it, simple and solid as the floor under her boots, nothing hidden. Nothing that slid away from her touch. She had spent three years learning the shape of him. She knew what Anakin felt like when he was lying; she had felt it in a dozen small fibs about mission reports and late returns. This wasn't that.

"I believe you," she said.

He made a sound that wasn't quite a laugh and put his face in his hands.

"They don't," he said into his palms. "Did you see them? Obi-Wan wouldn't even look at me."

"I saw."

"Eleven minutes, and I was in the building, and I'd already —" He stopped. "I'd already done it once. In front of you. Of course they think it was me. *I'd* think it was me."

She put her hand on his back, between his shoulder blades, and felt him shaking. She left it there. She was thinking, with a kind of cold clarity that seemed to come from somewhere outside her, that he was right. Anyone looking at the records would think it was him. Eleven minutes, on exactly the night he'd been asked to sit alone in that building. Bruises already around her throat that everyone had seen, and a Jedi Master who had suspected him of putting them there. If someone had wanted to kill Barriss Offee and leave a single name standing in the wreckage, they could not have built it better.

*He puts people exactly where he wants them.*

She didn't want to think it. She thought it anyway, and the thought had teeth.

"What did she really say?" Anakin asked. He'd lifted his head. "Last night. You've got that face."

"What face?"

"The one you used to make when you'd broken something on the ship and were hoping I wouldn't find it."

*Tell him.* She opened her mouth. *Barriss said the Chancellor knew. Barriss said —* And she saw it as clearly as if it had already happened: Anakin on his feet, white around the mouth, either refusing her outright or, worse, *not* refusing, going up the long corridors to that red office with the dark rising off him like heat off a reactor. On the very morning half the Council was wondering if he was a murderer.

"She said she wished she'd been braver," Ahsoka said. "About a lot of things."

He looked at her a beat too long. Then he nodded, and let it go, because he was too tired not to.

His comlink chimed. He fumbled it out of his belt, and a small blue figure rose above his palm: Dace Orrin, the Chancellor's aide, his young face arranged in careful sympathy.

"General Skywalker. The Chancellor asked that you hear this from his office before it goes to the Holonet." The aide's voice was gentle and practiced. "In light of the circumstances, the Chancellor has personally closed the inquiry into the death of the prisoner Offee, and ordered the detention-level records sealed. He feels very strongly that the Republic must protect a hero of the Republic from baseless suspicion at such a painful time. He sends his warmest regards, and asks you not to trouble yourself further."

The image flickered out.

Anakin sat there through three slow breaths with the dark comlink in his hand. Then he let out a breath that seemed to come all the way up from the floor of him, and Ahsoka felt it go through him in the Force — relief, enormous and helpless, like a man pulled out of deep water.

"He closed it," Anakin said. His voice was unsteady. "Before the Council could even —" He laughed, once, raggedly, and pressed the comlink to his forehead. "Snips. He didn't even ask me whether I did it."

Ahsoka kept her hand on his back. She made it stay still.

*He didn't need to,* she thought.

Somebody had walked into a sealed detention level in the middle of the night and killed Barriss without opening a door, in eleven unrecorded minutes, a few hours after a girl had promised to answer her tomorrow. Somebody had made the cameras forget. And now somebody had made sure that no one, ever, would be allowed to ask who.

Beside her, Anakin was smiling at the place where the bomb had gone off, his eyes wet, grateful with everything he had left.

Ahsoka had stood in front of a firing line of Guard troopers, and a Trandoshan with an ice pick, and Cad Bane. She had never in her life been as afraid as she was now, sitting on a crate in the bright Temple hangar, with her hand on her master's back and a dead friend's last secret in her mouth, unsaid.

## Chapter 29: The Steps

The fifth candle would not light.

Ahsoka struck the igniter a third time. The flame jumped, caught the wick, shivered, and drowned in its own wax. Behind her the rebuilt hangar was still dim with the hour before dawn, the starfighters asleep in their cradles, a single maintenance droid ticking along the far wall. In front of her, on the low shelf beneath the wall of names, four candles were already burning in a row. Those had lit on the first try.

One for Jackar Bowmani, who had carried a bomb into this room and never known it. Three for CT-2261, CT-4408 and CT-9170, who had died outside a cell door with her lightsabers in someone else's hands.

They were Barriss's candles. For five months Barriss had come down here before the Temple woke and lit four flames without names, and no one had asked her who they were for. Ahsoka had wondered once, at Narkis, and gotten the answer wrong.

*Will you light them?* Barriss had asked, in the voice the choke had left her. *Someone should.*

*Ask me tomorrow.*

Twelve minutes. Then a corridor, a medical bay, a narrow bed in Temple guest quarters, and Obi-Wan in the doorway at first light with a face that had already said it before he did.

It had been tomorrow for a full day now. She had spent most of that day unable to come here.

She pinched the drowned wick straight and tried again. This time the flame took, small and stubborn, and stood up.

There was nothing to write beside it. Barriss's name would never go on the wall above; she was not one of the honored dead, and no one in the Order would ever argue that she should be. So Ahsoka left the fifth candle the way Barriss had left hers. Unmarked. Burning.

She sat back on her heels. Her left forearm still pulled where the saber burn was healing. Her neck didn't hurt at all anymore, which was the strangest part — five months of cold metal, and the skin had nearly forgotten. Her fingers went to it anyway, every few minutes, checking for something that wasn't there.

A woman in the deep blue of the hangar families came in while the light was still grey. She was human, older, a scarf over her hair. She saw Ahsoka and stopped, and Ahsoka watched her recognize the face from five months of Holonet bulletins. The woman stood very still. Then she walked the length of the shelf, lit a candle of her own beneath one of the names at the far end, and stood with it a while. When she left, she did not look at Ahsoka. She had not left early, either.

Ahsoka found she could breathe around that.

She felt Plo Koon before she heard him: the old shape of him in the Force, steady as weather she had grown up under. He came down the line of fighters without hurrying and stopped a respectful distance away.

"I came to light one for her," he said. The antiox mask gave his voice its faint, breathing hiss. "I see I am late."

"There's room."

He lowered himself to one knee beside her, a long way down for a Kel Dor, and lit a sixth candle from her fifth. For a while neither of them spoke. The flames leaned together in the draft from the hangar mouth.

"Before the vote," Plo said, "I told myself that the Republic's law deserved its chance to be fair to you. I said so to Master Windu. I said it very wisely." His clawed hands folded and unfolded on his knee. "Then I sat in my chair and looked at the floor while they took you out of the room. I have thought about that floor every day since. I know its pattern better than my own hands."

Ahsoka didn't answer. She didn't trust what would come out.

"I found you once," he said, "in the grass on Shili. You bit me."

"I was three."

"You had excellent teeth." Something behind the mask might have been a smile. Then it was gone. "I found you when you were too small to know you needed finding. And when you were old enough to know, and you needed it, I did not come. I am sorry, Little 'Soka. There is nothing I can put behind the word to make it larger. I am only sorry."

*Little 'Soka.* No one had called her that since she'd grown taller than the training remotes. In the Deep, in the dark of the Well, she had not let herself remember voices like his. Remembering had been a kind of warmth she couldn't afford.

She put her hand over his. The claws were cool and careful under her palm, the way they had always been careful.

"I tried to be angry with you," she said. "At Narkis there was a lot of time to try." She looked at the six small flames. "It never fit. You were the one person I could never make it fit on."

"You may yet."

"Maybe." She pressed his hand once and let go. "Not today."

He bowed his head. When he rose it was slowly, one hand on the shelf. "The Council will send for you at the third hour. You should know what they mean to offer before you walk in. It would not be fair to let you be surprised in that room twice."

"I know what they're going to offer," Ahsoka said.

Plo looked down at her through the dark lenses for a long moment. Whatever he felt in her then, he didn't ask about it. He only touched the top of her head between her montrals, the way he had when his hand could cover her whole crown, and left her with the candles.

* * *

The Council chamber had not changed. That was the thing she hadn't prepared for.

The same ring of high-backed chairs. The same tall windows full of Coruscant, the traffic lanes stacked into the haze like thread on a spindle. The same circle of floor at the center, worn pale by generations of Padawans standing exactly where she now stood. Five months ago she had stood there in binders. Her wrists remembered. She kept her hands loose at her sides so no one would see them wanting to find each other.

Most of the chairs were filled in person this time. Yoda, small and very still. Windu, upright as a pillar. Ki-Adi-Mundi, Saesee Tiin, Shaak Ti. Plo, who had his head up today. Obi-Wan, with his hands folded and his eyes on her and not, for once, on the door. Anakin was not there. He wasn't on the Council and no one had asked him to come, and after the silence in this room yesterday morning, she understood that hadn't been an accident.

"Ahsoka Tano." Windu let her name sit in the air a moment before he went on. "The confession of Barriss Offee, the recording entered at tribunal, and the depositions of Commandant Vorne and her staff establish that you were innocent of every charge brought against you. This Council expelled you on the strength of those charges. We were wrong."

He didn't soften it and he didn't dress it. He set it down like a man setting down something heavy that he fully intended to carry again later.

"The Council has voted to restore you to the Order," he said. "In light of your conduct on Narkis and since, we have also voted to waive the trials. You would be raised to the rank of Knight."

She felt it go around the room in the Force. Warmth — real, and from more than one chair. Shaak Ti's, open and kind. Plo's, steady. Obi-Wan's, carefully contained and almost hopeful. A year ago, that word would have been the whole sky.

She thought of what a Knight was, in this war. Knights got battalions. She thought of the image Fives had shown them in Padmé's sitting room, the small pale shape grown into every clone's head since before they could open their eyes. She thought of Tup with his hand pressed to his temple, and three hundred men standing to attention in front of her, waiting for orders, and her not knowing who else could give them orders, or what those orders would be.

And she thought of a ruined voice in a cell three doors from her old one. *He knew. From the very beginning.*

"Thank you," she said. "I mean that. But I'm not going to accept."

The warmth in the room went very quiet.

"May we know why?" Ki-Adi-Mundi asked.

"If it's the vote—" Windu began.

"It isn't." She met his eyes. "You took my beads yourself, Master Windu. You said someone who'd voted for it should have to be the one. I hated you for that for about a month." She let out a breath. "Then I decided it was the most honest thing anybody in this room did that day. I'm not refusing so you'll feel it. I don't think it would work on you anyway."

Something moved at the corner of Windu's mouth and went away again.

"Something you carry." Yoda's ears were low, his hands crossed on the head of his stick. "Since the night the healer died, I have felt it. Heavy. And you have decided to carry it alone."

"Yes, Master."

"Tell us, will you?"

"Not yet."

Obi-Wan stirred. "Ahsoka—"

"Five months ago I stood in a room a lot like this one and told the truth," she said. Her voice came out level, which surprised her. "I didn't have anything to hold it up with. It fell over, and it took me with it. Now I have something I think might be true. If it is, it's bigger than me. Bigger than Barriss. And all I have to hold it up with is the word of a dead woman who lied to my face for five months. If I bring it in here like that, you'll weigh it, and you'll be right to, and it'll fall over again." She looked around the ring. "I'd rather bring it to you when it can stand."

"And you cannot find that proof as one of us?" Shaak Ti asked gently.

Ahsoka thought about how to say it so it would be true without being cruel.

"A Knight reports to this Council," she said. "This Council answers to the Senate and the Chancellor's office for every battalion and every ship, every week. Anything I found, I'd find from inside that. I need to look at it from outside first." She hesitated. "And some of what I'd be asking about — I don't think you could hear it yet. Not because you're bad. Because you're tired, and at war, and the war needs the answers it already has."

That landed. She felt it land. She felt Windu go still in a way that was not anger, and something in Yoda turn toward her and stay turned.

"That," Windu said, "is a dangerous thing to say in this room."

"I know. I said it anyway." She almost smiled. "That might be the part of me you'd want back, if you wanted any of it."

Obi-Wan cleared his throat. "In fairness, it has been a difficult year for listening."

"One more thing." Ahsoka made herself say it before she lost the nerve. "Whatever this Council is thinking about Anakin. He didn't kill her."

The room cooled by a degree she could feel on her skin.

"The inquiry has been closed," Ki-Adi-Mundi said.

"By the Chancellor," said Windu. Only that. Three words, flat as a blade laid on a table. Ahsoka looked at him, and for one heartbeat she thought — *you feel the shape of it too.* Then he looked away toward the windows, and the moment closed.

"I hope you're right," Obi-Wan said quietly. "About Anakin. I would like very much for you to be right."

"I usually am," Ahsoka said. "It just takes everyone a while."

Plo made a sound behind his mask that might, in a Kel Dor, have been a laugh.

Yoda tapped his stick once against the floor. "Go, then. Not as Jedi. Not as stranger, either." His great eyes held hers, and they were sadder than she had ever seen them. "Outside the walls, you may see further than we can. Come back, and tell us what you find. Listen, we will try."

She bowed to him — properly, the full Temple bow her body still knew better than her mind did — and walked out of the circle on her own feet.

In the corridor outside, a Padawan runner of perhaps twelve was waiting with a grimy square of flimsi held at arm's length, as if it might bite.

"This came to the public gate by relay courier," he said. "From the Undercroft. The Temple Guard scanned it four times. Master Windu said to give it to you before you left, because if it was going to explode he'd prefer it did that outside."

The outside of the fold said, in a sharp, slanting hand: TO THE JEDI WHO ISN'T.

Ahsoka opened it.

*So they asked you back. Of course they did. They always want the knife back once they've finished cutting themselves with it.*

*Say no. Not for my sake. You'd hate it, and you'd be terrible at hating it quietly, and eventually I'd have to hear about it.*

*I have my sabers. I have no further use for you. We're even, which is generous of me. Somewhere there's a Jedi Master still combing ore out of his beard. Tell him I think of him often.*

*Don't come looking for me. I'll know.*

*If someone hurts you again, I may come looking for them. Don't read anything into that.*

*— V.*

Ahsoka laughed out loud in the middle of the Temple corridor. The runner flinched. She folded the flimsi twice and put it inside her coat, against her ribs, where it crackled every time she breathed.

* * *

Kix's hands were shaking.

Rex watched them from the surgical couch, because there was nothing else worth watching in the Resolute's medbay at twenty-three hundred, with the ship cold in its cradle at the fleet yards and most of her crew on leave. The overheads were down to a third. On the tray at Kix's elbow lay a hypo of local anesthetic, a micro-probe, a laser scalpel no longer than a finger, and Rex's old cadet datapad, the cracked screen still showing the last image Kix had pulled from Fives's files: a scan of a clone's skull and, inside it, small and neat as a seed, a shape that had no business being there.

"Say it again," Kix said.

"It's in me," Rex said. "It's in you. It's in Jesse. It was in Tup."

Kix's jaw worked. He'd read the files for three hours without a word — the embryo records, the scan logs, the droid's notes — then played the forty-second video once, gone into the head, and been sick. Rex had heard it through the hatch. Kix had come back with his face washed and his eyes hard and said, "On the couch, Captain," and that had been twenty minutes ago. Since then he'd scanned Rex twice, checked the result against Fives's images three times, and not touched the scalpel.

"A droid did this," Kix said. "A Kaminoan medical droid with a surgical arm and fifty years of their own research. Not a field medic with a cutting laser in the middle of the night."

"Fives is walking around."

"Fives is lucky."

"Tup wasn't," Rex said.

Kix looked at the scan, then at the side of Rex's head, then at his own hands. "If I go a hair wide—"

"Then you write down what you did wrong, and you do Jesse better." Rex kept his voice flat, the voice he used on a comm channel when the line was coming apart. "That's an order. The writing it down part."

"That's not funny."

"Wasn't trying."

He hadn't done this at the Seventh Refinery. Fives had all but begged him, shivering in the outflow chamber with his shoulder gone bad, and Rex had said *not yet*. He'd told himself he needed proof, needed time, needed the Commander cleared first. Then the tribunal. Then Barriss Offee dead in a cell whose door had never opened, and the Chancellor's aide on the Holonet closing the matter before the Council could sit down to discuss it. Something about the speed of that had gone into Rex like cold through the seams of his armor.

He had stood in a gallery five months ago and said nothing while they sentenced her. He had knocked Fox's arm and then let his report say nothing about it. He was done waiting to find out what he would do with an order until after it came.

"Kix," he said. "Do it, or give me the scalpel and a mirror."

Kix picked up the hypo.

The anesthetic went in cold above Rex's right ear and spread until that side of his head felt like it belonged to someone in the next room. Kix lined up the scanner, muttered numbers, and set the probe. There was no pain. There was pressure, and then a sound Rex heard from inside his own skull, small and gritty, like a boot shifting on gravel. He fixed his eyes on a rivet in the overhead and counted its edges.

The probe trembled against him.

"Steady," Rex said.

"I am steady."

"You're shaking."

"I'm steady on the inside, Captain. Shut up."

Rex shut up. He listened to Kix breathe — ragged, then slower, then slower again, until the breathing was something a man could set a chrono by. The trembling at the side of his head stopped. Kix was counting under his breath now, very low, the way he counted compressions on a battlefield. Rex had heard him count like that on Umbara. Over Tup. Over men who lived and men who didn't.

A click, small and precise.

"Got it," Kix said, and his voice cracked straight down the middle.

He held up the forceps. Pinched in the tips was a thing no bigger than a fingernail paring, pale grey shot faintly with pink, organic and somehow neat. It didn't look like much. It didn't look like anything that could make a man shoot a Jedi who was kneeling to check on him.

Kix dropped it into a specimen vial, sealed the cap, and then sat down on the deck rather suddenly, with his back against the cabinet.

Rex waited to feel different.

He didn't. That was the worst of it, and maybe the best. The medbay looked the same. The rivet looked the same. Somewhere far back in his head there was no sudden silence, no lifting weight, nothing at all to tell him what the thing had been doing all these years — whether it had ever leaned on him, or only waited. He couldn't tell which of his old orders had been his. He decided, lying there with half his head numb, that it didn't much matter anymore. The next ones would be.

Kix got up eventually, sealed the incision, and smoothed a bacta patch over it with his thumb. "How do you feel?"

"Like I've got a hole in my head."

"You do."

"Then the medic's accurate. Good to know."

Kix almost laughed. He labeled the vial in his small square print — CT-7567 — looked at it, scratched the number out, and wrote REX instead. Then he hid it at the back of the refrigerated cabinet behind a rack of expired fever shots, where nobody on this ship would ever look.

"Who else knows?" he asked, with his back still turned.

"A dead man," Rex said. "A Senator. A bounty hunter who'd prefer you never mention her. And the Commander."

Kix turned around. "The Commander. Then she's—"

"Leaving tonight."

"With him." Kix didn't say the name. He didn't have to. Something in his face eased, and then set. "I'm next."

"Not tonight."

"No. Not tonight." Kix looked down at his hands, which were quiet now. "Somebody with steady hands has to do me, and it isn't going to be you."

"We'll find someone."

"You keep saying *we*, Captain."

"Get used to it," Rex said.

At midnight his comlink chirped on the dead Guard fault channel: a routine power-cell error, a fault class retired years ago, signed at the bottom in the old cipher. Rex read the signature twice.

*5555.*

All four digits. None of them flipped. Whole.

Rex winced at it anyway, the way he did every night. Fives was still using the Guard's own band to tell him he was alive. A dead fault class was a good hiding place right up until some bored tech in Fox's comm section ran an audit and wondered why a retired error kept turning up at midnight.

Rex keyed the acknowledgment. Then, against every rule he'd set for this channel, he added a line of plain text and sent it before he could think better of it.

*Tell her her sense of direction is still terrible. Tell her I said so.*

He lay back on the couch with the numbness fading from the side of his head and, for the first time he could remember, listened to the inside of his own skull, and found it quieter than he had expected. Quieter, and entirely his.

* * *

Anakin found her on the Temple steps at the second hour of night, with a grey Judiciary evidence case under his arm and its seal already broken.

She had been sitting halfway down for a while, her pack beside her, watching the lanes stream past the Processional Way. The wind up here came off the city in long warm gusts that smelled of ozone and engine heat. Far across the haze, the Senate district glittered. She had been trying not to pick out one particular window high in the Executive Building, glowing a deep, comfortable red. She kept finding it anyway.

Anakin sat down a step above her without being asked, the way he always had.

"Fox made me sign for these four times," he said. "Four. I think he enjoyed it."

"Fox doesn't enjoy anything."

"He enjoyed that." He opened the case across his knees.

They lay in grey foam, side by side, each still wearing its yellow tag. *#417. #418.* She hadn't held them since the night Fox took them from her at a cell door.

Ahsoka lifted the green one out first. The hilt settled into her palm like a word she'd been trying to remember. Through it, faintly, the crystal knew her — and beneath that, like a cold spot in deep water, it remembered what someone else's hand had made it do outside her cell. Three men. She held it until the cold spot was just a part of it, the way a scar is part of skin. Then she took the shoto, and the two of them together were the right weight at last, and she clipped them on her belt and could not, for a moment, say anything.

"There's one more." Anakin reached into the case's lid and drew out the brushed-steel hilt with the dented emitter and the worn black grip. "You handed me this one in the rain. I'm told it belongs to a pirate."

She took it from him. It was heavier than hers. It always would be.

"I'll keep it a while," she said. "Someone gentle carried this before Hondo got his hands on it. He's going to tell me who, even if I have to sit through six versions first. Then I'll give it back."

"You're going to Florrum."

"Eventually. It's a big galaxy." She slid the blue saber into the inner pocket of her coat, next to Ventress's letter and the datacard Padmé had pressed into her hand at the door. One string of characters on it, copied out of Vorne's deposition: the off-world authorization code on the order that had sent her to Cut Three in her first week, to a terrace whose camera nobody had fixed in two years. Padmé's people could not trace it. Neither, apparently, could the Judiciary. *Somebody traded somebody something,* Tess had said, that first week, and Ahsoka still did not know who had done the trading, or for what. Florrum meant Hondo's, and Hondo's meant Tess Varro, still mending in one of his back rooms; Ahsoka found she didn't yet know what she would say to her. "I've got time now. That's new."

He was quiet. Below them an air-taxi idled at the foot of the steps, its running lights pulsing amber. She had asked it to wait. It was costing her credits she had borrowed from Padmé, and the driver had stopped looking patient some time ago.

"They offered you Knighthood," Anakin said.

"They did."

"Obi-Wan told me. He said it to the wall behind my head, but he told me." Anakin turned his mechanical hand over on his knee, the glove creased at the knuckles. "I would've stood up there with you. You know that."

"You'd have cried."

"I would not."

"You'd have cried in front of Master Windu, and he'd have pretended not to see it, and it would've been the best day of his life."

That got a laugh out of him, a short broken one. It didn't last. "So where?"

"Somewhere I can think." She picked her words. "Somebody's waiting for me."

"Your friend who smells like bacta."

She looked at him. He looked back, steadily, and she saw that he had known for days and had chosen, every one of those days, not to pull on the thread.

"Don't ask," she said.

He didn't.

"Tell Senator Amidala thank you for me," Ahsoka said. "For the tea."

Something passed behind his eyes, quick as a shutter. "I'll see that she gets it."

She didn't ask how he planned to reach a Senator at this hour. She let it go by, the way he had let the bacta go by. It seemed fair.

The wind gusted. Across the city the red window burned on, patient as a coal. She had carried it for two days in her mouth, unsaid, and it had grown heavier with every hour, and she had come out here tonight knowing she would not leave Coruscant with it still there.

"Barriss told me something," she said. "The night she died."

"She told you she was sorry. You said."

"That's what I told you. It wasn't all she said." She made herself look at him. "She said the Chancellor knew. From the beginning. That he had a recording of her killing Letta before my trial ever started, and he sealed it and sat on it. That he used her. That he's the one who sent her to the warehouse to make sure I never testified."

At first he didn't move at all. Then he laughed — not the broken laugh, something worse, a sound with nothing behind it.

"No," he said.

"Anakin—"

"No. Snips. Don't." He shook his head, slowly, as if something had struck him there. "He's the only one who did anything. The Council threw you out. The Senate wanted you dead. He's the one who stood up in front of all of them and—"

"Said *death*," Ahsoka said, "so he could say *life*. So you'd be grateful."

"He gave you your life."

"He gave me my life back after he watched them take it. He forgave me for something he knew I didn't do." It came out quieter than she meant. "That's not a gift. That's a leash, and it isn't even on me."

"She was lying." His voice rose, then caught. "She lied to you for five months. To your face. She healed your shoulder while she was letting you rot. That was the last thing she had left to do to anyone, and she did it to you."

"Maybe."

"She *was*."

"Maybe," Ahsoka said again. "I don't know. That's why I'm going." She turned on the step so she was facing him. "I'm not asking you to believe it. I'm asking you to be careful of him. Watch what he asks for. Watch what it costs, and who pays. That's all. Just watch."

He stared past her at the city. She felt him in the Force the way she had in the canyon, stones lifting at the edges of him — but nothing lifted tonight. He was holding all of it down, and it was costing him.

"I can't hear this," he said at last. "Not about him. Not from you. Don't ask me to."

"I know you can't." She kept her voice gentle. "Hold onto it anyway. Put it somewhere you don't look. Someday you might need it."

He didn't answer.

But somewhere in it he had taken her hand. She wasn't sure when. He'd taken it with his right, the metal one, which couldn't feel her at all; she could feel the servos in his knuckles shift and tighten. He didn't believe her. He didn't let go.

She let that be enough. It was more than she'd had in a long time.

They sat like that while the lanes ran on overhead, two small figures on the long white steps, until the air-taxi driver leaned on his horn. Ahsoka stood and shouldered her pack. Anakin stood with her, and still didn't let go, and so they went down together — one step, and the next, and the next, the way you walk someone home. He didn't speak, and neither did she. There wasn't anything left to say that the hand didn't.

"Look after Rex," she said at the bottom.

"Rex looks after me."

"Then let him."

He nodded once. His grip tightened, and for a heartbeat she thought he wouldn't loosen it at all — and then she stepped off the last stair onto the plaza, and the distance between the Temple and the city did what neither of them could.

Somewhere below, at Berth Nine, a scrap hauler with a dead man aboard was warming its engines. Somewhere above, a red window glowed. She was free, and she was not in exile, and she had chosen both. And she knew, with a certainty that sat in her as calm and cold as ice, that the galaxy was darker than anyone behind her on those steps believed.

Twice now the Republic had given its verdict on Ahsoka Tano — guilty, and then forgiven — and both times it had been wrong. With her own sabers at her hip and his grip still warm in her palm, she walked into the city and gave her own.

*Not guilty. Not forgiven. Not theirs.*

## Chapter 30: Epilogue — The Long Game

The aide's wrist no longer troubled him, and the Chancellor found that he noticed it every time.

Dace Orrin set the morning's datapads on the corner of the desk in a neat fan, as he always did, and squared their edges with two fingers of his left hand, which he could not have done four months ago without wincing. The healer had done good work that afternoon. She had always done good work. It seemed a small, fitting irony that the last trace of her in this office was a young man's joint that bent cleanly and without pain, and that the young man had no idea whose hands had mended it, or what had been agreed over tea while he waited outside.

"The Judiciary Committee's vote, sir," Orrin said, touching the first pad. "Passed fourteen to six. The Admiral is in the outer office. He asked whether you'd see him before the session or after."

"Before. He will want to enjoy it, and I should like to watch."

Orrin smiled politely, not understanding, and went to fetch him.

Outside the tall window the morning traffic stacked itself in its patient lanes, and the light came through the glass the color of weak tea. The Chancellor did not look at the city. He looked at the second datapad, the one Orrin had placed slightly apart from the others because it bore the Temple's courtesy seal, and read it again, although he had read it once already before dawn.

*The Jedi Council wishes to inform the Office of the Supreme Chancellor that former Padawan Ahsoka Tano has declined the offer of reinstatement and elevation to the rank of Jedi Knight, and has departed Coruscant as a private citizen. The Council thanks the Chancellor for his clemency in this matter.*

Declined. He let the word sit on his tongue like a lozenge.

He had expected them to offer. He had not entirely expected her to refuse. A girl of seventeen, raised inside those walls since she was three, given back everything she had lost with an apology and a promotion attached, and she had said no and walked down the steps. He had watched that, too, on a traffic-control feed that no one would ever think to ask him about: two small figures on the long white staircase, the boy holding her hand all the way down, and then only one of them going on. She had not looked back at the Temple. She had, once, looked up — toward the Senate district, toward this window — before the crowd took her.

Interesting.

Tarkin came in with his hat beneath his arm and his face arranged into the precise expression of a man who did not believe in showing pleasure and was showing it anyway.

"Chancellor."

"Admiral. Fourteen to six."

"Fifteen, had Senator Organa not talked a colleague into a recorded abstention." Tarkin did not sit until he was asked, and then sat as if the chair were a post he had been assigned. "The program extends to every Judiciary facility holding a Force-sensitive detainee, and to all future transfers. Eleven installations in the first year. Commandant Vorne has been asked to draft the protocols. She has a talent for rules."

"She has a talent for keeping them," the Chancellor said. "That is rarer. Keep her close, Wilhuff. Close enough that she never wonders whom she serves."

"Of course." A pause, very slight. "The Narkis incident has been useful. The Senate saw what a single unregulated Jedi could do with a collar removed — four minutes, an ice shelf held in the air. Several of them asked, quite reasonably, what a dozen could do. Or a hundred."

"And what did you tell them?"

"That the Republic would never need to find out." Tarkin's thin mouth moved. "I did not say that the Republic should be prepared in case it did."

"You didn't need to." The Chancellor folded his hands. "You've done this Office a considerable service, Admiral, from the first day of the first tribunal to this morning's vote. I don't forget service. When the war ends, there will be territories that need a firm hand more than a popular one. I should like to see that hand be yours."

Tarkin inclined his head. That was all. He was too disciplined to thank a man for a promise, which was exactly why the promise had been made to him and not to someone warmer.

When he had gone, Orrin put his head back in. "General Skywalker sent a message, sir. He wanted you to know he's returned to the Resolute, and — he said to thank you again. For the inquiry."

"Did he."

"He said you'd know what he meant."

"I always do," the Chancellor said kindly, and Orrin withdrew.

He did know. The boy had stood in this room the night before last, still in yesterday's tunic, grey with the kind of exhaustion that is half grief and half fear, and told him how the Council had looked at him. Not what they had said; they had said nothing. Only how they had looked, when the Guard commander read out his entry and exit times and the building fell quiet. Windu's stillness. Kenobi refusing to meet his eyes. Even the Mirialan master, who had the most cause, looking at him a heartbeat longer than she should have.

*They think I did it,* the boy had said, and his mechanical hand had closed and opened at his side as though it wanted something to hold. *They actually think I could have.*

And the Chancellor had said that he had never thought so, not for one moment, and that this was precisely why he had closed the matter that morning, before anyone could ask: he would not permit a fine young man's name to be dragged through a second inquiry for the sake of a tidier report. He had sealed the records with his own code. And Anakin Skywalker had thanked him with his voice breaking, the way a man thanks the one person who has stood between him and the fire, never once asking who had set it.

The pardon had been one link. The closed door was the second. The boy forged them himself, each one, out of his own gratitude, and fastened them with his own hands. The Chancellor had only to stand still and be grateful in return.

He returned to the window. Somewhere in that stacked haze, the Council was meeting without the boy, and speaking of him in his absence, and thinking the thought none of them would say aloud. Every glance would widen the space between them. Every space would be a place where only one voice reached him.

He was content to let them do the work.

* * *

Night on Coruscant was never dark. It was only a different color of light, and in the rooms beneath the Chancellor's residence, where the walls were older than the office above them and no aide had ever been, he preferred no light at all.

The blue figure that bloomed over the projector stood a little too straight, as Count Dooku always did when he had something to conceal.

"The Outer Rim campaigns proceed as planned, my lord. Grievous has consolidated at the Mygeeto approaches. The Banking Clan's last payment cleared this afternoon."

"Good," said Darth Sidious, and let the silence after the word lengthen until it had a shape. "And Narkis?"

The hologram flickered — signal noise, perhaps. Perhaps not.

"An embarrassment for the Republic," Dooku said smoothly. "The Holonet insists I was behind it. I've seen no reason to correct them. A Jedi convict in my service, even a rumored one, does their morale no good."

"Indeed. And yet she is not in your service."

"She was unwilling. Bounty hunters are an imprecise instrument."

"They are," Sidious agreed. "Which is why one should be careful whose credits pay for them."

He watched the old man's face do nothing at all, which was its own confession. Dooku had been so careful. A private relay; beacons out in Hutt space that no one used; a Duros who drank in places where no one asked questions. He had wanted a sword of his own, a young one, angry, wronged by the Jedi and freshly unchained — a weapon he could sharpen in secret and point, someday, at his own master. It was not a stupid idea. It was simply an old one, and Sidious had been the one to have it first, a long time ago, about a different master.

The girl had refused him. Of course she had. But Dooku had not come home empty-handed. Bane's second hauler had carried a Separatist commander out of the Deep — a schoolteacher's son from Raxus, a man of real convictions, a man who had spent more than a year with the lights always on, thinking. Dooku would be pleased with him. Dooku would tell himself that a man like that could be useful, that ideals were a lever like any other.

Merrick Tal. Sidious filed the name where he filed such things. Let the Count have his toy. It would be instructive, later, to take it away.

"You have nothing else to report, my apprentice?"

"Nothing, my lord."

"Then go."

The hologram folded into itself. Sidious sat in the dark for a while afterward and allowed himself to imagine — idly, not yet in detail — the shape of the lesson. It would not come soon. It would come at the moment when Dooku had begun to believe he had been forgiven, which was the only moment a lesson ever truly taught anything. There was no hurry. There was never any hurry.

He rose and went to the cabinet set into the wall, and the cabinet knew his hand.

Inside, on a square of grey cloth, lay a tiny thing no larger than a seed, dried now to the brown of old leaves. The clone's chip. He had taken it from the Kaminoan's tray himself, the same afternoon the clone came at him, or appeared to; the bruise on his knuckles from that little performance had faded weeks ago. It had cost a great deal to make, this seed, and a great deal more to hide. Millions of its twins rested, quiet and patient, behind millions of identical brows, and each of them held the same single line of instruction, waiting to be read aloud.

This one held nothing anymore. It was only a souvenir of the one soldier who had looked behind his own eyes and found the door.

Beside it in the cabinet lay a datacard holding a single frozen frame from a tribunal gallery: a hooded Mirialan face caught at the instant of sentencing, too stricken to hide what it knew. He had saved that image five months ago because he had seen at once what it would buy him. It had bought a great deal. He looked at it now without regret, the way a man looks at a ledger that has balanced.

He had not needed to open her door.

He remembered that more clearly than the rest — the corridor of the detention level at a quarter to three in the morning, the lights dimmed to their night setting, the floor freshly washed and smelling faintly of it. The single Guard at the junction had looked up as he passed and then down again at nothing, and in the morning he would find in his memory only a small grey stretch, a long blink, a lapse he would be too ashamed to report. Fox would find eleven minutes missing from the logs and assume a fault. A fault was always easier to believe in.

He had stopped at her cell, three doors short of the one where she had once stood herself to visit a friend. Beyond the faint blue field the healer had been sitting on her bench with her back very straight, her ruined throat still dark from the boy's hand, and she had looked up before he ever stepped into the light, as though she had felt him coming down the corridor.

She had known him. He was nearly sure of that. There had been no surprise in her face, only a kind of weary recognition, as if she had already confessed this too, to someone, in her heart. She had not called out. She had not tried to fight. Her hands, he remembered, had been perfectly steady.

The field had stayed lit. The door had not needed to open.

He had come and gone by a route the building's logs had never recorded, and been back in the residence well before dawn, and the boy had been sitting all that while in a waiting room two floors above, because the Chancellor had asked him to stay in the building. The boy had stayed. He always did what he was asked, when it was asked kindly.

A loose end, cut. And the cutting had cast its shadow exactly where it was wanted.

He closed the cabinet and turned to the evening's last reports, which were not the kind that came by courier.

The first was a diagnostic summary from the Coruscant Guard's communications service, routed — as every item touching one particular registry number had been routed for two months — directly to a private terminal no one else could read. A Guard-issue comlink, logged missing on the night CT-5555 had fled into the lower levels and been shot and fallen and been declared, with every proper signature, dead. Body unrecoverable. The Chancellor had never in his life believed in an unrecoverable body.

The comlink had been silent for weeks after the fall. Then, some time ago, it had begun to complain. A power-cell fault ping, regular as breathing, of a class the Guard had retired from its equipment years before — a fault that could not, in fact, occur on that model at all. Each ping carried a small numeric tail that the diagnostic service had dutifully logged as corrupted data. Five-five-five-four, for a while. And lately, as of last night: five-five-five-five.

Someone was telling someone that he was well again.

Sidious read the numbers twice and felt something he had not felt in some time, which was mild and genuine interest. A dead clone with an empty skull, alive, and careful, and talking. And someone, somewhere, answering him — because a man does not keep sending a signal into silence for that long unless he knows it is being heard.

He could have had the pings traced in an hour. He could have had a Guard squad at the source by morning, and Fox would have asked no questions, because Fox never did.

He did not reach for the console.

A thread that believes itself unseen is the most honest thing in the galaxy. It goes home. It goes, without being told, to everyone who has been keeping it secret — and that list, he suspected, would be far more valuable than one feverish trooper. Let the clone run. Let him find whomever he meant to find, and talk to whomever he meant to talk to, and every word he said would draw a little more of the map.

He noted, without urgency, that a scrap hauler had left Berth Nine in the small hours with a Togruta girl aboard who had declined to be a Jedi. He did not yet know whether these two facts were acquainted. He suspected he would enjoy finding out.

The last report was the briefest, and it came from a woman who cleaned offices in a mid-level medical tower and was paid, very modestly, to notice who came and went.

*Senator Amidala of Naboo, cloaked, without staff or droid. Arrived late afternoon by public transit. The practice of Doctor Venn Ashari, fourth floor, private, discreet. Remained forty minutes. Paid in loose credit chips. On leaving, stood a while on the landing before going down.*

Nothing more. The cleaner had not been asked to wonder, and did not.

The Chancellor wondered.

He thought of the senator in her defense robes, so composed, so sharp, spending herself without stint for the boy's Padawan. He thought of the boy's face at the word *pardon*, and of how, all through those months, the boy had always seemed to know her schedule a little before her staff did. He thought of what a man will do — what he will forgive, what he will believe, what he will become — to keep one thing in the galaxy from being taken away from him.

He did not reach a conclusion. He did not need to, yet. He only set that report beside the others and let them rest together in his mind, each in its place: the girl who refused, the clone who would not stay dead, the apprentice who thought himself clever, the admiral with his collars, the senator on a landing in the evening light. And the boy, at the center of it all, holding everyone's hand and believing every hand he held was a friend's.

So many people had been judged this season. A girl condemned, and then forgiven for what she had not done. A healer who had judged herself, and found the sentence carried out before dawn. A boy whom a room full of Jedi had judged with nothing more than their eyes. The Republic had loved every minute of it. It always did. A people that wants someone to be guilty will thank whoever gives them one, and never ask about the price.

He sat back in the dark and thought of the seed on its grey cloth, and of its millions of patient twins, and of the single line each of them carried.

Let them have their verdicts. He had written the sentence years ago.
